# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 29

## Page 14260

VOLUME TWENTY-NINE
BOOK OF JURISPRUDENCE
PART NINE:
SALES

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

As for contracts governing financial transactions, marriage, and other matters, we shall set out comprehensive principles of great benefit, for the same approach applies to them as to acts of worship. Among these topics is:

“The Form of Contracts”

The jurists hold three positions on this matter.

The first:

The basic rule is that contracts are valid only through a verbal formula: the expressions that some jurists may specifically designate as “offer and acceptance.” This applies equally to sales, leases, gifts, marriage, manumission, endowments, and other transactions. This is the apparent import of al-Shāfi‘ī’s position, and it is also a position within Ahmad’s school. In some matters, such as sales and endowments, it is explicitly transmitted from him; in others, such as gifts and leases, it is inferred from his positions on other questions.

Those who hold this view allow gestures to take the place of spoken expressions when a person is unable to speak, as in the case of a mute person’s gestures. They also allow writing to take the place of spoken expressions when needed.

---

## Page 14261

and they may make exceptions in cases that the scriptural texts permit when the need arises. An example is a sacrificial animal that becomes incapacitated before reaching its appointed destination: it is slaughtered, and the sandal hanging around its neck is then smeared with its blood as a sign to people; whoever takes it acquires ownership of it. The same applies to gifts and similar matters. Nevertheless, their basic rule requires a verbal formula,

because the foundation of contracts is the mutual consent mentioned in Allah’s words:

“Unless it is trade by mutual consent among you,”

and His words:

“If they willingly give you any part of it.”

What people inwardly intend can be determined precisely only through words, which have been devised to express what is in the heart. Acts such as handing over and receiving goods may bear many interpretations. Moreover, contracts belong to the category of verbal acts: in transactions, they are like remembrance of Allah and supplication in worship.

The second position:

Contracts are valid through actions in matters where concluding them by conduct is customary. Examples include sales concluded by handing over and receiving goods; endowments, as when someone builds a mosque and permits people to pray in it, dedicates land for burial, or builds a purification facility and dedicates it to public use; and certain forms of hiring, as when someone hands his garment to a launderer or tailor who works for payment, or boards a boatman’s vessel. The same applies to gifts and similar matters.

If such contracts could not be concluded through actions indicating them, people’s affairs would fall into disorder. Furthermore, from the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, until our own day, people have continually concluded contracts of this kind without a verbal formula, using instead actions that indicate their intent. This is the prevailing approach in the legal principles of

---

## Page 14262

Abū Hanīfa. It is also one opinion within Ahmad’s school and a juristic view within al-Shāfi‘ī’s school. This does not extend, however, to transactions involving valuable property concluded by handing over and receiving goods, since there is no need for this and it is not customary.

The third position:

Contracts are concluded through any words or actions that indicate their intended purpose. Whatever people regard as a sale or a hiring contract is a sale or a hiring contract. Even if people’s conventions concerning words and actions differ, each community concludes contracts through the formulas and actions whose meaning they understand among themselves. There is no fixed standard for this in either Islamic law or language. Rather, it varies with people’s conventions, just as their languages vary. The Arabic terms for sale and hire are not the same as those in the languages of the Persians, Byzantines, Turks, Berbers or Abyssinians. Indeed, even varieties of a single language may differ.

People are not obliged to adhere to one particular set of conventions in their transactions, nor are they forbidden to conclude contracts differently from others, provided that the means they use indicate their intent, although certain forms may be recommended. This is the prevailing approach in Mālik’s legal principles and the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. Accordingly, under the apparent position of his school, sales concluded by handing over and receiving goods are valid without restriction, even if

---

## Page 14263

one party speaks and the other acts—for example, one says, “Take this for Allah’s sake,” and the other takes it; or one says, “Give me a dirham’s worth of bread,” and the other gives it to him. Or neither party speaks: one simply sets down the price and takes a bunch of vegetables, some sweets, or something else, as most people commonly do. Alternatively, someone sets out goods for payment to be left in exchange; once the other party sets down a payment he finds acceptable, he takes it, as is customary with goods brought by merchants among some people in the East. Whatever people regard as a sale is therefore a sale.

The same applies to gifts, such as presents, or furnishing a bride with property to take with her to her husband’s home, when the prevailing custom is that this is a gift rather than a loan for use.

The same also applies to hiring arrangements: boarding a boat operated for hire, or riding an animal belonging to someone who hires out camels, donkeys, or mules, in a manner customarily understood as hiring. Other examples include entering a bathhouse that people use for a fee; handing a garment to a washer or tailor who works for payment; or giving food to a cook or someone who roasts food for others, whether the meat is sliced or unsliced.

Indeed, the scholars of his school even differed over whether a divorce in return for compensation can take effect through an exchange by conduct. For example, a woman might say, “Release me from marriage in return for this thousand or this garment,” and the husband accepts the compensation in the customary manner indicating his agreement to the exchange. The jurists of ʿUkbarā, such as Abū Hafs and Abū ʿAlī ibn Shihāb, held that this constitutes a valid divorce in return for compensation. They cited statements by Ahmad and by others

---

## Page 14264

among the early generations—the Companions and Successors—that supported their view. This may well be the predominant position expressed in Ahmad’s recorded statements. Indeed, he expressly stated that divorce takes effect through action as well as speech. As evidence that it takes effect in writing, he cited the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Allah has pardoned my community for what they say to themselves, so long as they neither speak of it nor act upon it.”

He said: “When someone writes, he has acted.”

The Baghdad scholars of that time, such as Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hāmid, and their followers, such as Judge Abū Yaʿlā and those who followed his approach, held that marital separation takes effect only through speech. They cited statements by Ahmad on which they relied for this position, reasoning that such separation is a dissolution of marriage: since marriage requires a verbal formula, its dissolution likewise requires one.

As for marriage, these scholars—including Ibn Hāmid, the Judge and his followers, such as Abū al-Khattāb, and most later scholars—held that it can be contracted only using the terms inkāh or tazwīj (giving in marriage), as al-Shāfiʿī also held. Their reasoning was that marriage cannot be contracted through indirect wording, because such wording requires an intention, while witnesses are a condition of a valid marriage, and witnessing an intention is impossible. They therefore did not recognize a marriage contracted using the terms “gift,” “bestowal,” or other expressions denoting a transfer of ownership.

Most of these scholars—including Ibn Hāmid, the Judge, and the later scholars—also held that anyone proficient in Arabic can contract marriage only using Arabic wording. If someone is unable to learn it, the marriage can be contracted in any language using wording that conveys its specific meaning. If he is able to learn it, there are two views;

---

## Page 14265

based on the view that marriage is restricted to these two expressions and has an element of prescribed ritual observance. Yet this position, besides not being expressly stated by Ahmad, conflicts with his principles. Ahmad did not expressly state this, nor did they transmit any explicit statement from him to that effect. They merely transmitted his statement, as reported by Abū al-Hārith:

“If a woman gives herself to a man, that does not constitute marriage, for Allah Most High says: ‘Exclusively for you, and not for the other believers.’”

This is only an explicit prohibition of what was exclusive to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), namely marriage without a dower. Indeed, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, he expressly stated that marriage is contracted when a man says to his female slave, “I have freed you and made your freedom your dower,” or says, “I have made your freedom your dower,” or, “I have made your dower your freedom.” He stated this in several of his responses.

His followers differed over this. Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hāmid applied his analogy consistently and said: “In addition to that, he must say, ‘I have married her’—tazawwajtuhā or nakahtuhā—because marriage can never be contracted in Arabic except through these two formulas.”

Judge Abū Yaʿlā and others, however, treated this case as an exception to the analogy on which they agreed with Ibn Hāmid, regarding it as a case of juristic preference.

Ibn ʿAqīl mentioned a view within the school that marriage can be contracted using words other than inkāh or tazwīj, on the strength of Ahmad’s explicit statement to this effect. This accords more closely with Ahmad’s explicit statements and principles. Mālik’s position on this resembles his, for Mālik’s followers differed over whether marriage can be contracted using words other than inkāh or tazwīj, with

---

## Page 14266

two views. What is explicitly reported from him is only the prohibition of what was exclusive to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), namely a woman’s giving herself in marriage without a dower.

Ibn al-Qāsim said:
“If a man gives his daughter as a gift, intending thereby to marry her off, I do not recall a statement from Mālik on this. In my view, it is permissible.”

The assertion of some followers of Mālik and Ahmad that marriage can be contracted only through these two expressions is far removed from the principles of both imams. This ruling rests on two premises. The first is that all other expressions are indirect, and that indirect wording requires intention.

Yet their well-known position is that contextual indications make indirect expressions explicit and take the place of an express declaration of intent. Thus, in divorce, accusations of unchastity, and similar matters, both treated indirect expressions accompanied by contextual indications as equivalent to explicit wording.

The contextual indications surrounding marriage are well known: people gather for that purpose and discuss what they have gathered for. If a man then says, “I have made her yours for a thousand dirhams,” those present know beyond doubt that marriage is intended.

This expression has become so widespread in customary usage that people call the marriage contract imlāk or malāk. For this reason, people have transmitted the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) to the suitor of the woman who offered herself in marriage—the man who searched but could not find even an iron ring—in one version as:

“I have married her to you for what you know of the Quran,”

and in another as:

“I have made her yours.”

It has not, however, been established that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) confined himself to saying, “I have made her yours.” Rather, he either said both expressions or said one of them. But since, in their understanding, the two expressions were equivalent in such a context,

---

## Page 14267

they transmitted the hadith sometimes in one wording and sometimes in the other. Moreover, requiring a particular Arabic expression in such a case is entirely at odds with Ahmad’s principles and explicit statements, as well as with the principles underlying the scriptural evidence. Marriage is valid whether contracted by a non-Muslim or a Muslim; although it is an act of devotion, it is comparable in this respect to freeing a slave or giving charity. It is well known that freeing a slave requires no particular wording, whether Arabic or otherwise. Likewise, by consensus, no particular Arabic wording is required for charitable giving, endowments, or gifts. Furthermore, a non-Arab who has only just learned Arabic may not understand the intended meaning of that expression as he would in the language he is accustomed to.

Admittedly, it would be reasonable to say that concluding contracts in a language other than Arabic without need is disliked, just as other forms of communication in a language other than Arabic are disliked when there is no need. Statements reported from Mālik, Ahmad, and al-Shāfiʿī indicate that they disliked habitual communication in languages other than Arabic without need. We have discussed this issue elsewhere.

The followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad—including Judge Abū Yaʿlā, Ibn ʿAqīl, and later scholars—have stated that the marriages of non-Muslims are assessed according to their customs. Whatever they regard among themselves as marriage may be allowed to stand when they embrace Islam and bring their case before us, provided that no impediment exists at that time. If, however, they do not regard it as marriage, it may not be allowed to stand. They even said: “If a harbī man overpowered a harbī woman and had intercourse with her

---

## Page 14268

or she willingly submitted to him, and they both regarded it as marriage, they would be allowed to remain together on that basis; otherwise not.” Clearly, the capacity of words or actions to express the intended purpose of a contract is not confined to Muslims rather than unbelievers. What distinguishes Muslims here is that Allah has commanded that marriage be distinguished from fornication, as He, Most High, says:

“Seeking chastity, neither committing fornication nor taking secret lovers.”

And He says:

“Chaste women, neither committing fornication nor taking secret lovers.”

He therefore prescribed a guardian, witnesses, and similar requirements to distinguish marriage unmistakably from fornication and to protect women from resembling prostitutes. He even prescribed beating a frame drum and holding a wedding feast to make the marriage publicly known. Hence the statement found in a transmitted report:

“A woman may not marry herself off.”

For it is the prostitute who marries herself off. He commanded that marriage be witnessed, publicly announced, or both: these are three views, corresponding to three positions transmitted within Ahmad’s school. Those who required only witnesses explained that their presence provides the publicity that distinguishes marriage from fornication and safeguards lineage in the event of denial.

The wisdom behind these requirements, which the Lawgiver established in the Quran, the Sunnah, and transmitted reports, is clear. Requiring a particular wording, however, has no basis in either transmitted evidence or sound reasoning. The comprehensive principle we have mentioned—that contracts are valid through any words or actions expressing their intended purpose—is supported by the foundational principles of Islamic law and is recognized by people’s hearts.

For Allah, Glorified and Exalted, says:

---

## Page 14269

“Then marry women of your choice.”

And He says:

“Marry off the unmarried among you.”

And He says:

“Allah has permitted trade.”

And He says:

“But if they willingly give you any of it, then enjoy it with pleasure and satisfaction.”

And He says:

“Except through trade conducted by mutual consent among you.”

And He says:

“If they breastfeed for you, give them their payment.”

And He says:

“When you contract a debt for a specified term, write it down,”

—up to His words—

“Unless it is an immediate transaction that you conduct among yourselves; then there is no blame upon you if you do not write it down. Call witnesses when you trade with one another. Let neither scribe nor witness be harmed. If you do so, that is sinful conduct on your part. Fear Allah. Allah teaches you, and Allah has knowledge of all things.”

“If you are on a journey and cannot find a scribe, then let pledges be taken into possession.”

And He says:

“Who will lend Allah a good loan?”

And He says:

“The example of those who spend their wealth in Allah’s cause is that of a grain that grows seven ears.”

And He says:

“Allah destroys usury and increases charity.”

And He says:

“Indeed, the men and women who give charity and lend Allah a good loan—it will be multiplied for them.”

And He says:

“Then a slave must be freed.”

And He says:

“Divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period.”

And He says:

“Then either retain them honourably or release them honourably.”

There are other such verses that prescribe these contracts, either by commanding or permitting them, and prohibit some of them, such as usurious transactions. These verses support our position in several ways.

First, He makes mutual consent sufficient for a sale in His words:

“Except through trade conducted by mutual consent among you.”

And He makes willingness sufficient for a gift in His words: “But if they willingly

---

## Page 14270

give you any of it, then enjoy it with pleasure and satisfaction.” The former verse concerns transactions involving an exchange, while the latter concerns gratuitous transfers. He stipulates neither particular words nor a particular act to indicate mutual consent and willingness. We know directly from people’s customary words and actions that they recognise mutual consent and willingness in many different ways. In most customary contracts, these are known immediately; in some, they are apparent. Whenever they are present, the legal ruling follows from them, as the Quran indicates.

Some people’s contentious insistence on defending a particular position may lead them to deny what people recognise as mutual consent and willingness. Such denial carries no weight. Self-evident truths are often denied through collusion and indoctrination concerning reports and doctrines. What counts, therefore, is sound human nature, unaltered by any corrupting influence.

For this reason, we have said that mass-transmitted reports yield knowledge where there is no collusion to lie, because sound human natures do not concur in falsehood. Where there is collusion and agreement, however, groups may indeed agree to lie.

The second point:

These terms occur in Allah’s Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger with rulings of the revealed law attached to them. Every term must have a definition. Some definitions are known through language, as with the sun, the moon, land, sea, heaven and earth. Others are known through revealed law, as with the believer, the disbeliever and the hypocrite, and with prayer and zakat

---

## Page 14271

and fasting and Hajj.

Whatever has no definition in either language or revealed law is referred to people’s customary usage, as with taking possession, mentioned in the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever buys food must not sell it until he takes possession of it.”

It is well known that the Lawgiver has laid down no definition for sale, hire, gift and the like, either in Allah’s Book or in the Sunnah of His Messenger. Nor has it been reported from any of the Companions or Successors that he prescribed a particular form of words or other conduct for contracts, or said anything indicating that contracts are concluded only through specific formulas. Indeed, it has even been said that this view contradicts the early consensus and is an innovation.

Nor is there a definition in the Arabic language by which one could say that its speakers call one transaction a sale but do not call another a sale, so that one falls within the scope of Allah’s address while the other does not. Rather, the fact that Arabs customarily call these contractual dealings sales is evidence that they are called sales in their language. The presumption is that established linguistic usage is retained and upheld, not displaced or altered. Thus, when something has no definition in revealed law or language, reference must be made to people’s customary usage and practices. Whatever they call a sale is a sale, and whatever they call a gift is a gift.

The third point:

People’s words and deeds are of two kinds: acts of worship through which their religious life is set right, and customary practices which they need in their worldly lives. By surveying the principles of the revealed law, we know that the command to perform acts of worship which Allah has made obligatory or loves can be established only through revealed law. As for customary practices, these are what people habitually do in their worldly lives, involving matters that

---

## Page 14272

they need. The basic principle regarding such practices is that they are not prohibited; only what Allah, Glorified and Most High, has prohibited is forbidden. This is because commands and prohibitions belong to Allah’s revealed law, and an act of worship must be something He has commanded. How, then, can something be judged an act of worship when it has not been established that it is commanded? And how can an act of worship be judged prohibited when it has not been established that it is forbidden? This is why Ahmad and other jurists among the scholars of hadith would say:

The basic principle concerning acts of worship is that they require authorization through revelation: none is prescribed except what Allah Most High has prescribed. Otherwise, we would fall within the meaning of His words:

“Or do they have partners who have ordained for them in religion what Allah has not authorized?”

As for customary practices, the basic principle is that they are allowed: only what He has forbidden is prohibited. Otherwise, we would fall within the meaning of His words:

“Say, ‘Have you considered the provision Allah has sent down for you, some of which you have declared unlawful and some lawful?’”

This is why, in Surah al-An‘ām, Allah condemned the polytheists who ordained religious practices He had not authorized and prohibited things He had not forbidden, beginning with His words, Most High:

“They assign to Allah a share of the crops and livestock He has created, saying, ‘This is for Allah’—so they claim—‘and this is for our partners.’ Whatever is for their partners does not reach Allah, while whatever is for Allah reaches their partners. Evil is their judgment!”

“Likewise, their partners have made the killing of their children seem appealing to many of the polytheists, to bring them to ruin and confuse them about their religion. Had Allah willed, they would not have done so. So leave them and what they fabricate.”

“And they say, ‘These livestock and crops are restricted; none may eat of them except those we permit’—so they claim. There are livestock forbidden to be ridden, and livestock over which they do not mention the name of

---

## Page 14273

Allah, fabricating lies against Him. He will repay them for what they used to fabricate.” Thus, He mentioned the acts of worship and prohibitions they had invented. In Sahīh Muslim, ‘Iyād ibn Himār, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High said: ‘I created My servants upright in faith, but the devils led them astray, forbade them what I had made lawful for them, and commanded them to associate with Me that for which I had sent down no authority.’”

This is a “great and beneficial principle.” That being so, we say:

Sales, gifts, leases, and similar transactions are customary practices that people need for their livelihood, like eating, drinking, and clothing themselves. The revealed law has established good standards of conduct for these practices: it prohibits what is harmful, makes obligatory what is indispensable, discourages what is inappropriate, and recommends what has a preponderance of benefit, with regard to their kinds, quantities, and characteristics.

Accordingly, people may buy, sell, and lease as they wish, except where the revealed law prohibits something, just as they may eat and drink as they wish, except where the revealed law prohibits something—even though some of these practices may be recommended or disliked. Wherever the revealed law has set no limit, the original unrestricted allowance remains in effect.

As for the Sunnah and consensus, whoever examines the various sales, leases, and gratuitous transactions reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Companions, and the Successors:

---

## Page 14274

will know with certainty that they did not insist on verbal formulas from both parties. The reports concerning this are numerous, but this is not the place to discuss them, since the purpose here is to draw attention to general principles. Detailed discussion of individual cases belongs elsewhere.

Examples include the following:

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, built his mosque, and the Muslims built mosques during his lifetime and after his death. Yet he did not command anyone to say, “I dedicate this mosque as an endowment,” or anything similar. Rather, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever builds a mosque for Allah, Allah will build him a house in Paradise.”

He thus made the ruling depend on the act of building itself.

The two Sahīhs report that when he purchased the camel from ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, he said:

“It is yours, ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar.”

Yet Ibn ‘Umar uttered no words of acceptance. The Prophet would give and receive gifts, and taking possession of a gift constituted acceptance of it.

When he slaughtered the sacrificial camels, he said:

“Whoever wishes may cut himself a portion.”

He said this even though he could have divided the meat among them. This constituted an offer, while cutting off a portion constituted acceptance. When asked, he would give; he would also give without being asked, and the recipient would take possession of what was given. Giving constituted the offer, and taking constituted acceptance.

This occurred on very many occasions. He did not instruct the recipients to utter any particular words, nor did he undertake to address them with a verbal formula, as when he gave to those whose hearts were to be reconciled, to al-‘Abbās, and to others.

He also treated presenting an item for sale as having certain qualities as equivalent to stipulating those qualities verbally, as in the case of an animal whose milk has been allowed to accumulate in its udder, and other deceptively presented goods.

---

## Page 14275

Furthermore, legal transactions fall into two categories: contracts and acts of taking possession. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought both together in his words:

“May Allah have mercy on a servant who is gracious when selling, gracious when buying, gracious when paying what he owes, and gracious when claiming what is owed to him.”

People likewise speak of buying and selling, taking and giving.

The purpose of contracts is simply to take possession and receive what is due. Concluding a contract makes taking possession obligatory or permissible, in a manner analogous to the Lawgiver’s imposition of obligations. The subsequent exchange of possession and similar acts fulfil contracts, just as performing what is commanded fulfils religious obligations. Taking possession, like a contract, may be valid or invalid, and legal rulings attach to it, just as they attach to taking possession.

If taking possession is determined by people’s customs and practices, without a single definition applying equally to everyone in every circumstance and at every time, then the same applies to contracts. If you formulate the argument more precisely, you would say: Contracts constitute one of the two categories of legal transactions; their determination therefore rests on people’s customary practice, just as it does for the other category.

A related point is that permission conveyed through customary practice—whether to allow use, transfer ownership, or authorize action through agency—is equivalent to verbal permission. Both agency and permission to use something are established by words or actions indicating them. Knowing that the person entitled to grant permission consents takes the place of that person’s express declaration of consent. This also explains the pledge of allegiance given by the Prophet

---

## Page 14276

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on behalf of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān at the Pledge of al-Ridwān while he was absent, and his bringing the men of the Trench into the homes of Abū Talhah and Jābir without asking their permission, since he knew that both consented.

When the butcher invited him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as the sixth of six guests, another man followed them. He did not let the man enter until he had asked permission from the butcher who had issued the invitation.

Likewise, it is reported that when al-Hasan al-Basrī’s companions entered his home and ate his food, he said, “You have reminded me of the ways of people now gone.”

This is also the meaning of Abū Jaʿfar’s statement: “Brothers are those among whom one may put his hand into his companion’s pocket and take whatever he wishes.”

Another example is his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to someone who asked him for a ball of hair:

“As for whatever belongs to me and Banū ʿAbd al-Muttalib, I have given it to you.”

The same applies to his giving gifts to those whose hearts he sought to win over, according to those who hold that he gave them from the four-fifths of the spoils. On this basis, Imam Ahmad explained the sale by Hakīm ibn Hizām and ʿUrwah ibn al-Jaʿd: when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commissioned him to buy a sheep for one dinar, he bought two sheep and sold one of them for a dinar.

For acting without seeking specific permission—whether by an exchange, a gratuitous transfer, or the use of something—rests on customary permission, either general or specific.

---

## Page 14277

Section: The Second Principle Concerning Contracts—What Is Lawful and What Is Unlawful

The foundation of this is that Allah has forbidden in His Book our consuming one another’s wealth wrongfully. He condemned the rabbis and monks who wrongfully consume people’s wealth, and condemned the Jews for taking interest despite having been forbidden to do so, and for wrongfully consuming people’s wealth. This encompasses everything wrongfully consumed through exchanges and gratuitous transfers, as well as whatever is taken without the consent of the person entitled to it and without a rightful claim.

Wrongfully consuming wealth through an exchange takes two forms mentioned by Allah in His Book: interest and gambling. He mentioned the prohibition of interest, which is the opposite of charity, at the end of Sūrat al-Baqarah, and in Sūrat Āl ʿImrān, al-Rūm and al-Muddaththir. He condemned the Jews for it in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ, and mentioned the prohibition of gambling in Sūrat al-Māʾidah.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then explained in detail what Allah had stated comprehensively in His Book. He prohibited sales involving uncertainty, as Muslim and others narrated from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him.

Such uncertainty means that the outcome is unknown. Selling something whose outcome is unknown is a form of gambling, or wagering.

To illustrate: if a slave runs away

---

## Page 14278

or a horse or camel strays, its owner sells it as a gamble, and the buyer purchases it for far less than its value. If the buyer obtains it, the seller says, “You have won this gamble against me and taken my property for a paltry price.” If he does not obtain it, the buyer says, “You have won this gamble against me and taken my money without giving me anything in return.” This leads to the harm caused by gambling: sowing enmity and hatred, alongside wrongfully consuming wealth, which is a form of injustice. Thus, sales involving uncertainty entail injustice, enmity and hatred.

Among the forms of uncertainty prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are habal al-habalah, malāqīh and madāmīn sales; selling the produce of future years; selling fruit before it shows signs of ripeness; sales concluded by touching or tossing an item; and similar transactions. All these are forms of uncertainty.

As for interest, the Quran prohibits it in even stronger terms. Thus Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of interest, if you are believers.”

“If you do not, then be warned of war from Allah and His Messenger.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also counted it among the major sins, as al-Bukhārī and Muslim both narrated in their Sahīh collections from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him. Allah stated that He forbade the Jews certain wholesome things previously permitted to them because of their wrongdoing, their barring others from Allah’s path, their taking interest, and their wrongfully consuming people’s wealth. He, glorified be He, also declared that He brings interest to nothing, just as He makes charity grow. Both are borne out by people’s experience.

---

## Page 14279

This is because interest, in its basic form, is resorted to only by someone in need. A person of means would not take a thousand now in return for twelve hundred payable later unless he needed that thousand. Only someone who needs the money takes it in return for an equivalent sum plus an increase payable later. That increase therefore constitutes an injustice against the needy person. Gambling is different: the person wronged in it is neither destitute nor in need of entering the transaction. Some forms of it may involve no injustice if the item sold subsequently turns out to have the qualities both parties expected. Interest, however, involves a definite injustice against someone in need.

For this reason, interest is the opposite of charity. Allah has not left the wealthy without obliging them to give to the poor, for the welfare of rich and poor alike, in both their religious and worldly affairs, cannot be fully secured otherwise. If the wealthy man then charges the poor man interest, it is like the case of someone who is owed a debt but is denied repayment and subjected to a further injustice, even though he needs the money owed to him. This is among the gravest forms of injustice. Because of its enormity, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the one who consumes interest—that is, the recipient—and the one who pays it—that is, the needy person who pays the additional amount—as well as its two witnesses and its scribe, because they assist in it.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also prohibited certain things whose harmfulness is less apparent because they lead to definite harm, just as he prohibited a small quantity of wine because it invites consumption of a larger quantity. One example is interest through unequal exchange. The wisdom behind its prohibition may not be apparent, since a rational person would not sell one dirham for two unless their qualities differed: for example, the single dirham might be intact while the two dirhams are broken, or the single dirham might consist of worked silver or belong to a readily accepted currency, and so forth. Thus, the wisdom behind its prohibition was not apparent to Ibn ‘Abbās and Mu‘āwiyah

---

## Page 14280

and others, who therefore saw nothing wrong with it until leading Companions—such as ‘Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit, Abū Sa‘īd, and others—informed them that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had prohibited interest through unequal exchange.

As for contractual uncertainty, it is of three kinds. The first concerns something nonexistent, such as selling the future offspring of an unborn animal or selling crops for future years. The second concerns something that cannot be delivered, such as a runaway slave. The third concerns something unspecified, or a specific item whose category or quantity is unknown, as when someone says, “I sell you a slave,” “I sell you whatever is in my house,” or “I sell you my slaves.”

As for a specific item whose category and quantity are known, but whose type or qualities are unknown—as when someone says, “I sell you the garment in my sleeve,” or “the slave I own,” and similar cases—there is a well-known disagreement concerning it. Prominent here is the question of selling specific goods that are not present. Three positions are reported from Ahmad concerning this.

The first is that such a sale is invalid under all circumstances, which is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s later position.

The second is that it is valid even without a description, and that the purchaser has the option to rescind the sale upon seeing the item. This is also Abū Hanīfah’s position. However, Ahmad is also reported to have held that the purchaser has no such option.

The third—and the well-known position—is that the sale is valid if the item is described, but invalid without a description, just as with generic goods owed under a contractual obligation. This is also Mālik’s position.

The harm arising from contractual uncertainty is less than that arising from interest; concessions were therefore granted in matters called for by

---

## Page 14281

need, since prohibiting them causes greater harm than the uncertainty they involve. Examples include selling a property as a whole, even though the interiors of its walls and its foundations are unknown; and selling a pregnant or lactating animal, even though the extent of the pregnancy or the quantity of milk is unknown. This is permissible even though selling the unborn offspring separately is prohibited, as is selling the milk separately according to most scholars. The same applies to selling fruit once it has begun to ripen: the sale is valid, with the right to leave the fruit on the tree, as the Sunnah indicates and as the majority—including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad—hold, even though the further growth needed for full ripeness has not yet come into existence.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also permitted the purchaser of date palms that had been pollinated to stipulate that their fruit be included. The purchaser would thus have bought fruit before it began to ripen, but the sale is directed primarily at the trees themselves. It is therefore clear that a small degree of contractual uncertainty is permissible when incidental and subordinate to a sale, though it would not otherwise be permissible.

When people needed arāyā transactions, he granted a concession allowing their sale on the basis of estimation. He did not permit a definite disparity between the exchanged quantities; rather, he allowed equality to be determined by estimation for the small quantity required to meet that need. This is either the minimum zakāh threshold—five wasqs—or an amount below that threshold, according to the two differing positions reported from al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, although Ahmad’s well-known position limits it to less than the threshold.

With this established, Mālik’s principles governing sales are sounder than those of others;

---

## Page 14282

for he learned these principles from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib, of whom it was said: “He is the most knowledgeable of people concerning sales.” Likewise, it was said: “‘Atā’ is the most knowledgeable concerning the pilgrimage rites, Ibrāhīm concerning prayer, and al-Hasan the most comprehensively knowledgeable in all these matters.” Accordingly, anyone who examines Ahmad’s answers systematically will find that he agreed with each of these Successors on most matters within that scholar’s particular field of distinction.

Imam Ahmad generally agrees with Mālik on these matters. Both prohibit ribā and take a properly stringent stance against it, given its emphatic prohibition and the enormity of its harm, as explained above. They prohibit every means of circumventing its prohibition, even barring means that lead to it without themselves constituting a legal stratagem. Although Mālik goes further in blocking such means in matters on which Ahmad’s position does not vary, or adopts positions Ahmad does not hold, Ahmad nevertheless agrees with him, with no differing position reported from him, that all such stratagems are prohibited.

These stratagems fall into two broad categories: adding something not genuinely intended to one of the two items exchanged, or attaching a contract not genuinely intended to the principal contract.

The first is the issue known as “a mudd of ‘ajwa dates.” Its defining feature is exchanging a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for another of the same kind, with something of a different kind added to both sides or to one of them. For example, the parties may intend to exchange silver for silver in unequal amounts, or something similar, and so add another item to the smaller amount of silver. This can go so far as selling one thousand dinars wrapped in a cloth for two thousand dinars. Whenever the purpose is to exchange a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for an unequal amount of the same kind, the “mudd of ‘ajwa dates” transaction is prohibited, without disagreement according to Mālik, Ahmad, and others. Only those Kufan scholars who permit legal stratagems allow such transactions, even though

---

## Page 14283

the earlier Kufan scholars prohibited this. If, however, both items are genuinely intended—as when one mudd of ‘ajwa dates and one dirham are exchanged for one mudd of ‘ajwa dates and one dirham, or for two mudds of dates, or for two dirhams—two positions are reported from Ahmad. Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī prohibit this, whereas Abū Hanīfah permits it. This is a matter open to scholarly judgment.

If what is intended on one side of the exchange is something other than the commodity subject to the rules of ribā—as when a sheep bearing wool or producing milk is sold for wool or milk—the better-known of the two positions reported from Ahmad permits it.

The second category of stratagems involves attaching a contract that is not genuinely intended to the prohibited contract. For example, the parties may agree that one will sell the other gold in exchange for the latter’s beads, which are then bought back for a greater amount of gold. Alternatively, they may arrange with a third party for one of them to sell some merchandise; the purchaser then sells it to the ribā dealer with whom he does business, and the dealer then sells it to his counterpart. This is the three-party stratagem.

Another example is to combine a loan with preferential terms in a sale, lease, musāqāh (tending trees for a share of their fruit), or a similar transaction. For instance, someone might lend another a thousand and sell him an item worth ten for two hundred, or rent him a house whose rental value is thirty for five, and so forth.

These and similar stratagems do not eliminate the harm on account of which Allah prohibited ribā. It is authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the hadith narrated by ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr, that he said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, nor to stipulate two conditions in a sale, nor

---

## Page 14284

to profit from what one has not borne liability for, nor to sell what one does not possess.” Al-Tirmidhī said: “A good and authentic hadith.”

This is akin to the stratagems of the Jews, who treated ribā as lawful only by resorting to stratagems, calling it al-mashkand. Allah cursed them for this.

Ibn Battah narrated, through a good chain of transmission, that Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful what Allah has forbidden through the slightest of stratagems.”

The two Sahīh collections also report that he said:

“May Allah curse the Jews! When animal fats were forbidden to them, they melted them down, sold them, and consumed the proceeds.”

The Sunan collections report that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever enters a horse in a race with two others, when it may beat them, is not gambling. But whoever enters a horse in a race with two others, when there is no chance of it beating them, is gambling.”

As reported by the compilers of the Sunan collections through ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from his father, from his grandfather, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The buyer and seller retain the option to cancel the sale until they part, and neither may leave the other for fear that the other will ask him to rescind it.”

There is abundant evidence prohibiting stratagems in the Quran, the Sunnah, scholarly consensus, and sound reasoning. In our writings on this subject, we have mentioned some thirty proofs. We have also discussed the evidence invoked by those who permit such stratagems, such as Job’s oath, the hadith concerning the dates of Khaybar, and the equivocal expressions of

---

## Page 14285

the early generations, and we have set out the answers to these arguments.

Among the means leading to this is:

The Question of ‘Īnah

This is where someone sells another person an item on deferred payment, then buys it back from him for less. When this is prearranged, both sales are invalid, because it is a stratagem.

Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd narrated through two good chains of transmission that Ibn ‘Umar said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you trade through ‘īnah, follow the tails of cattle, and abandon jihad in the path of Allah, Allah will inflict humiliation upon you which He will not remove until you return to your religion.”

Even if the two parties have not colluded, both Mālik and Ahmad deem the second sale invalid, to block the means to what is forbidden.

As for the reverse of ‘īnah without prior collusion, there are two reports from Ahmad concerning it. This is where someone sells an item for immediate payment, then buys it back for a higher, deferred price. With prior collusion, however, it is ribā effected through a stratagem.

If the buyer’s aim is to obtain money, and he purchases an item on deferred payment in order to sell it and receive its price, this is called tawarruq. There are two reports from Ahmad concerning whether it is disapproved. Disapproval is the position of ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz and, I believe, Mālik. This differs from the case of a buyer whose purpose is trade, use, or keeping the item in his possession: there is agreement that he may purchase it on deferred payment.

In sum, the scholars of Medina and the jurists of hadith firmly prohibit the various forms of ribā, giving due regard to the purpose and principles of the Sharia. Their position on this is the one

---

## Page 14286

that has parallels in reports from the Companions and is supported by the meanings of the Quran and Sunnah.

As for gharar (contractual uncertainty), Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī—may Allah be pleased with them both—take the strictest positions concerning it. Al-Shāfi‘ī includes under this heading kinds of transactions that other jurists do not, such as selling grain and fruit within a husk that does not serve as a protective casing: broad beans, walnuts and almonds in their green husks, and grain in its ears. His later position is that this is impermissible, even though he purchased green broad beans during his final illness. A permissive position was therefore inferred from his conduct, and a group of his followers, including Abū Sa‘īd al-Istakhrī, adopted it.

It is related that he was told that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade selling grain until it hardens.”

This indicates that it may be sold once it has hardened, even while still in its ears. He replied, “If this is authentic, I would exempt it from the general rule,” or words to that effect. It is likewise reported that he retracted his position prohibiting it.

Ibn al-Mundhir said: “Its permissibility is the position of Mālik and the scholars of Medina, ‘Ubayd Allah ibn al-Hasan and the scholars of Basra, the scholars of hadith, and the jurists of independent reasoning. Al-Shāfi‘ī once held that it was impermissible, but when the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar reached him, he retracted that position and adopted the ruling it indicated.”

Ibn al-Mundhir said: “I know of no one who departs from this view.” Some of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers mentioned two positions from him, identifying permissibility as his earlier position. He even prohibited

---

## Page 14287

the sale of unseen specific goods, whether described or not, on the interpretation that selling unseen goods involves contractual uncertainty even when they are described. He went so far as to require descriptions and precision concerning goods owed as an obligation—such as goods due under an advance-payment sale—that no other jurist required. Under such a position, transactions involving specific goods and goods owed become impossible or difficult for people.

He extended the rules governing sales involving contractual uncertainty by analogy to all contracts, whether gratuitous or involving an exchange. Thus, he imposed on a hired worker’s wages, compensation for a wife’s release from marriage, contractual manumission payments, settlements with parties to a truce, and the jizyah payable by non-Muslims under Muslim protection the same requirements that he imposed on sales involving specific goods or goods owed. In these matters, he permitted only such kinds, quantities and descriptions as he would permit in a sale, even though these contracts are not invalidated by defects in their consideration, or are subject to other conditions.

As for Abū Hanīfah, he permits the sale of broad beans and similar produce within both their coverings. He permits hiring a worker in return for food and clothing, and allows the bridal due to be unspecified to the same extent as the customary bridal due for a woman of comparable standing. He also permits the sale of unseen specific goods without a description, provided there is an option to rescind, because he recognizes that contracts may remain pending. Yet he categorically prohibits crop-sharing arrangements for tending orchards and cultivating land, and similar transactions.

Al-Shāfiʿī permits the sale of some of these things. He also prohibits many conditions in sales, leases, marriages and other contracts that depart from what an unqualified contract entails. Abū Hanīfah permits some of these conditions, as well as forms of agency and partnership that al-Shāfiʿī does not permit. He even permits mufāwadah partnerships and agency concerning wholly unspecified matters.

---

## Page 14288

Al-Shāfiʿī said:
“If a mufāwadah partnership is not invalid, then I know of nothing that is invalid.”

Thus, their positions in this area overlap, with each permitting things the other prohibits. However, al-Shāfiʿī’s principles entail more prohibitions in this regard than those of Abū Hanīfah.

As for Mālik, his school’s position is the best in this matter. He permits the sale of these things and everything for which there is a need, or whose contractual uncertainty is slight enough to be tolerated in contracts. He even permits selling cucumber crops as a whole and selling produce concealed in the ground, such as carrots, radishes and the like.

Ahmad’s position is close to his in this respect, for he permits these things. According to his explicitly recorded position, he also permits the bridal due to be a slave without further specification, or an unspecified slave from among the husband’s slaves, and similar things whose uncertainty is no greater than that involved in the customary bridal due for a woman of comparable standing. Some of his followers, however, such as Abū al-Khattāb, permit an unspecified choice from a defined group but not an unrestricted designation. Others, such as Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, agree with al-Shāfiʿī, permitting as a bridal due, compensation for a wife’s release from marriage, and similar payments only what they permit as the subject of a sale.

According to his explicitly recorded position, Ahmad permits greater latitude in compensation for a wife’s release from marriage, even allowing what may validly be bequeathed, although it would not be permissible as a bridal due. This accords with Mālik’s position, though there are differences of opinion within Ahmad’s school that this is not the place to discuss. Nevertheless, his explicitly recorded position is that he does not permit the sale of produce concealed in the ground, such as carrots and the like, unless it has been uprooted.

He said:

---

## Page 14289

“This is contractual uncertainty: how can he buy something he cannot see?”

His explicitly recorded position is that he does not permit the sale of cucumbers of both kinds, eggplants, and similar crops except one picking at a time. Of the produce in cucumber and melon plots, only what has appeared may be sold, not what remains concealed. Green fodder may likewise be sold only one cutting at a time. This accords with the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, because such a sale involves contractual uncertainty and constitutes selling fruit before its soundness becomes apparent.

His followers then differed. Most applied this ruling without qualification to everything concealed underground, such as carrots, radishes, onions, and similar produce, in agreement with al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfah.

Shaykh Abū Muhammad said:

If both the above-ground growth and the roots are sought, as with onions sold green, leeks, and radishes, or if the above-ground growth alone is sought, the preferable view is that its sale is permissible, because the intended part is visible. It thus resembles trees and walls, with the unseen portion included in the sale as a subordinate element. If, however, the roots are the principal object sought, selling it while it remains in the ground is impermissible, because the ruling follows the predominant element. If the two are equally sought, the sale is also impermissible, because the basic rule is that the condition must be observed; it is waived only for the lesser, subordinate portion.

Ahmad’s wording admits of two interpretations, for Abū Dāwūd said:

I asked Ahmad, “What about selling carrots while they are still in the ground?”

He replied, “Only those that have been uprooted may be sold. This is contractual uncertainty: how can he buy something he cannot see?”

Thus, he based the ruling on the lack of sight of the item.

It may therefore be said: unless the whole of it has been seen, it may not be sold.

Alternatively, it may be said: seeing part of the item being sold is sufficient when it indicates what the rest is like

---

## Page 14290

as when only a slave’s face is seen. They likewise differed concerning cucumber crops sold together with their roots, as is generally customary.

Some later scholars said:

This is permissible, because selling vegetable plants is like selling trees. If someone sells a tree bearing fruit whose soundness has not yet become apparent, the sale is permissible; the same therefore applies here. This was also stated to be the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī.

The earlier scholars said:

This is impermissible under all circumstances. This is both the meaning of Ahmad’s words and his explicitly recorded position. What he prohibited was precisely the customary practice, and it is not customary to sell melons or either kind of cucumber without their roots. Moreover, he does not concede the ruling in the case on which they base their analogy.

His explicit ruling, as transmitted by al-Athram and Ibrāhīm ibn al-Hārith, concerning trees bearing fruit whose soundness has not yet become apparent, is that the sale is permissible if the tree itself is the purchaser’s principal object. But if his object is the fruit and he purchases the tree along with it as a legal stratagem, it is impermissible.

Likewise, if someone purchases land containing crops or fruit-bearing trees before their produce has shown signs of soundness, then, if the land is his intended object, the fruit and crops may be included with it as subordinate elements. But if the fruit and crops are his intended object, and he purchases the land for their sake, it is impermissible.

If this is his position concerning the fruit of trees, then it is clear that what is sought from cucumber and melon plots is the produce, not the plants themselves, whose value is small compared with that of the vegetables.

Ibn ʿAqīl and others have derived two juristic views concerning this:

---

## Page 14291

The first follows the ruling permitting the sale of concealed produce, based on one of the two positions reported from him concerning the sale of something the purchaser has not seen. This is clearly a sound view, since prohibition would apply only if we held that the sale of something the purchaser has not seen is invalid. If we recognize the validity of selling unseen goods, this falls within that category.

The second permits their sale without qualification, as in Mālik’s school, by analogy with walnut kernels.

This latter view accords with the principles of Ahmad and others for two reasons:

First, experts can determine the true condition of these underground crops by examining their leaves, and their knowledge of them is more reliable than their knowledge of a slave gained by seeing his face. In every matter, reference should be made to upright people with expertise in it. Such experts affirm that they can assess these things as well as—or even better than—other goods whose sale Muslims unanimously agree is permissible.

Second, people have a pressing need to sell such produce. If it cannot be sold until it is uprooted, its owners suffer considerable harm: they may be unable either to uproot it themselves or to appoint someone to do so. If they uproot it all at once, it spoils as a result. Leaving it in the ground is therefore like leaving walnuts, almonds and similar nuts in their green husks. Ahmad and other jurists of the hadith tradition permit ʿarāyā sales despite the muzābanah they involve—the exchange of fresh dates on the palm for dried dates—because the purchaser needs fresh dates to eat, or the seller needs dried dates to eat. The seller’s need here is far more pressing. We shall establish this in due course, if Allah Most High wills.

---

## Page 14292

Likewise, reasoning from the principles of Ahmad and other jurists of the hadith tradition entails permitting the sale of the produce of cucumber patches, both concealed and visible, once signs of ripeness appear, even if this includes selling produce not yet in existence. Similarly, there is agreement that once signs of ripeness appear in some of the fruit of a date palm or another tree, all its fruit may be sold, even though some of it has not yet begun to ripen.

The most they have offered to justify treating this as an exception to analogy is to say that the ripening fruit of a single palm cannot be sold separately: if a single unripe date were sold under a separate contract, it would become indistinguishable from the others within a single day, since a date can turn yellow that very day. Yet precisely the same applies to a cucumber patch.

Some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad have sought to explain away the sale of nonexistent produce as an adjunct by arguing that any growth in the fruit after the contract is not an adjunct to what already exists; rather, it belongs to the purchaser because it comes into being within his ownership. The majority in both schools recognize that this explanation is unsound. The seller is obliged to water the fruit, and the purchaser is entitled, by the contract alone, to leave it on the trees. Were the purchaser not entitled to its further growth under the contract, the seller would not be obliged to provide what brings that growth about. For the seller’s obligation under the sale is to deliver in full the goods stipulated by the contract, not whatever follows from ownership itself.

Moreover, the reports from Ahmad differ concerning the case where signs of ripeness appear in

---

## Page 14293

an orchard: may all its fruit be sold, or may only the fruit that has begun to ripen be sold? There are two reports from him on this question.

The better-known report is that only fruit showing signs of ripeness may be sold. This was the position adopted by his early followers, such as Abū Bakr and Ibn Shāqlā.

The second report is that the appearance of signs of ripeness in some of the fruit counts as ripeness for all of it. This was the position adopted by most of his followers, such as Ibn Hāmid, al-Qādī and those who followed them.

His explicit statement in this second report is:

“If some fruit in an orchard is ripe and some is not, it may be sold if most of it is ripe.”

Some of his followers therefore distinguished between a small and a large proportion of ripe fruit. These include al-Qādī in his later position, Abū Hakīm al-Nahrawānī, Abū al-Barakāt and others who restricted this ruling to cases where most of the fruit showed signs of ripeness. Others, such as Abū al-Khattāb and numerous other scholars, made no distinction between a small and a large proportion. This is also the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and al-Layth.

Mālik went further, saying:

“This also counts as ripeness for the fruit in adjoining plots.”

They also related this as a report from Ahmad.

These scholars differed over whether signs of ripeness in one variety—such as Barnī fresh dates—count as ripeness for all other varieties of fresh dates. There are two views within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

The first does not permit this; it is the position of al-Qādī, Ibn ʿAqīl and Abū Muhammad.

The second permits it; it is the position of Abū al-Khattāb.

Al-Layth went further than these scholars, saying:

---

## Page 14294

“Signs of ripeness in one kind of fruit—such as apples or almonds—count as ripeness for all other kinds of fruit.”

Those who permit any of these sales reason that practical need calls for this: selling some of the produce but not the rest leads to difficulties in shared ownership and competing claims to possession. This is the rationale of those who distinguish between a single orchard and several orchards.

Those who treat the two alike say that the purpose is to ensure safety from blight, and this is achieved once the fruit begins to ripen.

Those who prohibit this reason that the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), “until they show signs of ripeness,” require signs of ripeness to appear in all the fruit.

The point of reviewing these positions is this: if someone permits the sale of an orchard’s crop of a single kind because some of it shows signs of ripeness, his position implies, by analogy, that the produce of a melon-and-cucumber plot may be sold once some of it shows signs of ripeness. The produce that does not yet exist in such a plot is analogous to the parts of a fruit that do not yet exist. Indeed, the practical need is greater here, since separating trees for sale is easier than separating individual melons, long cucumbers, and cucumbers. Even if distinguishing one picking from another were not difficult, it would still be impossible to establish a consistent boundary between them, since people’s judgments about this vary.

The point is that Ahmad’s foundational principles entail agreement with Mālik on these questions, as has indeed been reported from him in some of his answers, or deduced by his followers from his principles.

---

## Page 14295

Just as a scholar among the Companions, the Successors, or the imams often holds two opinions on a single question at different times, he may likewise hold two opinions concerning a single class of questions at different times. He may give one answer concerning some individual cases at one time, and another answer concerning other cases at another time. If the individual cases are equivalent, yet he has expressed two opinions concerning them, then, if there is no distinction between them that a jurist exercising independent judgment might recognize, there is no disagreement that his position applies uniformly to them. If, however, a jurist might recognize such a distinction, a group of scholars, including Abū al-Khattāb, maintain that the ruling may not be extended from one case to the other.

The majority, including Judge Abū Ya‘lā, maintain that the ruling may be extended, provided that the original scholar does not recognize such a distinction, as indicated by his principles. Some of these scholars extend the ruling whenever they consider the two cases equivalent, even if it is not known whether the original scholar distinguishes between them.

If he distinguishes between individual cases while having both in mind, and the distinction rests on a consideration derived from Islamic law, then that distinction constitutes a position of his. If, however, it rests on ordinary experience, sensory observation, or similar matters about which specialists may know more than jurists who lack firsthand experience, then there is, in reality, no distinction between the two cases under Islamic law. Rather, this is a worldly matter of which the scholar was unaware. Scholars are the heirs of the prophets, and the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:

“You know best about your worldly affairs. As for matters of your religion, refer them to me.”

Such differences of scholarly opinion concerning the very same question, or the same class of questions, may be called a contradiction

---

## Page 14296

as well, since contradiction is a conflict between two statements, one affirming what the other denies. Thus, if at one time he says, “This is forbidden,” and at another time says of the same thing or something equivalent, “It is not forbidden,” or says something implying that it is not forbidden, then his two statements contradict each other. Yet he is correct in both according to those who hold that every jurist exercising independent judgment is correct, and that Allah has no ruling binding upon that jurist in reality other than what he believes it to be.

The majority, however, maintain that Allah has a ruling in reality, which the scholar recognized in one of his two statements but failed to recognize in the contradictory statement. His failure to recognize it despite his efforts is forgiven, and he is also rewarded for seeking the truth and striving to discover it.

For this reason, some liken conflicting scholarly judgments to abrogating and abrogated rulings in the laws of the prophets. There is, however, a distinction between them: both the abrogating and the abrogated ruling are established by Allah’s pronouncement of a ruling, both in reality and outwardly, unlike one of the scholar’s two contradictory statements. This concerns someone who fears Allah in what he says, while knowing himself to be acting piously and following the right path.

As for those driven by whims and contentiousness, they are blameworthy for their contradictions, because they speak without knowledge and without sincerely seeking what they ought to seek.

Accordingly, the implications of a person’s statement are of two kinds:

---

## Page 14297

The first is what follows necessarily from his statement and is true. He must accept this implication, for whatever follows necessarily from the truth is itself true. It may be attributed to him if his disposition is known to be such that he would not refuse to accept it once it became clear to him. Much of what people attribute to the teachings of the imams falls into this category.

The second is what follows necessarily from his statement and is not true. He is not obliged to accept this implication, for at most it means that he has contradicted himself. It has already been established that every scholar, apart from the prophets, falls into contradiction.

If his disposition is known to be such that he would accept the implication once it became clear to him, it may be attributed to him. Otherwise, it is impermissible to attribute to him a statement whose falsity, if apparent to him, would lead him to reject it, merely because he has said something that entails it while being unaware that the statement is false. Nor is it binding upon him.

This distinction provides a better resolution of the disagreement over whether the implications of a position are themselves part of that position than an unqualified endorsement of either answer. Whatever implication the speaker would accept once it became clear to him is his position; whatever he would not accept is not his position, even if he is inconsistent. This is the distinction between an implication that must be accepted together with its underlying proposition and an implication that requires the underlying proposition to be abandoned because it necessarily entails it. Once this is understood, the distinction becomes clear between the positions one ought to hold and those one actually holds.

This applies to implications that he has not expressly denied follow from his position. If, however, he himself denies that they follow, then under no circumstances may the implication be attributed to him; otherwise,

---

## Page 14298

everything we believe the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said would be attributed to every scholar, since he accepts the Prophet’s message. Yet since we do not attribute to a scholar what he denies the Messenger said—even if it necessarily follows from his position—the distinction becomes clear between an implication he has not denied and one he has denied. A scholar’s explicit statement of a ruling does not necessarily mean that he denies an implication of his position, for the two statements may result from separate exercises of scholarly judgment at different times.

The distinction between scholars and followers of desires—despite inconsistencies in the statements of both—is that the scholar has fulfilled what he was commanded to do in sincerity of intent and diligent inquiry. On the basis of the evidence available to him, he is commanded to believe what that evidence supports, even if it does not correspond to reality; his belief, however, is not one of certainty. Likewise, a judge is commanded to accept the testimony of two upright witnesses, even if, in reality, they have erred or lied. Similarly, a mufti is commanded to accept the report of an upright, accurate informant, or to follow what is apparent. He therefore believes what this indicates, even if that belief does not correspond to reality. Thus, the belief required is what appears most probable on the basis of what people are commanded to follow, even though it may not correspond to reality. Yet they are never, in actual truth, commanded to hold a belief that does not correspond to reality.

If a scholar holds two contradictory beliefs concerning one or two issues, while intending to reach the truth and following what he has been commanded to follow of the Book and wisdom, he is excused for what he did not know. This is the error for which we are not held accountable. The followers of desires, however, are different, for:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire.”

They assert with certainty what they say on the basis of conjecture and desire

---

## Page 14299

with a certainty that admits no contrary possibility, despite lacking knowledge to warrant it. Thus, they believe what they have not been commanded to believe, either inwardly or outwardly. They pursue aims they have not been commanded to pursue and undertake inquiry they have not been commanded to undertake. Neither their inquiry nor their intent, therefore, warrants forgiveness for what they did not know. They are consequently either wrongdoers resembling those who have incurred wrath, or ignorant people resembling those who have gone astray.

One who undertakes purely scholarly inquiry has no aim other than the truth and has followed the path leading to it. By contrast, one who follows desire alone knows the truth but stubbornly resists it. There is also another category—to which most people belong—whose desires are entangled with misconceptions, so that desire and misconception combine.

For this reason, a mursal (disconnected) hadith reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah loves keen discernment when doubts arise, and He loves sound judgment when desires arise.”

Thus, one whose scholarly inquiry is untainted is forgiven and rewarded, whereas one who follows desire alone deserves punishment. As for someone whose inquiry combines misconception and desire, he is at fault. Such people differ in degree according to which predominates and according to the good deeds that efface their misdeeds.

---

## Page 14300

Most later scholars affiliated with jurisprudence or Sufism are afflicted by this.

This view, supported by Mālik’s principles, Ahmad’s principles, and some of the principles of other scholars, is the soundest view. Most of the early Muslims’ dealings also support it. People’s livelihoods cannot function properly without it. Anyone who goes too far in prohibiting what he considers uncertainty in transactions will inevitably find himself compelled to permit what Allah has forbidden. He must then either depart from the school he follows on this issue or resort to a stratagem. We have observed people and received accounts of their conduct, yet we have never seen anyone consistently adhere to his school’s prohibition of these transactions; indeed, he cannot do so. We know with certainty that the harm involved in prohibition is not removed by the stratagem they propose.

It is therefore inconceivable that the Lawgiver would forbid something we need, then permit it only through a pointless stratagem that amounts to mere play.

I have reflected on most of what has led people to resort to stratagems and found that it comes down to one of two things. The first is sins for which they were punished by restrictions in their affairs. They could find no way to escape these restrictions except through stratagems, but those stratagems only increased their affliction. This is what happened to the Sabbath-breakers among the Jews, as Allah Most High says:

“Because of wrongdoing on the part of the Jews, We forbade them good things that had been made lawful to them.”

This is a sin in conduct. The second is excessive strictness arising from their belief that the Lawgiver has prohibited certain things. This belief then compels them to seek permission through stratagems. This is an error in scholarly judgment. Otherwise, whoever fears Allah and takes what He has made lawful

---

## Page 14301

for him and fulfils his obligations, Allah will never leave him in need of innovated stratagems. For He, glorified be He, has imposed no hardship upon us in religion, and He sent our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the easy religion of pure monotheism.

The first cause, then, is wrongdoing, and the second is lack of knowledge. Wrongdoing and ignorance are the two human traits mentioned in His words:

“But man undertook it; indeed, he was unjust and ignorant.”

The underlying principle is this: Allah, glorified be He, prohibited certain substances, such as blood, carrion, and pork, and certain dealings, such as gambling, usury, and the sales involving uncertainty and other transactions that fall within them, because of the harms they entail. Allah and His Messenger drew attention to these harms, as in His words, glorified be He:

“Satan only wants to cause animosity and hatred between you through intoxicants and gambling and to turn you away from the remembrance of Allah and from prayer. Will you not then desist?”

He thus tells us that gambling breeds animosity and hatred, whether it involves money or play. Pointless competition and taking wealth without right instil such feelings in people’s hearts.

Likewise, Zayd ibn Thābit, may Allah be pleased with him, the Companion who was a jurist of Medina, related:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, people used to buy and sell fruit. When they harvested it and payment fell due, the buyer would say, ‘The fruit was affected by rot; it was affected by disease; it suffered premature shedding’—afflictions they invoked to support their claims. When disputes over this became frequent in his presence, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘In that case, do not buy or sell until there are visible signs of

---

## Page 14302

the fruit’s ripening.’ He offered this as advice to them because of their frequent disputes and disagreements.” Khārijah ibn Zayd mentioned:

“Zayd would not sell the fruit of his land until the Pleiades rose, when the red could be distinguished from the yellow.”

Al-Bukhārī related it with the beginning of its chain of transmission omitted, and Abū Dāwūd related it up to the words “their disputes.”

Ahmad also related in his Musnad that he said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came to Medina while we were buying and selling fruit before it showed signs of ripening. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, heard a dispute and asked, ‘What is this?’ He was told, ‘These people bought fruit and are saying that it suffered rot and premature shedding.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then said, ‘Do not buy or sell it until it shows signs of ripening.’”

He thus reported that the reason for the Prophet’s prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was the disputes to which these transactions led. The same applies to sales involving uncertainty. His prohibition against selling fruit before it shows signs of ripening is established in the two Sahīh collections through reports from Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, Jābir, and Anas. Muslim also records it through a report from Abū Hurayrah.

The report of Anas explains the reason for this prohibition. In the two Sahīh collections, Anas reported:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling fruit until it began to ripen.
