It was asked, ‘What is meant by ripening?’ He replied, ‘Until it turns red or yellow.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then said, ‘Tell me: if Allah withholds the fruit, on what grounds would any of you take his brother’s money?’”

Another version states:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling fruit until it began to ripen. We asked Anas, ‘What is meant by its ripening?’ He replied, ‘It turns red

---

## Page 14303

or yellow. Tell me: if Allah withholds the fruit, on what grounds would you consider your brother’s money lawful for you?’”

Abū Masʿūd al-Dimashqī said:

“Mālik and al-Darāwardī included Anas’s words, ‘Tell me: if Allah withholds the fruit,’ in the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, interpolating them into it. This is considered an error.”

Whether this explanation came from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or from Anas, it makes clear that this entails consuming wealth wrongfully: a person takes money under an exchange contract without guaranteeing anything in return.

If the harm in sales involving uncertainty is that they are liable to cause enmity, hatred, and the wrongful consumption of wealth, then it is evident that when an overriding benefit outweighs this harm, that benefit takes precedence. Thus, because horse racing, archery contests, and camel racing serve a benefit recognized by Islamic law, prizes are permissible in these competitions, even though they are not permissible in others.

Likewise, the amusement in which a man engages is vain if it serves no benefit. If, however, it does serve a benefit—as in the activities mentioned by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he said:

“All amusement in which a man engages is vain except shooting with his bow, training his horse, and playing with his wife, for these are worthwhile”—

then such amusement becomes worthwhile.

It is evident that prohibiting these transactions would cause people greater harm than the mutual hatred and wrongful consumption of wealth that might be feared from them, because the uncertainty involved in them

---

## Page 14304

is slight, as noted earlier, and the need for these transactions is pressing. A pressing need overrides a slight degree of uncertainty. The whole of Islamic law rests on the principle that when the harm warranting a prohibition is outweighed by a need, what is prohibited becomes permissible. How much more so when that harm is absent? Thus, because there is a need to leave fruit on the trees after its sale until it has fully ripened, Islamic law permits this, as do the majority of scholars. We shall explain the underlying principle, Allah Most High willing.

Accordingly, the position of the people of Medina and the jurists of the hadith tradition is that if the fruit is destroyed by a calamity after its sale, the seller bears the loss. Muslim narrated in his Sahīh that Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take anything from him. On what grounds would you take your brother’s wealth without right?”

In another report narrated by Muslim, Jābir said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered remission of the price for fruit lost to calamities.”

Since this hadith had not reached al-Shāfiʿī, may Allah be pleased with him—he had received only a report transmitted by Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah with conflicting versions—he adopted the position of the Kufan jurists on this matter: the buyer bears the loss, because the purchased goods have perished after possession was taken, and granting the buyer access to them constitutes taking possession.

This is more consistent with the Kufan jurists’ own principle, since the buyer has no right to leave the fruit on the trees. Rather, in their view, the contract requires immediate possession in all circumstances. This consistently applies an analogy whose basis and weakness we shall explain, together with

---

## Page 14305

the fact that human welfare cannot be sustained on that basis. Moreover, I know of no explicit report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establishing that when sold goods perish before the buyer can take possession, the seller bears the loss and the contract is dissolved, except this hadith concerning calamities.

Even if there were no prophetic teaching on this matter, sound reasoning would support this ruling. This is what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, indicated when he said:

“On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s wealth without right?”

The buyer of fruit can harvest it only when it is fully grown and ripe, not at the time of the contract, just as a lessee can obtain the benefit of a lease only gradually. Thus, fruit perishing before it can be harvested is like leased property perishing before its benefit can be obtained. In a lease, the lessor bears the loss by scholarly agreement; the same therefore applies to a sale. Abū Hanīfah distinguishes between the two on the grounds that the lessee has not acquired ownership of the benefit, while the buyer has not acquired the right to leave the fruit on the trees. Al-Shāfiʿī does not accept this distinction, whose basis we shall discuss.

Thus, since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had prohibited selling fruit until it showed signs of ripening—in one version narrated by Muslim, Ibn ʿUmar said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sell fruit to one another until it shows signs of ripening and the danger of blight has passed.”

In another version narrated by Muslim from him:

“He forbade selling dates until they began to colour, and ears of grain until they turned white and were safe from blight. He prohibited both the seller and the buyer.”

And in the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling dates until they were safe from every hazard.”—

---

## Page 14306

it is clear that the reason for the prohibition was not the fruit’s prior nonexistence. Even after it shows signs of ripening and is safe from blight, it develops additional parts that did not exist when the contract was concluded. Nor is what is meant safety from rare calamities, for that is unattainable. The fruit may suffer what Allah described concerning the owners of the garden who

“swore to harvest its fruit in the morning, making no exception,”

or what He described in Sūrat Yūnus:

“Until, when the earth has taken on its adornment and is beautified, and its people think they have power over it, Our command comes upon it by night or by day, and We make it a harvested field, as though it had not flourished the day before.”

Rather, what is meant is that the danger of regularly occurring blight has passed. Such blight strikes crops before the grain hardens and before signs of ripening appear in the fruit; after that, it is rare compared with its occurrence beforehand. Moreover, if selling it were prohibited beyond this stage, there would be no time at which it could lawfully be sold until it was fully ripe. Yet selling fruit on the trees after it has fully ripened is impracticable, since it does not all ripen at once. Requiring its owner to harvest it would cause harm outweighing the harm of contractual uncertainty.

It is thus clear that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave precedence to the benefit of permitting a sale that people need over the harm of minor contractual uncertainty. This accords with the principles of wisdom with which he was sent, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and which he taught his community.

Whoever consistently applies an analogy he has formed in his own mind, without considering an overriding impediment that counters its underlying rationale, will undermine much of religion and find both his understanding and his religion unduly constricted.

---

## Page 14307

Furthermore, Abū Rāfiʿ reported in Sahīh Muslim:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, borrowed a young camel from a man. When some camels collected as zakat were brought to him, he instructed Abū Rāfiʿ to repay the man with a young camel. Abū Rāfiʿ returned to him and said, ‘I found only a fine, mature camel among them.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Give it to him, for the best people are those who are best in repaying their debts.’”

This establishes the permissibility of borrowing animals and other things besides commodities measured by volume or weight. This is the position of the jurists of the Hijaz and the hadith scholars, contrary to those Kufan jurists who prohibit it on the grounds that a loan requires the return of an equivalent, whereas animals are not fungible. Their position also rests on the view that things other than commodities measured by volume or weight cannot become a liability owed in exchange for property. The hadith also establishes that an animal of approximately equivalent description may be owed as a liability, as is the well-known position in the schools of the former jurists. This is contrary to the Kufan position and one opinion within Ahmad’s school, according to which what is owed is the animal’s value.

This shows that an approximate description suffices to identify the subject matter of a contract. Otherwise, finding an animal exactly like the original would be exceedingly difficult—especially according to those who hold that animals are not fungible and that compensation for their wrongful appropriation or destruction is assessed by value.

Furthermore, the jurists have differed over making debts payable at the grain harvest or the date harvest. Two positions are reported from Ahmad. One permits this, in agreement with Mālik’s view, and Jābir’s hadith in the Sahīh supports it.

---

## Page 14308

Furthermore, the permissibility of concluding a marriage contract without specifying a dowry is established by the Book, in the words of the Most High:

“There is no blame upon you if you divorce women before you have touched them or specified a dowry for them.”

It is also established by the Sunnah, in the hadith of Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq, and by scholarly consensus. If the marriage is consummated, she is entitled, by their consensus, to the dowry customary for a woman of her standing. The same applies if her husband dies, according to the hadith jurists and the Kufan scholars who follow the hadith of Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq. This is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions. It is well known that such a customary dowry is approximate rather than precisely fixed. Had precise specification been required for the dowry, marriage without such specification would not have been permissible.

Ahmad also narrated in al-Musnad from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, may Allah be pleased with him:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade hiring a worker until his wage had been made clear to him, as well as sales concluded by touch, sham bidding, and sales concluded by casting a stone.”

Thus, the Sharia permits marriage before the dowry is specified, whereas hiring is permissible only when the wage is made clear. This establishes a distinction between the two.

The reason is that the subject matter of the marriage contract—the benefits of marital intimacy—is not precisely delimited; rather, it is governed by custom. The same therefore applies to the corresponding consideration, because the dowry is not the principal object of the contract but an ancillary gift. It thus resembles fruit included with a tree in a sale before the fruit begins to ripen.

Likewise, when the delegation of Hawāzin came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he gave them the choice between the captives and the property. They chose the captives, and he told them:

“I shall rise and address the people. Then say, ‘We seek the intercession of the Messenger of Allah

---

## Page 14309

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with the Muslims, and the intercession of the Muslims with the Messenger of Allah.’”

He then rose and addressed the people, saying:

“I have returned these people’s captives to them. Whoever wishes may willingly consent to this; whoever wishes otherwise, we shall give him ten young she-camels for each person, from the first spoils Allah grants us.”

This was compensation for emancipation, like the payment under a contract of emancipation, consisting of camels owed without further specification, payable after a variable, undefined period.

Al-Bukhārī narrated from Ibn ʿUmar, in the account of Khaybar:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fought them until he drove them into their fortress and gained control of the land, crops, and date palms. They then concluded a settlement with him whereby they would leave, taking whatever their riding camels could carry, while the gold, silver, and arms—that is, weapons—would belong to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. They were to depart, and he stipulated that they must conceal or hide nothing. If they did, they would have neither protection nor covenant.”

This was a settlement in return for property that was identifiable but not fully known.

Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded a settlement with the people of Najrān requiring them to deliver two thousand sets of clothing to the Muslims: half in Safar and the remainder in Rajab. In the event of a hostile plot or raid in Yemen, they were also to lend thirty coats of mail, thirty horses, thirty camels, and thirty of each kind of weapon for use in military expeditions. The Muslims would be liable for these until they returned them.”

Narrated by Abū Dāwūd. Thus, this was a settlement in return for unspecified garments of a known

---

## Page 14310

kind, without the specifications required for an advance-payment sale. Likewise, the horses, camels, and various kinds of weapons were to be lent without specification or description, contingent upon a condition that might or might not arise.

These texts therefore show that, where consideration is given in exchange for something other than property—as with the bridal due, payment under a contract of emancipation, compensation for release from marriage, a settlement in lieu of retaliation, the jizyah (poll tax), and a peace settlement with a belligerent people—knowledge of the price or fee is not obligatory. Nor should every contract involving uncertainty be treated by analogy with a sale involving uncertainty, because financial payments are either not obligatory under these contracts or are not their principal purpose. Where uncertainty affects something that is not the principal purpose, it does not lead to the harm mentioned in connection with sales. Rather, requiring precise specification in such matters entails hardship and difficulty, which Islamic law seeks to remove, exceeding the harm caused by leaving them unspecified.

Section

Among the much-needed applications of this principle, and among the questions concerning the sale of fruit before its soundness becomes apparent, is a situation that has become widespread in many, or most, Muslim lands, particularly Damascus. A property may contain planted trees and land suitable for cultivation, and sometimes dwellings as well

---

## Page 14311

and its owner wishes to lease it to someone who will irrigate and cultivate it, or live there as well. Where the property includes both land and planted trees, the jurists hold three opinions:

The first is that this is impermissible under all circumstances. This is the position of the Kufan jurists and al-Shāfiʿī, and it is the well-known position in Ahmad’s school according to most of his followers.

The second opinion is that it is permissible if the trees are few and the tree-free land constitutes two-thirds or more of the property. The same applies when someone rents a house containing a few date palms, grapevines, or the like. This is Mālik’s position, and both opinions have been reported from Ahmad.

Al-Kirmānī said:

“Ahmad was asked about a man renting land containing some date palms. He replied, ‘I fear that he may have rented trees that have not yet borne fruit.’ He seemed to disapprove of it. I think he meant when the trees were what the tenant intended to obtain. I understood no more than this from Ahmad.”

As noted earlier, two positions are reported from him concerning the sale of a commodity subject to the rules of usury in exchange for something of the same kind, when it is accompanied by something of a different kind and that other item is the principal object of the transaction—for example, a sheep with wool or milk sold in exchange for wool or milk. Most of his legal principles support permissibility, in agreement with Mālik’s position. For he says:

“If someone purchases a slave who possesses property, and his intended object is the slave, the transaction is permissible, even if the property is of unknown quantity or of the same kind as the price.”

This is also because he says:

“If someone purchases land or trees with fruit or crops that have not yet ripened, the transaction is permissible if his intended object is

---

## Page 14312

the land and the trees.” In sales, this is analogous to the leasing arrangement under discussion, for buying land is equivalent to purchasing it. Purchasing date palms, with fruit not yet safe from damage included in the sale as an accessory to the trees, is comparable to including the fruit of date palms and grapevines as an accessory to a lease.

Both groups base their prohibition on the established reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling future years’ fruit and selling fruit before its soundness becomes apparent. Thus, both al-Bukhārī and Muslim relate in their Sahīhs from Ibn ʿUmar:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling fruit before its soundness becomes apparent. He forbade both the seller and the buyer.”

They also relate from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, may Allah be pleased with them both, who said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling fruit until it ripens.” He was asked, “What does ‘ripens’ mean?” He replied, “It turns red or yellow and becomes edible.”

A narration recorded by Muslim states that this explanation was given by Saʿīd ibn al-Muthannā, who transmitted the report from Jābir.

Both Sahīhs also relate that Jābir said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited exchanging standing grain for harvested grain, exchanging dates on the palms for dried dates, selling future years’ crops (muʿāwama), and sharecropping.”

In another narration recorded by both, “and selling fruit for future years” appears in place of “muʿāwama.”

They also relate through Zayd ibn Abī Unaysa, from ʿAtāʾ, from Jābir:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited exchanging standing grain for harvested grain, exchanging dates on the palms for dried dates, and sharecropping, as well as purchasing dates on the palms before they ripen. Ripening means:

---

## Page 14313

that it turns red or yellow, or some of it is eaten. Exchanging standing grain for harvested grain means selling a field’s crop for a specified measure of grain. Exchanging dates on the palms for dried dates means selling the dates on the palms for a number of wasqs (standard measures) of dried dates. Sharecropping means an arrangement for a third or a quarter of the yield, or a similar share. Zayd said: I asked ʿAtāʾ, ‘Did you hear Jābir relate this from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

Both Sahīhs also relate from Abū al-Bakhtarī, who said:

“I asked Ibn ʿAbbās about selling dates on the palms. He replied, ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling dates on the palms until one eats some of their fruit—or some of it is eaten—and until it is weighed.’ I asked, ‘What does “weighed” mean?’ A man beside him replied, ‘Until it is secured.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sell fruit to one another before its soundness becomes apparent, and do not exchange dates for dates.”

Ibn al-Mundhir said: “The scholars unanimously agree that selling the fruit of date palms for future years is impermissible.”

They argue that when someone leases out the land and trees together, he has thereby sold the fruit before it comes into existence, selling its yield for one or two years. This is precisely what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited. Those who prohibit this without qualification consistently apply the general wording and the analogy.

Those who permit it when the fruit constitutes only a small part of the transaction argue that minor uncertainty is tolerable in contracts. This is comparable to purchasing date palms bearing fruit that has not yet been pollinated, or that has been pollinated but whose soundness has not yet become apparent. Such a purchase is permissible, even though the fruit may not be sold under a separate contract. This reasoning is particularly sound according to the legal principles of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and other jurists

---

## Page 14314

of the hadith tradition. However, this reasoning does not hold under Abū Hanīfa’s legal principles, because he does not permit purchasing fruit on condition that it remain on the trees, although he permits purchasing it before its soundness becomes apparent. The contract requires its immediate removal. Thus, when someone purchases the fruit together with the trees, his entitlement to leave it on them arises only because the trees belong to him. We shall discuss this principle, Allah willing.

Abū ʿUbayd stated that there is a consensus prohibiting the leasing of land containing many trees.

The third view is that land containing trees may be leased, with the trees included in the lease, without qualification. This is the view of Ibn ʿAqīl, and Harb al-Kirmānī inclined towards it. This view amounts virtually to a consensus among the early generations, although it differs from the position generally reported from the imams whose schools are followed. Saʿīd ibn Mansūr narrated—and Harb al-Kirmānī narrated it from him in his Masāʾil (Legal Questions)—saying: ʿAbbād ibn ʿAbbād narrated to us, from Hishām ibn ʿUrwa, from his father:

“Usayd ibn Hudayr died owing six thousand dirhams. ʿUmar summoned his creditors and assigned them his land for a number of years, including its date palms and other trees.”

Furthermore, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb imposed land tax on the Sawād and elsewhere. He left the land containing date palms and grapevines in the hands of its inhabitants, and levied on every jarīb (a unit of land area) of land, whether planted with trees or treeless, a land tax

---

## Page 14315

at a fixed rate.

The well-known account is that he levied ten dirhams on each jarīb of grapevines, eight dirhams on each jarīb of date palms, six dirhams on each jarīb of green fodder, and one dirham and a qafīz of grain on each jarīb of cultivated crops.

The generally accepted position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad is that this land-tax arrangement is treated as a lease. He refrained from setting a fixed term for it only in the public interest, and the land tax constitutes rent for the land. This is precisely the leasing of land containing trees, something upon which ʿUmar and the Muslims reached consensus in his time and thereafter. Hence Abū ʿUbayd expressed surprise at this in his Kitāb al-Amwāl (The Book of Wealth), considering this arrangement contrary to what he knew of the jurists’ positions.

Ibn ʿAqīl’s argument is that leasing land is permissible and needed. When it contains trees, it cannot be leased without leasing the trees as well; whatever is indispensable to something permissible is itself permissible. The tenant will not water the trees gratuitously, and he may not enter into an agreement to tend them for a share of their fruit.

Similarly, although Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī held that analogical reasoning precludes sharecropping, when a worker enters into an agreement to tend trees for a share of their fruit, and there is unplanted ground among them, they permit sharecropping on that ground as an adjunct to the fruit-sharing agreement. Mālik permits this if it amounts to less than one third, just as he held concerning the sale of trees as an adjunct to the land. Likewise, al-Shāfiʿī permits it if the unplanted ground is small in extent

---

## Page 14316

and the date palms cannot be watered without watering it. If the unplanted ground is extensive and the date palms few, his followers hold two opinions on the matter. This applies when both arrangements are combined in a single contract and the stipulated share is the same in each, such as one third or one quarter. If the two shares differ, his followers hold two opinions. Likewise, if he concludes two separate contracts, beginning with the fruit-sharing agreement, there are two opinions. If, however, he begins with the sharecropping contract, that contract is invalid according to the sole opinion on the matter. Thus, he permits sharecropping—which both of them otherwise disallow—as an adjunct to a fruit-sharing agreement. By the same reasoning, he can permit the leasing of trees as an adjunct to leasing the land. Ibn ʿAqīl’s position undoubtedly follows by analogy from one of the two opinions held by al-Shāfiʿī’s followers.

A further reason is that those who prohibit this either resort to a stratagem to make it permissible, engage in what they believe to be unlawful, or cause harm and suffer harm themselves.

The Kufan jurists have resorted to stratagems to make it permissible. Sometimes the owner leases only the land and grants the tenant permission to take the fruit of the trees. This resembles their position on selling fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent: he sells it to the buyer, either without qualification or on condition that it be picked, for the full amount of the rent, and then permits him to leave it on the trees. This stratagem is reported from Abū Hanīfa, al-Thawrī, and others.

At other times, he leases the land to him for the full rent and concludes a fruit-sharing agreement concerning the trees on preferential terms—for example, stipulating that the owner receive only one thousandth of the fruit. This stratagem is permitted only by those who permit fruit-sharing agreements, such as Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad

---

## Page 14317

and al-Shāfiʿī in his earlier position. Abū Hanīfa, however, does not permit it under any circumstances; and al-Shāfiʿī, in his later position, permits it only for date palms and grapevines. Thus, in this transaction, they have been compelled to designate the rent as payment for the use of the land, while the owner gratuitously grants the tenant either the fruit of the trees or preferential terms in the fruit-sharing agreement. The need for this transaction is so pressing that one of Imam Ahmad’s followers who wrote against legal stratagems included this stratagem among those that are permissible—I mean the stratagem of granting preferential terms in a fruit-sharing agreement.

Yet the explicit position transmitted from Ahmad and most of his followers is that this very stratagem is invalid, as is also the position of Mālik and others. Prohibiting these stratagems is unquestionably correct, on account of the report of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, to stipulate two conditions in a sale, to profit from something for which one has not assumed liability, or to sell what you do not have.”

This was narrated by the five imams: Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasāʾī, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah. Al-Tirmidhī said: “A good and authentic hadith.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus prohibited combining a loan with a sale. Combining a loan with a lease constitutes combining a loan with a sale, or something equivalent to it. Every gratuitous benefit combined with a sale or lease—such as a gift, a loan for use, a grant of tree-fruit, or preferential terms in a fruit-sharing or sharecropping agreement, and so forth—is equivalent to a loan in this respect.

The overall meaning of the hadith, then, is that one must not combine an exchange for consideration with a gratuitous benefit, because that

---

## Page 14318

gratuitous benefit was granted only for the sake of the exchange, not unconditionally. It therefore becomes part of the consideration. If the two parties agree that it is not consideration, they combine two mutually contradictory things.

For someone who lends a man one thousand dirhams and sells him an item worth five hundred for one thousand agrees to lend only because of the inflated price of the item. The buyer, in turn, agrees to pay that inflated price only because of the thousand he has borrowed. Thus, the sale is not truly a sale for one thousand, nor is the loan a pure loan. Rather, the reality is that he has given him one thousand and the item in return for two thousand. This is the case known as “a mudd-measure of ʿajwa dates.” If the purpose is to exchange one thousand for more than one thousand, it is unquestionably forbidden; otherwise, its ruling falls under the well-known scholarly disagreement.

The same applies when someone leases land with a rental value of one hundred for one thousand, while its owner grants him the trees’ fruit free of charge or agrees to receive only one-thousandth of their yield. It is self-evident that the owner granted the fruit only because of the thousand he received, and that the tenant paid the thousand only for the sake of the fruit. The fruit is therefore the principal object of the contract, or part of it. The stratagem is nothing but a form of manipulation and corruption; otherwise, the intended object of the contract is clear.

Those who do not resort to stratagems, or who do so despite having recognized the invalidity of this stratagem, face two alternatives. Either they engage in this transaction out of need, believing that they are doing something forbidden—as we have seen most people do—or they refrain from it and forgo obtaining the fruit covered by this transaction, thereby suffering such harm

---

## Page 14319

and distress as only Allah knows.

Even if one or two people could abide by this, Muslims as a whole could not do so without ruining their wealth—something no revealed law has ever prescribed, let alone a law concerning which Allah says:

“He has placed no hardship upon you in religion.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Allah intends ease for you and does not intend hardship for you.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Allah wishes to lighten your burden.”

In the two Sahīh collections:

“You were sent only to make things easy.”

“Make things easy and do not make them difficult.”

“So that the Jews may know that there is latitude in our religion.”

Thus, prohibiting anything indispensable to people’s livelihood constitutes hardship, which the Sharia rules out.

The point here is that the Muslim community could never abide by a prohibition of this kind, because of the unbearable harm it would entail. It is therefore clear that this is not forbidden. Indeed, such a prohibition would be more severe than the shackles and burdens imposed upon the Children of Israel, which Allah lifted from us through Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Anyone who comprehensively examines the Sharia in its various applications will find it founded upon His words, Most High:

“But whoever is compelled by necessity, without wilful disobedience or transgression, incurs no sin.”

And His words:

“But whoever is compelled by severe hunger, without inclining to sin—then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

Whatever people need for their livelihood is therefore not forbidden to them, provided that the need has not arisen from a sin—whether neglecting an obligation or committing a forbidden act. For they are in the same position as someone compelled by necessity who is neither wilfully disobedient nor transgressing. If, however, the need has arisen from a sin—as with someone who undertakes a sinful journey and is compelled during it to eat carrion, or someone who spends his wealth

---

## Page 14320

on sinful acts until he falls into debt—he is instructed to repent and is permitted whatever relieves his necessity. Thus, he is permitted to eat carrion, and his debt may be paid from zakat. If he does not repent, then he is wronging himself and resorting to stratagems. His situation resembles that of those concerning whom Allah said:

“When their fish came to them openly on their Sabbath day, but did not come to them on days when they did not observe the Sabbath. Thus We tested them because of their disobedience.”

And His words:

“For the wrongdoing of those who were Jews, We forbade them good things which had been lawful to them, and for their hindering…”

to the end of the verse. This is an important principle to which we may draw attention, Allah willing.

This view, transmitted from the early generations and adopted by Ibn Aqīl, follows by analogy from Ahmad’s principles and some of al-Shāfi‘ī’s principles. It is the correct view, Allah Most High willing, for several reasons, in addition to the scriptural and rational evidence showing that this is not forbidden. The force of this evidence, however, is fully established only after answering the arguments advanced by those who hold the first view.

The first reason:

What we have already mentioned of Umar’s action in the case of Usayd ibn al-Hudayr: he leased out the land together with its trees in return for the sum owed to the creditors. This is precisely the issue under discussion. This cannot be explained by supposing that the date palms and other trees were few, for it is well known that trees occupied most of the gardens of the people of Medina. Usayd ibn al-Hudayr was among the leaders and wealthy men of the Ansār, so it is unlikely that most of his garden consisted of land without trees. Moreover, this incident must have become widely known, yet no objection to it has reached us; it therefore constitutes a consensus. The same applies to the land tax he imposed on

---

## Page 14321

al-Sawād; calling it kharāj indicates that it is payment for the benefit they derive from the land and its trees. Likewise, people today call the rent paid by someone who plants trees on land kharāj when a specified amount is due for each tree. The same usage occurs in His words:

“Or do you ask them for payment? But the reward of your Lord is best.”

Another example is the kharāj paid by a slave: a fixed sum he pays his master from his own funds. Whoever regards it as rent must accept the permissibility of such an arrangement, since it is established by the consensus of the Companions. Whoever regards it as a purchase price or an independent form of payment acknowledges that it is unlike other payments. The Companions permitted it—despite its having no parallel—only because of the need for it. That same need exists in every tract of land containing trees, just as it does in conquered lands.

If it is said, “Orchard sharecropping or crop sharecropping is possible,” the reply is: Umar, too, could have used orchard or crop sharecropping, as was done during the Abbasid era, either during al-Mansūr’s caliphate or afterwards. They converted the system applied to the lands of al-Sawād from a land tax to produce-sharing, which is orchard and crop sharecropping.

If it is said, “The rent could be charged for the land alone, while use of the trees is granted free of charge or on preferential terms,” the reply is: Umar could also have done that. The common basis between the two cases is therefore clear.

Furthermore, we know with certainty that Muslims have always owned lands containing trees

---

## Page 14322

that are leased out; indeed, such properties make up the greater part of the wealth of town-dwellers. We know that not all the early Muslims cultivated their lands themselves—not even most of them. We also know that orchard and crop sharecropping may not always be feasible, since they require a trustworthy worker. Not everyone is willing to enter into orchard sharecropping, nor is everyone who takes land willing to share its produce. They must, therefore, have leased out tree-covered land.

It is well known that resorting to a gratuitous grant as a legal device was uncommon, and was not practised by the early Muslims among the Companions and Successors. The only remaining possibility is that they acted as Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, did with the property of Usayd ibn al-Hudayr, and as most Muslims have done from those times down to the present day. Since no report has been transmitted that the early Muslims prohibited this lease, or that they were instructed to resort to the device of a gratuitous grant—despite the existing need for this transaction—we know with certainty that Muslims engaged in it without objection from the Companions and Successors. Its practice thus constituted a consensus among them.

Perhaps those who differed over leasing bare land and practising crop sharecropping on it did not differ over leasing tree-covered land or practising orchard sharecropping, because the benefit derived from the land does not amount to much compared with the benefit derived from the trees.

If it is objected:

Harb al-Kirmānī said: Ahmad was asked about the meaning of

---

## Page 14323

the report of Ibn Umar:

“Qabāla contracts (estate-leasing arrangements) constitute usury.”

He replied: “It means taking on a village estate containing date palms and non-Arab, non-Muslim peasants.”

He was asked: “What if it contains no date palms and is simply bare land?”

He replied: “There is nothing wrong with that; in this case, he is merely a tenant.”

He was asked: “What if there are such peasants on it?”

He replied: “That is the disapproved form of qabāla.”

Harb said: Ubayd Allah ibn Muādh narrated to us: My father narrated to us: Shuba narrated to us, from Jabala, who heard Ibn Umar say:

“Qabāla contracts constitute usury.”

The answer is: In transactions where deferment is permissible, usury arises only through an unequal exchange of things of the same kind. Thus, when rent, a purchase price, or something similar is described as usury despite deferment being permissible, it is because the transaction involves exchanging things of the same kind in unequal quantities. Usury is either usury through deferment—which occurs only where deferment is permissible—or usury through an excess, which occurs only within a single kind. Once usury through deferment, meaning delay, is ruled out, only usury through an excess remains: an increase in the quantity of one of two things of the same kind.

This occurs when the estate is leased in return for produce of the same kind as its yield—for example, when someone takes on land containing fruit-bearing date palms. It is then like muzābana.

This resembles leasing land in return for produce of the same kind as that grown on it, when the rent is owed as a personal liability. An example is leasing land to grow wheat in return for a specified quantity of wheat. Two positions are reported from Ahmad on this matter. One is that it constitutes usury, which is also Mālik’s position. This resembles the qabāla that Ibn Umar disapproved of: the person has taken on the land to grow wheat in return for a specified quantity of wheat. It is therefore as though he had purchased wheat in exchange for wheat that might be greater or smaller in quantity; thus the usurious element becomes apparent.

---

## Page 14324

Thus, the qabāla contracts that Ibn Umar described as usury involve leasing land containing date palms and peasants for a specified quantity of produce of the same kind as its yield. For example, a man owns a village estate containing trees and land, with peasants working for him. It yields him wheat and dates after the peasants’ wages or share have been deducted. Another man then leases it from him for a known quantity of wheat, dates, or similar produce. This explains why it is called usury.

Leasing land for dirhams or dinars, however, does not fall within the category of usury at all. Whoever prohibits it regards it instead as a case of contractual uncertainty. Moreover, Ahmad did not disapprove of this arrangement when it involved bare land, because, according to one of the two positions reported from him, leasing is permissible even when the rent is produce of the same kind as the yield. This is because the tenant works the land using his own labour and resources, so the yield results from his own activity.

This differs from the case where the land contains non-Arab, non-Muslim peasants who undertake the work. The tenant contributes neither his own labour nor his resources to working it; rather, the peasants cultivate it. He pays the lease charge and collects the proceeds in return. This amounts to seeking profit through an exchange of wealth without engaging in any craft or trade, and this is usury.

A comparable case is the practice reported to have been described as usury by Ibn Umar: renting bathhouses, mills, inns, and similar properties that the tenant does not himself use, whether for trade or for practising a craft. He rents them solely to sublet them. This has been described as usury.

In sum, these contracts were not usurious on account of the date palms or the land if

---

## Page 14325

the rent was of a different kind from the produce yielded; rather, they were usurious because of the peasants. There is no need for this arrangement, since the peasants undertake the work; leasing the estate to someone else therefore amounts to dealing in usury with him. This is why Ahmad disapproved of it even when the land was bare, if there were peasants working it.

Harb al-Kirmānī cited as evidence on this question the Prophet’s arrangement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with the people of Khaybar: they were to receive half of its fruit or crops, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense. In substance, this amounted to leasing the land to them for a portion of its yield, together with leasing the trees for half their fruit. By analogy, the land and trees may therefore be leased for a guaranteed payment. For if granting the fruit were tantamount to selling it, granting a portion of it would likewise be tantamount to selling it, which is impermissible.

This question rests on two principles:

The first principle is that whenever there is land or housing among the trees, the need arises to lease both together. This is permissible because of that need, even if it involves a slight degree of contractual uncertainty, particularly if the orchard is an endowment or belongs to an orphan. It is impermissible to leave its benefits unrealized, while leasing the land or housing alone is not customary, and no one would enter into a lease on those terms. If anyone did lease it, they would do so only at a rent far below its value.

Whatever is necessary for carrying out a permissible act is itself permissible. Thus, whenever something is established as permissible by a scriptural text or scholarly consensus, whatever it necessarily entails must also be deemed permissible, provided that no scriptural text or consensus prohibits those necessary concomitants. If, however, evidence indicates that those concomitants are prohibited—and whatever must be avoided in order to avoid the prohibited is itself prohibited—then the two proofs conflict. In the present case, what has been established is the permissibility of

---

## Page 14326

leasing land, as established by the Sunnah and the agreement of the leading jurists. By contrast, the prohibition on including trees in the lease is disputed, and there is no scriptural text prohibiting it.

Furthermore, whenever the land alone is leased while the trees are retained, the tenant cannot be trusted with the fruit, leading to conflicting control and an unsatisfactory partnership. This is comparable to the case in which ripeness becomes apparent in one variety. This position yields a ruling similar to that of al-Layth ibn Sa'd: if ripeness becomes apparent in one kind of fruit, and selling the different kinds separately would cause harm, all the kinds may be sold together, because dividing the transaction would entail hardship.

Moreover, if the owner subsequently wishes to sell the fruit, he will find no one willing to buy it while the land and dwellings are held by someone else, except at a substantial discount. Further, if he leases the land and stipulates that the tenant must irrigate the trees—when irrigation is itself part of what the lease covers—then part of what the tenant is paying for becomes his payment. If he does not stipulate irrigation, then, when the tenant waters them—if the lessor contracts with him to irrigate them for a share of the fruit—the lease becomes valid only in conjunction with that fruit-sharing irrigation agreement. If he makes no such agreement with him, the tenant must lose the use of what he has leased. Thus, the alternatives are that the rent consists of part of the leased benefit, that the lease is valid only in conjunction with a fruit-sharing irrigation agreement, or that the tenant is deprived of the benefit of his lease.

Then, if the lessor receives all or part of the fruit, selling it while the land and dwellings are held by someone else reduces its value in many circumstances. The matter therefore comes down to this: when dividing a transaction would cause harm, the two things may be combined in an exchange, even if neither could permissibly be contracted for separately, because the ruling on combining them differs from

---

## Page 14327

the ruling on separating them.

For this reason, according to Ahmad and most jurists, when partition is impossible, one co-owner must join the other in selling the jointly owned property, or in leasing it if what they jointly own is a right of use. This is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever frees his share in a slave and possesses enough wealth to cover the slave’s price must have the slave assessed at a fair value and pay his co-owners their shares, whereupon the slave becomes entirely free at his expense. Otherwise, only the share he has freed becomes free.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded this in their Sahīh collections. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus ordered that the entire slave be valued and that the co-owner be given his share of that value. It is well known that the value of his share sold separately is less than his proportion of the value of the whole. It follows that his entitlement is to half of the half-share. If he is entitled to this through emancipation, then he is all the more entitled to it in other cases of destruction of property. What is due as compensation for destruction is only what would be due in an exchange. It follows that he is entitled, through an exchange, to half the value, and this can only be obtained by selling the whole. The property itself must therefore be divided whenever division causes no harm; if it would cause harm, its value is divided instead.

If we require a co-owner to sell his share because separating the shares would diminish the value of his partner’s share, then it is all the more appropriate to permit the sale of two things together when separating them would cause harm. Likewise, a ewe may be sold together with the milk in her udder, even though the two could be separated by milking and the milk could not permissibly be sold on its own.

On this principle, this is permissible whenever the trees are accompanied by a benefit sought for its own sake

---

## Page 14328

such as the use of land for cultivation or of a building for residence. But if the fruit alone is intended, and the use of the land or dwelling is not part of what is sought, but has merely been included as a legal stratagem—as sometimes occurs in “mudd ʿajwa” (a measure of ʿajwa dates) transactions—this principle does not apply.

The second principle: It may be argued that leasing trees for their fruit is analogous to leasing land for cultivation and hiring a wet nurse to breastfeed.

This is because yields that are replenished while their sources remain are treated like usufructs, even though they are tangible substances. These include the fruit of trees, the milk of women and livestock, wool, and fresh water. Whenever any of these is produced and taken, Allah creates a replacement while the source remains; in this respect, they are exactly like usufructs. Accordingly, they are treated as usufructs in endowments, gratuitous loans, and arrangements in which compensation consists of a share of the yield. An endowment can consist only of something that may be used while its underlying substance remains. Thus, just as bare land or residential properties may be endowed for their use, orchards may be endowed for their fruit, livestock for their milk and wool, and wells and springs for their water. This differs from things consumed through use, such as food and the like, which cannot be endowed.

As for gratuitous loans, granting someone the use of a mount for riding is called ifqār; one says, “He lent him the mount to ride.” An animal whose milk is made freely available is called a manīha; a tree whose fruit is made freely available is called an ʿariyya; other such loans are called ʿāriya. They likened this to a loan that the borrower uses and then repays with its equivalent. An example is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The loan of an animal for its milk, or a loan of silver.”

---

## Page 14329

Thus, leasing trees in order to tend them and take their fruit is comparable to hiring a wet nurse for her milk. The only hiring contract explicitly mentioned in the Quran is that of a wet nurse, in the words of Allah, glorified be He:

“If they breastfeed for you, give them their payment.”

Some jurists, believing that a hiring contract can concern only a usufruct and not a tangible substance, yet recognizing the permissibility of hiring a wet nurse, said: “The subject of the contract is placing the child in her lap; the milk is included implicitly and incidentally, like the water in a well.” This flies in the face of both reason and sensory experience. We know beyond question that the milk is the object of the contract, as Allah indicates by His words:

“If they breastfeed for you.”

Holding the child in her lap, if done, is merely a means to that end.

The underlying reason is simply what I have mentioned: a yield that is replenished while its source remains is treated as a usufruct. This is not a sale in the strict sense, for Allah calls the compensation a payment for services, not a purchase price. This differs from the case where the milk has already been drawn: exchanging it for compensation is then called only a sale, because the yield is not being obtained from its source in the way that a usufruct is obtained from its source.

Thus, tangible yields that can be separated from their sources have two states. In one, they resemble pure usufructs: while still attached to their sources, they are obtained in the same way as a usufruct. In the other, they resemble tangible goods as such: once separated, possession of them is taken in the same way as possession of tangible goods.

If the owner of the trees is the one who waters and tends them until the fruit is fit for harvest, he is selling nothing but fruit, just as if he were the one who tills the land

---

## Page 14330

and sows it and waters it until the crop is ready: he is selling nothing but a crop, even if the buyer is the one who picks the fruit and reaps the crop. This is like selling produce on the land, leaving the buyer to move it and carry it away. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, coupled the two in prohibiting the sale of grain before it hardens and the sale of fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent, for these are simply sales of fruit and crops.

But if the owner hands the tree over to a lessee to water it, pollinate it, and protect it from harm, this is comparable to handing land over to someone to till, sow, and water it. Hence he treated the two alike in fruit-sharing and crop-sharing arrangements. Just as leasing land is not a sale of its crops, leasing a tree is not a sale of its fruit. Rather, leasing trees stands in the same relation to leasing land as fruit-sharing does to crop-sharing. The sharing arrangement is a transaction in return for a portion of the yield, whereas the lease is for a specified payment.

Since these yields are treated on a par with usufructs when their sources are endowed, when the yields themselves are donated, when a partnership is formed for a share of their increase, and when they are exchanged for compensation once ripe, they should likewise be treated on a par with usufructs in contracts for obtaining and procuring them.

If someone were to distinguish between the two on the grounds that crops are produced only through work, whereas fruit is produced without work, this distinction would have no bearing on the ruling, as fruit-sharing and crop-sharing arrangements demonstrate. Nor is it correct, for work affects the production of fruit just as it affects the growth of crops. Without tending the trees, the fruit may fail altogether or be diminished, for

---

## Page 14331

some trees would bear no fruit unless they were watered. If tending a tree had no effect whatsoever, it would not be permissible to entrust it to a worker in return for a share of its fruit. Nor, in such a case, would it be permissible to lease it before its fruit showed signs of ripeness, for that would be purely a sale of the fruit, not a lease of the tree. It would be like leasing one’s land to someone to take whatever Allah causes it to produce without anyone’s labour at all, before that produce exists.

If it is said, “What is sought through this contract is uncertain, since the tree may yield little or much,” the reply is: “The same applies to leasing land. On that reasoning, what is sought through the contract is also uncertain, since the land may produce little or much.”

If it is said, “What is contracted for there is the opportunity to cultivate, not the crops that actually grow,” the reply is: “What is contracted for here is the opportunity to produce fruit, not the fruit that actually emerges.” It is well known that what is sought in both cases is the crops and the fruit. Payment, however, is due merely for the opportunity to obtain them. Likewise, the purpose of renting a house is to live in it, although payment becomes due upon being given the opportunity to do so.

Thus, what is sought in leasing land for cultivation is the actual produce that

---

## Page 14332

is harvested. This differs from leasing it for residence or construction, where the purpose is the use itself, by placing things upon the land. This becomes clear upon reflection, and further examination only makes it clearer. Thus it becomes evident that what the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) prohibited—selling fruit before it shows signs of ripeness and selling grain before it hardens—is not, Allah willing, leasing trees or land to someone whose labour and watering produce their fruit or crops. Such leasing falls under neither the wording nor the meaning of his prohibition.

This is further clarified by the following: the seller of the fruit must continue watering and tending the trees until the buyer is able to harvest it, just as the seller of crops must continue watering them until the buyer is able to reap them. This is part of fulfilling the obligation to deliver what has been sold, and the costs of doing so fall upon the seller, as do the costs of measuring and weighing. By contrast, someone who leases trees to a person who tends them until they bear fruit is like someone who leases land to a person who cultivates it until it produces crops. The lessor is not required to perform any work at all; he is required only to enable the lessee to carry out the work through which the fruit and crops are produced.

It may, however, be objected: “Consistent application of this reasoning would permit leasing animals to someone who feeds and waters them and milks them.”

The reply is that if, according to one of the two reported opinions, we permit livestock to be entrusted to someone who

---

## Page 14333

feeds and waters them in return for a share of their milk and offspring, then it is permissible to entrust them to someone who tends them for their milk and offspring in exchange for a guaranteed payment.

If it is asked, “Why, then, should leasing them for milking not be permissible, just as hiring a wet nurse is permissible?”

The reply is: Hiring a wet nurse is for her to provide suckling, with the work, in the case of sheep, being performed by their owner. For the wet nurse herself suckles the child. Since she herself provides the service, the analogous arrangement is one in which the lessor himself provides the service of suckling. In that case, analogy entails its permissibility. If a man owned sheep and hired another man’s sheep to suckle his own, there would be nothing impermissible about this.

If, however, the lessee himself milks them or takes possession of the milk, he is purchasing milk, rather than receiving a usufruct or obtaining the tangible product through his labour. This resembles purchasing fruit, and his milking resembles picking it. This is what the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) prohibited when he said:

“Milk still in an udder must not be sold.”

This differs from leasing the animals in order to tend them and milk them, which is analogous to leasing land and trees.

---

## Page 14334

Section

This applies when he leases him the land and trees, or a tree alone, so that he may tend them and take the fruit in return for a specified payment.

If, however, he sells him only the fruit and leases him the land for residence, then only the first principle applies: the one mentioned on the authority of Ibn ʿAqīl and, in part, of Mālik and Ahmad according to one of the two reports, where residence is the predominant purpose. This principle is that there is a need to combine the two transactions. Combining them therefore permits what would not be permissible separately, as in the analogous cases discussed earlier. This applies when both residence and the fruit are intended benefits, as is customary in the orchards of Damascus. An orchard is leased for the summer so that the lessee may reside there and take its fruit without undertaking any work whatsoever to cultivate the fruit. Rather, that work falls on the lessor who undertakes to supply it.

On the basis of that principle, this is permissible even if the fruit has not yet appeared at all, whether it is of one kind or several different kinds, just as the corresponding arrangement is permissible in the first category. It is permissible only because it combines the fruit with the usufruct. In reality, it combines a sale and a lease, unlike the first category, which may be described as a lease. For here, the expense of supplying the fruit falls on the lessor, and the fruit is produced through his work;

---

## Page 14335

unlike the first category, in which the trees bear fruit only through the lessee’s work.

For this reason, people call it damān (an undertaking of liability), since it is neither purely a sale nor purely a lease. It is therefore given the general name for an obligation undertaken in transactions for consideration and in other dealings: damān. Jurists likewise use this term in the statement: “Throw your goods into the sea, and I undertake liability for them.” The first category is also called damān, but that one is called a lease. When this category is called a lease or a rental, it is because part of it is a lease or rental, while it also includes a sale.

If, however, the usufruct is not sought at all for its own sake, but only for harvesting the fruit—for example, someone buys grapes or unripe dates and wishes to stay in the orchard to pick them—then this is not permissible before the fruit shows signs of ripening. Here, the usufruct is sought only for the sake of the fruit, so the fruit cannot be treated as incidental to it. Nor is there any need to lease the usufruct unless the sale of the fruit is permissible.

This differs from the preceding category. When the usufruct is itself an intended benefit, he needs to lease it and also needs to purchase the fruit; hence the need to combine the two transactions. If the lessee rents the property for residence, he cannot let someone else buy the fruit, nor can he fully obtain the benefit he seeks unless he has fruit to eat. His purpose is to enjoy residing in that place and eating its fruit.

Thus, when residence is the principal purpose and there are only a few trees—for example, a house containing a few date palms, a grape arbor, or the like—permissibility is Mālik’s position and follows by analogy from most of the express rulings of Ahmad and others.

---

## Page 14336

If, alongside residence, the purpose is to trade in the fruit, and its value exceeds that of the residential benefit, prohibition is more justified here than in the preceding case, as reflected in the distinction drawn by Mālik and Ahmad.

If the purpose is residence and eating the fruit, this resembles seeking residence and drinking water from the well. Even if the fruit consumed is worth more, the case for permissibility is stronger here than in the preceding case, though weaker than in the first, according to those who distinguish between them. According to Ibn ʿAqīl’s position, transmitted from the early generations, however, all these arrangements are permissible, as we have established, because the transactions are combined.

If he also stipulates that the lessor cultivate a cucumber plot for him, this is like leasing land from someone for cultivation on condition that the lessor till it. He has thus leased his land and contracted with him to provide work. This is permissible, just as one may hire a camel or donkey on condition that its owner transport the hirer’s belongings on it. This combines the hire of a specified asset with a contract to provide work, unless he stipulates that the provider must perform the work himself, in which case he has hired both a specified asset and a specified worker.

If residence is not an intended purpose, and the sole purpose is to purchase fruit in an orchard containing several varieties, with watering being the seller’s responsibility, this is permissible according to al-Layth. It also follows by analogy from the third position that we have mentioned from our fellow jurists and others and have substantiated. The need to combine the two varieties is like the need to combine the sale of fruit with the usufruct, and may be even greater. It may not be possible to sell each variety when it first shows signs of ripening: often this cannot be achieved at all, and sometimes it can be achieved only at the cost of considerable harm.

---

## Page 14337

I have seen someone make a prior arrangement with the purchaser on this basis, then allocate part of the price to each kind of fruit as it begins to ripen. These are feeble legal stratagems whose true nature is obvious, as discussed above. Scholars and believers of sound natural disposition continue to object to prohibiting transactions of this kind, and the fundamental principles of the Sharia are incompatible with such a prohibition. Yet what they have heard of general rules based on scriptural wording and analogy, which they believe encompass such transactions, together with the statements they have heard from scholars who include them within those general rules, is what has led to this result.

This also follows by analogy from the permissibility we have established of selling the entire crop of a cucumber plot once it begins to ripen. Separating its successive yields is difficult or impossible, just as separating the different varieties in a single orchard is difficult. The difficulty is, however, greater with a cucumber plot; hence some, such as Mālik, permitted its sale while prohibiting the combined sale of different varieties.

If it is objected:

This arrangement falls within the general scope of the Prophet’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—on selling fruit before it begins to ripen. This differs from leasing someone the land and trees for him to work, which, as you have explained, does not fall within that general prohibition, since it is a lease to someone who will work, not the sale of an asset. Here, however, it is a sale of fruit, so it falls within the prohibition. How, then, can you contravene that prohibition?

We reply:

The answer to this is the same as the answer concerning the purchase—permitted by the Sunnah and scholarly consensus—of trees together with their fruit before it begins to ripen, and of land together with its crops before their grain has hardened; and concerning our defence of purchasing cucumber crops even though some of

---

## Page 14338

their vegetables have not yet come into existence.

All these cases may be answered in two ways.

The first is to say that the wording of the prohibition does not encompass this case. The Prophet’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—against selling fruit referred to the sale familiar to those addressed and to similar transactions, because the definite article refers to what the audience knows. If a particular individual or category is already understood, the words refer to it. Thus, the wording refers to a particular Messenger in Allah’s words:

“Do not treat the Messenger’s summons to you like your summons to one another.”

And in His words:

“But Pharaoh disobeyed the Messenger.”

Or it refers to a particular category, as in his prohibition against selling fruit. There is no disagreement among Muslims that “fruit” here means fresh dates, not grapes or other fruit. If neither a particular individual nor a particular category is understood, the wording has general application. Thus, the sale of fruit mentioned is the sale of fruit familiar to them; its inclusion is like the inclusion of the second and third generations in the words the Messenger addressed to his Companions.

A parallel to this is Ahmad’s discussion of “the Prophet’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—against a man urinating in stagnant water that does not flow, then bathing in it.” He understood this to refer to the standing bodies of water familiar during the Prophet’s lifetime, such as the wells and pools between Mecca and Medina. As for the large reservoirs constructed after his time, which cannot be drained, he did not include them within the prohibition’s general scope, because of the substantive distinction and the absence of

---

## Page 14339

general scope in the wording.

Evidence that the wording in our case is not general is found in the report in the two Sahīhs from Anas ibn Mālik, may Allah be pleased with him:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling fruit until it begins to ripen. It was asked, ‘What does beginning to ripen mean?’ He replied, ‘It turns red and yellow.’”

One wording in Muslim reads:

“He forbade selling fruit until it begins to ripen.”

Another wording in Muslim reads:

“He forbade selling the fruit of date palms until it begins to ripen.”

It is clear that this means the fruit of date palms, as the explicitly qualified wording states, because this is the fruit that ripens by turning red or yellow. Other fruits ripen by becoming white, such as mulberries, apples, white grapes, white plums—which the people of Damascus call khawkh—and white peaches, known as firsik and called durrāq by the Damascenes. Others ripen by softening without changing colour, such as figs and similar fruits.

Accordingly, the two Sahīhs contain the following report from Jābir:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling fruit until it takes on its ripe colour. It was asked, ‘What does taking on its ripe colour mean?’ He replied, ‘It turns red or yellow and becomes edible.’”

This fruit is fresh dates. Likewise, Sahīh Muslim contains a report from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not buy fruit until its soundness becomes apparent, and do not buy dates in exchange for dates.”

The second mention of dates undoubtedly refers to fresh dates. The first therefore does too, since the wording is the same.

Sahīh Muslim also records that he said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not buy

---

## Page 14340

fruit until its soundness becomes apparent and the danger of blight has passed.” He also said:

“Its soundness becomes apparent when it turns red or yellow.”

These, then, are the hadiths containing the word “fruit.” The others explicitly mention date palms, such as the hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās recorded in both Sahīhs:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling date palms until one eats of their fruit—or, ‘until their fruit is eaten.’”

In a narration recorded by Muslim from Ibn ‘Umar:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling date palms until their fruit begins to ripen, and ears of grain until they turn white and are safe from blight. He forbade both the seller and the buyer.”

By agreement, “date palms” here means their fruit, because he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted buying pollinated date palms with the buyer stipulating that their fruit be included. Thus, the wording of these texts does not encompass every fruit on earth. Rather, their wording encompasses everything familiar to those addressed, while their meaning encompasses everything analogous to it. The cases we have described as not forbidden are neither expressly prohibited nor analogous to what is prohibited. The evidence establishing prohibition therefore does not encompass them, and they remain permissible.

This alone is evidence that they are not forbidden. It completes the point we made at the outset: the revealed evidence negating prohibition, together with the evidence based on the presumption that an established ruling continues, establishes this conclusion—but only once the absence of any evidence displacing and changing that ruling has been established. We have demonstrated its absence.

The second approach:

We say: Even if we concede the general scope of the wording, it is nevertheless not

---

## Page 14341

intended; rather, its scope is restricted by the evidence we have mentioned, which qualifies general statements of this kind. Indeed, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus exclude fruit sold together with its trees from this general prohibition, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever buys date palms that have not been pollinated, their fruit belongs to the seller unless the buyer stipulates otherwise.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded this from Ibn ‘Umar. Thus, he assigned the fruit to the buyer if he stipulated its inclusion after pollination. Yet it is well known that its soundness has not become apparent at that point and that selling it separately is impermissible.

According to the majority of jurists across the various schools, when a general statement has been qualified by a revealed text or scholarly consensus, analogous cases may also be excluded from its scope. Its scope may likewise be restricted by consensus and strong analogy. We have already cited reports from the early generations and considerations that would restrict such a statement, were it general in scope.

Or through hardening without a change of color, as with walnuts and almonds. The ways in which the soundness of fruit becomes apparent vary: sometimes through becoming moist after being dry, sometimes through drying after being moist, sometimes through softening, and sometimes through changing color to red, yellow, or white. Sometimes its color does not change.

Since he prohibited selling fruit until it turns red or yellow, it is clear that this wording does not encompass every kind of fruit. It encompasses only those kinds that turn red or yellow. Indeed, the wording has also been expressly qualified as referring to date palms.

Reflect, then, on what we have explained concerning this issue, for it is immensely beneficial in this matter, which affects people so widely, and in similar cases. Consider the general scope of the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and His Messenger, both in wording and meaning, so that you give it its due. And the best evidence you can adduce

---

## Page 14342

for its meaning is the reports from the Companions, who best understood the intended purposes. Understanding this precisely ensures that the principles of the Sharia are harmonious and accord with the established principles mentioned in the words of Allah Most High:

“He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from doing what is wrong, makes good things lawful for them and bad things unlawful, and relieves them of their burden and the shackles that were upon them.”

As for his prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, of mu‘āwamah—explained in another narration as selling the produce of successive years—it is, and Allah knows best, like his prohibition of the sale of habal al-habalah. He only prohibited the buyer from purchasing the fruit that the owner of the tree would produce. As for leasing the land and the tree in order to obtain their yield, this does not fall under sale in its ordinary sense; rather, it is a form of leasing.

A parallel is found in the previously cited authentic hadith of Jābir: “He prohibited leasing land,” “He prohibited mukhābarah (sharecropping),” and “He prohibited muzāra‘ah (sharecropping),” and he said, “Do not lease land.” What is meant is the leasing they customarily practised, as explained in other narrations: the forms of sharecropping they customarily practised. Thus, he prohibited their customary forms of leasing and multi-year produce sales, which amounted to selling fruit before its soundness became apparent and to sharecropping in which a particular portion was stipulated. This prohibits practices whose harm outweighs their benefit. This prohibits uncertainty in the category of

---

## Page 14343

sales, whereas that prohibits uncertainty in the broader category of leasing, which includes orchard and crop sharecropping. In both cases, it has been explained that these forms of sale and leasing led to disputes and animosity.

This is what Allah mentions when explaining the wisdom behind prohibiting gambling. He, Most High, says:

“Satan only seeks to stir up enmity and hatred among you through wine and gambling.”

Section

Among the matters that some scholars have included under prohibited uncertainty are various forms of leasing and partnership, such as orchard and crop sharecropping and similar arrangements. Some jurists have held that orchard and crop sharecropping are unlawful and invalid, on the grounds that they are forms of hiring, since they involve work in return for compensation. In a hiring contract, the remuneration must be known, just as the price must be known in a sale. They also rely on Ahmad’s report from Abū Sa‘īd that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “prohibited hiring a worker before specifying his remuneration to him, artificial bidding, sales concluded by touch, and sales concluded by casting a stone.”

The compensation in orchard and crop sharecropping is unknown: the crop or fruit may be little or abundant, it may be of inferior quality, or it may not grow at all. If Allah withholds the yield, the worker’s labour will have been taken without compensation. This is the view of Abū

---

## Page 14344

Hanīfah. He takes the strictest position on prohibiting this. As for Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī, analogical reasoning leads them to Abū Hanīfah’s position, since they classify these arrangements as involving prohibited uncertainty. However, they permit those for which there is a need.

Thus Mālik, and al-Shāfi‘ī in his earlier position, permitted orchard sharecropping without restriction. Leasing trees is impermissible, since it amounts to selling fruit before it shows signs of ripeness. Yet an owner may be unable to water and tend his trees himself, and so be compelled to resort to orchard sharecropping. Crop sharecropping is different: he can lease the land for a specified rent, which dispenses with the need for crop sharecropping on it as an ancillary arrangement. Nevertheless, they permitted crop sharecropping when it forms an ancillary part of orchard sharecropping. Thus, if there is a small amount of open ground between the trees, crop sharecropping on it is permissible as an adjunct to orchard sharecropping.

Mālik’s position is that the crop grown on that open ground belongs to the worker by virtue of the contract itself. If the two parties stipulate that it is to be shared between them, that is permissible. This applies provided that it does not exceed one third. Al-Shāfi‘ī does not assign it to the worker. Rather, he says that if the trees cannot be watered without watering the open ground, crop sharecropping on it is permissible. The jurists of his school hold two views concerning open ground that is extensive and exceeds the area occupied by the trees.

This applies when the two arrangements are combined in a single contract. If, however, they are concluded separately in two contracts, there are two views:

---

## Page 14345

the first is that it is never permissible, because it is permitted only as an ancillary arrangement and therefore cannot be made the subject of a separate contract. The second is that it is permissible if orchard sharecropping is contracted first, followed by crop sharecropping, since the latter is then needed. If crop sharecropping is contracted first, however, it is impermissible, with no disagreement within the school. This applies when the stipulated share is the same in both arrangements, such as one third or one quarter. If different shares are stipulated, there are two views.

A prohibition on leasing land for a specified rent, even in dirhams or dinars, has been reported from a number of the early scholars—including Tāwūs and al-Hasan—and some later scholars. Harb reported that al-Awzā‘ī was asked, “Is it permissible to rent land?” He replied, “There is disagreement over this. A number of scholars see nothing wrong with renting it for dinars and dirhams, while others disapprove of it.”

This is because it is akin to a sale involving prohibited uncertainty: the tenant undertakes to pay the rent on the strength of the crops he expects to obtain, yet the crops may not grow.
