It is thus comparable to renting trees for their fruit. Tāwūs himself practiced crop sharecropping. Moreover, crop sharecropping involves less uncertainty than leasing, because the two parties either both profit or both bear a loss: the labor of one party and his cattle, and the use of the other party’s land, yield no return. This is closer to justice than for one party to receive a guaranteed return while the other remains exposed to risk, since the purpose of the contract is to obtain crops, not merely the ability to plow, sow, and irrigate the land.

---

## Page 14346

The justification for both groups, despite this analogy, lies in the reports that reached them of the Prophet’s prohibition of crop sharecropping and leasing land, such as the hadiths of Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj and Jābir. Nāfi‘ reported:

“Ibn ‘Umar used to lease his farmland during the Prophet’s lifetime (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), during the rule of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān, and during the early part of Mu‘āwiya’s rule. Then he was told, on the authority of Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj, that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) had forbidden leasing farmland. Ibn ‘Umar went to Rāfi‘, and I accompanied him. He questioned him, and Rāfi‘ replied, ‘The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) forbade leasing farmland.’ Ibn ‘Umar said, ‘I know that we used to lease our farmland in return for what grew along the irrigation channels and some straw.’”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim included this in their Sahīh collections; this is al-Bukhārī’s wording. Muslim’s wording is:

“Until, towards the end of Mu‘āwiya’s caliphate, he learned that Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj was reporting a prohibition concerning it from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). He visited him, and I accompanied him, and questioned him. Rāfi‘ said, ‘The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) used to forbid leasing farmland.’ Ibn ‘Umar thereafter gave up leasing it. Whenever he was subsequently asked about it, he would say, ‘Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj reported that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) forbade it.’”

Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar reported:

“‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar used to lease his land until he learned that Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj al-Ansārī was forbidding the leasing of land. ‘Abd Allāh met him and said, ‘Ibn Khadīj, what do you report from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) concerning the leasing of land?’ Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj said to ‘Abd Allāh, ‘I heard my two paternal uncles

---

## Page 14347

—both of whom had taken part in Badr—tell the members of the household that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) had forbidden leasing land.’ ‘Abd Allāh said, ‘I knew that land was leased during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).’ Then ‘Abd Allāh feared that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) might have issued a ruling on the matter of which he was unaware, so he gave up leasing land.” Muslim narrated this.

Al-Bukhārī also narrated ‘Abd Allāh’s statement at the end of this report, on the authority of Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj, from his paternal uncle Ẓahīr ibn Rāfi‘:

“Ẓahīr said, ‘The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) forbade us something that had been beneficial to us.’ I asked, ‘What was that? Whatever the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) says is true.’ He said, ‘The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) summoned me and asked, “What do you do with your fields?” I replied, “We lease them, Messenger of Allah, in return for a quarter of the crop or for a number of wasqs (measures) of dates and barley.” He said, “Do not do that. Cultivate them yourselves, let others cultivate them, or keep them.”’ Rāfi‘ said, ‘I replied, “We hear and obey.”’”

Both included this in their Sahīh collections.

Abū Hurayra reported that the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Whoever has land should cultivate it or allow his brother to use it freely. If he refuses, let him keep his land.”

Both narrated this. Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh reported:

“They used to cultivate it in return for a third or a quarter of the crop. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘Whoever has land should cultivate it or allow his brother to use it freely. If he does neither, let him keep his land.’”

Both narrated this; the wording is al-Bukhārī’s. Muslim’s wording is:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), we used to take land to cultivate in return for a third or a quarter of the crop, together with what grew along the main irrigation channels. The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) then stood up to address this matter and said:

---

## Page 14348

‘Whoever has land should cultivate it. If he does not cultivate it, he should allow his brother to use it freely. If he does not do that, let him keep it.’” Another narration in the Sahīh adds, “and not lease it out.” Another narration in the Sahīh states, “He forbade leasing land.”

It is also established in both Sahīh collections that Jābir said:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) forbade muhāqala, muzābana, selling future years’ produce, and sharecropping.”

Another narration in both Sahīh collections, transmitted by Zayd ibn Abī Unaysa from ‘Atā’, from Jābir, states:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) forbade muhāqala, muzābana, sharecropping, and purchasing the fruit of date palms before it begins to ripen. Ripening means that it turns red or yellow, or that some of it becomes fit to eat. Muhāqala means selling the standing crop in a field for a specified measure of grain. Muzābana means selling dates on the palms for a number of wasqs of dried dates. Sharecropping means an arrangement for a third or a quarter of the crop, or something similar. Zayd said: I asked ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, ‘Did you hear Jābir report this from the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace)?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

These hadiths may be cited as evidence by those who prohibit leasing land and sharecropping, since they prohibit letting land, a term that encompasses both arrangements. For he said:

“Let him cultivate it or allow his brother to use it freely. If he does neither, let him keep it.”

Thus, he permitted only cultivating it oneself or allowing someone else to use it freely. He did not permit receiving compensation for its use, whether through a lease or through sharecropping. Those who permit sharecropping—but not leasing—say: What is meant by letting land is

---

## Page 14349

leasing, or the invalid form of sharecropping that they used to practise, as distinct from valid sharecropping, the evidence for which will follow. This was the arrangement the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) employed with the people of Khaybar, and which the Rightly Guided Caliphs after him and the rest of the Companions practised.

This is supported by the fact that Ibn ‘Umar, who stopped leasing land after Rāfi‘ related the hadith to him, continued to narrate the hadith concerning the people of Khaybar as one who based his legal rulings upon it. Moreover, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prohibited muhāqala, muzābana, sharecropping, and selling future years’ produce, all of which involve uncertainty. Such uncertainty is more apparent in leasing than in sharecropping, as explained earlier.

Those who permit leasing but not sharecropping cite the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Thābit ibn al-Dahhāk:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) prohibited sharecropping and instructed that land be leased, saying, ‘There is nothing wrong with it.’”

This explicitly prohibits sharecropping and instructs that land be leased. Furthermore, as will be related below, Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj—who narrated the hadith from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace)—reported:

“The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not prohibit them from leasing it for a specified, guaranteed payment. Rather, he prohibited the form of sharecropping they used to practise.”

The view adopted by all the hadith jurists who brought together all its chains of transmission—including Ahmad ibn

---

## Page 14350

Hanbal and all his followers, early and late; Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh; Abū Bakr ibn Abī Shayba; Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd al-Hāshimī; Abū Khaythama Zuhayr ibn Harb; most of the Kufan jurists, such as Sufyān al-Thawrī, Muhammad ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Laylā, and Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad, the two disciples of Abū Hanīfa; al-Bukhārī, the compiler of the Sahīh; Abū Dāwūd; the great majority of later hadith jurists, such as Ibn al-Mundhir, Ibn Khuzayma, al-Khattābī, and others; the Zāhirīs; and most of Abū Hanīfa’s followers—was that sharecropping, leasing, and similar arrangements are permissible. In this they followed the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), the practice of his caliphs and Companions, the way of the early generations, and the practice of the majority of Muslims. They also explained the meanings of the hadiths on this subject that appear to conflict.

Among the evidence is the arrangement that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) made with the people of Khaybar, which his caliphs continued after him until ‘Umar expelled them.

Ibn ‘Umar reported:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) made an agreement with the people of Khaybar in return for half of its yield of fruit or crops.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded this. They also both recorded from Ibn ‘Umar:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) entrusted the land of Khaybar to its people on condition that they work and cultivate it, receiving half of its yield.”

This is al-Bukhārī’s wording. Muslim’s wording is:

“When Khaybar was conquered, the Jews asked the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) to allow them to remain there on condition that they work the land in return for half its yield of fruit and crops. The Messenger of Allah then said

---

## Page 14351

(may Allah bless him and grant him peace), ‘We will let you remain there on those terms for as long as we wish.’ The fruit from half of Khaybar was distributed according to the allotted shares, and the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) would take one fifth.”

In another narration recorded by Muslim, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar reported concerning the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“He entrusted the date palms and land of Khaybar to the Jews of Khaybar on condition that they cultivate them at their own expense, with half their yield going to the Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).”

Ibn ‘Abbās reported:

“The Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) entrusted Khaybar—its date palms and land—to its people in return for half the yield.”

This was recorded by Imam Ahmad and Ibn Mājah. Tāwūs reported:

“Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal leased out land during the time of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān in return for a third or a quarter of the yield. This practice continues to this day.”

This was recorded by Ibn Mājah. Tāwūs lived in Yemen and learned from Mu‘ādh’s companions there, who were prominent men who had lived through both the pre-Islamic and Islamic periods. His words “and ‘Umar and ‘Uthmān” mean: “We used to do likewise during the time of ‘Umar and ‘Uthmān.” He omitted the verb because the circumstances made its meaning clear: his audience knew that Mu‘ādh had left Yemen during the caliphate of Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, arrived in Syria during ‘Umar’s caliphate, and died there during that same caliphate.

Al-Bukhārī said in his Sahīh:

Qays ibn Muslim reported from Abū Ja‘far—meaning al-Bāqir—“There is no emigrant household in Medina that does not cultivate land for a third or a quarter of the yield.”

He said: “‘Alī, Sa‘d ibn Mālik, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, al-Qāsim, ‘Urwa, the families of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Alī, and Ibn Sīrīn all practised sharecropping. ‘Umar made agreements with people on condition that, if ‘Umar supplied the seed from

---

## Page 14352

his own resources, he would receive half, whereas if they supplied the seed, they would receive such-and-such a share.” These reports cited by al-Bukhārī have been transmitted by several compilers of early reports.

If, then, all the Emigrants, the Rightly Guided Caliphs, and the leading Companions and Successors practised sharecropping without anyone objecting, there could be no stronger consensus than this. Indeed, if there is any consensus in the world, this is it—especially since all those who gave the Pledge of Divine Pleasure practised sharecropping during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and afterwards, until ‘Umar expelled the Jews to Taymā’.

Those who held sharecropping and fruit-sharing contracts to be invalid have sought to explain this away through untenable interpretations. For example, some claimed that the Jews were slaves belonging to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) and the Muslims, thus likening this to the payment arrangement between a slave and his master.

Yet it is known through mass-transmitted reports that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) made a treaty with them and did not enslave them. They remained free until ‘Umar expelled them, and he neither sold them nor allowed any Muslim to enslave any of them.

Another claim was: “This was a transaction with unbelievers, so it does not necessarily follow that it is permissible with Muslims.” This, too, is untenable. Khaybar had become part of the territory of Islam, and the Muslims have unanimously agreed that invalid transactions prohibited between Muslims are likewise prohibited, within the territory of Islam, between Muslims and those under covenant. Moreover, we have already mentioned that

---

## Page 14353

the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) established such arrangements between the Emigrants and the Helpers; that Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal made such arrangements with the people of Yemen after their acceptance of Islam, during the Prophet’s lifetime; and that the Companions practised them. Sound analogy also entails their permissibility, alongside the general provisions of the Quran and Sunnah establishing permissibility or removing hardship, and the presumption of continuity. This is so for several reasons.

First: this transaction is a partnership, not an ordinary contract of hire. The resulting yield comes from the productive use of two underlying resources: those belonging to one party, such as his body and cattle, and those belonging to the other, such as his land and trees. Similarly, spoils of war are obtained through the efforts of the combatants and the use of their horses, while property acquired without battle is obtained through the Muslims’ physical contributions—their strength and support.

Hiring is different: its object is the work or benefit itself. If someone is hired to build, sew, plough land, sow it, or harvest, then once he has completed that work, the employer has obtained what he sought through the contract, and the worker is entitled to his wages. Hence, a binding contract of hire requires the work to be precisely specified, just as a similar requirement applies to an item sold.

Here, the benefit contributed by the worker’s body, cattle, and iron tools is comparable to that contributed by the owner’s land and trees. Neither party seeks merely to obtain the benefit supplied by the other. Rather, both seek what results from combining their contributions. If there is a yield, they share it; if there is none, each has lost the benefit of his own resources. Thus they share in gain and loss, like all other

---

## Page 14354

partners in the yield generated by the underlying assets they own. This category of transactions differs from a straightforward contract of hire in its nature, purpose and legal ruling. Whatever element of exchange it contains is of the same kind as the element of exchange found in partnerships.

Transactions based on equity in this world fall into two categories: exchanges and partnerships. Exchanges include sale and hire. Partnerships include co-ownership and contractual partnerships. These encompass the Muslims’ shared entitlement to the funds of the public treasury; people’s common rights to freely available resources, such as the use of mosques, public marketplaces and roads, land reclaimed from wasteland, or ownerless resources that are found; heirs’ shared ownership of an inheritance; beneficiaries’ shared entitlement to bequests and endowments; and merchants’ and craftsmen’s participation in joint-capital or joint-labour partnerships, and similar arrangements. These two categories are where injustice arises, as Allah Most High relates that David, peace be upon him, said:

“Indeed, many partners wrong one another, except those who believe and do righteous deeds—and how few they are!”

Other transactions are acts of generosity, such as loans, lending things for use, gifts and bequests.

Since transactions based on equitable dealing are either exchanges or partnerships, it is certainly clear that orchard-tending and sharecropping arrangements, and others like them, belong to the category of partnerships, not straightforward exchanges. Selling what is uncertain is prohibited in exchange transactions only because it amounts to consuming wealth unjustly. Here, neither party consumes the other’s wealth, for if no

---

## Page 14355

crop grows, the landowner has not taken the benefit of the other party’s labour: he has neither received that benefit nor acquired it under the contract, nor is it the intended object of the arrangement. Rather, the benefit of one party’s labour has been lost, just as the benefit of the other’s land has been lost. The landowner has received nothing, so it cannot be said that he has taken something while the other party has received nothing. This differs from sales and contracts of hire involving uncertainty, in which one party receives something while the other remains exposed to risk, leading to regret on one side and a dispute between them. This consideration does not apply to these partnerships, which rest on complete equity and involve no injustice whatsoever, whether uncertainty is present or not.

Anyone who reflects on this will understand the basis of these principles and recognize that permitting these arrangements accords more closely with the principles of the Sharia, is more readily accepted by reason, and is further removed from anything objectionable than permitting the leasing of land—or indeed many sales and contracts of hire whose permissibility is unanimously agreed upon. These arrangements bring people unalloyed benefit without harm.

The confusion among our fellow jurists who prohibited these arrangements arose, following their understanding of the transmitted reports, from their belief that these were contracts of hire for unspecified work, since they involve work in return for compensation.

Yet not everyone who works to benefit from his work is a hired worker. Examples include two partners working with jointly owned wealth, two partners working in a joint-labour partnership, and combatants sharing in the spoils of war, along with countless similar cases. Certainly, if one party were working in return for a payment guaranteed to him by the other, rather than one generated by his work, this would be a contract of hire.

---

## Page 14356

The second argument:

These arrangements are of the same kind as profit-sharing partnerships. They involve an asset that yields an increase through labour, so it is permissible to work with it in return for a share of that increase, as with dirhams and dinars. All jurists have permitted profit-sharing partnerships, following the reports transmitted from the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, although no Sunnah report specifically concerning them is known from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Ahmad held that profit-sharing partnerships could be established by analogy with orchard-tending and sharecropping partnerships, since the latter are established by explicit texts and can therefore serve as the basis for analogy, notwithstanding the disagreement of some jurists concerning them. Analogy in either direction is valid: anyone for whom the permissibility of one is established can apply its ruling to the other, since they are equivalent.

If it is said:

“The profit in a profit-sharing partnership does not come from the original asset itself; rather, the original asset is disposed of and replaced. Thus, the wealth divided between the parties is generated by the labour itself, unlike fruit and crops, which come from the original asset.”

The reply is:

This is a difference in form with no bearing on the ruling in the Sharia. It is self-evident that the wealth earned results only from the combined benefit of the worker’s physical labour and the capital. Accordingly, the owner receives back the equivalent of his capital, and the two divide the profit, just as the worker retains his own person, which is the counterpart of the dirhams. Attributing the profit to one party’s bodily labour is no more justified than attributing it to the benefit derived from the other’s wealth.

Thus, the profit-sharing partnership you report from Umar actually arose without a contract, when

---

## Page 14357

Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī lent money from the public treasury to Umar’s two sons, who brought it to their father.

Umar demanded all the profit because he regarded this as akin to wrongful appropriation: Abū Mūsā had lent to them but not to other Muslims, although the money belonged to them all. When one partner trades with jointly owned wealth without the other’s permission, he is like someone who wrongfully appropriates his partner’s share. His son Abdullah said to him:

“We bore the liability, so the profit should be ours.”

One of the Companions then advised Umar to treat it as a profit-sharing partnership. These three opinions concerning such a case are found among the jurists—including three opinions within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere: when someone trades with another person’s wealth without his permission, does the profit belong to the owner of the capital, to the worker, or to both? These are the three opinions.

The best of them, and the most consistent with analogy, is that the profit should be shared between them, as Umar ruled, because the increase is generated by both contributing resources. If, in the foundational precedent for profit-sharing partnerships on which they rely, they took account of the partnership we have described, then receiving the equivalent of the dirhams is treated as receiving the original coins themselves. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Muslims after him called a loan a manīhah (a grant of use). Thus, the expression is used:

“A manīhah of silver.”

People also say:

“Lend me your dirhams.”

They treat returning the equivalent of the dirhams as returning the very object lent for use: the borrower has benefited from the dirhams and returned them. They also called a profit-sharing partnership qirād, because, among transactions involving consideration, it is the counterpart of a loan among gratuitous transactions.

It may also be said: If the distinction they mention had any bearing on the ruling, then its support

---

## Page 14358

for permitting sharecropping but not profit-sharing partnerships would be stronger than for the reverse; for an increase produced while both original resources remain intact has a stronger claim to validity than one produced while one of them is consumed.

If it is said, “The crop is an increase arising from the land, not the worker’s body,” one may reply, “Profit is an increase arising from the worker, not the dirhams—or vice versa.” All such claims are false. Rather, the crop is produced through the benefit provided by the land, with its soil, water and air, and through the benefit provided by the worker’s body, the cattle and the iron tools.

Even if a distinction between sharecropping and profit-sharing partnerships were conceded, sharecropping undoubtedly resembles a profit-sharing partnership more closely than a hire contract. In a hire contract, the intended object is the work itself, which must be specified, and the remuneration is either guaranteed as a liability or consists of a specifically identified asset. Here, however, the intended object is solely the increase; the work need not be specified, and the remuneration is neither an identified asset nor a liability owed, but a share of whatever increase is produced. Thus, whenever a particular item is stipulated as remuneration, the contract becomes invalid, just as a profit-sharing partnership becomes invalid if the parties stipulate for either of them a specified profit or a known remuneration owed as a liability.

This is perfectly clear. Since sharecropping resembles a profit-sharing partnership far more closely than a hire contract, since its distinction from a profit-sharing partnership is slight, while the differences between it and a hire contract have no bearing in Islamic law or reason, and since it must be assimilated to one of these two established types of contract, it is more appropriate to assimilate it to the one it more closely resembles. This is too obvious to require lengthy elaboration.

The third argument:

We say: The term ijārah (leasing or hiring) has both a broad and a restricted sense.

---

## Page 14359

It has three levels of meaning.

The first is its application to anyone who provides a benefit in return for compensation. This includes the bridal due, as in Allah’s words:

“For whatever enjoyment you have had from them, give them their due compensation.”

This applies whether the work involved is specified or unspecified, and whether the consideration is specified or unspecified, binding or nonbinding.

The second level is hiring in the sense of a reward contract: the benefit is unspecified, but the compensation is guaranteed. This is a revocable, nonbinding contract, as when someone says, “Whoever returns my slave to me will receive such-and-such.” He might return him from somewhere far away or nearby.

The third is leasing or hiring in its restricted sense: leasing a particular asset or engaging someone to perform work for which he is contractually liable, with the benefit specified. The remuneration is therefore specified, and the contract is binding. This is the form of leasing or hiring that resembles a sale in most of its rulings. When later jurists use the term ijārah without qualification, or speak of the “Chapter on Leasing and Hiring,” this is what they mean.

Thus, concerning orchard-tending partnerships, sharecropping, profit-sharing partnerships and similar arrangements for sharing an increase that is produced: whoever calls them ijārah in its broadest or broad sense is correct. Whoever calls them ijārah in its restricted sense is mistaken. And if they constitute ijārah

---

## Page 14360

in the broad sense, namely a reward contract, then, if the consideration is guaranteed—whether a particular asset or a debt—it must be specified. But if the consideration comes from what the work produces, it may be an undivided share of those proceeds. This is like a commander saying during a military campaign: “Whoever guides us to such-and-such a fortress will receive such-and-such a share of it.” Here, receiving the reward is conditional upon obtaining the property, even though this is purely a reward contract, with no partnership involved. Such an arrangement is therefore all the more permissible in a partnership.

Another line of argument may also be pursued here. It may be said: analogical reasoning from established rulings indicates that, in hiring in its restricted sense, the consideration must not involve uncertainty, by analogy with the sale price. As for hiring in the broad sense, in which the benefit need not be specified, it does not resemble this restricted form, for the reasons already given. It therefore cannot be assimilated to it by analogy, and remains subject to the basic presumption of permissibility.

The issue may thus be stated precisely: anyone who regards these arrangements as ijārah should be asked what he means by ijārah. If he means it in the restricted sense, that characterization is incorrect. If he means it in the broad sense, where is the evidence that it is prohibited unless the consideration is specified? If he adduces an analogy, the distinction should be explained to him—a distinction obvious even to a non-jurist, let alone a jurist. He will find no basis that encompasses this kind of ijārah. Once evidence of prohibition is absent, permissibility is established.

Another approach may also be pursued here: inverse analogy, which consists in

---

## Page 14361

establishing for the new case the opposite of the ruling governing the original case, because the effective cause underlying the original ruling is absent.

Thus, it may be argued that the reason requiring the rent to be specified is absent in sharecropping and similar arrangements. That requirement rests on the fact that an unspecified consideration involves uncertainty, making the transaction akin to a sale involving uncertainty that entails consuming wealth wrongfully, or on other reasons of this kind. These considerations are absent in the new case. If the only ground for prohibition is a particular factor, and that factor is absent, there is no prohibition.

As for the hadiths—the report of Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj and others—they explain and clarify the Prophet’s prohibition, peace and blessings be upon him. He was not prohibiting what he himself practised, or what the Companions practised during his lifetime and afterwards. Rather, what he permitted was different from what he prohibited.

Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj said:

“We cultivated more land than anyone else in Medina. We used to lease land in return for the produce of a designated portion reserved for the landowner.” He said: “Sometimes that portion would be damaged while the rest of the land was spared, and sometimes the rest of the land would be damaged while that portion was spared. So we were forbidden to do this. As for gold and silver, leasing land for them was not practised at that time.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated this. In another version he narrated, Rāfiʿ said:

“We had more farmland than anyone else in Medina. One of us would lease his land, saying: ‘This plot is mine, and that one is yours.’ Sometimes one would yield a crop while the other yielded nothing. So the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade them to do this.”

In another version he narrated:

“Sometimes this plot would yield such-and-such an amount while the other yielded nothing. So we were forbidden to do that, but we were not forbidden to lease land for silver.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Rāfiʿ said:

“We had the most farmland among the people of

---

## Page 14362

the Ansār. We used to lease land on condition that this plot would be ours and that one theirs. Sometimes one would yield a crop while the other yielded nothing. So he forbade us to do that. As for silver, he did not forbid us to lease land for it.”

Muslim also narrated that Hanzalah ibn Qays said:

“I asked Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj about leasing land for gold and silver. He replied: ‘There is nothing wrong with it. During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, people used to lease land in return for the crops growing along the large waterways, at the heads of the irrigation channels, and in certain other portions of the cultivated land. This portion would perish while that one was spared, and this one would perish while that one was spared. People had no other form of rent, and that was why they were forbidden to do this. But there is nothing wrong with a specified and guaranteed payment.’”

Here, then, is Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj—the principal transmitter of this hadith—stating that, during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, their only form of rent was the crop from a designated part of the field. This arrangement is undoubtedly forbidden according to all jurists, who have also forbidden its counterpart in profit-sharing investment partnerships. Thus, if one party stipulated that he would receive the profit from a particular garment, that would not be permissible.

Such uncertainty in partnerships is akin to uncertainty in exchange transactions. The fundamental principle governing these exchanges and reciprocal transactions is fairness between the two sides. If either side involves uncertainty or usury, injustice enters the transaction, so Allah forbids it: He has forbidden injustice for Himself and made it forbidden among His servants.

Thus, if it is impermissible for one party to a sale to take possession of the price while the other remains exposed to risk—and this is why the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade selling fruit before it begins to ripen—

---

## Page 14363

then likewise, when the two partners stipulate that a particular plot is to belong to one of them, they depart from what a partnership requires, for a partnership requires sharing in the yield.

If one partner has exclusive rights to a specified plot, the other has no share in it, and the arrangement involves risk and takes on the nature of gambling, as Rāfiʿ explained when he said:

“Sometimes this plot would yield a crop while that one yielded nothing.”

Thus one partner gains while the other receives nothing. This is the essence of gambling. Rāfiʿ also stated that “during the Prophet’s lifetime, peace and blessings be upon him, this was their only form of rent.” It was prohibited only because of the risk and the element of gambling it involved. The prohibition therefore concerned that customary form of leasing, not arrangements in which the rent is a guaranteed debt owed by the tenant. Allah willing, I shall point to a similar distinction concerning his prohibition of selling fruit before it begins to ripen. Rāfiʿ knew best precisely what the Prophet’s prohibition, peace and blessings be upon him, concerned.

This—and Allah knows best—is what ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar ceased doing. For when Rāfiʿ related the report to him, he said:

“I knew that we used to lease our fields in return for the crops growing along the large waterways and some of the straw.”

He thus made clear that they used to lease land in return for the crops from a designated plot. Ibn ʿUmar had followed this practice because they had done so during the lifetime of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, until the prohibition reached him.

The evidence for this is that Ibn ʿUmar continued regularly to narrate the hadith concerning the arrangement at Khaybar and to issue rulings based upon it. He also issued rulings permitting sharecropping on bare land, as did members of his household, even after Rāfiʿ’s report.

Thus Harb al-Kirmānī related: Ishāq ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us

---

## Page 14364

ibn Rāhawayh, who said: Muʿtamir ibn Sulaymān narrated to us: I heard Kulayb ibn Wā’il say:

“I went to Ibn ʿUmar and said, ‘A man approached me who had land and water, but neither seed nor oxen. I took the land in return for half its yield, sowed my own seed, worked it with my own oxen, and divided the yield equally with him—is that acceptable?’

“He replied, ‘That is good.’”

He also related: Hazm’s nephew narrated to us: Yahyā ibn Saʿīd narrated to us: Saʿīd ibn ʿUbayd narrated to us: I heard Sālim ibn ʿAbdullāh. A man came to him and asked:

“One of us approaches another man and says, ‘I shall bring my seed and oxen and cultivate your land. Whatever Allah causes it to yield, you will have such-and-such a share, and I will have such-and-such a share.’ Is that acceptable?”

He replied, “There is nothing wrong with it; we do it ourselves.”

Rāfiʿ’s relatives likewise reported this. In al-Bukhārī, Rāfiʿ said:

“My paternal uncles told me that, during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, they used to lease land in return for the crops growing along the large waterways, or for something the landowner reserved for himself. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade us to do that.”

Rāfiʿ was then asked, “What about leasing it for dinars and dirhams?”

He replied, “There is nothing wrong with leasing it for dinars and dirhams. The arrangement that was prohibited was one that anyone knowledgeable about what is lawful and unlawful would, upon examining it, disallow because of the risk it involved.”

Usayd ibn Zuhayr said:

“When one of us had no need to cultivate his land himself, he would give it to someone to cultivate in return for a third, a quarter, or half of the yield. He would also stipulate that he receive the crops along three irrigation channels, the grain left in the ears after threshing, and the crops irrigated by the watercourse. Livelihoods were difficult in those days. The cultivator would work the land with iron tools and whatever else Allah willed, and derive some benefit from it.

“Then Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj came to us and said, ‘The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him,

---

## Page 14365

forbids you to lease land in this way and says: “Whoever has no need to cultivate his land should let his brother use it freely or leave it uncultivated.”’” Reported by Ahmad and Ibn Mājah. Abū Dāwūd also reported the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him. Ahmad adds:

“He also forbids you to engage in muzābana.”

Muzābana is when a man owns a large grove of date palms and another man comes to him and says, “I will take it for such-and-such a number of wasqs of dried dates.” The threshing remnants are the grain that falls from the ears.

Sa'd ibn Abī Waqqās and Jābir likewise reported this. Sa'd related:

“During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, landowners used to lease their fields in return for the crops growing along the irrigation channels and those around the well that received water. They came to the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, disputing over this. He forbade them to lease land on those terms and said, ‘Lease it for gold and silver.’”

Reported by Ahmad, Abū Dāwūd, and al-Nasā’ī. This explicitly permits leasing land for gold and silver and makes clear that the prohibition concerned only stipulating entitlement to the crops of a particular part of the land.

Jābir, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“We used to practise sharecropping during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, in return for a share of the threshing remnants and of such-and-such. The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Whoever owns land should cultivate it, let his brother use it freely, or leave it uncultivated.’”

Reported by Muslim. Thus, these Companions of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, who transmitted his prohibition also explained the particular arrangement he prohibited and the reason for its prohibition. And if

---

## Page 14366

some versions of the hadith state without qualification that “he forbade the leasing of farmland,” the definite article refers to the form of leasing familiar among them. When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, told them, “Do not lease farmland,” he meant only the form of leasing they knew, as they themselves understood his words—and they knew best what he intended. This is also clarified by the report that “he permitted forms of leasing other than that.” A comparable case is the prohibition of muzābana and similar transactions, which accompanied this prohibition.

Although wording may be unqualified in itself, when addressed to a particular person—for example, in answer to a question or following an account of certain circumstances—it is often qualified by the circumstances of the person addressed. For instance, if a patient tells a physician that he has a fever, and the physician tells him, “Do not eat fatty foods,” it is understood that the prohibition is qualified by that condition.

This is because unqualified wording is understood to refer to an established referent, or to what the circumstances call for, even if the expression is indefinite. Thus, when one of two parties to a sale says, “I sell it to you for ten dirhams,” the wording is unqualified, yet it refers only to the kind of dirhams customarily used.

If, then, the people addressed customarily used the term “leasing” only for the arrangement they practised, and were subsequently addressed using that term, it would refer only to what they knew. This is an instance of restriction of meaning through customary usage. Similarly, if the word “animal” is conventionally understood among them to mean a horse or a hoofed animal, and someone says, “Do not bring me an animal,” that unqualified expression would refer only to that meaning. The prohibition issued by the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, was qualified

---

## Page 14367

by customary usage and the question asked. We have already cited the reports in the two Sahīh collections from Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj and from Zuhayr ibn Rāfiʿ, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, summoned me and asked, ‘What do you do with your fields?’ I replied, ‘We lease them in return for the produce along the irrigation channel and measures of dates and barley.’ He said, ‘Do not do that. Cultivate them yourselves, let others cultivate them, or retain them.’”

This explicitly states that the prohibition concerned what they had been doing. Sharecropping as such, however, was not covered by the prohibition. Nor did Rāfiʿ and the others mention it among the permissible forms of leasing, because—and Allah knows best—they regarded it as a category distinct from customary leasing. Leasing denotes an arrangement requiring a specified rental payment, whether a particular asset or a debt. If the payment is a guaranteed debt for which the lessee is personally liable, it is permissible. The same applies if it is a particular asset other than the crop. But if it is a particular portion of the crop, it is not permissible.

As for sharecropping in return for an undivided share of the entire crop, this is not leasing in the ordinary sense; rather, it is purely a partnership. There is no greater reason to regard the cultivator as leasing the land for a share of the crop than to regard the landowner as hiring the cultivator for the remaining share. Although some people also call this leasing, it is leasing only in the broad sense explained earlier—not in the specific sense in which Rāfiʿ and the others used the term. For this reason, Rāfiʿ explained one of the two permissible forms of leasing and also explained the other form, which they had been forbidden to practise. He did not address partnership, because it belongs to a different category.

---

## Page 14368

One objection remains: the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him,

“Whoever has land should cultivate it or let his brother cultivate it free of charge; otherwise, he should retain it,”

command a person to retain his land if he neither cultivates it nor grants its use free of charge. This would entail prohibiting both leasing and sharecropping, as discussed above.

The answer is that this command conveys encouragement and recommendation, not obligation. Alternatively, it was initially obligatory, to deter them from the invalid leasing arrangements to which they had become accustomed.

This resembles what happened when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade them to eat the meat of domestic donkeys. Concerning the vessels in which they were cooking it, he said:

“Pour out their contents and break them.”

Likewise, when Abū Thaʿlabah al-Khushanī asked him about the utensils of the People of the Book, he said, peace and blessings be upon him:

“If you find others, do not eat from them; but if you find no others, wash them with water.”

This is because, when people become accustomed to a sin, they may not break free of it completely unless they also abandon permissible things closely associated with it. As it has been said:

“A servant does not attain true piety until he places a barrier of lawful things between himself and what is unlawful.”

At other times, however, a person can abandon a sin only gradually, not all at once. Sometimes one approach is needed, and sometimes the other.

Thus, in the Prophet’s Sunnah, we find that someone who might be driven away from obedience is granted concessions in certain matters that enable him to dispense with what is forbidden. By contrast, someone whose faith and patience can be relied upon is directed to refrain from certain things that it is recommended for him to abandon, so that he may pursue what is better more fully. For this reason, it is recommended for one who is confident in his faith

---

## Page 14369

and patience to undertake certain voluntary acts of bodily and financial devotion—such as giving away all his wealth, as Abū Bakr al-Siddīq did—which are not recommended for someone whose circumstances are otherwise. Such was the case of the man who brought the Prophet an egg-sized piece of gold. He threw it at him; had it struck him, it would have hurt him. Then he said:

“One of you goes and gives away his wealth, then sits dependent on others.”

This is supported by the authentic report in Muslim mentioned earlier, from Thābit ibn al-Dahhāk:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade sharecropping and instructed them to lease the land, saying, ‘There is nothing wrong with it.’”

Likewise, we have mentioned the report of Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās:

“He forbade them to lease land in return for the crops grown on a specified part of it, and said, ‘Lease it for gold and silver.’”

This is also how the Companions understood it. Rāfiʿ ibn Khadīj narrated this and stated that there was nothing wrong with leasing land for gold and silver. The same understanding was held by the jurists among the Companions, such as Zayd ibn Thābit and Ibn ʿAbbās. In the two Sahīh collections, ʿAmr ibn Dīnār said:

“I said to Tāwūs, ‘Why do you not give up sharecropping? They claim that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade it.’

“He replied, ‘O ʿAmr, I give to them and help them. Indeed, the most knowledgeable among them told me’—meaning Ibn ʿAbbās—‘that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not forbid it. Rather, he said, “For one of you to grant his brother the use of his land free of charge is better for him than taking a specified rent for it.”’”

It is also reported from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, did not prohibit sharecropping;

---

## Page 14370

but instructed them to treat one another kindly.” Muslim reported this in summary, as did al-Tirmidhī, who said: “A good and authentic hadith.”

Tāwūs reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, merely called them to what was better: granting the use of their land free of charge. Tāwūs said: “I help them and give to them.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, enjoined kindness. Some forms of it are obligatory, such as refraining from usury and transactions involving uncertainty; others are recommended, such as lending property for use and making loans.

Since granting the use of land without rent is an act of kindness, a Muslim has the greater claim to it. Thus he said:

“For one of you to grant his brother the use of his land free of charge is better for him than taking a specified rent for it.”

He also said:

“Whoever has land should cultivate it, grant his brother its use free of charge, or keep it.”

Thus, it was one’s brother who was to be granted its use. Since the People of the Book were not among these brethren, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, entered into cultivation agreements with them rather than granting them its use free of charge. This is especially so because charitable giving should come only from surplus wealth. If someone needs the benefit of his land, it is not recommended that he grant its use free of charge. The Muslims needed the benefit of the land of Khaybar; likewise, in the early days of Islam, the Ansār (Helpers) needed their land when they entered into cultivation agreements with the Muhājirūn (Emigrants).

Islamic law may make charitable giving obligatory in times of need. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade them to store the meat of sacrificial animals because of the group of needy people who had arrived, so that they would feed the hungry, since feeding them was obligatory. Accordingly, when the Muslims needed the benefit of the land while its owners had no need of it, he forbade them to take compensation for it

---

## Page 14371

so that they would generously grant its use free of charge. He did not directly command them to do so, just as he had forbidden them to store meat. For someone who is forbidden to benefit from his property will readily offer it to others, since he will not leave it idle. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him—and indeed rulers as well—may prohibit certain otherwise permissible acts in particular circumstances for the benefit of those subject to the prohibition, as he forbade them during certain military expeditions … (1)

As for Jābir’s report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade mukhābara (sharecropping), it was this particular form of sharecropping that he forbade. The definite article refers to that known practice, and they knew no other form of mukhābara. This is clarified by the report in the Sahīh from Ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“We saw nothing wrong with sharecropping until last year, when Rāfi‘ stated that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had forbidden it, so we abandoned it because of his report.”

Thus Ibn ‘Umar related that Rāfi‘ had reported the prohibition of sharecropping. The meaning of Rāfi‘’s hadith has already been explained.

Abū ‘Ubayd said: “Al-khibr—with an i vowel after the kh—means mukhābara. Mukhābara is sharecropping in return for one-half, one-third, one-quarter, or a smaller or larger share.” Abū ‘Ubayd used to say: “This is why a cultivator is called a khabīr: he enters into a mukhābara agreement over the land. Mukhābara is mu’ākara (contracting for cultivation).”

Some have said that this term derives from Khaybar, because the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, left its land in their possession in return for half the produce. Hence the expression khābarahum, meaning that he entered into an agreement with them at Khaybar. This explanation has no substance, for he never prohibited the arrangement he made at Khaybar.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Both source copies have a blank space sufficient for two or three words.

---

## Page 14372

rather, the Companions practised it during his lifetime and after his death. The hadith concerning mukhābara was narrated only by Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj and Jābir, and both explained what people used to do.

A khabīr is a farmer, so called because he tills the land. Some jurists have distinguished between the two terms for sharecropping, mukhābara and muzāra‘a. They define “mukhābara” as an arrangement in which the cultivator supplies the seed, and “muzāra‘a” as one in which the landowner supplies it. They say that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited mukhābara, not muzāra‘a.

This view, too, is weak. We have already cited the authentic report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “forbade muzāra‘a,” just as he “forbade mukhābara” and “forbade leasing land.” In their basic linguistic meanings, these expressions encompass both the practices he prohibited and those he did not. Their application was restricted to what people actually practised because customary usage, in both speech and practice, narrowed their scope, and because of the verbal contextual indicators: the definite article referring to a known practice and the question posed by the questioner. Otherwise, the lexicographers have stated that mukhābara means muzāra‘a, and the derivation of the word supports this.

Section

Among those who permit sharecropping are some who stipulate that the landowner must supply the seed. They say: This is muzāra‘a. But if the cultivator supplies the seed, it is not

---

## Page 14373

permissible. This is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, adopted by a group of his followers and by followers of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī in those cases where they permit sharecropping.

Their argument rests on an analogy with mudāraba (a capital-and-labour partnership), an argument Ahmad also invoked. Al-Kirmānī said:

“Abū ‘Abd Allāh Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Hanbal was asked about a man who entrusted his land to a cultivator in return for a third or a quarter of the yield. He replied: ‘There is nothing wrong with that, provided the landowner supplies the seed and the cultivator supplies the cattle, iron implements and labour.’ He treated this as analogous to mudāraba.”

The reasoning is that seed is the source of the crop, just as capital is the source of profit. The seed must therefore come from the owner of the underlying property, so that one party supplies the labour and the other supplies the productive asset.

The second position reported from him does not stipulate this; rather, the cultivator may supply the seed. A great many of his followers—more than twenty—have transmitted from him that a man may lease his land in return for a third or a quarter of its yield, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, dealt with the people of Khaybar.

A group of his followers, including al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, therefore said: If someone entrusts his land to a person who cultivates it with his own seed, with a share of the crop going to the landowner, this is permissible if arranged as a lease, but not if arranged as sharecropping. They regarded this distinction as a means of upholding

---

## Page 14374

his explicit rulings; for they found throughout his statements numerous clear declarations permitting land to be leased in return for a share of its yield. They also understood his apparent position to be that, in sharecropping, the seed may not be supplied by the landowner, by analogy with mudāraba. They therefore distinguished sharecropping and mudāraba from leasing.

Others, including Abū al-Khattāb, said: “His statement, as transmitted by a number of his followers, ‘It is permissible to lease land in return for part of its yield,’ refers to sharecropping, with the labour supplied by the cultivator.”

Abū al-Khattāb and his followers said: “According to this report, if the cultivator supplies the seed, he is leasing the land in return for part of its yield. If the landowner supplies it, he is hiring the cultivator in return for the share stipulated for him.”

He said: “Accordingly, the seed owner is entitled to what he receives by virtue of supplying the seed, while whatever is received as remuneration is received by virtue of the stipulation.”

Their explanation—that his explicit ruling on leasing for a share of the yield refers to sharecropping in which the cultivator supplies the seed—is correct. Sound legal reasoning admits only this interpretation, or the conclusion that his explicit permission for the aforementioned lease entails, with even greater force, the permissibility of sharecropping. Permitting this transaction without qualification is the correct position; neither the transmitted evidence nor legal reasoning supports any other conclusion. This is the apparent purport of Ahmad’s widely transmitted statements, and the position adopted by a group of his followers. The first position—the view of those who require the landowner to supply the seed, and the view of

---

## Page 14375

those who distinguish between a lease and sharecropping—is as weak as, or weaker than, the view that equates a lease in the strict sense with sharecropping.

As for the explanation of Ahmad’s explicit ruling: he permitted leasing land in return for a share of the crop on the strength of the Prophet’s dealings with the people of Khaybar, peace and blessings be upon him. That arrangement was sharecropping; it was not expressed in the language of a lease. It is inconceivable that Ahmad would permit what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did only when it was called a lease, yet prohibit it when described by its familiar name. Moreover, it is established in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, stipulated that the people of Khaybar cultivate the land using their own resources.”

As mentioned earlier, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not supply them with seed. If, under the arrangement he made, they supplied the seed from their own resources, how could Ahmad cite this as evidence for sharecropping, then extend it by analogy to an arrangement expressed as a lease, only to prohibit the very precedent he cited—sharecropping in which the cultivator supplied the seed? Furthermore, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to the Jews:

“We shall allow you to remain there for as long as Allah allows you to remain.”

He stipulated no fixed term that would warrant calling it a binding lease.

However, when Ahmad stated—in one of the two reports from him—that the landowner must supply the seed, he was following those who required this by analogy with mudāraba. When a scholar issues a ruling on the strength of an argument against which there is a stronger counterargument, without having that counterargument in mind at the time

---

## Page 14376

and subsequently rules that leasing land in return for one-third of the crop is permissible, citing the sharecropping arrangement at Khaybar, he must have understood that the cultivators at Khaybar supplied the seed; otherwise, his argument would not be valid.

If we suppose that Ahmad distinguished between leasing land in return for a share of its produce and sharecropping in which the cultivator supplies the seed—as some of his followers did—this distinction has no basis in Islamic law. Ahmad does not hold that the rulings governing contracts change merely because their wording differs. Some of his followers, however, do hold this view: they permit this transaction when expressed as a lease but prohibit it when expressed as sharecropping. Likewise, they permit the sale of goods owed by the seller for immediate delivery when expressed as a sale, but prohibit it when expressed as salam (an advance-payment sale), because it then becomes a salam sale with immediate delivery.

Ahmad’s explicit rulings and underlying principles preclude this, as we have already shown when discussing his position on contractual formulas. In all legal acts effected through words, what counts is their meaning, not what is read into their wording. His answers concerning oaths, vows, bequests, and other legal acts bear this out. Even if he himself distinguished between the two arrangements, as some of his followers did, this distinction would constitute a less well-supported report from him, like the report prohibiting both arrangements.

The evidence that this is permissible comes from the Sunnah, scholarly consensus, and analogical reasoning.

As for the Sunnah, there is the previously mentioned arrangement that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, made with the people of Khaybar: they were to cultivate the land using their own resources, and he supplied them with no seed. Likewise, the Ansār made an arrangement with the Emigrants on the condition that they supply the seed. Harb al-Kirmānī said:

---

## Page 14377

Muhammad ibn Nasr narrated to us: Hassān ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us, from Hammād ibn Salama, from Yahyā, from Ismā‘īl ibn Hakīm, that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb expelled the people of Najrān, Fadak, and Khaybar and appointed Ya‘lā ibn Munya as administrator. Ya‘lā entrusted the grapevines and date palms to cultivators on condition that ‘Umar receive two-thirds of the yield and they receive one-third. He also assigned the bare ground—meaning the bare land—on these terms: if the wheat, cattle, and iron implements were supplied by ‘Umar, he would receive two-thirds and they would receive one-third; if these were supplied by the cultivators, ‘Umar would receive half and they would receive half.

Thus ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, and his administrator Ya‘lā ibn Munya, a Companion of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, put both arrangements into practice during ‘Umar’s caliphate, permitting the seed to be supplied either by the landowner or by the cultivator.

Harb also said:

Abū Ma‘n narrated to us: Mu’ammal narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from al-Hārith ibn Hasīra al-Azdī, from Sakhr ibn al-Walīd, from ‘Amr ibn Sulay‘ ibn Muhārib, who said:

“A man came to ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib and said, ‘So-and-so has taken some land and worked it, doing such-and-such.’ ‘Alī summoned him and asked, ‘What is this land you have taken?’

“He replied, ‘It is land I have taken on the understanding that I dredge its canals, improve it, and cultivate it. Whatever Allah causes it to produce, I receive half and its owner receives half.’

“‘Alī said, ‘There is nothing wrong with this.’”

The apparent meaning is that the cultivator supplied the seed, and ‘Alī did not forbid him to do so. It is enough that the man’s question was unqualified and that ‘Alī’s answer was likewise unqualified.

As for analogical reasoning, we have already explained that this transaction is a form of partnership, not

---

## Page 14378

a hire contract in the narrower sense. Even if it is classified as hire, it falls within hire in the broader sense, which includes contracts offering a reward for completing a task, as well as racing and archery contests.

On either understanding, the seed may be supplied by both parties. This is because seed in sharecropping is not among the assets returned to their owner, as invested money is in a profit-sharing partnership. Rather, seed is consumed, just as usufructs are consumed; what remains is the land, and the bodies of the ox and the worker. If seed were like invested capital, an equivalent quantity would have to be returned to its supplier before the two parties divided the surplus. But that is not how the arrangement works: they share the entire crop. Thus, the enduring assets contributed by one party or the other are the land, with its water and air, the worker’s body, and the cattle. Most of the cultivation effort and seed are expended, just as usufructs are consumed, and just as portions of water, air, and soil are consumed and transformed into crops.

Allah, glorified is He, creates crops from the grain itself, soil, water, and air, just as He creates an animal from the fluids of its two parents. Indeed, more of the crop is formed from transformed particles of earth than from the grain. The grain itself is transformed and does not remain intact; rather, Allah splits it open and transforms it, just as He transforms particles of water and air, and just as He transforms semen and His other creations among animals, minerals, and plants.

Yet many jurists came to hold the view that grain and fruit stones are the primary elements in crops and trees, while everything else is merely subsidiary. They even ruled in certain cases that the crops and trees belong to the owner of the fruit stones and grain, despite their small value, while the owner of

---

## Page 14379

the land receives rent for it. Yet the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, ruled to the contrary when he said:

“Whoever cultivates people’s land without their permission has no share of the crop, but is entitled to reimbursement of his expenses.”

Ahmad and other jurists versed in hadith adopted this hadith. Some who adopted it considered it contrary to analogical reasoning and an instance of juristic preference. This was because the aforementioned analogy had become established in their minds: that the crop follows the seed, and the tree follows the fruit stone. Yet what the Sunnah teaches is itself the sound analogy to which natural human disposition points. Casting grain into the earth is precisely like depositing semen in the womb. Thus Allah called women a field of cultivation when He, Most High, said:

“Your wives are a field of cultivation for you.”

Likewise, He called cultivated land a field of cultivation. In determining ownership of offspring, it is the maternal side that takes precedence. Thus a human child follows the mother, rather than the father, in freedom or slavery, while an animal’s unborn offspring belongs to the mother’s owner, not the owner of the sire, for whose breeding services charging a fee is forbidden. This is because the elements the offspring derives from its mother are many times greater than those it derives from its father. The father’s claim is limited to his role in initiating the process, though there is no doubt that the offspring is created from both parents together.

The same applies to grain and fruit stones: most of the elements from which Allah creates trees and crops come from soil, water, and air. This may affect the land, leaving it depleted through cultivation. But since these elements are continually replenished—for Allah, glorified is He, constantly supplies the land with water, air, and soil, whether formed by the transformation of other matter or drawn from what already exists, and the loss of soil particles does not affect the land at all, either because they are replaced through transformation or because of their abundance—

---

## Page 14380

and thus it becomes apparent that the constituents of the soil are akin to benefits derived from property, unlike the grain and fruit stones cast into it: these are tangible property that is consumed, neither replenished nor replaced. Yet this alone does not mean that the seed constitutes the sole capital contribution. Both the cultivator and his oxen require food and fodder throughout the period of work, and these too are consumed, whereas the landowner has no comparable need. Hence they agree that the seed is not returned to its provider as capital is returned in a profit-sharing partnership. Had they treated it as capital, it would have been returned.

It is therefore clear that this transaction comprises three things: enduring assets, namely the land, the cultivator’s body, the oxen, and the iron implements; benefits exhausted through use; and material constituents that are likewise consumed, namely the seed and some of the material constituents of the soil, the cultivator, and his oxen. These consumable constituents are exactly like the benefits exhausted through use. It is therefore for the two parties to decide which of them will provide these constituents, and they may form a partnership on whatever terms they wish, provided this does not entail any of the forms of contractual uncertainty, usury, or wrongful appropriation of wealth that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited. This is why Ahmad permitted all kinds of partnership resembling orchard-tending and sharecropping arrangements, such as entrusting one’s beast, boat, or other property to someone who works with it, with the earnings shared between them.

Section

What we have mentioned by way of indicating the rationale governing sales involving contractual uncertainty and matters resembling

---

## Page 14381

them brings ease to these areas as a whole. For you find that many who discuss these matters rely either on wording that has reached them, which they suppose to be general or unrestricted, or on some form of analogy based on meaning or resemblance.

May Allah be pleased with Ahmad, who said:

“Anyone discussing jurisprudence should avoid these two bases: ambiguous wording and analogy.”

He also said: “Most people’s errors arise from interpretation and analogy.” Such reliance then leads to positions that cannot possibly be followed.

Matters falling within this category include the sale of debts—whether arising from advance-payment sales or otherwise—various forms of settlement and agency, and other transactions. Were our purpose not to set out comprehensive principles encompassing several areas, we would have mentioned various examples of this.

Section

The Third Principle

Concerning contracts and their stipulations: which are lawful or unlawful, and which are valid or invalid. The questions falling under this principle are exceedingly numerous.

Two positions on this subject can be set out systematically:

The first is that contracts, their stipulations, and similar matters are prohibited by default, except where Islamic law has expressly permitted them. This is the position of the literalists, and many of Abū Hanīfa’s principles rest upon it, as do many of al-Shāfiʿī’s principles and those of

---

## Page 14382

a group of the followers of Mālik and Ahmad. Ahmad sometimes explains a contract’s invalidity by the absence of any transmitted report or analogy supporting it, as he did in one of the two positions reported from him concerning a person’s establishing an endowment for his own benefit.

Likewise, some of his followers explain the invalidity of stipulations by their conflicting with what the contract entails. They say: “Whatever conflicts with what the contract entails is invalid.”

The literalists, for their part, recognize no contract or stipulation as valid unless its permissibility is established by a scriptural text or consensus. Where its permissibility is not established, they declare it invalid and maintain the preceding legal ruling. They apply this consistently, but in many instances arrive at positions that others reject.

As for Abū Hanīfa, his principles entail that he does not recognize contractual stipulations that conflict with what the contract ordinarily entails when unqualified. He recognizes a stipulation concerning the subject matter of a contract only when that contract is capable of rescission. Thus, he held it invalid to stipulate in a sale an option to rescind—and, in his view, delivery of the item sold may not be delayed under any circumstances. For this reason, he prohibited selling property that is under lease. If someone purchases a tree bearing fruit that belongs to the seller, the purchaser may demand that the seller remove it. He permitted a lease commencing at a later date only because, in his view, a lease does not confer ownership until the benefit becomes available—or to stipulate the emancipation of the slave sold, or the use of him, or that the purchaser may leave the fruit on the trees, together with all the other stipulations that others invalidate.

He recognized no stipulation in marriage as valid at all, because, in his view, marriage is not subject to rescission. Thus, according to him, it is not dissolved on account of a defect, insolvency, or the like, nor is it invalidated by stipulations

---

## Page 14383

that are invalid, without exception. Abū Hanīfa recognized the validity of a three-day option to rescind only because of the transmitted report; he regarded this as a case of juristic preference.

Al-Shāfiʿī agrees with him that every stipulation contrary to what a contract entails is invalid, but makes exceptions where specific evidence warrants them. Thus, he does not permit stipulating an option to rescind lasting more than three days, reserving the use of the item sold, or similar arrangements involving delayed delivery. He even prohibited leases commencing at a later date, because taking possession—which the lease entails—does not immediately follow the contract. Nor does he permit stipulations restricting the purchaser’s unrestricted disposal of the property, except a stipulation requiring emancipation, because of the Sunnah evidence and underlying rationale supporting it. He does, however, permit the reservation of a benefit by operation of the Sharia, as in selling property already under lease, according to the correct position in his school, or selling trees whose fruit remains to be harvested under an established right to leave it on them, and similar cases.

He permits some stipulations in marriage but not others. He does not permit a woman to stipulate that she remain in her own home or town, nor stipulations barring her husband from taking another wife or a concubine. He permits stipulating that she be free and Muslim, and likewise, according to the correct position in his school, other desired qualities, such as beauty. He is among those who hold that marriage may be annulled on account of a defect or insolvency, and that it is rendered invalid by stipulations incompatible with it, such as specifying a fixed term or stipulating divorce, and by shighār marriage. An invalid dowry and similar matters, however, do not have this effect.

A group of Ahmad’s followers agree with the substance of these principles of al-Shāfiʿī, but allow more exceptions than he does, such as an option to rescind lasting more than three days, the seller’s reservation of the use of the item sold, and a woman’s stipulating that her husband not relocate her

---

## Page 14384

or take another woman alongside her, and similar stipulations serving their interests.

They therefore say: Every stipulation contrary to what a contract entails is invalid, unless it serves the interests of the contracting parties. Ahmad’s explicit statements indicate that he permitted more contractual stipulations than al-Shāfiʿī did. Thus, they may agree with al-Shāfiʿī on the general principle while allowing more exceptions on the basis of countervailing evidence, just as al-Shāfiʿī may agree with Abū Hanīfa on the general principle while allowing more exceptions on the same basis. These three groups differ from the literalists and allow greater latitude in stipulations because they accept analogical reasoning, underlying rationales, and reports from the Companions, and because they discern meanings in the scriptural texts that the literalists do not.

Their principal evidence is the well-known account of Barīrah, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections, from ʿĀʾisha, may Allah be pleased with her, who said:

“Barīrah came to me and said, ‘I have contracted with my owners to purchase my freedom for nine ounces of silver, payable at one ounce each year, so help me.’

I said, ‘If your owners are willing for me to pay them the sum, with the right of patronage over you belonging to me, I will do so.’ Barīrah went to her owners and told them, but they refused. She returned from them while the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sitting there, and said, ‘I presented that to them, but they refused unless the right of patronage remained theirs.’

ʿĀʾisha informed the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he said, ‘Take her and stipulate that the right of patronage shall be theirs, for the right of patronage belongs only to the one who emancipates.’

---

## Page 14385

So ʿĀʾisha did so. Then the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stood before the people, praised Allah and extolled Him, then said:

‘To proceed: Why do some men stipulate conditions that are not in the Book of Allah? Any condition that is not in the Book of Allah is invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions. Allah’s ruling is more binding, and Allah’s stipulation is firmer. The right of patronage belongs only to the one who emancipates.’”

In a narration recorded by al-Bukhārī:

“‘Buy her and emancipate her, and let them stipulate whatever they wish.’ So she bought her and emancipated her, and her owners stipulated that the right of patronage over her should belong to them. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘The right of patronage belongs to the one who emancipates, even if they stipulate a hundred conditions.’”

Another wording reads:

“Allah’s stipulation is more binding and firmer.”

In the two Sahīh collections, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar reported:

“ʿĀʾisha, the Mother of the Believers, wished to buy a slave woman in order to emancipate her. Her owners said, ‘Shall we sell her to you on condition that the right of patronage over her belongs to us?’ She mentioned this to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said, ‘Do not let that prevent you, for the right of patronage belongs only to the one who emancipates.’”

In Muslim’s collection, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“ʿĀʾisha wished to buy a slave woman and emancipate her, but her owners refused unless the right of patronage belonged to them. She mentioned this to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said, ‘Do not let that prevent you, for the right of patronage belongs only to the one who emancipates.’”

They draw two arguments from this hadith. The first is his statement:

“Any condition that is not in the Book of Allah is invalid.”

Thus, every condition that is neither in the Quran, nor in the hadith, nor in

---

## Page 14386

consensus is not in the Book of Allah. This differs from what is established in the Sunnah or by consensus, for that is in the Book of Allah through its instruction to follow the Sunnah and consensus. Those who accept analogical reasoning—and they are the majority—say: If a condition’s validity is established by analogy whose authority is supported by the Sunnah or by consensus supported by the Book of Allah, then it is in the Book of Allah.

Their second argument is that they draw an analogy between all conditions that conflict with what a contract entails and the stipulation concerning the right of patronage. The underlying reason is that this stipulation conflicts with what the contract entails. Contracts entail their effects by virtue of the revealed law, so altering those effects is considered an alteration of what the revealed law requires, just like altering acts of worship. This is the crux of the principle: contracts have been prescribed in a particular form, so stipulating something contrary to what they entail alters what has been prescribed.

For this reason, Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī—in one of his two opinions—do not permit conditions in acts of worship that conflict with what those acts entail. Thus, they do not permit a pilgrim in the state of consecration to stipulate that he may leave that state on account of an excuse. In this they follow ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, who rejected making such a stipulation during Hajj and would say, “Is the Sunnah of your Prophet not sufficient for you?”

They also supported this principle with the words of Allah Most High:

“Today I have perfected your religion for you.”

And His words:

“And whoever transgresses the limits set by Allah—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

They said: Conditions and contracts that have not been prescribed transgress the limits set by Allah and constitute an addition

---

## Page 14387

to religion.
