As for those conditions which these scholars declared invalid, although the scriptural texts indicate their permissibility, whether in general or specifically, they said: “That has been abrogated.” Some of them said this concerning the conditions the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, agreed upon with the polytheists in the year of al-Hudaybiya. Alternatively, they said: “This is general or unrestricted, and must therefore be restricted to conditions that are in the Book of Allah.”

They also cited a hadith related in an account involving Abū Hanīfa, Ibn Abī Laylā and Sharīk:

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited a sale coupled with a condition.”

A number of authors of works on jurisprudence have mentioned it, but it is not found in any of the hadith collections. Ahmad and other scholars rejected it, stating that it is unknown and that authentic hadiths contradict it. The recognized jurists unanimously agree—with no dissent known to me from others—that stipulating a particular attribute in the item sold or the like is valid: for example, stipulating that a slave be able to write or practise a craft, or specifying the length of a piece of cloth, the area of a plot of land, and similar matters.

The second view is that contracts and conditions are, in principle, permissible and valid. None is forbidden or invalid except what the revealed law establishes as forbidden and invalid, whether through an explicit text or through analogy, according to those who accept it.

Most of Ahmad’s expressly transmitted principles follow this view. Mālik’s position is close to his, but Ahmad is more ready to uphold the validity

---

## Page 14388

of conditions. Indeed, none of the four imams is more ready than he to uphold their validity. Most contracts and contractual conditions that Ahmad considers valid are supported by specific evidence, whether a transmitted report or an analogy. Yet he does not regard the argument advanced by proponents of the first view as an obstacle to their validity, nor does he object that a condition conflicts with what the contract ordinarily entails or that no scriptural text establishes it. Reports concerning contracts and conditions had reached him from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the Companions that are not found with the other imams.

He therefore ruled in accordance with these reports and extended their rulings by analogy to comparable cases. As for the texts on which others relied to invalidate conditions, he may question their authenticity or their force as evidence. Likewise, he may question the analogies on which they relied. Some of his followers also rely on the general statements of the Quran and Sunnah that we shall mention to establish the validity of conditions.

One example is allowing an option period exceeding three days without restriction, whereas Mālik permits it for as long as needed. According to one of the two positions transmitted from Ahmad, he also permits stipulating a right of cancellation in marriage. Ibn Hāmid and others permit it in guarantees and similar contracts.

Ahmad permits a person, in all contracts, to reserve some of the benefit of property passing out of his ownership, and to stipulate something beyond what those contracts ordinarily entail when no special conditions are specified. Thus, where a contract has certain implications in the absence of special conditions, he permits these to be increased or reduced by stipulation, provided this does not contravene the revealed law, as I shall explain, Allah willing.

He therefore permits a seller to reserve some of the benefit of the property sold, such as a slave’s service, residence in a house, and similar benefits, provided that the benefit is one that may lawfully be retained while the property is in the ownership of

---

## Page 14389

another person, following the hadith of Jābir, who sold his camel to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, while reserving the right to ride it to Medina.

He also permits someone who frees a slave to reserve the slave’s service for the slave’s lifetime, the master’s lifetime, or another period. This follows the hadith of Safīnah, whom Umm Salamah freed on condition that he serve the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, for as long as he lived.

According to most of Ahmad’s reported positions, a man may also free his slave woman and make her emancipation her dowry, as in the hadith of Safiyyah, and as Anas ibn Mālik and others did—even without the woman’s consent. It is as though he freed her while reserving the right to sexual relations with her. However, he reserves that right through marriage, since reserving it without marriage is impermissible, unlike reserving her service.

He also permits someone establishing an endowment to reserve all its benefits and revenue for himself throughout his lifetime, as the Companions are reported to have done. A report on this has also been attributed directly to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, by a Successor. As for whether a person may establish an endowment for himself, two positions are transmitted from Ahmad.

By analogy with his position, it is also permissible to reserve some of the benefit of property transferred as a gift, a dowry, compensation for a wife’s release from marriage, or a settlement in lieu of retribution, and in other similar ways of relinquishing ownership. This applies whether ownership is extinguished, as in emancipation, or transferred to another, whether for consideration, as in a sale, or without consideration, as in a gift.

---

## Page 14390

Ahmad also permits, in marriage, conditions generally in which the stipulating party has a legitimate interest, because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, as reported in the two Sahīh collections:

“The conditions most deserving of fulfillment are those by which you have made marital intimacy lawful.”

Those who uphold this hadith maintain that it makes conditions in marriage more binding than those in sales and leases. This contradicts the position of those who recognize conditions as valid in sales but not in marriage. Thus, Ahmad permits a woman to restrict rights that her husband would otherwise have, stipulating that she will not travel with him or move from her home. She may go further by stipulating that she have him exclusively, so that he neither marries another woman nor takes a concubine.

According to an explicitly transmitted position from Ahmad, deemed correct by a group of his followers, either spouse may stipulate that the other possess a desired quality, such as wealth, beauty, or the like, and has the right to annul the marriage if that quality is absent.

He takes one of the broadest positions on the annulment of marriage and its automatic dissolution. He permits its annulment on account of a defect—as when a husband takes another wife despite her having stipulated that he would not—or deception, as when he believed her to be a free woman but she proved to be a slave. According to the correct position, annulment is also permitted when a stipulated quality is absent, as when the husband stipulates that he possesses wealth but the facts prove otherwise.

In his view, marriage is automatically dissolved by invalid conditions incompatible with its purpose, such as setting a time limit or stipulating divorce. Does it also become void because the dowry is invalid, as when it consists of wine, carrion, or the like? Two positions are transmitted from him. One is that it does, as in shighār marriage.

---

## Page 14391

and this is also a position transmitted from Mālik.

The second position is that the marriage is not dissolved, because the dowry is subsidiary, whereas marriage is an independent contract. This accords with the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfi‘ī.

According to most of Ahmad’s explicit statements, he permits the seller to require the buyer to perform or refrain from an action concerning what is sold, where this serves a purpose of the seller or benefits what is sold itself. Most of his later followers, however, permit no such condition except emancipation. This latter position is also reported from him, but the former occurs more frequently in his statements.

In al-Khallāl’s al-Jāmi‘, Abū Tālib relates:

“I asked Ahmad about a man who bought a slave woman on condition that he take her as a concubine: she would be a valuable slave woman whose owners wanted him to take her as a concubine rather than employ her in domestic service.”

He replied: “There is nothing wrong with that.”

Muhannā said:

“I asked Abū ‘Abd Allāh about a man who bought a slave woman from another man, who told him: ‘If you wish to sell her, I have the first right to buy her at the price you paid me for her.’”

He replied:

“There is nothing wrong with that. However, he must neither have intercourse with her nor approach her sexually while the seller retains a stipulated right concerning her, because Ibn Mas‘ūd told a man: ‘You must not approach her sexually while anyone retains a stipulated right concerning her.’”

Hanbal reported: ‘Affān narrated to us: Hammād ibn Salama narrated to us, from Muhammad ibn Ishāq, from al-Zuhrī, from ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Utba, that Ibn Mas‘ūd bought a slave woman from his wife and agreed to her condition that, if he sold her, she would have her for the price he had paid for her. Ibn Mas‘ūd asked ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb about this, and he said:

“Do not have intercourse with her while a stipulation concerning her remains in force.”

Hanbal related that his uncle said:

“Every stipulation concerning sexual access is governed by this ruling. A single condition in a sale is permissible, except that ‘Umar disapproved of Ibn Mas‘ūd

---

## Page 14392

having intercourse with her, because he had agreed to his wife’s stipulation. Thus, ‘Umar disapproved of his having intercourse with her while a stipulation concerning her remained in force.”

Al-Kirmānī related that he asked Ahmad about a man who bought a slave woman and stipulated to her owners that he would neither sell her nor give her away. Ahmad seemed to permit this. However, if they made it a condition of his purchase that, should he sell her, the seller would have the first right to buy her at that price, he must not approach her sexually. Ahmad was following the report of ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb’s words to ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd. He explicitly stated in several places that, if the seller wished to sell her, he was entitled only to return her to the seller for the original price, as in rescission by mutual agreement.

Most of his later followers hold the position that invalidates this condition. They sometimes interpret his statement, “It is permissible,” to mean that the contract is permissible, although his other explicit statements make clear that he also means the “condition.” In this, he followed the account transmitted from ‘Umar, Ibn Mas‘ūd, and Zaynab, the wife of ‘Abd Allāh—three Companions.

The same applies to conditions concerning the property sold: that the buyer neither sell it nor give it away, or that he take the slave woman as a concubine, and similar conditions specifying a single purpose. Thus, ‘Umar ibn Shabba related in Akhbār ‘Uthmān that ‘Uthmān bought a house from Suhayb and stipulated that he would establish it as an endowment for Suhayb and his descendants after him.

The underlying principle is that ownership confers various powers of disposition. Just as there is consensus that part of the property sold may be excluded from the sale, and Ahmad and others permit reserving some of its benefits, he likewise permits excluding certain forms of disposition.

Accordingly, anyone who says, “This condition conflicts with what the contract entails,” is to be asked:

---

## Page 14393

“Does it conflict with what an unqualified contract entails, or with what a contract entails in all circumstances?”

If he means the former, then every condition does so. If he means the latter, his claim is not conceded. What is objectionable is a condition that conflicts with the purpose of the contract, such as stipulating divorce in a marriage contract or rescission in a contract. But stipulating something intended by the contract does not conflict with its purpose.

This is the correct view, supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, consensus, and sound reasoning, together with the presumption of continuity and the absence of contrary evidence.

As for the Quran, Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fulfill your contracts.”

Contracts are covenants. He also says:

“When you speak, be just, even if it concerns a close relative, and fulfill your covenant with Allah.”

And He says:

“Fulfill the covenant, for you will surely be questioned about the covenant.”

And He says:

“They had already made a covenant with Allah that they would not turn their backs in flight. And a covenant with Allah must be answered for.”

Thus, He—glorified be He—has commanded the fulfillment of contracts, and this command is general. Likewise, He has commanded the fulfillment of the covenant with Allah and of covenants generally. This includes commitments a person undertakes, as shown by His words:

“They had already made a covenant with Allah.”

This indicates that a covenant with Allah includes commitments a person undertakes, even if Allah had not commanded that particular undertaking before the covenant was made—as with a vow or a sale. Rather, He commanded that it be fulfilled. This is why He coupled it with truthfulness in His words:

“When you speak, be just, even if it concerns a close relative, and fulfill your covenant with Allah.”

For justice in speech concerns statements about the past and present, whereas fulfilling a covenant concerns speech relating to the future, just as

---

## Page 14394

He, Most High, says:

“Among them are those who pledged to Allah, ‘If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give in charity and be among the righteous.’”

“But when He gave them of His bounty, they became stingy with it and turned away in aversion.”

“So He caused hypocrisy to persist in their hearts until the Day they meet Him, because they broke their promise to Allah and because they used to lie.”

He—glorified be He—also says:

“And fear Allah, in whose name you make demands of one another, and be mindful of the ties of kinship.”

Quran commentators, such as al-Dahhāk and others, explained “you make demands of one another” as meaning: “You enter into covenants and contracts with one another.” This is because each contracting party asks the other to fulfill what the contract requires, whether an action, an abstention, a payment, a benefit, or the like. In this verse and throughout the rest of the surah, He—glorified be He—has brought together the rulings governing the bonds between human beings: innate bonds, such as kinship, and acquired bonds, such as contracts, which include ties through marriage, guardianship over an orphan’s property, and the like.

He—glorified be He—also says:

“Fulfill the covenant of Allah when you make a covenant, and do not break oaths after confirming them, having made Allah your guarantor. Surely Allah knows what you do.”

“And do not be like the woman who unraveled her yarn after spinning it firmly, using your oaths as a means of deception among yourselves.”

Aymān (oaths) is the plural of yamīn (oath), and every contract is an oath. It is said that it was given this name because people used to conclude it by shaking right hands. This is indicated by His words:

“Except those polytheists with whom you have made a treaty and who have not failed you in anything or supported anyone against you. Fulfill your treaty with them until its appointed term. Surely Allah loves the righteous.”

“Then, when the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you find them

---

## Page 14395

and capture them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them at every place of ambush. But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakah, then let them go their way. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“If any of the polytheists seeks your protection, grant him protection so that he may hear the words of Allah, then escort him to a place of safety. That is because they are people who do not know.”

“How can the polytheists have a treaty with Allah and His Messenger, except those with whom you made a treaty at the Sacred Mosque? So long as they remain true to you, remain true to them. Surely Allah loves the righteous.”

“How could they, when, if they gain the upper hand over you, they respect neither ties of kinship nor covenant with you?”

Here, ill means kinship, and dhimmah means a covenant. These are the two bonds mentioned in His words:

“In whose name you make demands of one another, and be mindful of the ties of kinship.”

The passage continues to His words:

“They respect neither ties of kinship nor covenant with a believer.”

Thus Allah condemned them for severing ties of kinship and breaking covenants. Then come His words:

“But if they break their oaths after making their covenant.”

This was revealed concerning the disbelievers of Mecca, with whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded a peace treaty in the year of al-Hudaybiyah. They subsequently broke the treaty by assisting Banū Bakr against Khuzāʿah.

As for His words—glorified be He:

“A declaration of release from treaty obligations, from Allah and His Messenger, to those polytheists with whom you made treaties.”

Those treaties were revocable, not binding, since they were open-ended. He had the choice of maintaining or terminating them, as with an agency agreement and similar arrangements.

As for those jurists, whether of our school or others, who say that a truce is valid only if it has a fixed term, their position—besides being contrary to Ahmad’s principles—is refuted by the Quran and by the practice of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with most of those with whom he concluded treaties, for he did not

---

## Page 14396

set a fixed term with them. As for those whose treaties had a fixed term, he was not permitted to break them, as indicated by His words:

“Except those polytheists with whom you made treaties and who have not failed you in anything or supported anyone against you. Honour your treaty with them until its term expires. Surely Allah loves the righteous.”

He also said:

“Except those with whom you made a treaty at the Sacred Mosque. So long as they remain true to you, remain true to them. Surely Allah loves the righteous.”

And He said:

“If you fear treachery from a people, openly renounce your treaty with them, so that both sides are equally informed.”

He thus permitted renouncing a treaty only when signs of treachery became apparent, since the threat came from their side. Allah Most High also said:

“O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do?”

—to the end of the verse.

It is also reported in Sahīh Muslim from Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī:

“Among the Quranic passages whose recitation was abrogated was a surah like Barā’ah: ‘O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do? A testimony will then be inscribed upon your necks, and you will be questioned about it on the Day of Resurrection.’”

Allah Most High said:

“And those who honour their trusts and their covenants.”

This occurs in both Surah al-Mu’minūn and Surah al-Ma‘ārij. It is among the qualities of those exempted from the blameworthy impatience described in His words:

“Indeed, man was created impatient:
Distressed when touched by adversity,
And withholding when touched by good—
Except those who pray,
Who are constant in their prayers;
And those in whose wealth there is a known share
For the beggar and the deprived;
And those who believe in the Day of Judgment;
And those who are fearful of their Lord’s punishment—
Indeed, none can feel secure from their Lord’s punishment;
And those who guard their chastity,
Except with their spouses or those whom their right hands possess, for then they are free of blame.”

---

## Page 14397

“But whoever seeks beyond that—it is they who are the transgressors.
And those who honour their trusts and their covenants.”

This entails that doing so is obligatory, because only those possessing all these qualities are exempted from blame. Hence, only obligatory duties are mentioned here. Likewise, near the beginning of Surah al-Mu’minūn, He says:

“It is they who are the inheritors,
Who will inherit Firdaws (Paradise), where they will remain forever.”

Anyone who does not possess these qualities is not among the inheritors, for the apparent meaning of the verse is exclusive: inserting a separating pronoun between the subject and predicate indicates exclusivity. Anyone who is not among the inheritors of Paradise is liable to punishment, unless Allah pardons him.

Since observing a covenant is obligatory, observing it means fulfilling it. Just as Allah joined covenants and trusts together, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, described their opposites as characteristics of a hypocrite, saying:

“When he speaks, he lies; when he makes a promise, he breaks it; when he makes a covenant, he betrays it; and when he disputes, he behaves wickedly.”

It is also reported from him:

“A believer may be disposed to any trait except treachery and lying.”

They have always enjoined truthfulness in speech and the faithful discharge of trusts. This is general in scope.

Allah Most High said:

“Yet He leads none astray through it except the defiantly disobedient,
Who break Allah’s covenant after it has been confirmed and sever what Allah has commanded to be joined.”

He thus condemned them for breaking Allah’s covenant and severing what He commanded to be joined, because an obligation arises either from revealed law or from a condition a person has voluntarily agreed to.

He also said:

“Those who fulfil Allah’s covenant and do not break their solemn pledge,
And those who join

---

## Page 14398

what Allah has commanded to be joined, fear their Lord, and dread an evil reckoning;
And those who remain patient, seeking their Lord’s Face, establish prayer, spend secretly and openly from what We have provided them, and repel evil with good—it is they who will have the final abode:
Gardens of everlasting residence, which they will enter along with the righteous among their parents, spouses, and descendants. The angels will enter upon them from every gate,
‘Peace be upon you for your patience. How excellent is the final abode!’
But those who break Allah’s covenant after it has been confirmed, sever what Allah has commanded to be joined, and spread corruption on earth—it is they who will be cursed and will have an evil abode.”

He also said:

“Is it that whenever they make a covenant, a group of them casts it aside? Rather, most of them do not believe.”

And He said:

“But righteousness is to believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Scripture, and the prophets; to give wealth, despite loving it, to relatives, orphans, the needy, stranded travellers, those who ask, and to free slaves; to establish prayer and give zakah; to fulfil covenants when made; and to remain patient in poverty, hardship, and the heat of battle. Those are the ones who have been true, and it is they who are mindful of Allah.”

Allah Most High said:

“Among the People of the Scripture are those who, if you entrust them with a great sum, will return it to you. Yet among them are those who, if you entrust them with a single dinar, will not return it unless you persistently stand over them. That is because they say, ‘We bear no responsibility towards the unlettered.’ They knowingly tell lies about Allah.
On the contrary, whoever fulfils his covenant and fears Allah—then Allah indeed loves those who fear Him.”

And He said:

“Indeed, those who exchange Allah’s covenant and their oaths for a small price will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them nor look at them on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them. They will have a painful punishment.”

And He said

---

## Page 14399

—exalted is He:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn. Guard your oaths. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be grateful.”

There are many hadiths on this subject. Among them is the report in the two Sahīhs from ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever possesses four traits is a pure hypocrite, and whoever possesses one of them has a trait of hypocrisy until he abandons it: when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks his promise; when he makes a covenant, he betrays it; and when he disputes, he behaves wickedly.”

Also in the two Sahīhs, ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A banner will be raised for every treacherous person on the Day of Resurrection.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Sa‘īd reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every treacherous person will have a banner at his backside on the Day of Resurrection.”

Another version states:

“Every treacherous person will have a banner on the Day of Resurrection by which he will be recognized, in proportion to his treachery. Indeed, no treacherous person commits greater treachery than a ruler of the people.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Buraydah ibn al-Husayb said:

“Whenever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed a commander over an army or a detachment, he would enjoin him personally to fear Allah and to treat the Muslims with him well. He would then say: ‘Go forth to battle in Allah’s name and in Allah’s cause. Fight those who disbelieve in Allah. Go forth to battle, but do not misappropriate the spoils, do not act treacherously, do not mutilate, and do not kill a child. When you encounter your enemies among the polytheists, invite them to three options—or alternatives. Whichever they accept, accept it from them and refrain from attacking them—’” (The hadith continues.)

---

## Page 14400

Thus he forbade them to act treacherously, just as he forbade them to misappropriate the spoils. In the two Sahīhs, Ibn ‘Abbās reports from Abū Sufyān ibn Harb that, when Heraclius questioned him about the character of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he asked:

“Does he act treacherously?”

He replied: “No, he does not act treacherously. We are currently observing a truce with him, and we do not know what he will do during it.”

Abū Sufyān said: “This was the only statement into which I could slip something disparaging.”

Heraclius said in his response: “I asked you whether he acts treacherously, and you stated that he does not. Likewise, the messengers do not act treacherously.”

He thus regarded freedom from treachery as an invariable characteristic of the messengers.

In the two Sahīhs, ‘Uqbah ibn ‘Āmir reports that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The conditions most deserving of fulfilment are those by which marital relations become lawful for you.”

This shows that stipulated conditions ought to be fulfilled, and that marriage conditions are more deserving of fulfilment than others.

Al-Bukhārī narrated from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah Most High said: ‘There are three whose adversary I shall be on the Day of Resurrection: a man who gives his word in My name and then betrays it; a man who sells a free person and consumes the proceeds; and a man who hires a worker, receives his work in full, and does not pay him his wages.’”

He thus condemned the treacherous person. Anyone who stipulates a condition and then breaks it has acted treacherously. The Book and the Sunnah therefore command the fulfilment of covenants, conditions, and solemn pledges

---

## Page 14401

and contracts, the discharge of trusts, and due observance of all these obligations; they also prohibit treachery, the breaking of covenants, and betrayal, and sternly condemn those who commit such acts.

On the premise that the basic rule concerning these matters is prohibition and invalidity, except for what the revealed law permits, it would not be permissible for it to command them without qualification and unreservedly condemn those who break them and act treacherously. Likewise, since taking human life is prohibited in principle, except where the revealed law permits or requires it, there can be no unrestricted command to take human lives, interpreted as applying only to permissible cases. This differs from acts that are obligatory as a category, such as prayer and zakāh: these are commanded without qualification, even though they have conditions and impediments. Thus, prayer without ritual purification is prohibited, as is giving charity in a manner that harms oneself, and similar acts. Likewise, truthfulness in speech is commanded, although particular circumstances may sometimes make it forbidden to speak the truth, requiring silence or indirect speech instead.

Since fulfilment and the observance of covenants are commanded as a category, it follows that contracts and conditions are valid in principle. For declaring a contract valid means nothing other than that it produces its intended effects and achieves its purpose. The purpose of a contract is its fulfilment. Thus, the Lawgiver’s command to fulfil the purpose of covenants indicates that validity and permissibility are the basic rule concerning them.

Abū Dāwūd and al-Dāraqutnī narrated through Sulaymān ibn Bilāl, who said: Kathīr ibn Zayd narrated to us, from al-Walīd ibn Rabāh, from Abū Hurayrah, who said:

---

## Page 14402

The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Settlement is permissible among Muslims, except a settlement that makes the unlawful lawful or the lawful unlawful. Muslims are bound by their conditions.”

In one report, Yahyā ibn Maʿīn said of Kathīr ibn Zayd, “He is trustworthy,” while in another he judged him weak.

Al-Tirmidhī and al-Bazzār narrated through Kathīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf al-Muzanī, from his father, from his grandfather, that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Settlement is permissible among Muslims, except a settlement that makes the lawful unlawful or the unlawful lawful. Muslims are bound by their conditions, except a condition that makes the lawful unlawful or the unlawful lawful.”

Al-Tirmidhī said, “This is a sound and authentic hadith.” Ibn Mājah narrated the first part of it. However, the hadith critics judged Kathīr ibn ʿAmr weak. Ahmad struck out his report in the Musnad and did not narrate it. Perhaps al-Tirmidhī graded it authentic because it was transmitted through several routes.

Abū Bakr al-Bazzār also narrated from Muhammad ibn ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn al-Baylamānī, from his father, from Ibn ʿUmar, who said: The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“People are bound by their conditions insofar as those conditions accord with the truth.”

Although each of these chains of transmission is weak individually, their convergence through different routes allows them to strengthen one another. This meaning is supported by the Quran and the Sunnah, and it is the substance of this legal position. For a person laying down a condition has no right to permit what Allah has forbidden, nor to forbid what

---

## Page 14403

Allah has permitted, for his condition would then invalidate Allah’s ruling. Likewise, he has no right to waive what Allah has made obligatory. Rather, a person may stipulate a condition that makes binding what would not otherwise be obligatory. The purpose of conditions is to make obligatory what was neither obligatory nor forbidden. The absence of an obligation does not preclude imposing one, such that the person stipulating it would be contradicting the revealed law.

Every valid condition must establish an obligation that did not previously exist. In a sale, each party becomes obliged to deliver to the other what he was not previously obliged to deliver. Likewise, things become permissible for each that were not previously permissible, and things become forbidden to each that were not previously forbidden. The same applies to the parties to contracts of hire and marriage. Likewise, if someone stipulates a particular quality in the item sold or requires a pledge as security, or a woman stipulates a dower exceeding that customary for women of her standing, the condition makes things obligatory, forbidden, or permissible that were not so before.

This is what misled those who believed that conditions are invalid by default. They argued:

“They must either permit something forbidden, forbid something permissible, make binding what is not due, or waive an obligation. None of this is permissible without the Lawgiver’s permission.”

This difficulty occurred to some people, leading them to imagine that this hadith was self-contradictory. That is not the case. Rather, whatever is forbidden without the condition cannot be made permissible by it, such as usury, intercourse with another person’s slave woman, or assigning the patronage arising from emancipation to someone other than the emancipator. Allah has forbidden intercourse except by virtue of marriage or slave ownership. Thus, if a man wished to lend his slave woman to another for intercourse, he would not be permitted to do so, unlike lending her for service, which is permissible. The same applies to patronage:

---

## Page 14404

for “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling patronage or giving it away.” Allah made patronage like lineage: it belongs to the emancipator just as lineage is established through the father. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever claims descent from someone other than his father, or takes patrons other than his own, incurs the curse of Allah, the angels, and all humanity. Allah will accept from him neither compensation nor ransom.”

Allah abolished the practices of the pre-Islamic age whereby a man would take another man’s son as his own, or a person would claim affiliation through emancipation with someone other than his patron. Such conduct is impermissible without a condition, so a condition cannot make permissible what was forbidden.

As for what is permissible without a condition, a condition makes it obligatory. Examples include an increase in the dower, the price, the goods sold, or the pledged security, and deferring collection of what is due. A man may give to a woman, voluntarily provide a pledge, grant a respite, and so forth. Once he stipulates this, it becomes obligatory. Once it becomes obligatory, demanding payment—which was lawful without the condition—becomes forbidden, because demanding payment was not lawful in the absence of the condition. The Lawgiver did not permit demanding payment from a debtor unconditionally. Thus, a condition does not change what is unconditionally lawful or unlawful.

As for what Allah has permitted in a particular circumstance, rather than unconditionally, if a condition changes that circumstance, it has not thereby forbidden what Allah made lawful. Likewise, regarding what Allah has forbidden in a particular circumstance, rather than unconditionally, the condition has not thereby permitted what Allah forbade, even though, without the condition, one would presume the continued applicability of the ruling of permissibility

---

## Page 14405

and prohibition; yet there is a difference between permissibility or prohibition established by a revealed pronouncement and that established merely through the presumption of continuity. A contract or condition overrides what that presumption entails, but it does not override what the Lawgiver’s words establish. Reports from the Companions accord with this, as Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “The stipulated conditions determine the limits of rights.”

As for rational reasoning, there are several considerations:

First: Contracts and conditions belong to the sphere of customary acts. The default regarding such acts is that they are not prohibited, and this presumption continues until evidence establishes their prohibition. Likewise, the default regarding tangible things is that they are not prohibited. Allah’s statement,

“He has already explained to you in detail what He has forbidden to you,”

applies generally to both tangible things and actions. If contracts and conditions are not prohibited, they are not invalid, for invalidity arises only from prohibition. If they are not invalid, they are valid.

Moreover, nothing in the Sharia indicates that contracts and conditions are prohibited as a class, except those whose lawfulness has been specifically established. Allah willing, we shall explain the meaning of the hadith of Ā’ishah and show that the absence of evidence of prohibition is itself evidence that something is not prohibited. Thus, the rational presumption of continuity and the absence of scriptural evidence establish that they are not prohibited. Entering into them is therefore either lawful or left unregulated, like tangible things that have not been prohibited.

---

## Page 14406

Most of the evidence used to establish that tangible things are not prohibited by default—general scriptural texts, sound analogies, the rational presumption of continuity, and the absence of a ruling where evidence for it is absent—also establishes that contracts and their conditions are not prohibited. This holds whether they are termed lawful or left unregulated, in accordance with the familiar disagreement among our scholars and others.

For Allah Most High’s censure in the Quran of disbelievers for declaring things forbidden without revealed authority concerns, in some instances, tangible things and, in others, actions. For example, they forbade a pilgrim in a state of ritual consecration from wearing his own clothes and circumambulating in them unless he belonged to the Hums. They ordered him to strip unless a member of the Hums lent him a garment, and forbade him from going beneath a roof. Likewise, the Ansār forbade a man from having vaginal intercourse with his wife while she was bent forward, and they forbade going between al-Safā and al-Marwah.

Yet they would sometimes break covenants they had concluded without revealed authorization. Allah, glorified be He, commanded them in Surah al-Nahl and elsewhere to fulfil those covenants, except those involving something prohibited. Thus, covenants must be fulfilled if they are not prohibited, even when their lawfulness has not been established by a specific revealed ruling. Such were the covenants they concluded in the pre-Islamic age of ignorance and were commanded to fulfil.

We have already drawn attention to this principle and explained that only what Allah has prescribed may be instituted as religion, and only what Allah has forbidden may be declared forbidden. For Allah censured the polytheists who instituted religious practices He had not authorized and declared forbidden what He had not forbidden. Thus, if we declare prohibited the contracts and conditions people enter into in their customary dealings without evidence

---

## Page 14407

from revealed law, we would be prohibiting what Allah has not prohibited. This differs from contracts that entail instituting religious practices Allah has not authorized, for Allah has forbidden instituting any religious practice He has not authorized. No act of worship may be prescribed except by Allah’s legislation, and no customary practice may be prohibited except by Allah’s prohibition. Contracts in ordinary dealings belong to customary practices, undertaken by Muslims and disbelievers alike, even if, in another respect, they may be a means of drawing near to Allah. They are not acts of worship requiring revealed prescription. Such is the case with manumission and charity.

If it is argued:

“Contracts alter what was established by revealed law. For if the right to marital intimacy or ownership of property is established in a particular state, and someone concludes a contract that changes that state, he has altered what was established by revealed law. This is unlike tangible things that have not been prohibited, for permitting them entails no change.”

The reply is:

There is no distinction between the two. Tangible things either belong to someone or they do not. If they belong to someone, their transfer by sale or otherwise does not change the things themselves, and this falls within the category of contracts. If they belong to no one and someone acquires ownership of them by taking possession or by similar means, this is an action that changes their legal status, just as contracts do. Moreover, they are prohibited before ritual slaughter; slaughtering them is comparable to concluding a contract concerning property.

Thus, just as our actions concerning tangible things—taking possession of them and slaughtering them—are lawful by default, even when they change the legal status of the thing, so too our actions concerning property through contracts

---

## Page 14408

and the like are lawful by default, even when they alter a person’s ownership rights.

The reason is that, in the case of legal effects arising from our actions—such as ownership established through sale and the right to marital intimacy established through marriage—we ourselves bring about the causes of those effects. The Lawgiver establishes the legal effect because we have brought about its cause; He does not establish it independently, as He does when directly prescribing obligations and prohibitions.

If it is we who bring that legal effect into being, and the Lawgiver has not forbidden us to undo it, then undoing it is not forbidden to us. When someone purchases an item, the Lawgiver makes it lawful for him and forbidden to others because he has established the cause of that status, namely, ownership acquired through sale. So long as the Lawgiver has not forbidden him to undo this, he may undo what he has established in whatever manner he wishes, provided that the Lawgiver has not forbidden it.

Likewise, if someone gives a man property, the default is that the recipient is not forbidden to dispose of it, even if doing so extinguishes the ownership established by the giver, unless something precludes this. This is the crucial point that makes the basis of the issue clear: particular rulings—such as this property being lawful for Zayd and forbidden to ʿAmr—have not been legislated by the Lawgiver individually. Rather, He has legislated them through general rulings, such as His words:

“Allah has permitted trade and forbidden usury.”

And His words:

“All women beyond these are lawful to you, provided that you seek them in marriage with your wealth.”

And His words:

“Then marry women of your choice, two, three, or four.”

This general ruling remains in force whether or not this particular sale takes place. When a particular sale takes place, it establishes a particular ownership. The cause of this particular ownership is

---

## Page 14409

the individual’s act. When the individual undoes it, he merely undoes what he himself established through his own act, not the general ruling established by Allah. For the particular ruling established by Allah merely follows the individual’s act as its cause; the Lawgiver did not establish it independently. Some people have mistakenly supposed that extinguishing rights through contracts and their dissolution is like abrogating rulings. This is not so: a general ruling can be removed only by the One who established it, namely, the Lawgiver. This particular legal effect, however, arose only because the individual brought the case within the scope of the general ruling. Just as bringing it within that scope is up to him, so too is removing it.

For the Lawgiver has never prescribed a ruling for him concerning the particular item itself, such as saying: “Sell this garment,” “Do not sell it,” “Give it away,” or “Do not give it away.” Rather, He has prescribed a general ruling that applies to a particular case once that case is brought within its scope. Reflect on this: there is a distinction between changing the specific ruling that the individual established by bringing a case within the scope of the general ruling, and changing the general ruling established by the Lawgiver when the individual brings about its cause.

Once it is clear that no contracts are forbidden except those forbidden by the Lawgiver, the obligation to fulfil them follows from the Lawgiver’s general command to fulfil them, except where specific evidence establishes an exception. Moreover, fulfilling contracts is among the obligations upon which all religious traditions—indeed, all rational people—agree. Those who hold that reason establishes obligations have included their fulfilment among rational obligations. Thus, entering into contracts in the first place is not forbidden unless the Lawgiver forbids it, while fulfilling them is obligatory by the Lawgiver’s command and also by the dictates of reason.

---

## Page 14410

Furthermore, the basis of contracts is the consent of the contracting parties, and their binding effect consists in what the parties have bound themselves to through their agreement. For Allah says in His Mighty Book:

“Except through trade by mutual consent among you.”

And He says:

“But if they willingly give up to you any part of it, then enjoy it with pleasure and satisfaction.”

He thus made permission to consume it dependent upon willing consent, as a consequence depends upon its condition. This indicates that willing consent is a cause of that permission. Here, a ruling is made contingent upon a relevant attribute expressed in a derivative form, indicating that this attribute is the cause of the ruling.

If willing consent permits the consumption of the bridal gift, the same applies to all other gratuitous transfers, by analogy with it on the basis of the effective cause explicitly indicated by the Quran.

Likewise, in His words:

“Except through trade by mutual consent among you,”

no condition is stipulated for trade other than mutual consent. This entails that mutual consent is what makes trade permissible. Accordingly, when the contracting parties mutually consent to a commercial transaction, or a donor willingly makes a gift, its permissibility is established by the Quran—unless it involves something Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, such as trading in wine and the like.

Furthermore, a contract may be either unqualified or qualified by stipulations. There is a distinction between an unqualified contract and the concept of a contract as such. Thus, if someone says, “This condition conflicts with what the contract entails,” and means that it conflicts with an unqualified contract, then the same is true of every additional condition, and this does not impair the contract. But if he means that it conflicts with what the contract entails whether unqualified or qualified, evidence is required to establish that claim. Such a claim is valid only when the condition conflicts with the purpose of the contract.

---

## Page 14411

For when a contract has a purpose intended in all its forms, and a stipulation is included that conflicts with that purpose, two contradictory things have been combined: affirming that purpose and negating it. Nothing is thereby accomplished. Such a stipulation is invalid by agreement; indeed, according to us, it also invalidates the contract.

Defective stipulations may be invalid because they conflict with the Lawgiver’s purpose, such as stipulating that patronage rights belong to someone other than the emancipator. This does not conflict with what the contract entails or with its purpose, for its purpose is ownership, and emancipation may also be an intended purpose of the contract: a slave is often purchased with the intention of freeing him. Establishing patronage rights does not conflict with the contract’s purpose; rather, it conflicts with Allah’s Book and His stipulation, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, explained when he said:

“Allah’s Book takes precedence, and Allah’s stipulation is more binding.”

Thus, if a stipulation conflicts with the purpose of the contract, the contract is futile. If it conflicts with the Lawgiver’s purpose, it contravenes Allah and His Messenger. But if neither is the case—if it is neither futile nor contains anything Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—there is no basis for prohibiting it. Rather, it must be deemed permissible, for it is something people deliberately undertake and need. Were it not for their need, they would not do it; undertaking an action is an indication of a need for it. Since its prohibition has not been established, it is permissible, in light of the provisions in the Quran and Sunnah that remove hardship.

Furthermore, regarding contracts and stipulations, one of the following positions must be adopted:

They are neither permissible nor valid unless their permissibility is established by specific evidence from Islamic law, whether a scriptural text, consensus, or

---

## Page 14412

analogical reasoning, according to the majority, as we mentioned when presenting the first position. Or it may be said:

They are not permissible or valid unless evidence from revelation establishes their permissibility, even if that evidence is general.

Or it may be said:

They are valid and are not forbidden unless the Lawgiver prohibits them through specific or general evidence.

The first position is invalid, because the Quran and Sunnah establish the validity of contracts concluded and property received while people were unbelievers. Allah commanded that these arrangements be honoured, provided they involve nothing prohibited after embracing Islam. Thus, the Exalted said in the verse concerning interest:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of interest, if you are believers.”

He commanded them to relinquish the interest still owed to them, but did not command them to return what they had already received under interest-bearing contracts. Rather, the implication of the verse—which has been unanimously acted upon—is that retaining it is not forbidden. Likewise, in the year of the Farewell Pilgrimage, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, cancelled outstanding interest debts but did not command people to return what they had already received. He also said:

“Any division made in pre-Islamic times shall stand as made, while any division still pending when Islam comes shall be made according to Islam.”

He also allowed people to retain the marriages they had contracted in pre-Islamic times, without asking anyone whether the marriage had been contracted during a waiting period or outside one, with or without a guardian, or with or without witnesses. He did not command anyone to renew his marriage contract or separate from his wife unless the grounds for prohibition remained present when he embraced Islam, as when he “instructed Ghaylān ibn Salama al-Thaqafī, who embraced Islam while married to ten women, to retain four and part with the rest.” Likewise, he “instructed Fayrūz al-Daylamī

---

## Page 14413

who embraced Islam while married to two sisters, to choose one and part with the other.” Likewise, the Companions instructed those Magians who embraced Islam “to separate from wives within the prohibited degrees of kinship.”

For this reason, the Muslims agree that contracts concluded by unbelievers are deemed valid after they embrace Islam, provided those contracts are not forbidden to Muslims, even though the unbelievers did not conclude them with the Lawgiver’s permission. Had they regarded contracts as being like acts of worship, valid only when sanctioned by revelation, they would have deemed them invalid—or at least deemed invalid those contracts whose parties had not adhered to a revealed law in concluding them.

If it is said:

The hadith jurists and the scholars of the Hijaz agree that, if these contracts were concluded in a manner forbidden in Islam, but the parties then embraced Islam after the grounds for that prohibition had ceased to exist, the contracts stand and the parties are not instructed to conclude them anew. This is because Islam effaces what came before it. Contracts they concluded without revealed sanction therefore stand on no lower footing than those they concluded despite a revealed prohibition; in your view, the two are alike.

We reply:

That is not so. Rather, contracts they concluded despite a prohibition are deemed valid only if possession was taken in connection with them. If, however, they embrace Islam before the exchange of possession, the contract is rescinded. This differs from contracts they concluded without revealed sanction, which are not rescinded, whether before or after possession is taken.

I have not seen the jurists of our school or others require consummation in the case of marriage. Rather, they treat embracing Islam before consummation and after it alike. This is because the marriage contract itself establishes legal effects, such as ties of affinity and the like, even if the marriage has not been consummated. Similarly, intercourse itself establishes legal effects even without marriage. Since both the contract and intercourse are intended in their own right—even when neither is accompanied by the other—they were allowed to continue

---

## Page 14414

in that state by the Lawgiver. Property transactions are different: the purpose of their contracts is the exchange of possession. If that exchange has not occurred, their purpose has not been achieved, and the Lawgiver therefore invalidates them because their purpose remains unfulfilled. Thus, it is clear that the Lawgiver does not invalidate what people seek to achieve through transactions unless He prohibits it, because He validates it only by permitting it.

Moreover, when Muslims enter into contracts with one another without knowing whether they are forbidden or permitted, all jurists—as far as I know—deem those contracts valid, provided the parties do not believe them to be forbidden, even if, at the time, a contracting party did not know them to be permitted, whether through independent legal reasoning or by following a scholarly authority. No one says that a contract is valid only if the party concluding it believes that the Lawgiver has permitted it.

If the Lawgiver’s specific permission were a condition for the validity of contracts, no contract would be valid until His permission had been established—just as a judge who rules without exercising independent legal judgment is sinful even if his ruling happens to be correct.

If, however, it is said that there must be scriptural evidence establishing their permissibility, whether general or specific, there are two answers:

First: we reject this requirement, as explained above.

Second: we say that the general scriptural evidence establishes the permissibility of contracts and contractual conditions as a whole, except those excluded by the Lawgiver. We shall discuss the evidence they have raised in objection, if Allah wills. Thus, all that remains is

---

## Page 14415

the third view, which is the position we seek to establish. As for the Prophet’s statement (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, it is invalid, even if there are a hundred conditions. Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

The word “condition” sometimes denotes the act of stipulating and sometimes the matter stipulated. The same applies to “promise” and “breach of promise.” Another example is the expression “a dirham of the ruler’s minting.” What is meant here—and Allah knows best—is the matter stipulated, not the speaker himself. Hence he said:

“Even if there are a hundred conditions.”

That is, even if there are a hundred stipulated matters. The point is not the number of times a condition is uttered, but the number of matters stipulated. The evidence for this is his statement:

“Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

That is, Allah’s Book has greater authority than this condition, and Allah’s condition is more binding than it. This applies only when that condition contradicts Allah’s Book and His condition, by stipulating something Allah Most High has forbidden.

If, however, the matter stipulated is something Allah has not forbidden, it does not contradict Allah’s Book or His condition such that one could say:

“Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

The meaning, then, is: whoever stipulates something not sanctioned by Allah’s ruling or His Book, whether indirectly or directly, that stipulation is invalid. For the stipulated matter must be something permissible to do without the condition before it can validly be stipulated and thereby become obligatory. Since Allah’s Book never assigns the right of patronage to anyone other than the emancipator, this stipulated matter—namely, assigning the right of patronage to someone other than the emancipator—was a condition not in Allah’s Book.

---

## Page 14416

Consider, then, what is stipulated, whether it is an act or a legal ruling. If Allah has permitted it, it may validly be stipulated, and fulfilling it becomes obligatory. If Allah Most High has not permitted it, it may not be stipulated.

Thus, if a man stipulates that he will not take his wife travelling, this stipulation is in Allah’s Book, because Allah’s Book permits him not to take her travelling. By stipulating that he will not travel with her, he has stipulated something permissible in Allah’s Book.

The substance of the hadith, then, is that if the matter stipulated is not among permissible acts, [the stipulation is invalid]. Alternatively, “not in Allah’s Book” may be explained as meaning that its negation is not in Allah’s Book, just as he said:

“There will be people who tell you things that neither you nor your forefathers have known.”

That is, things contrary to what you know. Otherwise, there are many things one does not know.

We further say: the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not mean that contracts and conditions which the Lawgiver has not permitted are invalid in the sense that they entail no binding consequences—neither obligations nor prohibitions. This would contradict the Quran and Sunnah. Rather, prohibited contracts and conditions may entail legal consequences.

Allah prohibited the declaration of zihār (declaring one’s wife as forbidden to oneself as one’s mother) in His Book itself, calling it a reprehensible utterance and a falsehood. Yet He made expiation obligatory for anyone who goes back on that declaration. For one who does not go back on it, He gave effect to the intended prohibition: refraining from intercourse and from entering into a marriage contract.

The same applies to vows. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) forbade making vows, as is authentically reported from him in the hadiths of Abū Hurayrah and Ibn ‘Umar, and said:

“It brings no good.”

Yet he required a vow to be fulfilled when it involved obedience to Allah, as stated in

---

## Page 14417

his saying (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Thus, a prohibited contract may give rise to an obligation or a prohibition. It cannot, however, confer permission. For example, when the Prophet prohibited sales involving uncertainty, usurious transactions, marriage to women within the prohibited degrees, and similar transactions, carrying out what he prohibited did not confer any lawful entitlement upon the person concerned. This is because the prohibited act is a sin, and the general rule concerning sins is that they do not bring about Allah’s blessings and mercy. Permission is one of Allah’s blessings and mercies. Although sins may lead to respite and the opening of worldly opportunities, that belongs to divine decree, not religious legislation. Indeed, they may lead to punishment from Allah Most High.

Imposing obligations and prohibitions may constitute punishment, as Allah Most High says:

“For wrongdoing on the part of the Jews, We made unlawful for them good things which had previously been lawful to them.”

Yet obligations and prohibitions may also be a mercy, as in our upright, monotheistic Law.

Those among the literalists and others who disagree with this principle may hold that any contract lacking specific permission is prohibited, and that every prohibited contract is legally equivalent to no contract at all. Both premises are disputed, as explained above.

This argument may be answered in a second way—if the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) meant that conditions which Allah has not permitted, even though He does not forbid them

---

## Page 14418

are invalid—then we say:

We have already cited evidence from the Book, the Sunnah, and transmitted reports establishing the general obligation to fulfil covenants and conditions, and that the point is that fulfilling them is obligatory. On this understanding, the obligation to fulfil them entails their permissibility: if fulfilling them is obligatory, they cannot be invalid; and if they are not invalid, they are permissible.

This is because his words, “not in the Book of Allah,” apply only to what is absent from the Book of Allah both in its general provisions and in its specific provisions. Whatever the Book of Allah establishes as permissible through its general provisions is indeed in the Book of Allah. For when we say, “This is in the Book of Allah,” we include what it contains both specifically and generally. This is how we understand the words of Allah Most High:

“And We have sent down to you the Book as a clarification of all things,”

and His words:

“But a confirmation of what came before it and a detailed explanation of all things,”

and His words:

“We have neglected nothing in the Book.”

This last verse applies here according to those who understand “the Book” to mean the Quran. According to those who understand it to mean the Preserved Tablet, however, it is not applicable here.

This is demonstrated by the fact that a condition whose permissibility is established by the Sunnah or scholarly consensus is valid by agreement. It must therefore be in the Book of Allah. It may not be specifically mentioned in the Book of Allah, but the Book of Allah commands us to follow the Sunnah and the path of the believers. In this sense, it is in the Book of Allah. For whatever encompasses an encompassing whole also encompasses its contents, and evidence establishing a proof is itself proof in this respect.

---

## Page 14419

One objection to this answer remains:

If the Book of Allah requires the fulfilment of conditions in general, then a condition concerning patronage falls within that general rule.

The answer is:

A general text applies only where no specific evidence excludes the case in question, for the specific clarifies the general. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled out this stipulated arrangement by prohibiting the sale or gift of patronage and by saying:

“Whoever claims descent from someone other than his father, or takes patrons other than his own, upon him is the curse of Allah, the angels, and all people.”

The Book also indicates this in the words of Allah Most High:

“Allah has not placed two hearts within any man’s body, nor has He made your wives whom you liken to your mothers your mothers, nor has He made your adopted sons your own sons. These are merely words you utter with your mouths. Allah speaks the truth, and He guides to the right way.”

“Call them after their fathers; that is more just in the sight of Allah. If you do not know their fathers, then they are your brothers in faith and those bound to you by patronage.”

He thus made it obligatory for us to ascribe a person to the father who begot him, rather than to the man who adopted him, and prohibited adoption. He then commanded that, when the father is unknown, the person be called a brother in faith and one bound by patronage, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Zayd ibn Hārithah:

“You are our brother and our freedman.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Your servants are your brothers whom Allah has placed under your authority. Whoever has his brother under his authority should feed him from what he himself eats and clothe him from what he himself wears.”

Thus He, glorified be He, made patronage analogous to lineage and explained the basis of patronage in His words:

---

## Page 14420

“And when you said to the one whom Allah had favoured and you too had favoured.”

He thus explained that patronage is founded on the favour of emancipation, just as lineage is founded on the favour of begetting. Since He has forbidden transferring one’s affiliation away from the one who begot him, it is likewise forbidden to transfer it away from the one who emancipated him, for the two cases are analogous. Whoever stipulates that the buyer must emancipate the slave, while patronage belongs to someone other than the buyer, is like someone who stipulates that, if a man entering into marriage fathers a child, the child’s lineage must be ascribed to someone else. This is the meaning to which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, pointed when he said:

“Patronage belongs only to the one who emancipates.”

Since the Book of Allah indicates, both specifically and generally, that this stipulation is forbidden, it does not fall within the covenants Allah has commanded us to fulfil, for He, glorified be He, does not command what He has forbidden.

So much for this point. Nevertheless, what seems most likely to me is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended only the first meaning: invalidating conditions that conflict with the Book of Allah, and warning against stipulating something Allah has not permitted—which would mean that He has forbidden the stipulated matter, since the Book of Allah grants general permission for whatever He has not forbidden—or against stipulating anything that conflicts with the Book of Allah, as indicated by his words:

“The Book of Allah has greater right to be followed, and Allah’s stipulation is more binding.”

Once it is clear that the general permissibility and validity of contracts and conditions rest on two foundations—the general scriptural evidence, and the rational evidence consisting of the presumption of continuity and the absence of anything establishing prohibition—this principle may not be applied to categories of

---

## Page 14421

issues and individual cases without first exercising scholarly judgment concerning that particular category or case: is there any scriptural evidence that entails prohibition, or not?

If the evidentiary basis is the presumption of continuity and the absence of scriptural evidence, Muslims are unanimously agreed—and it is an essential, necessarily known teaching of Islam—that no one may adopt a position or issue a fatwa on this basis without first searching for evidence specific to the matter, provided that he is qualified to do so. Everything that Allah and His Messenger have made obligatory or forbidden overrides this presumption of continuity. A qualified person may therefore rely on it only after examining the scriptural evidence.

If, however, the evidentiary basis consists of general scriptural texts, then a general text whose scope has been restricted by numerous, widely scattered exceptions likewise cannot be invoked without first investigating whether the case in question is among those excluded or those remaining within its scope. There is no disagreement about this either.

The scholars disagree only concerning a general text whose scope is not known to have been restricted, or from which certain specific cases are known to have been excluded: may it be applied to other cases before searching for contrary evidence that restricts its scope? The followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others have differed on this.

Two positions have been reported from Ahmad on this question. Most of his statements indicate that people of his time and others like them may not apply the apparent meanings of the Quran before searching for what explains them in the Sunnah and in the statements of the Companions, the Successors and others. This is the correct position, adopted by Abū al-Khattāb and others. For an apparent meaning regarding which it is not considered likely that

---

## Page 14422

no countervailing evidence exists cannot make its implication probable. If it is considered likely that no countervailing evidence exists, then its implication is likewise considered probable. For most general texts, later scholars can attain this degree of probability only after searching for countervailing evidence. This applies whether one regards the absence of countervailing evidence as part of the proof itself—in which case the proof is the apparent meaning unaccompanied by any contrary contextual indication, as held by those among our fellow scholars and others who do not allow exceptions to either a proof or an effective legal cause—or regards countervailing evidence as an impediment to the proof’s operation. On the latter view, the proof is the apparent meaning itself, but the contextual indication prevents it from establishing its implication, as held by those among our fellow scholars and others who allow exceptions to a proof and an effective legal cause. The disagreement here, however, concerns only a conceptual distinction, a choice of wording, or a convention of scholarly debate; it has no bearing on any substantive scholarly or legal question.

Accordingly, the evidence negating the prohibition of contracts and conditions and affirming their permissibility is qualified by all the contracts and conditions that Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. This principle therefore cannot be usefully applied to particular categories of questions without knowledge of the evidence specific to that category. It thus more closely resembles the principles of jurisprudence—which concern general evidence—than legal maxims, which express general rulings. Nevertheless, a jurist who considers it likely that no countervailing evidence exists concerning a disputed or newly arising question may benefit from this principle. We shall therefore set out general legal rules for some of the categories it encompasses.

---

## Page 14423

Examples include the following:

As we have mentioned, anyone who transfers an asset out of his ownership, whether in exchange for compensation—as in a sale or khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation)—or gratuitously—as in an endowment or manumission—may reserve some of its benefits. If the transaction does not permit uncertainty, as in a sale, the reserved benefit must be clearly defined. Al-Bukhārī, Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasāʾī narrated that Jābir said:

“I sold it—meaning his camel—to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on condition that I could ride it back to my family.”

If the transaction is not of this kind, as in manumission or an endowment, he may reserve the slave’s service for the lifetime of his master or of a named person, and may reserve the endowment’s income for the founder’s lifetime.

Another example is when a seller stipulates that the purchaser must free the slave. This condition is valid according to the apparent position of the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others, on the basis of the hadith of Barīrah, although a contrary opinion is also reported from both of them. Does freeing the slave then become binding upon the purchaser, just as it becomes binding through a vow, so that the judge carries it out if the purchaser refuses? Or does the seller acquire the right to rescind the sale if the purchaser refuses to free him, just as he may rescind it when the item sold lacks a stipulated attribute? There are two views within their respective schools.

Al-Shāfiʿī and a group of Ahmad’s followers consider this a departure from analogy, because it prevents the purchaser from disposing of his property in any way other than manumission. This runs contrary to what the contract entails, namely ownership that gives its holder unrestricted power of disposal.

---

## Page 14424

They said:

“The Sunnah permitted this only because the Lawgiver has a special concern for emancipation unmatched in other matters. For this reason, He required emancipation to extend to a co-owner’s share, even though this deprives the co-owner of his property without his consent. Since emancipation rests on giving freedom precedence, extending it to other shares, and making it effective against another person’s property, no other disposition can be treated analogously to it. Thus, no other disposition may be stipulated.”

Ahmad’s principles and explicit statements, however, entail that any disposition serving a legitimate purpose may be stipulated, even if this precludes other dispositions.

Ibn al-Qāsim reported that Ahmad was asked about a man selling a slave woman on condition that the purchaser free her, and he permitted it. He was then told:

“But these people—meaning the followers of Abū Hanīfah—say that a sale with this condition is not permissible.”

He replied:

“Why should it not be permissible? The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, purchased Jābir’s camel, and Jābir stipulated that he could ride it to Medina. Ā’ishah purchased Barīrah on condition that she free her. Why, then, should this not be permissible?”

He added: “This is only one condition. The prohibition concerns only two conditions.”

He was asked: “If he stipulates two conditions, is that permissible?”

He replied: “It is not permissible.”

Thus, he disputed the position of those who prohibited it, arguing for its permissibility on the basis of the Prophet’s stipulation, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Jābir retain the right to ride the camel, the hadith of Barīrah, and the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited only two conditions in a sale.

Yet Jābir’s hadith involves reserving some of the benefit of the property sold, thereby reducing the rights conferred by an unqualified contract. Stipulating emancipation, meanwhile, requires an intended disposition that necessarily reduces the rights conferred by an unqualified contract. It is therefore clear that Ahmad makes no distinction between a restriction affecting the right of disposal and one affecting the property owned.

---

## Page 14425

And his reliance on the hadith concerning two conditions shows that this entire category of stipulations is permissible. Had emancipation been an exception to analogical reasoning, he would not have established its permissibility by analogy with other matters, nor supported it with evidence encompassing both emancipation and other matters.

Likewise, Ahmad ibn al-Husayn ibn Hassān said:

“I asked Abū Abdullāh about someone who purchased a slave and stipulated, ‘He is free after my death.’ He replied, ‘This slave is designated for emancipation upon his owner’s death.’”

He thus permitted a condition stipulating emancipation upon death. The followers of al-Shāfi‘ī disagreed over such a condition; al-Rāfi‘ī considered the view that it is invalid to be correct.

He likewise permitted a condition stipulating that a slave woman be taken as a concubine. Abū Tālib said:

“I asked Ahmad about a man who purchased a slave woman on condition that he take her as a concubine—a valuable slave woman whose owners wished him to take her as a concubine rather than use her for service. He replied, ‘There is nothing wrong with it.’”

Since the seller of the slave woman had a legitimate purpose in stipulating concubinage, he permitted it. He likewise permitted the seller of a slave woman or similar property to stipulate that the purchaser not sell her to anyone other than the seller, and that, should the purchaser wish to sell her, the seller would take her back at the original price, as they reported from ‘Umar, Ibn Mas‘ūd and his wife Zaynab.

The underlying principle is this: with respect to the property sold, whose constituent parts and benefits are included in an unqualified contract, the two parties may stipulate an addition to what that contract includes. Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sells date palms that have been pollinated, their fruit belongs to the seller, unless the purchaser stipulates otherwise.”

---

## Page 14426

He thus permitted the purchaser to stipulate an addition to what an unqualified contract entails, which is permissible by consensus. The two parties may also stipulate a reduction in what it includes by making an exception. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited “making exceptions unless they are clearly specified,” indicating that they are permissible when clearly specified. Likewise, Jābir reserved the right to ride his camel as far as Medina. To my knowledge, Muslims unanimously agree that it is permissible to reserve an undivided share—for example, to sell someone a house except for one-quarter or one-third of it. They also agree that a specified portion may be excluded if it can be separated without harm—for example, to sell the fruit of an orchard except that of certain specified date palms, or to sell clothing, slaves or livestock that both parties have seen, except for particular items or individuals that they have identified.

They disagreed, however, about reserving some of the benefits, such as living in the house for a month, retaining the slave’s services for a month, or riding the animal for a specified period or as far as a particular town. This is despite the agreement of the renowned jurists, their followers and the majority of the Companions that this may be beneficial. An example is purchasing a married slave woman: the right to marital intimacy with her, which belongs to her husband, is not included in the contract. Thus, ‘Ā’ishah purchased Barīrah while she was married. However, she purchased her on condition that she emancipate her, so the only disposal of her that she was entitled to make was emancipation, and emancipation does not conflict with her marriage.

For this reason, Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him and his father—who was among those who narrated the hadith of Barīrah—held, along with a group of the Companions, that the sale of a slave woman constitutes her divorce. This was their interpretation of the words of Allah Most High:

“And married women, except those whom your right hands possess.”

They said:

---

## Page 14427

“Thus, if he purchases her, receives her as a gift, or inherits her, his right hand possesses her. She therefore becomes lawful to him, and this can only occur if her husband’s marital rights cease.”

Some jurists cited the hadith of Barīrah in support of their position. Ahmad, however, did not accept this argument, because Ibn ‘Abbās narrated it yet held a contrary view. This was—and Allah knows best—for the reason I have mentioned: ‘Ā’ishah did not acquire unrestricted ownership of Barīrah.
