Nevertheless, all the jurists and the majority of the Companions hold that when ownership of a married slave woman is transferred—through sale, gift, inheritance, or the like—and her owner’s title is legally protected, her husband’s marital rights do not cease. The purchaser or other recipient acquires ownership of her, except for the right to marital intimacy.

Among their arguments is that even the seller himself could not extinguish the husband’s marital rights if he wished to do so. The purchaser, whose position is subordinate to that of the seller, cannot have greater authority than he does, nor can the purchaser’s ownership be more complete than the seller’s. The husband’s rights are legally protected and may not be seized. The case of a woman taken captive in war is different and involves a disagreement that need not be discussed here, since the lives and property of those at war with the Muslims may lawfully be taken; the same applies to the marital rights they possess.

Likewise, the hadith-oriented jurists and the scholars of the Hijaz agree that when someone sells trees whose fruit has already appeared—such as date palms that have been pollinated—the fruit belongs to the seller, who is entitled to leave it on the trees until it has fully ripened. The seller has thus reserved the use of the trees until the fruit has fully ripened.

---

## Page 14428

Likewise, most of them permit the sale of leased property—such as a house or a slave. The purchaser acquires ownership, but not the use to which the lessee is entitled. Hadith-oriented jurists, such as Ahmad and others, also permit reserving some of the benefit conveyed by a contract, as in the cases on which there is agreement, just as one may reserve a specified part of the property or an undivided share. It is likewise permissible to reserve a specified part when it is customarily separated, as when selling a sheep while reserving certain parts removed at slaughter: its head, hide and feet.

The same applies to leases and contracts of hire. In contracts whose duration is specified, a contract without special stipulations entails a certain kind of use. Examples include renting land for cultivation, a shop for trade or a craft, or hiring a worker for sewing, building or similar work. Increasing or reducing what such a contract would ordinarily entail is permissible, without any disagreement known to me.

In marriage, a contract without special stipulations entitles the husband to marital intimacy to the full extent recognized by custom, wherever and whenever he wishes. He may therefore move her wherever he wishes, provided this causes no harm. Unlawful forms of intimacy and anything harmful are excluded, since custom does not entail them. The contract also entitles her to a dowry equivalent to that of comparable women, and gives her a general right to marital intimacy. Thus, if his penis has been amputated or he is impotent, she has the right to annul the marriage according to the early scholars and the leading jurists. If he swears to abstain from intercourse with her, she is entitled to separation from him if he does not return to her, as established by the Quran and scholarly consensus. Nevertheless, some jurists do not require him to have intercourse or to initiate the apportionment of nights, relying instead on natural desire, as in the school of Abū Hanīfah

---

## Page 14429

and al-Shāfiʿī, and according to one report from Ahmad. Yet the correct view, supported on many grounds, is that he is obliged to have intercourse with her, as indicated by the Quran, the Sunnah, reports from the Companions, and sound reasoning.

Some hold that the required frequency of intercourse is once every four months, by analogy with an oath of abstinence. Whether he must have intercourse with her according to what is customary, just as he must provide for her according to what is customary, is disputed within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

The correct view, indicated by most of Ahmad’s statements and held by most of the early scholars, is that the obligations which the marriage contract imposes upon each spouse towards the other—such as maintenance, marital intimacy, and overnight companionship for the wife, and marital intimacy for the husband—have no fixed measure. Rather, custom is the reference point, as indicated by the Quran in such statements as Allah’s words:

“Women have rights similar to their obligations, according to what is fair.”

And by the Sunnah in such statements as the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to Hind:

“Take what is sufficient for you and your child, according to what is reasonable.”

If the spouses dispute these matters, the judge determines what is due through his own considered judgment, just as the Companions prescribed a specified number of occasions on which a husband was to have intercourse. Those among Ahmad’s followers who fixed the frequency of intercourse to which a wife is entitled are comparable to al-Shāfiʿī in fixing the amount of maintenance: the wife needs both, and the marriage contract makes both obligatory. Fixing such measures is a weak position according to the great majority of jurists, far removed from the meanings conveyed by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning.

Al-Shāfiʿī fixed its amount only to apply consistently the principle we have mentioned concerning his position: that indeterminacy must be excluded from all contracts, by analogy with the prohibition of sales involving uncertainty. Thus, he assigned a fixed measure to the maintenance due under the marriage contract in consistent application of that principle.

---

## Page 14430

This principle has already been noted.

Likewise, according to the great majority of jurists, an unqualified marriage contract requires that the husband be free from severance of the penis and impotence. According to the majority, it also requires that the wife be free from impediments to intercourse, such as closure of the vaginal opening, and from insanity, leprosy, and leukoderma. According to one of the two juristic opinions within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere, it likewise requires that both spouses be free from defects that prevent the full enjoyment of intercourse, such as discharges of impurities from either spouse and similar conditions. It does not, however, require beauty or similar qualities. It also requires that the husband be a suitable match, but nothing beyond that.

If either spouse stipulates that the other possess a desired quality, such as wealth, beauty, virginity, or the like, the stipulation is valid, and the spouse making it has the right to annul the marriage if that quality is absent. This is the sounder of the two reports from Ahmad, the stronger of the two positions in al-Shāfiʿī’s school, and the apparent position of Mālik’s school.

According to the other report, the right of annulment arises only when free status or religion has been stipulated. Under this report, there are two opinions concerning a stipulation about lineage. This applies whether the woman stipulates a quality in the man or the man stipulates a quality in the woman. Indeed, a woman’s stipulation concerning the man carries even greater force, by agreement of the jurists among Ahmad’s followers and others. What some of Ahmad’s followers have stated to the contrary has no basis.

Likewise, if a deficiency in a quality required by an unqualified contract is expressly stipulated—for example, if the husband stipulates that his penis has been severed or that he is impotent, or the woman that her vaginal opening is closed or that she is insane,

---

## Page 14431

then this stipulation is valid by agreement of the jurists. Thus, they agree that a stipulation reducing the entitlements ordinarily arising from the contract is valid, but disagree here about stipulating additional entitlements, as I have explained to you.

Abū Hanīfa’s position is that a man has no right to annul a marriage on the grounds of a defect or a contractual stipulation.

As for the dower, there is agreement that it may exceed or fall below the customary dower for a woman of comparable standing. Likewise, most of the early Muslims—or many of them—and the jurists of hadith, as well as Mālik according to one of the two reports from him, permit restrictions on the husband’s rights: the wife may stipulate that he not move her from her town or her home. They also permit granting her rights beyond those conferred by an unqualified contract, so that he binds himself not to marry another woman alongside her or take a concubine. A group of the early Muslims, Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī, and Mālik according to the other report hold that such a stipulation is invalid. According to Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, however, it does affect the specification of the dower.

Sound analogical reasoning in this matter, consistent with the principles of Ahmad and other jurists of hadith, is that stipulating either an addition to or a reduction in the entitlements arising from an unqualified contract is permissible, unless Islamic law prohibits it. Thus, if additions to and reductions in the property or benefit forming the subject of the contract are as I have explained, then the same applies to additions to and reductions in the rights of ownership established by the contract. Thus, if he stipulates that the purchaser emancipate the slave, make the property an endowment benefiting the seller or someone else, or use the property to discharge a debt he owes

---

## Page 14432

to a specified or unspecified creditor, or use it to maintain his ties of kinship, or the like, then this is a stipulation requiring a disposition of the property that serves a definite purpose. The same applies to obligatory and voluntary charitable giving.

As for distinguishing emancipation from other dispositions on the grounds of its merit, which the Lawgiver seeks to promote, this is a weak argument. Some forms of charitable giving are superior to emancipation. Supporting a needy relative is better than emancipation, as Ahmad explicitly stated. For it is reported:

“Maymūna, the Prophet’s wife (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), emancipated a slave woman of hers. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said, ‘Had you given her to your maternal uncles, it would have been better for you.’”

Hence, if a deceased person has relatives who do not inherit, a bequest to them is preferable to a bequest for emancipation. I know of no disagreement on this point. The only disagreement I know of concerns whether making a bequest to them is obligatory. There are two reports from Ahmad on this: according to the first, it is obligatory, as held by a group of earlier and later scholars; according to the second, it is not obligatory, as held by the other three leading jurists and others.

If someone makes a bequest to others while excluding these relatives, should the bequest be redirected to his relatives rather than the designated beneficiary? Or should the beneficiary receive one-third of it and his relatives two-thirds, just as an estate is divided between the heirs and the beneficiary of a bequest? Both views are reported from Ahmad, although the prevailing view among most of his followers is that the bequest takes effect.

Thus, if some forms of charitable giving are superior to emancipation, it is unsound to justify this distinction by claiming that emancipation alone possesses greater merit. Moreover, the act required of the purchaser may be of greater merit, as when he owes Allah an obligation in the form of zakat, an expiation, or a vow, or owes a debt to another person, and stipulates that the purchaser

---

## Page 14433

discharge his debt from the property sold; or the purchaser stipulates that the seller discharge his debt out of the price paid, or something similar. Such a stipulation is more compelling than a stipulation requiring emancipation. As for the extension of emancipation to a co-owner’s share, its sole purpose is to secure complete freedom. The Lawgiver has prescribed an equivalent concerning property: the right of pre-emption. This was prescribed to give its holder full ownership, because of the harm entailed by co-ownership.

We maintain that this has been prescribed in all forms of co-ownership: a co-owner is entitled to obtain a partition. If the property itself can be divided, it is divided; otherwise, we divide the proceeds of its sale if either co-owner requests this. Completing emancipation is one instance of this, since co-ownership is ended sometimes by partition and sometimes by making ownership or freedom complete.

The underlying principle is that ownership is the legally recognized power to dispose of the property itself, analogous to physical power. One may therefore have the legal power to make one kind of disposition but not another, just as one may have the physical ability to do one thing but not another. Hence, ownership takes various forms in the revealed law, just as power takes various forms. Full ownership confers the right to dispose of the property itself through sale or gift, and the property passes to the owner’s heirs. It also confers the right to dispose of its benefits through lending, leasing, personal use, and so forth.

Yet a man may own a Zoroastrian slave woman, or slave women forbidden to him through milk-kinship, without having the right to sexual relations with them. He may, however, obtain compensation for that benefit by giving such a woman in marriage—for example, by marrying the Zoroastrian woman to a Zoroastrian man. He may also own a slave woman who has borne him a child, without having the right to sell her or give her away; nor does she pass to his heirs, according to the vast majority of Muslims. By their unanimous agreement, he has the right to have intercourse with her and to employ her services. Likewise, he may obtain compensation for these benefits by giving her in marriage and hiring out her services

---

## Page 14434

according to most of them, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. A man may also own pledged property and be responsible for its upkeep, yet have no right to dispose of it in any way that extinguishes the pledgee’s right, whether by sale or gift. There is a well-known disagreement concerning emancipation.

As for a slave whom one has vowed to emancipate, a sacrificial offering, specific property that one has vowed to give in charity, and similar things that must be devoted to a devotional purpose, jurists of our school and others have disagreed over whether this causes one’s ownership to cease. Neither position fits the model of unrestricted ownership.

Those who maintain that his ownership has not ceased—as most jurists of our school may hold—regard it as ownership that permits him to devote the property only to the designated purpose: emancipation, sacrifice, or charity. This is analogous to a slave purchased on condition that he be emancipated, given in charity, or given as a gift to maintain ties, or to a compensatory offering purchased on condition that it be sent to the Sacred Precinct.

Those who maintain that his ownership has ceased nevertheless hold that he retains the right to emancipate it, send it as an offering, or give it in charity. This, too, differs from the ordinary consequences of ownership ceasing in other circumstances.

Likewise, jurists disagree concerning an endowment for a specified beneficiary: does the endowed property become Allah’s property, pass into the beneficiary’s ownership, or remain the property of the person who established the endowment? These are three positions within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

Whichever position is adopted, the ownership described here is a distinct kind, unlike ownership in sales and gifts. Likewise, the recipient’s ownership of a gift, where the donor is entitled to revoke it

---

## Page 14435

—as when a father gives a gift to his son, according to hadith jurists such as al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad—is a distinct kind of ownership, since someone other than the owner is entitled to reclaim the property from him and rescind the contract.

The same applies to all property held under a contract that either contracting party is entitled to rescind. Examples include property sold subject to a condition, according to those who hold that ownership passes to the purchaser, as do al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad in one of their respective opinions; property sold to a purchaser who becomes insolvent and cannot pay the price, according to the hadith jurists and the scholars of the Hijaz; and property sold in which a defect or the absence of a stipulated attribute subsequently becomes apparent, according to all Muslims. In these cases, whether the transaction involves an exchange or a gratuitous transfer, the contracting party is entitled to reclaim the property, whereas a father’s property is not subject to such reclamation. Yet both fall within the general category of ownership.

The same applies to a son’s ownership according to Ahmad and other hadith jurists, who followed the meaning of the Book and the explicit teaching of the Sunnah on this matter. Groups among the early generations say: “The property is lawful for the father to take, while belonging to the son.” Thus, for the father, it resembles unowned things that may be acquired by taking possession, while the son’s ownership remains established, allowing him unrestricted disposal of it.

Since ownership takes different forms, with the degrees of unrestricted and restricted disposal that I have described, as well as others I have not described, there is no reason why establishing these rights should not be left to the individual: he may establish those he considers beneficial to himself and refrain from establishing those that offer him no benefit. The Lawgiver prohibits a person only from what entails harm that rebounds upon him or is unmitigated. If something entails no harm, or its harm is outweighed by its benefit, He never prohibits it.

---

## Page 14436

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Contracts involving some form of exchange—which constitute most human transactions and are indispensable to human life—fall into four categories. Such exchanges may involve property for property, as in sales; a benefit for property, as in leasing and contracts for a stipulated reward; or a benefit for another benefit, as in mutual assistance, mutual support, and similar arrangements. Governorship, military recruitment, and other such public appointments may also fall within this discussion.

They may be permissible for both parties, as in sales, leasing, and cooperation in righteousness and piety.

Alternatively, they may be forbidden for both parties, as in exchanging wine for swine, hiring someone for illicit sexual intercourse in return for wine, or hiring someone to give false testimony in return for false testimony. As one judge used to say of a group of leaders: “They lend one another false testimony”—likening this to an exchange of loans.

Or they may be permissible for one party and forbidden for the other. Muslims ought to understand this category, for neither religion nor worldly life can be sustained without it.

---

## Page 14437

As for the first category alone, it can sustain only a weak form of religion. As for the third, it sustains a wicked worldly life and a religion of innovation.

But religion as prescribed and a sound worldly life can be sustained only through the third category. Examples include giving to those whose hearts are to be won over, to secure their benefit or avert their harm; bribing governors to ward off injustice or obtain one’s rightful due—not to withhold anyone’s rights; and giving to someone whose harmful words or actions one fears, whether a poet, an oppressor, a highway robber, or anyone else. This also includes giving to those whose assistance is sought in righteousness and piety, such as helpers, supporters, governors, and others.

The basis for this in the Quran, the Sunnah, and the practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs is that Allah assigned those whose hearts are to be won over a rightful share of the obligatory alms, whose permitted recipients He specified in His Book, taking charge of their apportionment Himself. This indicated that they may receive from funds for public interests—and, according to the correct view, from fay’ (wealth acquired without fighting)—whose permitted uses are broader than those of zakat. Everyone eligible to receive alms may receive from funds for public interests, but the converse does not hold. A recipient of alms receives either because of his need or because of the benefit he brings. Both grounds apply to expenditure for public interests; indeed, public interests comprise nothing else. Those whose hearts are to be won over belong to the category of people who bring benefit and thus have a greater claim to funds for public interests and fay’. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave them from fay’ and the spoils of war, just as

---

## Page 14438

he did with the small piece of gold that ‘Alī sent from Yemen. He likewise distributed the spoils of Hunayn among the leaders of Quraysh and the people of Najd, saying:

“I give to some men while leaving others who are dearer to me without giving to them. I give to some because of the anxiety and distress in their hearts, while entrusting others to the contentment and goodness Allah has placed in their hearts.”

He also said:

“I give one of them a gift, and he carries it away under his arm as fire.” They asked, “Messenger of Allah, why, then, do you give to them?” He replied, “They insist on asking me, and Allah does not allow me to be miserly.”

He also said:

“By Him in whose hand is my soul, no man who asks me for something, and whose asking secures for him what we had not intended to give him, will be blessed in it.”

Or words to this effect.

This category encompasses all three divisions. As for wealth in the form of tangible assets, this includes ransoming captives and freeing people from the hands of disbelievers and those who unlawfully hold them. A free Muslim may fall into the power of disbelievers or of evildoers: they may enslave him unjustly, or free him and then deny having freed him. They may also compel him to work without his consent or authorization from the Lawgiver, as when someone unjustly forces workers, such as tailors and farmers, into service. Or they may imprison him unjustly and wrongfully.

Whenever one human being subjugates another without right and prevents him from acting freely, the oppressor resembles a captor, and the oppressed resembles a captive. Likewise, someone held under lawful coercion is a captive. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to a creditor who would not leave his debtor’s side: “What has become of your captive?”

---

## Page 14439

Just as taking control of property without right constitutes wrongful appropriation—even when this includes breach of trust and theft—so taking control of people without right constitutes captivity, including when the oppressors are Muslims. The same applies to rescuing enslaved people from those who transgress against them and wrong them, for lawful slavery has limits, and exceeding them constitutes aggression. This also includes rescuing a wife from an oppressive husband, for marriage is bondage, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah. Allah Most High says:

“And they both found her master at the door.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning women:

“They are like captives in your keeping.”

And ʿUmar said: “Marriage is bondage, so let each of you consider into whose bondage he places the woman dear to him.”

The same applies to rescuing a boy or girl from an oppressor, such as someone who withholds what is due to them and subjects them to what is forbidden. It also includes recovering property from those who have seized it unjustly or on the basis of an interpretation, whether it has been wrongfully appropriated, stolen, or otherwise taken, by paying the oppressor something so that he returns it to its owner.

In both categories, it makes no difference whether payment is made to prevent an oppressor from subjugating people or seizing property—as when, out of necessity, a truce is concluded with a warring enemy in return for money paid to them—or to secure release or recovery from the oppressor after the subjugation or seizure has occurred.

---

## Page 14440

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

A Principle Concerning Obligatory Transactions Involving Compensation and Related Matters

Section

Providing benefits and property, whether for compensation or gratuitously, is either obligatory or recommended. Obligatory provision is either an individual duty or a collective duty. As for obligatory gratuitous contributions—whether of property or benefits—their discussion belongs elsewhere.

Financial obligations involving no compensation fall into four categories, mentioned in the transmitted hadith:

“Four deeds absolve whoever performs them of miserliness: paying zakat, showing hospitality to guests, maintaining ties of kinship, and giving in times of need.”

Accordingly, a miser is defined as someone who neglects any one of these four, according to the sounder of the two views held by the scholars of our school, which Abū Bakr and others preferred. Zakat is the regular obligation incurred on account of wealth, comparable to

---

## Page 14441

the obligatory prayers. The other three, however, become obligatory as circumstances arise. Providing hospitality to guests is obligatory according to our school, and al-Shāfiʿī explicitly affirmed this. Maintaining ties of kinship is obligatory by scholarly consensus, as in providing maintenance for relatives, the blood-money payments borne by the group collectively liable for them, and the emancipation of a blood relative within the prohibited degrees of marriage. The disagreement concerns only which relatives one must maintain ties with and the extent of that obligation. Likewise, giving in times of need is obligatory, as in supporting jihad in Allah’s cause, feeding the hungry, and clothing the naked. Ahmad explicitly stated that if someone asking for help were truthful, whoever turned him away would not prosper.

As for obligatory services rendered without compensation, these include teaching knowledge, enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, and helping the oppressed. They are very numerous. Most such obligations involve the use of one’s bodily faculties. These are covered by the authentic hadiths narrated by Abū Dharr, Abū Mūsā, and others:

“Charity is due for every joint of a son of Adam.”

They also fall within the broad meaning of zakat and spending, as in His words:

“And they spend out of what We have provided for them.”

An interpretation to this effect has been transmitted from the early generations, including al-Hasan al-Basrī and others. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every good deed is charity.”

It is also narrated that no servant has ever given any charity greater than an exhortation he gives to companions of his, who then depart having benefited from it through Allah. There is abundant evidence for this, but this is not the place to discuss it.

As for benefits derived from property, an example is someone compelled by necessity to use another person’s property, such as a rope and bucket with which to draw water he needs, a garment with which to keep warm against the cold, or something similar. It must be made available to him; but must it be provided free of charge or in return for compensation

---

## Page 14442

as with the property itself? There are two views on this. There are several grounds for requiring it to be provided free of charge, including His words, Exalted is He:

“And withhold simple assistance.”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, Ibn Masʿūd said: “We used to understand this as lending a cooking pot, a bucket, or an axe.” Likewise, a neighbour must be allowed to use one’s wall when he needs to, according to our school’s principle, which follows the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. There are other such instances as well.

In short, the wealth, use of property, or personal services that must be provided without compensation are discussed in detail elsewhere, even though many students of jurisprudence have an inadequate understanding of this, to the point that they may deny obligations explicitly imposed by the Sharia. Some mistakenly believe that the statement, “There is no financial obligation other than zakat,” applies universally. They are unaware that the hadith narrated by al-Tirmidhī from Fātimah states:

“There is indeed a financial obligation other than zakat.”

Whoever makes the former statement means the financial obligation that arises from possessing wealth itself and is therefore a standing obligation. Beyond that, we know as a self-evident teaching of Islam that Allah has made spending wealth obligatory in situations other than the prescribed zakat: for example, jihad through financial contributions when needed, spending on Hajj, and maintaining one’s wife, relatives, slaves, and animals. These also include obligatory expiations involving emancipation and charitable giving

---

## Page 14443

and the sacrificial offerings required as expiation for Hajj, as well as expiations for oaths, killing, and other matters. These obligations also include fulfilling financial vows and similar duties. Indeed, wealth is fully subject to obligations prescribed by the Sharia, whether recurring or incidental, and whether occasioned by the individual’s own actions or otherwise. This is not the place to explain this general point in detail.

Our concern here is with transactions for compensation that are obligatory, such as sales, leases, and similar arrangements, as well as partnerships, such as tending trees for a share of their yield, cultivating land for a share of its crops, and the like.

This is a matter in which people frequently err. The Sharia firmly establishes that injustice is forbidden and that, as a general rule, these contracts are permissible only by mutual consent, except in cases exempted by the Lawgiver, where compelling people to enter into them is rightful. Consequently, two groups fall into error: some regard compulsion to enter into certain contracts as rightful when it is wrongful, while others regard it as wrongful when it is rightful. In some cases, compulsion rests on an interpretation of what is right, and thus falls within the realm of matters subject to juristic judgment—whether that judgment rests solely on juristic reasoning or is mixed with personal inclination. The same applies to transactions for compensation.

We know with certainty that if providing wealth or benefits without compensation is obligatory under the Sharia in very many circumstances, for reasons that give rise to such an obligation—and this is not injustice, which consists in taking another’s rightful due without right—

---

## Page 14444

then it is all the more appropriate that providing wealth and benefits in return for compensation should be obligatory in certain circumstances. Indeed, obligations to enter into transactions for compensation are more numerous and greater than obligations to give without compensation; they are broader in both extent and nature. Whoever surveys the Sharia may come to see that a transaction for compensation becomes obligatory when Muslims need it, provided it causes no harm outweighing their need. In the absence of such need, however, and where the property owner’s need equals that of the person seeking the exchange, the owner has the stronger claim. Harm is not to be removed by inflicting harm, and a man has a greater right to his wealth than his child, his father, or anyone else.

“Begin with yourself, then those you support.”

This is a sound and fitting principle, supported by much evidence in the Sharia. With Allah Most High’s help, I shall mention some of that evidence.

Transactions for compensation fall into four main categories: exchanging property for property, as in sales; providing wealth in return for a benefit, as in contracts offering a reward for a task; providing a benefit in return for wealth, as in leases and hiring contracts; and providing a benefit in return for a benefit, as in partnerships, including capital-and-labour partnerships and the like, where one party contributes the use of his labour and the other the use of his wealth. Mutual assistance, mutual support, and similar arrangements also fall into this last category.

In short, making transactions for compensation obligatory is a necessity of both worldly life and religion. A person cannot secure his own welfare single-handedly, but must seek the assistance of his fellow human beings. For if human beings were not obliged to provide others with what they need, and others in turn to provide what

---

## Page 14445

they need, people would fall into ruin, as would their worldly and religious affairs. Their welfare can therefore be secured only through transactions for compensation, and these are set right by the justice for which Allah revealed the Books and sent the messengers. Allah Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance, so that people might uphold justice.”

There is no doubt that people are naturally disposed to engage in such exchanges because they need them. Thus, when what is needed is provided without compulsion, the Lawgiver does not prescribe coercion, but leaves both the transaction itself and the amount of compensation to mutual consent. When it is not provided, however, the Lawgiver may sometimes make the transaction obligatory, sometimes require a specified amount of compensation, sometimes require both, and at other times require compensation for a particular individual.

An example of the first case is someone whose debt has been demanded, but who possesses only non-monetary property. He must sell it to repay the debt, for repayment is obligatory and can be accomplished only through the sale. Whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory. The judge may compel him to sell the property to settle his debt, and may sell it on his behalf if he refuses. This is because it is an obligation binding upon him that admits of delegation, so the person vested with authority over the people acts in his place—just as he does in settling debts, arranging the marriage of an unmarried woman to a suitable match at her request, and other such matters, and just as he collects zakah from the person’s wealth. This applies whether the debt arose with the creditor’s consent, as with the price of a purchase or repayment of a loan, or without his consent, as with compensation for destroyed property or bodily injuries.

---

## Page 14446

This also includes liability for wrongfully seized property when the property itself cannot be returned. Entrusted property is likewise treated as wrongfully seized if its custodian betrays his trust. Such trusts include Muslim property entrusted to officials administering fay’ revenues, zakah and charitable endowments; orphans’ property; a principal’s property held by a business partner, a profit-sharing investment manager or the like; and fay’ revenues—when those entrusted with them betray their trust and the property itself cannot be returned. The same applies to selling a person’s property to meet his obligatory maintenance expenses for his wife, his children or himself. In short, whenever someone owes a financial obligation that can be discharged only by selling property, the sale becomes obligatory: he may be compelled to undertake it, and it may be carried out without his consent.

An example of the second case is someone in dire need of another person’s food, whose owner offers it to him for more than its value. He may take it at its fair market value, for the owner is obliged both to sell it and to sell it at that value. If he refuses both requirements, he is compelled to fulfil both; if he agrees to one, he is compelled to fulfil the other. This issue is discussed in the “Book of Foods.” Indeed, if the owner refuses to provide the food, the person in dire need may fight him for it, since he is in the position of someone fighting to defend his own life.

For this reason, we hold those who withhold it liable for his blood money if he dies. It is reported that a man asked some people for water, but they gave him none until he died, so Umar held them liable for his blood money. Ahmad adopted this ruling. For if feeding a person in dire need without payment is obligatory when he cannot afford to pay, then providing food in return for payment is all the more clearly obligatory. The same applies when people face a widespread emergency while some people have surplus food

---

## Page 14447

in storage, they are obliged to sell it, and the ruler must compel them to do so or sell it on their behalf. This is an obligation that may be discharged through a representative, so they must be compelled to fulfil what Islamic law requires of them. It is a right belonging to the Muslims that is in their possession, and must therefore be secured from them.

The same applies to everything people urgently need, such as clothing, weapons and other things their owners can do without: they must make it available at its fair market value. I have previously recorded the hadith of Samurah ibn Jundub concerning the owner of the palm tree whom the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, ordered to sell it, but who refused. I explained how it establishes the obligation to enter into an exchange needed by the buyer when this causes no harm to the seller.

For this reason, the Lawgiver prohibited hoarding that harms people, as in the saying of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Only a sinner hoards.”

This was narrated by Muslim, and there are other reports to this effect. A hoarder is someone who buys for trade; but because he buys what he does not need in a way that harms people, this is forbidden to him. Buying and selling are, in principle, permissible rather than obligatory. Yet people’s needs may make selling obligatory in one instance and buying forbidden in another. This concerns the transaction itself.

As for the amount of the price, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited a town-dweller from selling on behalf of a desert-dweller because of the harm caused to the buyer when the town-dweller acts as a sales agent for someone bringing goods into town while people need those goods. This may be adduced as evidence for

---

## Page 14448

the obligation to sell them at their fair market value. For this reason, he, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Leave people to trade, so that Allah may provide for some through others.”

The same applies when one co-owner sells to the other his share in something that cannot be divided: the co-owner needs the sale in order to receive his share, and the other suffers no harm. Likewise, the Prophet’s having a co-owner’s share valued when the other co-owner frees his own share of a slave: emancipation needs to be completed because partial emancipation causes harm, whereas selling his share either causes the seller no harm or causes harm outweighed by the need to complete the emancipation.

The same applies when another person has a right attached to someone’s property, as when someone owns legally protected improvements—plantings, a building or a well—on another person’s land. Examples include a purchaser when the share he purchased is taken by pre-emption; a seller to whom the item sold is returned because of a defect, when its price had been paid in real estate; and a borrower or tenant when the agreed term expires. If the owner does not remove these improvements, the landowner may purchase them at their value or allow them to remain in return for a fair market rent. Both arrangements involve an exchange: either for the improvements themselves or for the use of his land.

Likewise, compelling one co-owner to join the other in excavation or repair work is compelling him to enter into an exchange. Repair work entails purchasing materials and hiring workers, so this amounts to compelling him to purchase and hire. Since his co-owner needs this and the person making the contribution suffers no harm, he is obliged to enter into the exchange with him—sometimes to enable the property to be divided, and sometimes to preserve its joint ownership. Accordingly, if the Muslims need

---

## Page 14449

trades such as farming, weaving and building, those who practise them must provide their services to them at their fair value, just as they must supply needed property at its fair value. There is no difference between providing property and providing services. Indeed, where providing services causes no harm, there is all the more reason to require their provision in return for compensation. Providing these services is a collective obligation.

A number of scholars, both among our own scholars and others, have stated that the basic trades, such as farming, weaving and building, are a collective obligation.

The precise position is that they are obligatory when needed; when people can do without them, however, they are not obligatory. We have discussed the sale of these services, for those who require their provision require it only in return for compensation, not gratuitously. Thus, this is an obligation to practise a trade for payment because it is needed. I specify “when needed” because Muslims may have no need to practise a trade themselves, owing to the food and clothing they bring in or that are brought to them.

The fundamental principle is that people are obliged to help one another obtain food, clothing and shelter. The ruler may require this and compel people to fulfil it; doing so is not injustice. Indeed, the Lawgiver’s imposition of jihad—which entails risking life and property in order to guide people in their religion—goes much further than any of this. If the courage and generosity that Muslims need are obligatory, how much more so are the exchanges they need? Yet most people do this out of habit, natural inclination and obedience to

---

## Page 14450

the ruler, without being mindful that this constitutes obedience to Allah and His Messenger, and obedience to those in authority in matters in which Allah has commanded obedience to them. For this reason, they regard it as injustice and hardship. If they knew that it was obedience to Allah, they would seek His reward for it, and their aversion would disappear. If they knew that it was obligatory under Islamic law, they would not regard it as injustice.

Likewise, when they need to fight and undertake jihad in person, and they provide funds from the public treasury or elsewhere, jihad remains obligatory under Islamic law when a person is provided with funds, even though it involves risking one’s life and the prospect of harm. The interests of religion cannot be fully secured unless it is obligatory. A person is required to undertake jihad at his own expense; when funds are provided for him, the obligation is all the more binding.

Thus, anyone skilled in warfare—shooting, striking, thrusting or riding—is obliged to take part and is to be compelled to do so, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When you are called to go forth, go forth.”

For this reason, the jurists state that it becomes an individual obligation when the ruler commands it. Likewise, when those undertaking jihad need craftsmen and merchants—such as those who produce food, clothing and weapons, or provide for the needs of horses and the like—and these services are requested of them in return for payment, they are obliged to provide them and are to be compelled to do so.

Likewise, merchants must sell whatever is needed for jihad. And if

---

## Page 14451

the army needs a group of merchants to accompany it to sell food, clothing, weapons and other supplies that the army cannot carry, then trade is like a craft. The army is like a community in a town: just as its members must help one another meet their needs through exchanges that cause no harm, so too is this obligatory within the army. Just as the ruler may make jihad obligatory upon a group and order them to travel somewhere for that purpose, he may also order whatever supports it, direct some people to acquire knowledge, and direct others to assume public office. A just ruler must be obeyed in whatever is not known to constitute disobedience to Allah; an unjust ruler must be obeyed in whatever is known to constitute obedience to Allah, such as jihad.

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

Section

In our view, statements made under wrongful coercion have no legal effect, such as a person’s utterance of disbelief, pronouncement of divorce, or words effecting a sale or purchase. If both parties to a sale are coerced into entering the contract, it is void. If they are coerced into exchanging possession, this is coercion to perform actions, not to make statements; thus each of them will have

---

## Page 14452

received and delivered possession under coercion. Each must therefore return what he received to the other whenever he is able, because it was received without right, even though the recipient was coerced. If the property received under coercion is lost or destroyed while in the recipient’s possession, he is liable if he caused its destruction through his own action, negligence or transgression. For, at most, he holds it in trust, and someone holding property in trust is liable if he destroys it or it is lost through his negligence or transgression. This applies to a lessee, a depositary, a manager of a profit-sharing investment, and an agent.

If, however, it is lost without negligence on his part, does his possession entail liability because he received another person’s property without that person’s permission in order to ward off harm to himself? Or does he hold it in trust because his taking possession of it was not unlawful?

We say: its loss while in the coerced person’s possession is equivalent to his destroying it under coercion, concerning which there is a difference of opinion. In some respects, this resembles a loan for use: the borrower receives the property for his own benefit, just as the coerced person receives it to ward off harm to himself. The former receives it with the owner’s permission, while the latter receives it with the Lawgiver’s permission. If, however, the coerced recipient has taken payment from it toward a debt, he is then liable for it, because it has been applied to his benefit—like someone compelled by necessity to take another person’s food to eat.

---

## Page 14453

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A group of people had their wealth confiscated and were then coerced and compelled to sell assets consisting of real estate, livestock, and orchards. They sold them, although some belonged to the sellers’ children, some were charitable endowments, and some belonged to other people. The purchaser took possession of these assets. The sellers feared for their physical integrity but were unable to recover them from him. They therefore nominally repurchased them, to ascertain their continued existence and safeguard them, for a specified price payable on a fixed date. When payment fell due, he demanded the price from them.

Is their sale invalid because of coercion and because it involved selling property belonging to others, or not? Does their purchase from him, together with their acknowledgment of his ownership, establish his valid ownership?

He replied:

If, in these circumstances, the seller offers to reimburse the purchaser for the price he paid, but the purchaser refuses to settle on that basis and demands the deferred price recorded as payable by the seller, then the purchaser is a wrongdoer and a sinner who deserves punishment. For even if the seller had willingly consented to this transaction, and the two had agreed that the assets were not to be sold to him by means of his first selling them on deferred payment for more than that

---

## Page 14454

price, the transaction would have been invalid and usurious according to the early generations of this Ummah—the Companions and Successors—and most Muslim imams. How much more so, then, when the seller was coerced! A sale made under wrongful coercion is not binding, by agreement of the Muslims. Even if it were further supposed that the purchaser had been compelled to purchase from him and had paid the price on his behalf, then, if the seller reimbursed him for what he had paid on his behalf, the property sold would have to be handed over to the seller, by agreement of the Muslims. How much more so when the purchaser was not compelled to purchase and the seller has offered to reimburse him for what he paid on his behalf! In these circumstances, the purchaser has no right to demand any additional amount, by agreement of the imams, nor to demand the return of the assets that belonged to the seller and are now in his possession, as stated.

He was asked:

A man’s mother died, and he inherited from her a house in which she had no co-owner. Someone wronged his father and coerced him until he entered into a written agreement with him concerning the house or sold it to him. Is that permissible, or must the house be restored to its owner?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he was wrongfully coerced into selling the house, the sale is invalid. The house must be restored to its owner, and the purchaser must be refunded the price taken from him. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14455

He was asked:

“There is an endowment for a group of people, established by the testimony of upright witnesses. The house is occupied by someone who wields considerable power over the heirs. They subjected him to coercion until they sold it by force, through the exercise of superior power. If witnesses testify that the endowment is valid, should it be taken from the usurper or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. A sale made under wrongful coercion is invalid, as is the sale of a valid, binding endowment. Whoever has knowledge of a matter should testify to it. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

If a ruler, thieves, or others wrongfully compel a man to pay money, and compel another man to lend him money or purchase something from him, and the latter pays the price on his behalf or to him so that they may take the money from the borrower or seller—whether the coercion requires handing the money over to the coerced man and then taking it from him, or merely paying it on his behalf:

---

## Page 14456

such cases occur frequently, and there are two juristic views concerning them. The same applies when a ruler takes from one of two owners whose livestock are combined more than the amount due from them both, without any basis in legal interpretation.

The first view is that the owner alone bears the loss of that excess, and the other owes no part of it, even if the ruler took it on the other’s behalf. For the wrongdoer wronged this owner by taking his property and intended it as a payment on the other’s behalf, yet he is neither the other’s guardian nor his agent for that intention to have legal effect. An unlawful intention alone does not establish a financial liability against the person on whose behalf the property was taken.

A consequence of this view concerns partners in real estate or movable property when the ruler and his deputies exact unjust levies on the property, or highway robbers take something from one of two merchant partners on account of the property they carry. For, if what is seized comes from the jointly owned property itself, one partner has no recourse against the other for his share.

According to this view, if the person making the payment is an agent or guardian, such as an endowment administrator or an orphan’s guardian, it follows that he must bear the loss if what was taken from him did not come from the property itself. However, this consequence applies only if the payment was not made to preserve the property, but because he was coerced into paying.

If, however, the property could not be preserved without the payment he made on its account, then this is an act undertaken to preserve it. It is comparable to paying for protection to safeguard the property, or paying watchmen to ward off thieves and wild beasts. Moreover, guardians and agents are authorized by customary practice to make such payments, for he

---

## Page 14457

did not undertake the agency on the understanding that he would be beaten and imprisoned over money demanded on account of the property, suffering harm rather than paying it. The case of an unrelated third party is different. Yet this very argument also applies to either partner, since each acts as the other’s agent in a contractual partnership. A distinction should also be made between governmental levies and incidental acts of oppression.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man sent some cloth with another man to deliver to his son in Cairo. The man entrusted with carrying it did not deliver it, but instead sold it and disposed of it. He sold it to someone other than a cloth merchant, for payment other than the customary currency, without the cloth’s owner authorizing him to do so. Does this constitute negligence? If he was negligent, must he pay its value? Whose account of its loss is to be accepted: that of the cloth’s owner or that of the man entrusted with carrying it? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He answered:

If he disposed of it without its owner’s permission, he acted wrongfully and became liable for it. If it can no longer be recovered, he must pay its value. If the custodian says, “You instructed me to sell it,” while the owner says, “I did not instruct you to sell it, but to deliver it to my son,” there is scholarly disagreement concerning this.

However, if he sold it in a manner departing from customary sales—for example, on deferred payment, for something other than the currency of the locality, or to an ignorant or insolvent person, or the like—then he is liable in all cases for any loss of the purchase price.

---

## Page 14458

Likewise, if he sold it for less than its fair market value and handed it over, he is liable for the shortfall. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman granted a property to her son, who then sold it. After the sale, she granted it to a second person, and a deed was drawn up against the first stating that he had no interest in the property after having sold it. Must the first return the property to the second, or is the first transaction valid?

He answered:

If he sold it through a valid, binding sale, it ceased to belong to him, and her subsequent transfer of ownership was invalid. The property remains the buyer’s. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man’s wife owned a property. Her husband stole its title deeds and sold the property; she subsequently died.

He answered:

Selling property without its owner’s permission and without lawful authority over it is a sale

---

## Page 14459

that is void. He must return to the purchaser the price he paid and restore the property to its owner.

He also said:

Section

The objection to purchasing land subject to kharāj (land tax) arose only because the purchaser would buy it and remove its kharāj liability, thereby extinguishing a right belonging to the Muslims. Likewise, they would sometimes allocate some of this land to certain soldiers as grants of ownership, rather than grants of revenue, as with grants of uncultivated land. Such use and allocation extinguish the Muslims’ rights to both the land itself and its benefits. The caliphs took it from the conquering troops so that its benefits would accrue perpetually to the Muslims. If those benefits are withheld from the Muslims, this becomes an injustice against them, comparable to usurping a public road used by the Muslims or building in Minā and similar places whose benefits are shared by the Muslims in perpetuity.

If, however, someone purchases the land while remaining liable for the same kharāj as the seller, it is as though the seller had assigned it to him without payment, or as though he had inherited it. For there is consensus concerning inheritance: the heir has the prior right to the land, subject to kharāj.

For concerning the granting of such land to someone subject to kharāj, it has been said:

---

## Page 14460

it is a sale with a price payable in perpetual instalments, as some Kufan jurists maintain.

It has also been said that it is a perpetual lease with rent payable in instalments, as maintained by the scholars of our school, the Mālikīs and the Shāfiʿīs. Both positions depart from the analogical rules governing sales and leases.

The correct analysis is that this is a distinct transaction sharing features of both sale and lease. In falling outside both categories, it resembles an agreement granting the use of someone’s property as a passageway, for depositing refuse, for placing a beam, or for similar purposes, in exchange for an immediate payment. The recipient has neither acquired outright ownership of the property itself nor leased it; rather, he has acquired this particular right of use in perpetuity.

Likewise, if the imposition of kharāj were purely a lease, and ʿUmar and others had retained the land for the Muslims and leased it out, then the dwellings should also have been leased out, since they too belong to the Muslims when conquered by force. It would also mean that he had wronged the Muslims, for the rental value of the land is many times the kharāj. Moreover, according to the prevailing view among those scholars, the recipient would be entitled only to the trees standing on the land—date palms, grapevines and the like—as with someone who leases land containing plantings.

Furthermore, entrusting the land under agreements to share the produce of its trees and crops—as al-Mansūr and al-Mahdī did with the land of al-Sawād—would have been more beneficial to the Muslims, following the Prophet’s practice, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the land of Khaybar. For the only difference is that the owners of Khaybar are specified individuals, whereas the owners of the conquered ʿumrā lands are not restricted to specified individuals. Apart from that, it is likewise permissible to lease the land out, and permissible to enter into agreements to share the produce of trees and crops on land held as an endowment.

---

## Page 14461

As for selling it, if that were the nature of the transaction, he would also have sold the dwellings. Moreover, no sale has a price that remains payable perpetually until the Day of Resurrection. The resulting arrangement is an independent legal category established by the Sunnah and consensus, and is not to be judged by analogy with other transactions. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Iraq has withheld its qafīz and its dirham; Syria has withheld its mudd and its dinar; and Egypt has withheld its irdabb and its dinar.”

The Companions also agreed with ʿUmar on what he did.

This is further clarified by the fact that the basis of kharāj lies in His words:

“Whatever Allah has granted His Messenger from the people of the towns.”

This distinguishes immovable from movable property. Nevertheless, He attributed the towns to their inhabitants, thus indicating their particular entitlement to them. Accordingly, if one dhimmī—a non-Muslim under Muslim protection—took the land over from its original dhimmī holder, assuming its kharāj and paying him compensation for the transfer, this would cause no harm whatsoever. There is therefore no reason to prohibit it.

For even if it is said to be an endowment, this exchange does not cause it to cease being an endowment. Rather, the entitlement of its beneficiaries remains unchanged. Selling endowed property is prohibited only because it extinguishes the beneficiaries’ rights, whereas here those rights remain intact.

Rather, this resembles subletting endowed land for more than one rented it for. It is as though the holder said: “I lease you this land in return for your paying the kharāj incumbent upon me, together with the additional sum you pay me in advance.” This is also why it passes to the heirs of the person holding it, although endowed property may neither be sold, given away nor inherited. If it may pass by inheritance while retaining its existing status—and a gift is no different—then the same applies to a transfer for compensation, whether called a sale or a lease. This is why Ahmad permitted land subject to kharāj to be given as a dowry; and whatever may lawfully

---

## Page 14462

serve as a dowry may also serve as a price or rent. Whatever may be given as a price may also be sold. This is therefore a matter that deserves careful consideration.

There remains the question of a Muslim acquiring it: is this disliked because of the humiliation it entails, or because farming distracts him from jihad? These are separate considerations, unrelated to its status as an endowment, and their rulings vary according to circumstances and the interests served. For example:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with the Jews concerning Khaybar because the Muslims were few. When the Muslims became numerous, Umar expelled them in accordance with the Prophet’s command, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Muslims began cultivating it.”

Likewise, when Muslims become numerous, their taking possession of land subject to kharāj and paying its kharāj is more beneficial to them than remaining poor and needy while the disbelievers derive income from the land in return for a small kharāj payment. In Umar’s time, the Muslims were few, while the non-Muslims under Muslim protection were numerous. The situation may be reversed.

Moreover, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made an agreement with them concerning Khaybar, but the Muslims subsequently cultivated it themselves once they became numerous and suffered harm from the continued presence of non-Muslims under Muslim protection in the land of the Arabs. The underlying considerations were thus the harm their presence caused the Muslims and the Muslims’ ability to meet their needs through one another.

How much more so, then, if Muslims need land subject to kharāj and suffer harm from its remaining in the hands of non-Muslims under Muslim protection, so that a Muslim in need decides to compensate its non-Muslim holder and take his place on it? If the payment constitutes rent, the Muslim has a greater right to lease and cultivate the Muslims’ land; and if it constitutes a purchase price, he has a greater right to buy it

---

## Page 14463

and if it is a third kind of payment, he has a greater right to it as well. Once the Muslims become numerous, neither humiliation nor jizyah remains; the humiliation and jizyah associated with it belonged to earlier times. Similarly, if the non-Muslim under Muslim protection who holds the land embraces Islam, it remains in his possession and he continues paying its kharāj, while his personal jizyah—the poll tax—is waived. How, then, can the two situations be treated as equivalent? If the land may remain in his possession after he embraces Islam, what prevents him from transferring it to another Muslim, with or without compensation? A Muslim is under no circumstances subject to humiliation. If the impediment were that this payment constituted humiliation, it could not coexist with Islam, just as the poll tax cannot.

Nor may it be argued: “It is like slavery: Islam prevents its initial imposition but does not prevent its continuation.” For we imposed slavery upon them against their will; we did not enter into an exchange with them for it. The poll tax is similar: we do not allow them to remain in Muslim territory without paying it. It is thus a form of servitude, since it is imposed without the consent of the person subjected to it. Kharāj, however, becomes binding only with the consent and choice of the person undertaking to pay it. If he did not accept the land from us, we would not hand it over to him.

It is exactly like the orchard- and crop-sharing arrangements which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded with the people of Khaybar. There, the payment was a share of the crop; here, it is a specified, known amount. There, payment becomes due only if they cultivate the land; here, it becomes due when they are able to cultivate it. A parallel case is that of a cultivator under a crop-sharing arrangement engaging someone else for less than the share to which he himself is entitled. For an investment manager may entrust the capital to another under a profit-sharing arrangement. This, however, requires the owner’s permission, because the entitled party has been specifically designated.

---

## Page 14464

In sum, the impediments connected with its being an endowment require examination. Its status as an endowment, however, can never in itself be regarded as an impediment under the principles of Islamic law. As for the argument that cultivation distracts people from jihad, this applies to all lands, whether subject to the tithe or to kharāj, and that is a separate issue.

Section

Mecca presents a parallel case. There is no doubt that it was conquered by force. The view that it was acquired by treaty, thereby confirming its owners’ title and permitting them to sell it and otherwise dispose of it as with other lands acquired by treaty—as al-Shāfi‘ī maintains—is weak on numerous grounds established by transmitted reports.

Moreover, such an arrangement is not permissible. If the Muslim ruler concludes a treaty with a group of polytheists without imposing either jizyah or kharāj, this is permissible only where there is a need, “as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did in the year of al-Hudaybiyah.” But if we acquire land by treaty whose inhabitants are polytheists outside the categories from whom jizyah may be accepted, then, by the consensus of the Muslims, they may not be allowed to remain without paying jizyah.

Furthermore, in the following year, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stipulated

---

## Page 14465

when Abū Bakr performed the pilgrimage, a four-month term for those among them who had not embraced Islam; otherwise, he would regard them as belligerents whose blood could lawfully be shed and whose property could lawfully be seized. Had he conquered Mecca by treaty, it would not have been permissible to break that treaty. Moreover, he deemed it lawful to kill a number of people whom he named . . . (1)

Rather, he conquered it by force and granted safe-conduct to those among them who ceased fighting, thereby guaranteeing the safety of their persons and property, apart from a few whom he expressly excluded. He had sent this offer of safe-conduct through Abū Sufyān. Some accepted it, and it became binding in their case; others refused it and either fought or fled. Like a truce, safe-conduct takes effect only when accepted by its intended recipient. As for those who did not cease fighting, he granted them no safe-conduct whatsoever. His declarations of safe-conduct included both specific and general formulations, but their intended meaning was the same.

His words:

“Whoever enters the Mosque is safe; whoever enters his own house is safe; whoever lays down his weapons is safe; and whoever enters the house of Abū Sufyān is safe”

all mean that whoever surrendered and refrained from fighting was safe. This is why he called them “those set free,” as though he had taken them captive and then released them all . . . (2)

The Hanafīs said: Since he conquered Mecca by force but did not divide it, instead leaving it in the hands of its inhabitants, this established a precedent permitting land conquered by force to be left in the hands of its inhabitants.

They, our scholars, and others offered, as one of two explanations, the following: This is why its sale and leasing were impermissible: it had been conquered by force and left undivided, like other land conquered by force. They sometimes say: The accommodation that the people of Mecca provided for others constituted the kharāj levied

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) A blank of approximately one line in the original.
> 
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 223):
> 
> Two points arise here:
> 
> First, the compiler’s placement of [faqad ammanahu (he has thus granted him safe-conduct)] in square brackets indicates that these words are his addition. They should be omitted: the passage reads coherently without them but becomes confused with them. Allah knows best.
> 
> Second, two lacunae are indicated here, each approximately one line long—as the compiler, may Allah have mercy on him, noted. The passage argues that Mecca was conquered by force, and the missing text concerns how two pieces of evidence support that conclusion:
> 
> The first is the authorization to kill certain people in the year of the conquest.
> 
> The second is the granting of safe-conduct to those who surrendered and refrained from fighting.
> 
> Ibn al-Qayyim, may Allah have mercy on him, discusses both pieces of evidence and their significance in *Zād al-Ma‘ād*, 3:108–110. The following passages address these two points and clarify the meaning of the missing text:
> 
> 1 — “Moreover, he ordered the killing of Maqīs ibn Subāba, Ibn Khatal, and two slave women. Had Mecca been taken by treaty, he would not have ordered the killing of any of its inhabitants, and these individuals would have been expressly excluded from the treaty. Furthermore, *al-Sunan* records, through an authentic chain of transmission, that on the day of the conquest of Mecca, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Grant everyone safe-conduct except two women and four men. Kill them even if you find them clinging to the coverings of the Kaaba.’”
> 
> 2 — “No one has ever reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded a treaty with its inhabitants at the time of the conquest, or that any of them came to him and negotiated a treaty concerning the city. Rather, Abū Sufyān came to him, and he granted him safe-conduct for whoever entered his house, closed his door, entered the Mosque, or laid down his weapons. Had Mecca been taken by treaty, he would not have said, ‘Whoever enters his house, closes his door, or enters the Mosque is safe,’ since a treaty entails general safe-conduct.”

---

## Page 14466

on them. As for those among our scholars who hold that kharāj is to be levied on its farmland, the falsity of their view is established by mass-transmitted reports, even though it follows consistently from their analogical reasoning.

This reasoning is weak for several reasons:

First: Land conquered by force may be leased by consensus. Yet the soundest view concerning the houses of Mecca is that they may not be leased; rather, they must be made available without payment to those who need them. This is what the Quran, the Sunnah, the transmitted reports, and analogy indicate. As for prohibiting their sale, that is open to question. If conquest by force were the reason for the prohibition, it would not preclude leasing them.

Second: In the case of land conquered by force, the prohibition on sale applies only to its farmland. It does not apply to dwellings; rather, these belong to their owners. In Mecca, however, the prohibition on transactions for payment concerns its residential properties, whereas no such prohibition applies to residential properties on land conquered by force. This clearly demonstrates the distinction.

Third: I know of none among our scholars or others who has prohibited the sale or leasing of Mecca’s farmland. The discussion concerns only its residential properties—that is, its dwellings, not its farmland. How different the two cases are!

Fourth: Those houses had belonged to the Emigrants, who asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to restore them to them, but he did not do so. Had they been like other property conquered by force,

---

## Page 14467

he would have restored them to their owners; for if land belonging to Muslims is seized by the unbelievers and we subsequently recover it, it is restored to its owner if he is identified before its division.

Fifth: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not seize any of their property, whether movable or immovable, nor any of their children. Had he applied to Mecca the rulings governing other territories conquered by force, he would have taken their movable property and children as spoils. Rather, the correct view is that leasing its properties is prohibited because it is the land of the pilgrimage rites, which all Muslims share the right to use. As Allah Most High says:

“The resident and the visitor are equal therein.”

Its residents have priority over what they need because they were the first to occupy what is open to all, just as someone may be the first to occupy an available space in a mosque, on a road, or in a market. As for any space beyond their needs, they must make it freely available, since they are entitled to build only on this condition; the underlying plot is common property.

This is like someone who builds a room within a lodge, a school, or a similar institution: he has a particular right to reside in it, but no right to take payment for it. The same applies to someone who builds a house beside a road or within a frontier garrison, and to similar cases where the land is for shared use—for pilgrimage, jihad, passage along the roads, learning, worship, or the like.

If he says, “The building is mine,” he is told: “The plot is not yours, nor are the stones themselves; rather, what belongs to you is

---

## Page 14468

the work of construction, or that work together with the salvaged building materials. You may not take payment for what is not yours; as for what is yours, you have already been compensated for it through your continued use of the plot.”

Alternatively, since people bring sacrificial offerings to the Meccans and are obliged to distribute them among them, the Meccans in turn are obliged to lodge people in their homes, repaying kindness with kindness. The person bringing a sacrificial offering may, for example, eat from it where this is permissible and give it to whomever he wishes, but may not take payment for it. Likewise, the owner of a house may live in it and accommodate others in it, but may not take payment for it.

This consideration we have mentioned may itself be the reason why these properties were left in their owners’ possession without any land tax being imposed upon them. Those residing in Mecca have both rights and obligations; Mecca is unlike other cities. From this perspective, its conquest by force becomes a relevant reason for prohibiting the leasing of its properties—as we have explained—not a basis for treating it like other land conquered by force.

If it is objected:

“When a territory is conquered by force, its inhabitants may likewise be granted protection for their lives and property.”

The reply is:

“Yes, before control is secured, someone who ceases fighting may be granted protection for his life and property, because of the benefit gained from his ceasing to fight. This is a conditional grant of protection. Indeed, if we permit a prisoner to be enslaved after capture when this serves the public interest, how could we not permit that before capture?”

---

## Page 14469

And here protection extends to his property as well, because this occurs before control has been secured. Likewise, if people surrender to an arbiter’s judgment, anyone among them who embraces Islam before judgment is delivered secures protection for his life and property, since their subjugation is not yet complete. His entry into Mecca was before noon, and he entered it by force. This clarification reveals the ambiguity that gave rise to each of the two views.

Once people have been subdued, however, they may be released as an act of grace, and the land may be handed over to them subject to land tax. As for those who fought in Mecca or fled, and whom he subsequently granted protection after subduing them and gaining power over them, granting them personal protection was permissible, just as releasing them as an act of grace was permissible. Hence he called them “the freed ones.” As for their property, I have already explained the reason for treating their land in this way. As for movable property and children… (1).

He was asked:

About a land-grant holder who has water within his grant and wishes to sell it to another land-grant holder and channel it into the latter’s locality: may the buyer purchase this water? And may the seller sell the water within his grant and channel it into the buyer’s locality?

He replied:

If the water is reserved for him as part of the grant—for example, if the land together with its water is reserved for him at a thousand dirhams, but without that reservation at five hundred

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 14470

dirhams—and he wishes to forgo the cultivation to which he is entitled and sell the water to someone else to irrigate the latter’s land, then this water may be sold. This differs from water that flows through his property without payment, as when someone brings uncultivated land into use and it contains a flowing spring.

Scholars hold two views on whether water of this kind may be sold, both transmitted from Ahmad. According to the first, it may be sold; this is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Mālik. According to the second, it may not be sold; this is the position of Abū Hanīfa and the well-known position transmitted from Ahmad. As for water and pasture found on land open to common use, scholars agree that neither may be sold.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man owns a flowing spring. May he sell one “finger” of its water, or a similar share, even though the water is not visible but emerges little by little?

He replied:

Someone who owns water issuing from the ground—for example, someone who owns a well dug on his property (the term “well” includes both those fitted with a waterwheel and those without one), or who owns a spring on land belonging to him—may sell the well

---

## Page 14471

and the spring outright. He may also sell an undivided share of it amounting to one or two “fingers”—out of twenty-four—just as an orchard or house may be sold together with its water entitlement, such as one or more “fingers” from a particular channel. This applies even if the water feeding that channel originates underground; how much more so, then, if the source of the water is on his own property. I know of no disagreement concerning this.

Even if the spring’s water emerges only gradually, a sale does not require that the buyer see everything being sold; rather, he need only see what is customarily inspected. As for what is continually renewed, such as benefits of use and the water accumulating in a well, no scholar requires that it be seen, whether in a sale or a lease.
