The scholars disagree only about selling the water separately from the ground containing it: is the sale valid because the water is owned, or invalid because it is not owned? There are two well-known views, both reported from Ahmad. Most scholars permit its sale. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik, and al-Shāfiʿī explicitly stated it; indeed, he explicitly stated that water is owned. They also disagreed over whether water is included when someone sells land without mentioning it.

As for selling a well or spring in its entirety, or selling a share of it, I know of no disagreement concerning this when the land is privately owned. Indeed, “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encouraged the purchase of the well of Rūma from its Jewish owner, and ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān bought

---

## Page 14472

half of it and endowed it for the Muslims, with the same right to draw water from it as any other Muslim. When the Jew saw this, he sold him the other half; ʿUthmān bought it and endowed the entire well for the Muslims.”

The jurists have cited this hadith as evidence for several rulings, including the validity of endowing an undivided share. They have also discussed whether a right of pre-emption applies in such cases. Most jurists hold that it does, including Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him. This is also one of the two positions in Mālik’s school and was the view preferred by Ibn Surayj among al-Shāfiʿī’s followers. However, the well-known position reported from al-Shāfiʿī is that no right of pre-emption applies. This is also the other report from Ahmad, preferred by many of his followers. The stronger view is that the right of pre-emption does apply in such cases. The point here is that they agreed that such property may lawfully be sold; the permissibility of giving it as a gift is even clearer than that of selling it.

He was asked about two men who held a land grant in a certain locality and disputed whether vegetation that grows naturally by Allah’s will could be sold.

One maintained that it was like wild vegetation and could not lawfully be sold because it was not his property.

The other replied: “Rather, it may be sold, for the ruler granted it to me, so it is my property. I may sell everything within my share and my allotted portion.” Are

---

## Page 14473

they both right, or both wrong? What are the positions of the imams on this matter?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Vegetation that grows without human intervention—such as herbage that Allah causes to grow on land a person owns or leases, or the like—may not be sold according to the school of Abū Hanīfa and the well-known position reported from Ahmad. This is also the view of some followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“People share in three things: water, herbage, and fire.”

It is clear that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not mean only what grows on land open to common use. People share in everything found on such land, of every kind, including fluid mineral substances such as pitch and petroleum, and solid minerals such as gold, silver, salt, and others. Thus, it is understood that he meant what grows on a person’s own land.

Furthermore, it is established in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are three whom Allah will neither speak to nor look at on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them, and theirs will be a painful punishment: a man who has surplus water but withholds it from a wayfarer, to whom Allah will say, ‘Today I withhold My bounty from you, just as you withheld the surplus of what your own hands did not produce’; a man who pledges allegiance to a ruler solely for worldly gain—if the ruler gives him something, he is pleased, but if he withholds it, he is displeased; and a man who falsely swears, after the afternoon prayer, that he has been offered more for an item of merchandise than

---

## Page 14474

he was actually offered.”

Allah threatened this man with punishment for withholding the surplus of what his own hands had not produced. Herbage that grows without a person’s intervention is likewise not produced by his hands. The well-known position in al-Shāfiʿī’s school permits its sale. This is also the well-known position in Mālik’s school regarding land that its owner customarily uses. As for uncultivated land that he does not till, Mālik’s followers differ: Ibn al-Qāsim permitted this, while others prohibited it. If, however, its owner deliberately leaves it unsown so that herbage will grow there, selling such herbage is less objectionable than selling other herbage, since this amounts to cultivating it.

He also said in another response:

As for his words:

“People share in three things: water, herbage, and fire.”

This is a well-known hadith reported by the compilers of the Sunan. Muslims agree that herbage growing on land open to common use is shared by all, and whoever reaches it first has the greater right to it. As for herbage growing on privately owned land, if the landowner needs it, he has the greater right to it. If he has no need of it, scholars hold two well-known views. Most permit taking it without payment, on the basis of this hadith, and permit grazing animals on it without payment.

---

## Page 14475

Likewise, water that springs from land open to common use is shared by all. If it springs from someone’s private property, he must provide its surplus without payment to those who need drinking water for themselves or their animals. This is based on the preceding hadith and on the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him) in the authentic hadith:

“There are three whom Allah will neither speak to nor look at on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them, and theirs will be a painful punishment: a man who has surplus water but denies it to a wayfarer—Allah will say to him, ‘Today I withhold My bounty from you, just as you withheld the surplus of what your own hands had not produced’; a man who pledges allegiance to a ruler solely for worldly gain, pleased if the ruler gives him something and displeased if he withholds it; and a man who sets out his merchandise after the afternoon prayer and says, ‘By Allah, besides whom there is no deity, I have been offered such and such for it.’”

The hadith continues. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Some people move their bees from one locality to another. Is it lawful for the inhabitants of a locality to charge them for what their bees gather there?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The owners of the land from which the bees gather have no claim against the beekeepers, since this does not diminish their property in any way. Rather, honey comes from dew, which is freely available for common use. The beekeeper must pay one-tenth, distributing it to

---

## Page 14476

those entitled to it, according to many scholars, including Abū Hanīfah and others, on the basis of reports concerning this from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions. This dew has an even greater claim than pasture to be freely available for gathering, since only bees can gather it. However, if the landowner has bees, his bees have a prior right to forage on his land. If the foraging of those other bees harms him, he may prohibit it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman owns property elsewhere which she has not seen but knows by description. She then sells it to someone who has seen it. Is this sale valid?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If she knows it by description, her sale is valid. Likewise, if her agent appointed to sell it has seen it, the sale is valid even if she has neither seen it nor had it described to her.

He was asked:

What of a man who needed a loan, and another person had broad beans which they agreed to buy and sell, although the purchaser had not seen them: he drew up the deed, then discovered that they were infested with weevils?

---

## Page 14477

He answered:

If he neither saw the goods nor had them described to him, the sale is invalid, and he must make restitution with their equivalent or their value.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man with a family to support wishes to sell himself. Is that permissible?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. As for a sale recognized by Islamic law, a free Muslim cannot be sold. However, he may attach himself to a king or emir, calling himself that ruler’s slave, while the king or emir counts him among his slaves whom he emancipates, without acquiring ownership of him as an actual slave. This resembles the original master’s ownership.

What these people practise is merely a sale according to custom, with the terminology likewise governed by custom. Most mamluks belong to the public treasury, and their bond of patronage is with the Muslims. However, they are attributed to whoever gains power, just as funds and similar things are attributed to him.

There is nothing wrong with a person attaching himself to someone who gives him his due from the Muslims’ public treasury, just as he was previously attached to someone else. They must obey whoever Allah has placed in authority over them in matters consistent with obedience to Allah, but must not obey anyone in disobedience to Allah. Thus, one form of ownership resembles another. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14478

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A slave belonging to a Muslim residing in the lands of the Tatars fled from his master there and came to Syria, while still enslaved. He now wishes to be sold. Is it permissible for someone to sell him, safeguard the proceeds for his master, and remit them to him?

He answered:

Yes, that is permissible if returning to those lands would harm him in his religion or worldly affairs. In that case, he may be sold here without his master’s permission. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A disbeliever living in the lands of the disbelievers owed a debt. He sold himself to a Muslim, received the price, and used it to repay his debt. He also sold his daughter, and they consented to being enslaved. The Muslim merchant bore the costs of their journey, maintenance, and clothing until they reached the lands of Islam. Is it permissible to sell and buy them?

---

## Page 14479

He answered:

If a Muslim enters a territory at war with the Muslims without a guarantee of safe-conduct, purchases their children from them, and brings them into the territory of Islam, they become his property by agreement of the imams. He may sell them to Muslims, who may purchase them from him, and he is entitled to receive the full price from the purchaser. Likewise, if a non-Muslim of that belligerent territory sells himself to the Muslim, who then brings him out, he becomes his property with even greater reason. Indeed, even if they gave him their children without payment and he brought them out, he would own them; how much more so if they sold them to him. The same applies if he abducted them or their children, or subdued them by any means. For the persons and property of disbelievers at war with the Muslims are lawful for Muslims to seize; when they take possession of them by a lawful means, they acquire ownership of them.

The scholars differed only concerning someone who had entered under a guarantee of safe-conduct: may he purchase their children from them? There are two opinions within the schools of Abū Hanīfa and Mālik. According to one report from Ahmad, purchasing from them is permissible. Indeed, Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad—in an explicitly recorded report from him—held that if the Muslims conclude a truce with the inhabitants of a town, and someone takes those inhabitants captive and sells them to the Muslims, it is permissible to purchase them from him. Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī disagreed with him on this in the other report. Likewise, if the people of a belligerent territory subjugate one another, or give one another away as gifts

---

## Page 14480

or buy one another, or someone abducts them and sells them or gives them to Muslims, the Muslims acquire ownership of them, just as they do when they acquire them by force.

He was asked:

A man purchased a slave who served him for several years. Then, when his master intended to sell him, he claimed that he was free, although he had acknowledged being a slave at the time of purchase. Must his purchase price be recovered from the person who sold him? And is he to be set free from his master?

He answered:

If it is established that he was free, he must be held financially liable to the man who sold him and for the deception he practised, since he had acknowledged to him that he was a slave. The purchaser may demand repayment of the price from the person who received it from him, and he may also demand it from this recipient who deceived him.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is the sale of walnuts, almonds, hazelnuts, pistachios, broad beans and chickpeas still in their shells or pods valid according to the school of al-Shāfi‘ī? According to his school, are buying and selling valid without verbally expressing the contractual agreement? And is the sale of turnips, walnuts and taro valid while they remain hidden underground, or not?

---

## Page 14481

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Al-Shāfiʿī’s explicitly stated position is that these sales are not permissible. The majority of scholars, however, hold otherwise, and theirs is the correct view.

As for the first type of sale, the three imams—Mālik, Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad—as well as other scholars hold that it is valid. This has also been reported as a view of al-Shāfiʿī, for during his final illness he purchased fresh green broad beans. This has been the established practice since the time of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his Companions and the Successors, throughout all periods and lands.

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade selling grain until it hardened, and selling grapes until they darkened.”

This indicates that grain may be sold once it has hardened, even while still in its ears. Yet according to those who prohibit selling broad beans in their pods, this would not be permissible. Hence al-Tarsūsī and others counted the prohibition of selling broad beans among newly introduced innovations, for none of the early generations is known to have prohibited it.

Those who prohibit it base their argument on:

“The Prophet’s prohibition—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—of sales involving uncertainty.”

They therefore supposed that the goods in question were unknown. But this is not so: these goods can be assessed just like other goods offered for sale, whose whole is judged by inspecting a part.

Likewise, the well-known position in al-Shāfiʿī’s school is that contracts require verbal formulas, so a sale concluded by exchanging goods and payment without a verbal agreement is not valid. The majority, however, disagree. Mālik’s position is that whatever people regard as a sale is a sale. Thus, sale by such an exchange is permissible in small transactions

---

## Page 14482

and large ones. This is also the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. Abū Hanīfah’s position permits this in transactions involving items of little value. This is also another view within Ahmad’s school and the view of a group of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

Moreover, contracts are governed by people’s customary usage. Whatever people regard as a sale, a lease or a gift is accordingly a sale, a lease or a gift, for these terms have no fixed definition in either language or Islamic law. Any term without a fixed definition in language or Islamic law is defined by customary usage.

As for selling produce concealed underground, such as carrots, turnips and taro, Mālik’s position permits it, and this is also one view within Ahmad’s school. Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī, along with Ahmad according to his well-known position, hold that it is impermissible. The first view—that selling such produce is permissible—is more correct. When experienced people see its visible leaves and other parts, these indicate to them the condition of the rest.

Furthermore, people need these transactions, and the Lawgiver does not prohibit sales that people need merely because they involve some uncertainty. Rather, He permits what is needed in this regard, just as He permitted selling fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent, leaving it until harvest, even though some of what is sold has not yet come into existence. Likewise, He permitted the purchaser to stipulate that the fruit of pollinated date palms be included in the purchase. This is a purchase of fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent, but it is incidental to the purchase of the tree. He also permitted ‘arāyā sales—exchanging fresh dates on the palms for dried dates—on the basis of an estimate, thus allowing estimation to take the place of measurement by volume

---

## Page 14483

when needed, even though this falls within usury, which is graver than a sale involving uncertainty. This is the “principle of Islamic law”: securing the greater of two benefits by forgoing the lesser, and averting the greater of two harms by accepting the lesser. Selling produce protected by its skin or shell—such as grapes, pomegranates, bananas, and walnuts and almonds with a single shell—is permissible by agreement of the imams.

He was asked:

A man purchased twenty-six feddans of taro from another man for nine thousand dirhams. The seller concluded the sale with him, and the purchaser began digging up the taro. Then another man came and offered a thousand dirhams more. The seller accepted the higher offer and expelled the first purchaser. The first purchaser then outbid the second by five hundred dirhams, took possession of the taro, dug up a boatload, sold it and remitted its price to the seller. Later, he was outbid again, whereupon the seller expelled him and recorded the taro in the name of the higher bidder. Is the first purchase valid, or the second?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. What this seller has done is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims; indeed, he deserves severe punishment. For the sale of taro and other produce concealed underground, such as carrots, turnips and the like, is either permissible according to one of

---

## Page 14484

the two scholarly opinions—as held by Mālik, in one view within Ahmad’s school, and by others—or impermissible, according to Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, and the well-known view reported from Ahmad. If it is permissible, making a second sale while the first remains in force is forbidden. This seller did not abandon the first sale because he believed it to be forbidden, but in order to sell to the second purchaser. Such conduct is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. The correct view is that selling taro is permissible and accepting the higher offer is unlawful; the taro therefore belongs to the first purchaser.

Those who hold the sale to be invalid say that the seller is entitled only to the fair price of what was taken from him, or to less than its fair value.

He was asked:

A man emigrated from the land of the Tatars. Finding no mount, he purchased one from the Tatars. Is he obliged to pay its price after emigrating to the land of Islam?

He replied:

Yes. If he purchased it from them, he must pay the price to the person who sold it to him, even if that person is a Tatar. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14485

He was asked:

A merchant was granted an exemption by a sultanic decree, stipulating that nothing should be collected from him in respect of his trade. He made one trading journey, then sold the decree in his possession to another merchant because of the exemption it granted. Is the sale of the entitlement contained in the decree valid? Furthermore, the purchaser’s journey was cancelled, and he derived no benefit from it. Is he obliged to pay the price?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The purpose of this sale is not to sell the sheet of paper: its value is negligible, and selling it is not the object at all. Rather, the purpose is that the purchaser should be relieved of liability for the levy collected by the sultan’s deputies, while the seller receives from him part of that amount, or compensation for it, because the seller himself had been exempted from it.

This resembles a disbursement from the public treasury, granted on condition that the recipient has come to the sultan, dispatched a courier, or performed some other such service. He is given it only if he performs that service. If neither he nor his substitute sets out, he is not given it. Accordingly, if this is due to an intervening circumstance, and neither he nor the holder of the decree sets out, nothing is to be released to him. In that case, the seller is not entitled to anything from the purchaser. Nor is what

---

## Page 14486

was mentioned binding, such that payment becomes due merely upon conclusion of the contract. At most, if it is held to be permissible, it would be permissible in these circumstances.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man sold a commodity at the going market rate, but a higher amount was subsequently demanded of him. The commodity has perished and is a fungible item. Is he entitled to recover an equivalent when one is available?

He replied:

He is not entitled to demand from him anything above the price prevailing when possession was taken, namely, the usual price of an equivalent item. Rather, what may be demanded is the price prevailing at that time, which is the value of an equivalent item.

There are two reported positions concerning the validity of this contract:

The first is that it is valid, just as a comparable arrangement is valid when hiring services: for example, when someone gives food to a person who cooks for payment, enters a bathhouse, or boards a ship. According to this position, the contract is valid and the stipulated amount is payable.

The second is that the contract is invalid, so the commodity has been received under an invalid contract. It may be argued that compensation must therefore be made with an equivalent if the commodity is fungible, and otherwise with its value, as in the case of wrongfully seized property. This is the position of a group of our scholars and others, including the Shāfiʿīs. Here, however, they have mutually agreed

---

## Page 14487

upon compensation consisting of its value, just as they may agree upon a dower below or above the customary amount. A comparable case is when the parties agree upon a specified settlement where an equivalent or its value is due. That settlement then becomes binding, since the right belongs exclusively to them and concerns no one else.

Another parallel is the position of Ahmad’s followers concerning an invalid partnership. Its invalidity affects whether it is lawful, not the determination of what the parties have mutually agreed upon, just as it does not affect liability for compensation. Whatever entails liability under a valid contract entails liability under an invalid one, and whatever does not entail liability under a valid contract does not entail liability under an invalid one. Since the two are alike regarding liability in principle, they are likewise alike regarding its extent. This is a valuable insight for anyone who reflects upon it. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man took some grain during a year of high prices and said to the other party, “Agree a price with me for it.” He replied, “Wait until the price stabilizes.” He waited several months, then came and took his share at 150 irdabbs. Is he entitled to money or grain?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The correct position in this matter is that he is entitled to what they mutually agreed upon, namely, the one hundred and fifty.

This applies whether one holds that what was originally due was the prevailing market price—according to one of the two scholarly positions, which is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, namely, that a sale at the prevailing market price is valid—or that the sale was invalid and a replacement was due. For if the two parties reach a settlement

---

## Page 14488

for the value of the replacement at the time of settlement, that settlement is valid and binding. Likewise, if spouses agree upon a dower equal to, less than, or greater than the customary dower, that is permissible, whether or not a valid dower had previously been specified.

Nor should it be said: “The recipient thought that he owed its value, whereas what he actually owes is an equivalent.” This objection should not be raised, for the matter is disputed.

Most scholars hold that once the property has been received and disposed of, returning the price is not required. This is based either on the validity of the contract, or on the position that property received under an invalid contract becomes the recipient’s property, according to Abū Hanīfah, or becomes his property if it perishes, according to Mālik. Since the matter is disputed, an agreement between the parties on this basis constitutes a settlement concerning a matter of scholarly disagreement, and such a settlement is binding.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is it permissible to sell an undivided share?

He replied:

Selling an undivided share is permissible by the consensus of the Muslims, as established in the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For example, he said, as recorded in Sahīh Muslim:

“Any man who has a share in land, a dwelling, or an orchard may not sell it until he informs his partner. If his partner wishes, he may take it; if he wishes, he may leave it. If he sells

---

## Page 14489

before informing him, his partner has the greater right to it at that price.”

Likewise, compensation is owed for destroying an undivided share, or for what amounts to its destruction, such as the extension of emancipation to the remaining shares in a slave. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections:

“Whoever emancipates his share in a slave, while possessing enough wealth to cover the slave’s price, must have the slave fairly valued, neither undervalued nor overvalued. He must then pay his partners the value of their shares, and the slave becomes fully emancipated at his expense.”

When an undivided share is sold, whether or not possession of it has been taken, the Muslims agree that the partner’s right to the other half remains intact, even if he has not exercised the forms of disposal permissible with jointly owned property. The co-owners may apportion its use by place or by time: one may occupy one part and the other another part; alternatively, one may occupy it for a month, then the other for a month. They may lease it out, and either may lease his share to the other. According to the majority of scholars, apart from al-Shāfi'ī, either party who refuses to lease it out may be compelled to do so. As for compelling them to apportion its use, there are three well-known scholarly positions.

---

## Page 14490

He was asked:

A man jointly owns some horses with a partner. The partner sold the horses, without his co-owner’s permission, to someone from whom the latter cannot recover them. Is he obliged to take possession?

He replied:

If he sells his share and hands over all the horses to the buyer, making it impossible for his partner to benefit from his own share, he is liable for his partner’s share. Either he must give his partner access to his share, or his partner may hold him liable—but not for its value.

He was asked:

A property is jointly owned, with witnesses attesting to the joint ownership. Some of the co-owners then sold the entire property, with one of those same witnesses witnessing the sale. Is the sale valid with respect to the seller’s own share and void with respect to the remainder, or is the entire sale void?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Selling someone else’s share is valid only when one has legal authority or acts as an authorized agent. If the rightful owner does not approve the sale, it is void by agreement of the imams. However, according to one of the two scholarly opinions, the sale is valid with respect to the seller’s own share alone, for its corresponding portion of the price, and the buyer has

---

## Page 14491

the option of rescinding or approving the sale. If the property can be divided without detriment, he may compel his co-owner to divide it. If it cannot be divided without detriment, he may demand that the whole property be sold so that they can divide the proceeds. If the witness knows that the seller is acting unjustly and witnesses the sale to assist him in doing so, he has assisted in sin and transgression. Assisting in unlawful contracts by witnessing them is impermissible.

Indeed, it is authentically reported that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Allah has cursed the recipient of usury, its payer, its two witnesses, and its scribe.”

He also said:

“I do not bear witness to injustice.”

Anyone who persists in such conduct compromises his moral integrity. Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Is it permissible to sell grapes to someone who will press them into wine if their owner is compelled by necessity to do so?

He replied:

It is not permissible to sell grapes to someone who will press them into wine. Indeed, the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) cursed anyone who presses grapes for someone who makes wine from them. What, then, of the person who sells them to him, thereby providing even greater assistance? There is no necessity to do this: if he can neither sell them fresh nor dry them into raisins, he can make them into vinegar, grape syrup, or something similar.

---

## Page 14492

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Is it permissible to purchase jifān for extracting oil, for fuel, or for both?

He replied:

Selling the oil is permissible even if the quantity obtainable is unknown, just as it is permissible to sell cottonseed, olives, and similar materials from which oil is extracted, as well as other goods sold by estimate without exact measurement. This applies whether it is purchased for extracting oil or for fuel. However, the person operating the press must not cheat his customer. If it has been stipulated that the jafna is to serve as payment to the owner of the press, and the person operating it has colluded with him to leave some oil in it for him, this constitutes unlawful deception, and purchasing it for its oil is unlawful.

He was asked:

A man rents a shop for twenty-five a month and has equipment and merchandise in it. Someone comes to him and says: “I will rent this shop for forty-five, use the equipment, and buy and sell the merchandise.” Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

This combines a sale and a lease, which is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14493

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man has secured an arrangement with the authorities whereby a particular commodity may be sold only by him. That commodity is available only from him in that locality, though it is available in nearby places, at a distance comparable to that between Misr and Cairo. Is it permissible to buy from this monopolist or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

As for the man himself, it is unlawful for him to do this for two reasons. First, he prevents others from engaging in lawful trade. Second, he forces people to buy from him on whatever terms he wishes, thereby wronging them by raising the price.

As for the goods purchased from him, if he bought them with lawful wealth of his own, buying them from him is not unlawful. The purchaser is the one being wronged: whoever buys incurs no sin, nor does what he acquires become unlawful merely because the seller has wronged him. In such a case, the prohibition applies only to the wrongdoer, not to the person wronged.

But if he bought those goods with wealth he had wrongfully taken, then that is wholly misappropriated property, and buying from him is like buying from someone who has misappropriated property. The ruling on this is clear.

---

## Page 14494

But if his original capital was lawful, and he increased it through profits earned by this means of livelihood, his wealth has become doubtful to the extent that other people’s wealth has been mixed into it. It cannot be described as unlawful, nor as wholly lawful. If the lawful portion predominates, buying from him is permissible, though refraining is an act of religious scrupulousness. If the unlawful portion predominates, there are two juristic views as to whether buying from him is lawful or unlawful.

There is no doubt that he is entitled to some of the profit he makes: namely, what someone in his position would ordinarily be entitled to. Even if his property had been taken from him under an invalid contract, he would be owed its equivalent or the value of its equivalent. The purchasers take his goods, so they owe him their equivalent or their fair value.

Moreover, those responsible for the monopoly arrangement take from him some of what he has wrongfully taken. A shop may cost twenty, yet they compel him to pay fifty because of the monopoly arrangement. Those additional thirty are unlawful for them, and have already been taken from him. As for the unlawful excess that remains with him, that is the portion of his wealth at issue. Where there is need, and another option is weighed against it, the grounds for a concession are stronger. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14495

He was also asked:

About goods sold under exclusive concessions in shops, such as sesame oil and other foodstuffs, as well as non-food items. This arrangement involves someone undertaking to sell a particular commodity exclusively, on condition that no one else sells any of it. He says to the owner of a shop, caravanserai, or other premises:

“I undertake to pay you such-and-such an amount each month, on condition that I buy and sell here a commodity that no one else may sell, or carry out such-and-such work”—specifying a particular activity—“on condition that no one else may do the same.”

Is it permissible to buy these goods from this person when one can obtain goods of the same kind elsewhere? Is it permissible to use these very goods because obtaining alternatives entails hardship, whether or not necessity requires their use?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If one has no need to buy from him, one should refrain from doing so, for he acts unjustly by preventing others from selling. Even if there were nothing doubtful about his wealth, it would still be preferable to avoid dealings with him and shun him as far as possible.

[As for buying from him—especially when there is a need—it is not judged unlawful] (*). Nor

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 222) said:
> His statement, “Nor is it judged unlawful,” is either a slip of the pen by the shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—or a copyist’s oversight. The correct wording is “Nor is it judged lawful.” The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said elsewhere (29/273): “If their wealth includes both lawful and unlawful property, dealings with them involve an element of doubt. Such dealings are not judged unlawful unless one knows that the other party is giving one something unlawful to give, nor are they judged lawful unless one knows that he has given one something lawful.” And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 14496

is it judged unlawful if one buys from him despite being able to buy from someone else. When there is a need, however, buying from him is not disliked. This man has wealth with which he buys and sells; but when he prevents others from selling and people need to buy from him, he charges them above the market price, thereby wronging them. At worst, his case is like that of oppressors who force goods upon people at more than their value, compelling them to buy. This does not make the purchased goods unlawful for the buyer; rather, what is unlawful is the seller’s taking money without right. It may, however, be argued that some of these unlawful excess charges have become mixed with his wealth, making it doubtful.

The first response is:

If most of a person’s wealth is lawful, it is permissible to deal with him, as the scholars of the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad have stated. If unlawful wealth predominates, there are two scholarly views as to whether dealing with him is prohibited or disliked.

It may then be said:

There is no specific, known person entitled to those excess charges. According to the majority of scholars, wealth whose owner is unknown must be spent on purposes serving the interests of the Muslims. We only prohibited this man from charging the excess so that he would not wrong people. If we were to declare what people buy from him unlawful, we would increase the harm they suffer: they would need to buy from him at more than the goods’ value, yet what they purchased would be unlawful, while they would be unable to buy elsewhere. Such a position cannot be maintained. Rather, it is permissible to buy from someone like this, and the buyer has wronged no one, for he has paid the seller the equivalent of what he purchased, and more besides. Whoever is entitled to the payment is also entitled to the wealth in his possession. Thus, if the party entitled

---

## Page 14497

to it is a group of Muslims or a particular individual among them, then it is the seller himself who has wronged all those people by taking their wealth without right. As for the buyer who has paid him the equivalent value and more besides, he has wronged no one. This is clear when the seller’s wealth is so intermixed that what he acquired unlawfully cannot be distinguished from the rest. The claims of those he has wronged remain debts owed by him; they are not entitled to the particular assets in his possession. It is therefore permissible to exchange payment for those assets, while he must repay the wronged party what he took without right. A number of scholars, as Allah has willed, have given this ruling in similar cases. His situation is like that of anyone who owes people debts and wrongs his creditors by delaying repayment, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Delay in repayment by one who can afford to pay is wrongdoing.”

Nevertheless, if he exchanges what he possesses for its equivalent value and an additional amount, this is permissible by scholarly consensus, and buying from him is not disliked. If, however, he makes a gratuitous transfer that leaves him unable to repay the debt he owes, scholars hold two views on whether that transfer takes legal effect before restrictions are imposed on his disposal of his property.

It may, however, be argued that when this wrongdoer took the excess payment and used it to make a purchase, the wronged party’s claim became attached to what was purchased with his money. This differs from a debt incurred with the creditor’s consent, since such a creditor has no claim to property other than the debtor’s own.

The response is:

This depends on several principles. The first is whether the owner’s claim attaches specifically to the dirhams taken from him without right as an excess payment, or whether the usurper may repay him from whatever source he chooses.

---

## Page 14498

Scholars hold two views on whether designating particular dirhams in contracts and when taking possession—including usurpation and safekeeping—establishes a claim to those specific coins.

One view is that it does so in all cases. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. Another view is that it does not do so in any case, as Ibn Qāsim held. A further view is that it does so in cases of usurpation and safekeeping, but not in contracts. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to the other report.

If usurped property is mixed with similar property so that the two cannot be distinguished—as oils, milk, grains, and other things may be mixed—is this mixing equivalent to destruction, so that the wronged party’s claim remains a debt owed by the wrongdoer, who may repay him from whatever source he chooses? Or does his claim remain attached to the property itself, entitling him to take his share of the mixture through division? There are two views on this within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad.

It is clear that those excess dirhams were not specifically designated, whether he purchased from him dirhams payable as a debt or a benefit. For what was taken from the wronged party was the amount exceeding the equivalent value, not specifically designated coins. Even if the coins had been specifically designated and were then mixed with others indistinguishably, his claim to those particular coins would lapse according to one of the two views. How much more so, then, if they had not been specifically designated in the first place? Thus, according to many scholars, his claim is only a debt owed by the wrongdoer.

This is analogous to the view that, if an investment manager or a custodian dies without identifying the property held for safekeeping or the investment capital, it becomes a debt owed by him. Those who hold this view do not recognize the owner as having a claim to the specific assets of the estate. The custodian’s negligence in failing to distinguish the deposit from other property makes him liable for it. Yet these scholars regard the owner’s claim to the deceased’s specific property as having lapsed, and therefore did not

---

## Page 14499

give him priority over the other creditors in recovering his specific property. Rather, they treated him as one creditor among the rest, even though his actual property was mingled with other assets. Wrongdoing consists both in neglecting an obligation and in committing a prohibited act. Thus, a custodian’s failure to fulfil his duty to keep the deposit separate constitutes wrongdoing on his part. Although we do not support this view—that the owner’s claim to the specific property lapses—our purpose is to explain the basis of this issue according to the scholars’ legal principles. Hence, those Mālikī scholars who addressed this issue based their treatment on the premise that the wronged party’s claim was a debt owed by the wrongdoer, rather than a claim to the specific property.

The second principle:

The wrongdoer ordinarily purchases by undertaking a personal obligation to pay, then makes payment with the specific property. Scholars hold two well-known views on the validity of such a transaction.

The third principle:

Let us concede that the wronged party’s claim attaches to the wrongdoer’s specific property, even when the original property is no longer available, because this property replaces his own. This is the view he put forward: the wronged party is given a choice between claiming half of his entitlement and having his claim attach to the property itself, thereby entitling him to claim any increase in that property through growth, profit, or otherwise.

However, it may be objected that the wronged party is entitled only to the amount originally due to him. As for the subsequent increase resulting from the wrongdoer’s dealings, its treatment depends on whether contracts may remain pending approval.

Those who hold that contracts cannot remain pending approval say that the wrongful seller did not acquire ownership of what he received from the purchaser, because he received it under an invalid contract. The price he paid, which he had usurped, is

---

## Page 14500

a debt for which he is personally liable, not the people he has wronged. He does not own what is in his possession; rather, it belongs to unidentified people whom he does not know. He may not dispose of their property without their permission.

On this basis, there are two views:

One view is that a person in authority, such as a judge or anyone else empowered to administer the affairs of absent persons, should settle the debts they owe the seller out of their funds in the seller’s possession.

The other view is that the seller may recover the debt they owe him from their funds in his possession, without seeking a judge’s permission. This is the sounder view, for someone with an established entitlement may recover it from the property of the person who owes it, without needing the judge’s permission. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted a guest denied his due hospitality to take what was due to him from his host’s crops without the host’s permission. Likewise, he instructed a woman to take, without her husband’s permission, what would reasonably suffice for herself and her child. However, where the entitlement is denied, he said:

“Return what has been entrusted to you to the one who entrusted you with it, and do not betray someone who has betrayed you.”

How much more, then, does this apply when someone has sold another person a commodity through an invalid sale and received the price from him! He is all the more entitled to recover what he is owed from that commodity.

As for the position of the majority of scholars, who hold that contracts may remain pending approval, allowing gratuitous dispositions to be given effect when necessary, they say that someone holding property acquired by usurpation, entrusted for safekeeping, or borrowed for use, whose particular owner he cannot identify, should give it in charity on the owner’s behalf. This is the view of Mālik and Abū

---

## Page 14501

Hanīfah, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others. However, if its owner appears, he may decline to ratify that disposition. As for an exchange involving such property, its owner, once identified, may not reject it. Rather, Islamic law confers authority to undertake the exchange because of the need. For example, if a man dies somewhere where there is no executor, heir, or judge, his travelling companions acquire authority over his property. They safeguard it and sell whatever they consider it beneficial to sell. Such a sale takes effect, and they may take possession of what they have sold; this does not depend upon the heirs’ approval. This is not an unauthorized disposition. Rather, it is recognized as an exercise of authority conferred by Islamic law because of the need, just as they have the authority to wash him, provide his shroud from his estate, bury him, and attend to similar matters. For the believers are guardians of one another.

With this understood, consider the seller who sold what he had purchased with that additional amount and received the price from the buyer. If it is held that the sale is invalid and cannot remain pending approval, and that the buyer is not required to return what he received from the seller, while the seller must return the price he received, then, if the buyer cannot return what he received, the seller may take its equivalent. This may be worth more or less than the price, though usually it is equal to it.

The same applies to what he purchased with that additional amount: he must return it to its owner, and its owner must return the additional amount to its owner. Thus, if the recipient of the wrongfully acquired additional amount does not return it, the wronged party is entitled

---

## Page 14502

to take, as the party originally wronged, an equivalent amount from his property now in the seller’s possession. Likewise, the person who received that additional sum in exchange for his property may, if his property is not returned, take an equivalent amount from that sum.

This possibility applies to any two parties who conclude an invalid sale and exchange possession, if it is held that possession acquired under an invalid contract does not confer ownership. Each party then has property held by the other: namely, whatever the other received from him. If restitution becomes impossible, each may take the equivalent of his entitlement, whether in property of the same kind or of another kind.

Accordingly, consider the unjust additional sums that have come into the possession of this wrongdoer liable for restitution, taken from purchasers’ funds that have become indistinguishably mixed. If he buys something with those funds, transfers ownership of the additional sums to the purchasers, and takes possession of what he bought, the property of theirs that he acquires stands against the additional sums they received, which are due to their rightful owner. Thus, buying from him at the fair market price is not unlawful; how much more so if someone buys from him at a higher price! This differs from accepting something from him gratuitously, which raises another discussion that does not belong here.

Ibn Masʿūd was asked about a man who deals in usury and offers hospitality to someone else. Ibn Masʿūd replied:

“Eat: the enjoyment is yours, and the accounting is his.”

The scholars have discussed this matter, but this is not the place for that discussion.

Several fundamental questions arise here. One is whether possession acquired under an invalid contract confers ownership. Another is whether, when someone disposes of a particular asset in a way that prevents its return in kind, the claim becomes a personal liability owed by him. Or does it remain attached to his property with which that asset has become mixed, and to that which he exchanged

---

## Page 14503

for that asset? There are other questions as well.

If, however, we hold that contracts may remain pending approval—particularly when seeking permission is impossible, as is the position of the three jurists—then the matter is clearer. People ordinarily consent to the sale, at a higher price, of property they have prepared for sale, unlike property they have acquired for their own use.

Furthermore, although the wronged person has a small entitlement in this property because of the additional sum wrongfully taken from him, part of it belongs to the wrongdoing shopkeeper, and the two shares cannot be distinguished. In such a case, if one co-owner requests its sale, the other, if unwilling, is compelled to sell for his partner’s sake. Thus, when two people jointly own property that cannot be divided—such as an animal—and one requests that it be sold and the proceeds divided, the other is compelled to agree, according to the majority of scholars. This is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfa, and Ahmad. Some Mālikī scholars have stated that there is consensus on this, because a co-owner’s entitlement is to half the value of the whole, not the value of a half-share.

The evidence is the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith:

“Whoever frees a share he owns in a slave, and has sufficient wealth to cover the slave’s price, must have the slave assessed at a fair value, neither understated nor excessive. He must then pay his co-owners their shares, and the slave becomes fully free at his expense. Otherwise, only the share he has freed becomes free.”

Thus, he established that the co-owner’s entitlement is to half the value of the whole, and instructed that the entire slave be valued, not merely the co-owner’s share.

---

## Page 14504

That being so, it is clear that the wrongfully taken additional sum cannot be distinguished from the amount to which it was added. The two can only be apportioned by dividing either the property itself or its substitute. Since returning the property itself has become impossible, the value of its substitute must therefore be used. This shows that one may transact with the shopkeeper for the property in his possession, while he must give the wronged co-owners their dues.

Either the wronged parties’ claims are solely against him personally, or their claims attach to the specific assets, while transactions involving those assets remain permissible so that their dues may be paid. There is no way to discharge their claims fairly otherwise. On this basis, the purchasers have taken possession of what they bought through lawful, valid purchases. Other evidence also shows that these purchasers wrong no one by buying, that their purchases are permissible, and that preventing people from buying from such sellers compounds the injustice—something neither Allah nor His Messenger has commanded. Accordingly, when there is a need to buy from him, doing so is not even disliked, let alone forbidden.

If, however, what he sold is assumed to be the very property covered by the contract, then this depends on whether contracts may remain pending approval and whether property belonging to an unidentified owner may be dealt with without his permission for a beneficial purpose. Most scholars hold that such contracts may remain pending approval, particularly when there is a need. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfa, and also of Ahmad when there is a need—for example, when the owner’s permission cannot be sought because his identity is unknown. Where no such need exists, two positions are reported from Ahmad. Al-Khiraqī chose the view that such contracts remain pending approval, in agreement with the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfa. This is also a position held by al-Shāfiʿī. It follows that

---

## Page 14505

his disposal of another person’s property remains subject to that person’s approval whenever his permission can be sought. As for an unidentified owner, such dealings do not require his permission; rather, transactions undertaken on his behalf take effect when they serve his interests. Even if he is subsequently identified, he cannot rescind transactions made for consideration; he may only revoke gratuitous dispositions, as in the case of the owner of lost property.

Experience shows that the owners of property such as that mentioned in the question have no particular interest in retaining any specific item. None of them objects to his goods being sold for a higher price; indeed, they would approve the purchaser’s resale. It is the seller, however, who has wronged them. Here, because the owner is unknown, it is permissible to conclude the contract and take possession. This differs from the case in which the owner is known, when, by scholarly consensus, his permission must be obtained before taking possession.

This is comparable to lost property whose owner is unknown. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is Allah’s property, which He grants to whomever He wills.”

According to the majority of scholars, if the finder gives it in charity, that donation is subject to the owner’s approval should he be identified. Before the owner is identified, however, the donation takes effect without awaiting approval. Yet the finder who sells the property is not a wrongdoer, whereas the seller in the present case is. The purchaser, however, is not a wrongdoer. The property cannot be destroyed, and it remains in the wrongful seller’s possession. Thus, taking the purchase money for himself, including the additional amount, is unlawful with respect to the unidentified owner. Islamic law therefore gives effect to the transfer of ownership in the interests of both the purchaser and the unidentified, wronged owner, even if the seller is a wrongdoer. This is comparable to supposing that an endowment administrator, an orphan’s testamentary guardian, the manager of a profit-sharing investment, and a partner

---

## Page 14506

betrayed their trusts and then conducted transactions despite this: their transactions would have to be upheld as valid with respect to both those purchasing from them and the owner of the property. Otherwise, invalidating such transactions would undermine dealings in most people’s property administered under legal authority or through agency, since breaches of trust are so prevalent among those entrusted with authority and those acting as agents. This includes, in particular, transactions by rulers that cannot be invalidated.

Islamic law came to secure and perfect benefits, and to avert and reduce harms. No one may, out of concern for an unidentified owner’s right to a particular item—for which something better has been obtained on his behalf—declare his property and that of the purchasers unlawful for them. This would be like demolishing a city to build a palace.

A detailed discussion of this issue and its ramifications belongs elsewhere. More supporting evidence and statements from scholars, the Companions, and the Successors have been cited on this subject than can be accommodated here.

---

## Page 14507

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is it unlawful to buy and eat any of these foodstuffs on which tolls are levied and which are subject to tax-farming or monopoly? If a man transacts with someone whose entire wealth is unlawful, such as a toll farmer or someone whose only wealth comes from tolls, does that make him a transgressor?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If a man sells his goods, whether food or anything else, and a levy is collected from either the seller or the buyer, this does not make the goods or their purchase unlawful for either party. There is no doubt whatsoever about their lawfulness. The same applies when part of the goods themselves is taken, such as the offal of a slaughtered sheep or a portion of the grain or fruit. Anyone who thinks this casts doubt on their lawfulness is mistaken. Property taken unjustly, whether from the seller or the buyer, does not cast doubt on the lawfulness of what remains. Likewise, if a man is wronged and some of his property is taken, this does not cast doubt on the lawfulness of his remaining property.

Among these levies imposed without any basis in Islamic law are those imposed

---

## Page 14508

on the seller, as in livestock markets and the like. If he sells his goods for a sum of money and part of that price is taken from him, he has been wronged, but the remainder of his money is lawful for him. The buyer has purchased with his own money, and the price may even have been raised to account for the levy, so that he pays extra. How, then, could there be any doubt about the lawfulness of what he has purchased? If the levy is collected from the buyer, he has paid the seller the price, with an additional payment to cover that governmental charge. This casts no doubt on the lawfulness of the transaction for either the seller or the buyer, since the benefits have been acquired in return for the payment rightfully due, and the buyer has paid what he owes and more.

If it is said:

“This is, in reality, an injustice to the seller, since he is entitled to the entire price,”

the reply is:

“Granted that this is so, the buyer has not wronged him. Rather, the one who took his money has wronged him, just as if the seller had received the entire price and the governmental charge had then been taken from him.”

In reality, the burden falls on both of them. If the seller knows that he must pay a charge, he raises the price; and if the buyer knows that he must pay a charge, he reduces the price he offers. Thus, both are wronged by the collection of the charge, while neither has wronged anyone. There is therefore no doubt about the lawfulness of their property on this account. Whatever Muslims sell, provided that it belongs to them, is not rendered doubtful by the levies collected from them.

As for a man who takes on a concession for a particular class of goods on condition that he alone may sell them,

---

## Page 14509

he commits injustice in two ways: first, by preventing others from selling those goods, which is impermissible; and second, by selling them to people at whatever price he chooses, thereby making them expensive.

Such people fall into two categories. The first includes someone who rents a shop from a muqtiʿ or someone else for more than its rental value, on condition that he alone may sell in that location; or someone who pays a stipulated sum to a muqtiʿ or someone else without renting a shop or anything else. Both are wrongdoers. The additional rent paid for the shop in return for preventing another person from selling puts the tenant in the same position as the holder of an exclusive concession.

The second category consists of those who have no concession payment to make but undertake to sell to the public, such as millers, bakers and others who pay no levy but are required to sell a specified quantity each day, while others are prevented from selling. For this reason, prices may be fixed for these people, even if price-fixing is not permissible in general. They have been required to trade in this class of goods, while others have been barred from doing so. Allowing them to sell at whatever prices they wished would therefore wrong the poor.

This differs from a situation in which everyone is free to engage in such trade. In that case, the applicable precedent is the report in the Sunan collections from Anas, who said:

“Prices rose during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The people said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, fix prices for us.’ He replied, ‘Allah is the One who sets prices, withholds, gives abundantly and provides sustenance. I indeed hope to meet Allah with no one seeking redress from me for an injustice concerning property.’”

---

## Page 14510

As for the situation under discussion: if they have been required to sell, they may not be compelled to sell below the fair market price, for that would wrong them. If others have been prevented from selling, these sellers may not be allowed to charge whatever they choose, for that would wrong the public.

One question remains: may they undertake such an arrangement on these terms—that they alone sell this category of goods, to the exclusion of others, and sell only at the fair market price, without any levy being imposed on them? May the ruler subject them to such an arrangement, or must he prevent anyone from doing so?

The answer is as follows: if they voluntarily undertake to supply the goods people need and to sell them only at the fair market price, on condition that others are prevented from selling, while anyone wishing to join them in this arrangement is allowed to do so, then it is not evident that this is prohibited. Indeed, it may serve the public interest. This resembles what has been reported from ʿUmar concerning price-fixing, namely that he said: “If you sell at the prices charged by the market traders, well and good; otherwise, do not sell.”

The public interest here lies in ensuring that people are supplied with what they need and that it is sold to them only at the fair market price. These are two considerable benefits. If the sellers choose this arrangement, they have not been coerced into it and therefore suffer no injustice. Other people have not been prevented from selling except when they participate in this

---

## Page 14511

public-interest arrangement by joining them in the work they undertake at fair market value. Thus, others are prevented from selling goods above the fair market price and may sell them only if they undertake to sell to one of those participating in the arrangement. A person may be unable to do that.

It may be said:

These are two forms of injustice: compelling a person to sell and requiring him to sell at the fair market price. This entails harm. If people’s affairs can proceed properly without this arrangement, such harm may not be tolerated without an outweighing benefit. If, however, without it people cannot obtain sufficient food and similar necessities, or can obtain them only at high prices, whereas this arrangement provides them with sufficient supplies at the fair market price, then this public benefit warrants tolerating the restrictions mentioned above.

As for compelling people to enter such an arrangement, this requires further distinction. When people are in pressing need of the goods or services someone possesses, he must make them available at fair market value. He is also prevented from withholding a commodity from sale until he has sold a specified quantity. This is not the place to discuss these questions in detail.

With this understood, someone who undertakes to pay a levy to the authority imposing it, on condition that he alone may sell the commodity and may sell it at whatever price he chooses, undoubtedly commits injustice of the same kind as that involved in governmental levies, in both respects previously mentioned. For this reason, those who disapproved of dealing with such a person did so because of the doubt concerning the lawfulness of his wealth. If he alone sells, at whatever price he chooses, it is as though he compels people to buy from him at his chosen price, thus taking from them more than they rightfully owe

---

## Page 14512

and this doubtful element has become mixed with his wealth, rendering it doubtful in this respect. This is why those who disapproved of dealing with such people did so.

This is the approach of the scrupulously pious who refrain from eating roast meat sold under such concessions and the like. Their scruples concern precisely such an arrangement: only the concession holder may roast meat or sell it, at whatever price he chooses; likewise, only he may sell salt, at whatever price he chooses.

Salt, however, differs from other commodities. It is originally among the freely available resources in which Muslims share, like fish and other such resources. If only one person is permitted to obtain these resources in return for undertaking to pay a levy, it is not unlawful for someone to purchase them from him with his own money, for the buyer has wronged no one in doing so. Indeed, he would have been entitled to take them directly from their source. Had he hired this person or someone else to collect them for him from their common source, that too would have been permissible. Had they remained available to Muslims in common, they would have been cheaper, and the buyer would have obtained them for less than the concession holder supplied them for. Thus, the concession holder wrongs both the buyer and others.

Those who purchase from him, however, wrong no one. Nor have they purchased from him something that he acquired with his own money. What is forbidden to him is the injustice; whoever relinquishes his ownership does not lose it and has wronged no one in the matter, since these resources are originally freely available. The Muslims who purchase them are the ones being wronged: were it not for this injustice, they could obtain them without paying the price. If, then, they are wronged and charged more than they owe, that would not render unlawful for them what had been

---

## Page 14513

lawful for them. For wrongdoing makes a transaction unlawful for the wrongdoer, not for the person wronged. Do you not see that when someone who conceals defects, cheats, or acts similarly sells another person something whose defects have been concealed, the purchase does not become unlawful for the buyer merely because he has been charged more than he ought to pay, even though the excess taken by the cheat is unlawful for him?

There are many such examples in Islamic law. Where a prohibition concerning people’s rights applies to one party, it does not necessarily apply to the other. For example, if a man buys back his own property from someone who has forcibly seized it, the seller is forbidden to take the price, but the buyer is not forbidden to recover his property or to pay the price he has offered.

For this reason, the scholars have said:

It is permissible to bribe an official to ward off injustice, but not to prevent the fulfilment of a right; bribing him is unlawful in either case. Likewise, a captive, or a freed slave whose master denies having freed him, may ransom himself with money. He is permitted to pay it, even though the person unjustly holding him is not permitted to take it. Similarly, if a woman has been divorced three times but her husband denies having divorced her, and she secures her release through what outwardly takes the form of a divorce in return for compensation, the payment is unlawful for him to take, while it frees her from the bondage of his control.

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I give one of them a gift, and he takes it away as a fire that burns him.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, why, then, do you give to them?” He replied, “They insist on asking me, and Allah refuses to let me be miserly.”

Another example is his saying:

“Whatever a person spends to protect his honour is charity.”

Thus, if

---

## Page 14514

a man pays a poet or someone else so that he will not spread lies about him in satirical verse or otherwise, or make unlawful attacks upon his honour, it is permissible for the man to make that payment. However, what the recipient takes in return for not wronging him is unlawful for him, since he is obliged to refrain from wronging him.

Lying about someone in satirical verse is akin to what ordinary people call “qata'a masāni'ahu.” This refers to someone who approaches people and, if they do not pay him, attacks them by helping others commit sin and aggression against them, spreading lies about them, or doing something similar. Anyone who takes money in return for not lying about people or wronging them is taking filthy, unlawful earnings. Wrongdoing and lying are forbidden to him, so he must refrain from them without taking payment from the person he would wrong. If he refrains only in return for payment, that payment is an unlawful gain.

As for resources that are originally available for all Muslims to use—such as land game, sea catches, things growing naturally on the land, and resources found in the mountains and open country, including minerals, salt, atrūn and the like—if the ruler restricts access to them and orders that only his deputies may collect them and that they must be sold to the people, it does not become unlawful for people to buy them. They are not wronging anyone by doing so; rather, they themselves are wronged by being denied access to them. How, then, could it be unlawful for them to buy what they are entitled to take without payment?

If the ruler’s deputies obtain these resources only by using the payments they have unjustly collected for them, or through some similar injustice, the reply is: those funds were taken unjustly from the Muslims, and it is the Muslims who are

---

## Page 14515

wronged. They have been denied their right to freely available resources unless they pay money, some of which is used to extract those resources while the remainder is taken. This does not make unlawful for them what was lawful for them. This is clear where the wrongful charges are proportionate—for example, where each quantity is sold at a fixed price and some of the proceeds are used to extract those resources. Here, there is no doubt whatsoever about the permissibility of the purchase, for the funds used to extract the resources also belong to them by right.

This is like someone seizing another man’s house and ordering the owner’s servants to prepare food from what is in it. That food does not become unlawful for the dispossessed owner, since he owns both the property itself and the benefits derived from it. All that has happened is that someone has acted without his authorization and without having authority over him. This does not make his property unlawful for him, nor even make giving his property away unlawful, by the unanimous agreement of the Muslims—even if the means used to extract those resources involve no transactions with the jointly held funds administered by the ruler.

If, however, the ruler’s deputies wrongfully press people into service to extract those resources, this is like forcing someone to cook food or weave a garment for him, or cooking food with stolen firewood, and similar cases in which the item itself is lawful but wrongdoing has occurred in transforming it from one state to another. This does give rise to doubt. The way to remove that doubt is to assess the benefit added to the item through the wronged person’s labour and pay that person his wages. If the wronged person cannot be identified, the amount should be given in charity on his behalf. At most, this amounts to a mixture of what is lawful and what is unlawful. Even if the assets he owns were mixed with money that

---

## Page 14516

he had seized and acquired unlawfully—for example, if his dirhams and dinars were mixed with dirhams and dinars he had seized, or his grain, fruit, flour, vinegar, or gold were mixed with goods of the same kinds that he had seized—such mixing would not render his own property unlawful for him. For unlawful things fall into two categories.

The first consists of things unlawful because of their inherent nature and characteristics, such as blood, carrion, and pork. If something of this kind is mixed with a liquid, and the taste, colour, or smell of the impurity becomes perceptible in it, the liquid becomes unlawful.

The second consists of things unlawful because of how they were acquired, such as gold and silver currency, grain, fruit, and the like. These things are never absolutely unlawful in themselves; rather, they are unlawful for whoever takes them wrongfully or by unlawful means. If a man takes some of them and mixes them with his own property, he must remove the unlawfully acquired amount from that mixture, while the amount corresponding to his own property remains lawful for him.

But what if he were to set aside an equivalent amount from other property? There are two views within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad:

The first is that mixing is treated like destruction, so setting aside an equivalent amount suffices.

The second is that the wronged person’s right remains attached to the property itself despite the mixing. An amount corresponding to that person’s entitlement must therefore be removed from the mixed property itself.

With this established, if the effect of the wronged person’s labour remains embodied in the item—as with his cooking, weaving, and the like—he is entitled only to the value of that benefit. Thus, if he pays the wronged person

---

## Page 14517

the value of that benefit, the latter has received his due. No one then shares ownership of the item with its owner, so it is not unlawful for him.

If, however, he does not know the wronged person, he should, according to the majority of scholars, give that amount in charity on his behalf. This is like someone holding money from property wrongfully seized, borrowed, or entrusted to him, whose owners he does not know: he gives it in charity on their behalf. In Islamic law, the unknown is treated as nonexistent, as is what lies beyond one’s ability. Hence the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said concerning lost property:

“If its owner comes, return it to him; otherwise, it is Allah’s wealth, which He gives to whomever He wills.”

Thus, if the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) assigned lost property to its finder when its owner could not be identified—even though appropriating it is unlawful because it was dropped by an owner—then how much more so when even that is unknown? Muslims do not dispute that the finder may give it in charity. They differ only over whether he may take ownership of it if he is wealthy, and the majority permit this.

There are well-known reports concerning this issue. One is the account of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, who bought a slave girl, then went out to pay the seller but could not find him. He began going around to the poor, saying:

“O Allah, this charity is on behalf of the slave girl’s owner. If he is content, I am discharged of my obligation. If he is not content, it is on my own behalf, and I owe him its equivalent on the Day of Resurrection.”

Another is the account of the man who misappropriated some of the spoils during the Cyprus campaign. He came to Muʿāwiyah to return what he had misappropriated, but Muʿāwiyah would not accept it. He then sought a ruling from one of the Successors, who instructed him to give it in charity on behalf of the army. He returned to Muʿāwiyah and told him, and Muʿāwiyah approved of this.

This is because Allah, Glorified and Exalted, says:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And property

---

## Page 14518

whose owner we do not know no longer has to be returned to him; it is therefore spent for the benefit of the Muslims, and charity is among the greatest ways of benefiting them. This is a general principle governing all property whose owner is unknown and to whom it cannot be returned. Such property—including wrongfully seized property, borrowed items, and deposits entrusted for safekeeping—is spent for the benefit of the Muslims according to Mālik, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and others. When it is distributed in this way, a poor person may accept it, because the person giving it does so on its owner’s behalf.

This differs from giving charity from misappropriated property, concerning which the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said in an authentic hadith:

“Allah accepts neither prayer without purification nor charity from misappropriated property.”

This refers to someone who takes possession of property and gives it in charity despite being able to return it to its owner, or gives it as an act of devotion, just as he would give his own wealth in charity. Allah does not accept this from him.

The former, however, gives it in charity only to free himself from wrongdoing and sin. His charity is therefore equivalent to paying a debt he owes, returning entrusted property to its owners, or handing property over to an agent entitled to receive it. It is not the kind of charity included in the Prophet’s words:

“Nor charity from misappropriated property.”

---

## Page 14519

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was also asked:

In a certain city, no sheep is slaughtered without the tax collector taking its offal, head, and feet as a levy. He then sets these out and sells them in the markets. Some people in the city, both residents and outsiders, do not refrain from buying and eating them. No heads, feet, or offal are sold in the city except under this arrangement, and no alternative is possible. In these circumstances, is buying and eating them forbidden or not?

He replied:

These come under the same ruling as the levies that rulers impose upon people. In reality, they are taken from the wealth of the sheep owners who sell their animals to butchers and others: the buyer allows for the offal that will be taken and reduces the price accordingly. The same applies to all such levies. Although they are collected from the buyer, they are actually taken from the seller’s wealth.

These levies involve interpretive justifications and doubts about their legal status. Some are outright injustice, but identifying the owners of the property and returning it to them has become impossible. It must therefore be used for the benefit of the Muslims. The authorities have the power to sell it and disburse it. Thus, someone who buys these items from them and pays them the price is not in the same position as someone purchasing wrongfully seized property

---

## Page 14520

in a clear-cut case involving neither interpretive justification nor legal doubt, and where its holder has no authority to sell it, such that one could say: “He has committed a forbidden act and becomes flagrantly sinful by persisting in it.”
