Preventing people from buying these goods harms them and wastes property without bringing any benefit to the wronged owner. The wronged owner may, if he wishes, demand from the wrongdoer the price he received, and may demand the equivalent of his property. Abstaining from such purchases out of religious scrupulousness is a matter of avoiding doubtful things; we do not rule that these goods are unequivocally forbidden. Whoever buys and eats them need not be censured, nor should it be said that he has committed a forbidden act for which there is no interpretive justification. Indeed, some jurists have issued fatwas to certain rulers permitting the imposition of such levies in principle. Abū al-Ma'ālī al-Juwaynī did so in his book Ghiyāth al-Umam, and some Hanafī jurists have also mentioned this.

A Muslim may purchase property acquired on the basis of an interpretive justification from the person who acquired it, even if the buyer believes that the underlying transaction is forbidden. For example, if a non-Muslim under Muslim protection sells wine and receives its price, a Muslim may transact with him using those proceeds, even though the Muslim himself is not permitted to sell wine. As Umar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“Leave its sale to them, and collect the proceeds.”

The occasion for this was that some of his officials had accepted wine in payment of the jizya and sold it to non-Muslims under Muslim protection. When Umar heard of this, he objected and said:

“Leave its sale to them, and collect the proceeds.”

This is reliably established from Umar and is the position of the imams. Likewise, if someone enters into a transaction that he believes to be permissible according to his school of law and receives the money

---

## Page 14521

another person may buy that property from him, even if he does not consider the underlying transaction permissible. Thus, if these levies were imposed by someone who believed them permissible because someone had issued him a fatwa to that effect, or who held that taking this property and spending it on jihad and other public interests was permissible, another person may buy that property from him, even if he does not believe that its initial collection was permissible.

Accordingly, anyone who believes that the authorities have a tenable interpretive justification for their actions may buy what they have collected, even if he himself does not consider their actions permissible. For example, a ruler might accept the equivalent value of the zakat due, and someone might buy some of what he has received. Or he might confiscate property from certain officials in a manner he believes permissible. Or he might hold that people are obliged to support jihad with their wealth, and that the levies collected constitute property that may legitimately be taken and spent on jihad. The same applies to other interpretations that may be mistaken but nevertheless fall within the legitimate scope of independent legal reasoning.

If the ruler has received the property on this basis, it is permissible to buy it from him, and likewise from the deputy whom he instructed to collect it, even if the buyer does not regard its collection as justified. The buyer has not wronged its owner, for he has purchased it with his own money from someone who received it in a manner he believed permissible. If this is the case, purchasing it is lawful according to the sounder of the two scholarly positions; it is not a doubtful matter. For if it is permissible to buy from non-Muslims what they have received through contracts they believe permissible—even though those contracts are forbidden in the religion of the Muslims—then

---

## Page 14522

it is all the more permissible and appropriate to buy from a Muslim what he has received under a contract he believes permissible—even if we consider it forbidden. For a non-Muslim’s interpretation that conflicts with the religion of Islam is unquestionably invalid, unlike a Muslim’s interpretation.

Thus, if they embrace Islam and bring their disputes before us, having received wealth through contracts they believed permissible—such as interest payments or the proceeds from selling wine and swine—that wealth does not become forbidden to them. Likewise, dealing with them in that wealth before their conversion is not forbidden. This is based on the words of the Most High:

“Fear Allah and give up what remains of interest.”

He did not forbid them what they had already received. The same applies to someone who engaged in interest-based transactions believing them permissible, then discovered that they were not permissible, provided these were transactions over which Muslims have differed. According to the sound position, what he received through those transactions is not forbidden to him.

The second consideration:

Whatever rulers have taken through outright injustice, if it has become mixed with the funds of the public treasury and cannot be returned to its owner, is to be spent on the public interests of the Muslims. For what is unknown is treated as nonexistent. Thus, property known to have been taken unjustly, but whose owner is unknown, is to be spent on public interests.

Whatever is received from a public treasury in which lawful and unlawful funds are mixed is not to be deemed forbidden. For when property becomes indistinguishably mixed, this is treated as equivalent to its destruction, and its owner is entitled to compensation from the public treasury. Therefore, according to the soundest scholarly position, it is permissible to receive payment for something sold from the mixed funds of the public treasury. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14523

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning sheep that are sold and on which a levy is collected from the butchers: the butchers are restricted to slaughtering in a single location and charged a slaughtering fee. Their offal is then taken as a second levy under a guarantee arrangement, after which it is cooked and sold. Is it forbidden for someone to buy this offal to eat, or not? Is earning money by trading in it forbidden, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

There is disagreement concerning this issue.

Some say: This property was taken from its owner without right and sold without either legal authority or authorization to act on his behalf. Its sale is therefore invalid; rather, it remains its owner’s property. It has been cooked and sold without his permission, so purchasing it is not permissible.

Others say: This is property held by the rulers, whether they acted on a legal interpretation or deliberately committed injustice. If they do not return it to its owners, the beneficial course is to sell it, because keeping it until it spoils causes harm that the Lawgiver does not command. Even if property is sold without its owner’s permission, according to most scholars the sale remains subject to the owner’s approval. As for what is sold by

---

## Page 14524

the rulers, they possess authority over property of unknown ownership received by their deputies that others do not possess. Once it has been taken, identifying the owner of each head has become impossible. The beneficial course is to sell the heads and distribute the proceeds among those entitled to them. If they sell them but do not distribute the proceeds, the purchaser bears no sin; the sin rests solely upon whoever withholds the proceeds from their rightful owners. The same applies if an orphan’s guardian, an endowment’s administrator, or the administrator of the public treasury sells property but does not pay the proceeds to those entitled to them: the sin rests upon him, not upon the person who bought from him.

Furthermore, those who bought them—even if the purchase was invalid—have paid for them and are entitled to recover the sums they paid. Several scholars, including Ahmad and others, have expressly stated that someone who buys something and subsequently discovers that it was wrongfully seized, but does not know its owner, may sell it and retain the amount he paid, while giving any profit in charity.

The cooks who bought the heads, when returning them has become impossible, may sell them and retain an amount equivalent to their purchase price, if the original sale was not valid. In that case, the purchase is valid. They have ratified the sale, so it is valid according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad according to the stronger of the two reports from him.

These, then, are several grounds advanced by those who permit purchasing them. Anyone who purchases them following these scholars is not to be censured. Nor is anyone to be censured who has grounds for doubt or believes purchasing them to be forbidden and therefore refrains. One cannot categorically declare transactions of this kind forbidden;

---

## Page 14525

for many things indispensable to Muslims fall into this category: the authorities monopolize them and sell them to the public, and people can obtain them only from those authorities.

This includes resources open to all, such as salt, atrūn, and the like. It also includes privately owned goods, such as wool, hides, and animal hair—the kinds of goods the authorities sell from the property of those whose assets they confiscate, and which people need. Some of this property is taken rightfully, some on the basis of an interpretation, and some through sheer injustice. Yet all of it is to be returned to its owners; returning it to them may even prove impossible, either because their identities are unknown or because the holder is unable to return it to them—for no obligation applies to what is unknown or beyond one’s ability—or because Muslims are compelled to commit injustice. In either case, selling it is better for its owner and for the Muslims than leaving it to spoil without benefiting anyone. Thus, if selling it is the most beneficial course under these circumstances, a purchaser may buy it, and it is lawful for him. The purchaser has wronged no one, for he has paid the price.

The person who has actually been wronged is entitled to the proceeds when selling the property serves his interests, just as the property of an absent person may be sold. Indeed, if a man dies somewhere where there is no governing authority, the majority of scholars hold that his travelling companions have the authority to take possession of his property and sell it.

Likewise, if someone holds wrongfully seized property, borrowed items, or deposits whose owners he does not know, the position of the majority—Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad—is that these may be sold when doing so serves the relevant interests, and that purchasing them is permissible.

---

## Page 14526

The principle underlying this is that Allah, the Majestic and Almighty, sent the messengers to secure and perfect benefits as far as possible, and to give precedence to the greater of two benefits by foregoing the lesser. Allah, Glorified be He, has forbidden His servants to commit injustice and has enjoined justice. Thus, when injustice and corruption occur and cannot be prevented, the duty is to mitigate them and pursue justice and benefit as far as possible. Allah has forbidden injustice concerning both resources open to everyone and privately owned property, because of the harm it causes those entitled to them.

If it were said, “These goods must not be purchased, and no one may lawfully use salt, hides, heads, hair, wool, or other goods sold in this manner,” such a prohibition would cause far greater harm to Muslims and corruption in their religious and worldly affairs than saying, “Rather, the wronged person’s claim lies against the wrongdoer who received the proceeds, while the purchaser bought the goods rightfully, so they are lawful for him.” This latter position safeguards the wronged person’s rights by directing his claim against the wrongdoer, while safeguarding the rights of the public by enabling them to benefit from these goods in return for payment.

This is especially so given that the owners of those heads and similar goods are known not to object, in reality, to their sale, since allowing them to spoil serves no interest of theirs. When the goods are sold, the seller has therefore done what the owners would choose and approve of. What they do not approve of, however, is being deprived of the proceeds; rather, they want the proceeds paid to them. Thus, they consent to the purchaser taking possession of the goods and to their use, but they will not be satisfied with the seller unless he gives them the price, so that he alone

---

## Page 14527

has wronged them, not the purchaser; the goods are therefore lawful for him. This issue is discussed at length elsewhere.

The crux of the argument for prohibiting these goods is that whoever deems them unlawful says: “They were sold without permission, agency, or legal authority.” But this premise is disputed. Rather, the owners approve of their sale and have authorized it; what they do not approve of is the proceeds being taken. Suppose, for example, that someone authorized another person to sell something, and that person sold it but took the price for himself. The owner approves of the sale, but not of the seller taking the price for himself.

Even supposing that the owner had not authorized the sale, his interests under Islamic law require that the goods be sold, for this is better than letting them spoil, and they can only be sold in this manner. Selling them and collecting the price—whoever does so—is better than letting them spoil. The seller can then be required to pay over the price, while people benefit from the goods. This is better than demanding their value from the usurper while the goods spoil. Much more could be said on such matters. Allah knows best what is correct.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning those whose wealth is predominantly unlawful, such as toll collectors, those who consume usury, and others like them; those engaged in forbidden occupations, such as makers of images and astrologers; and the assistants of rulers: is it lawful to obtain food from them through commercial dealings, or not?

---

## Page 14528

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If their wealth contains both lawful and unlawful funds, dealing with them involves doubt. Such dealings cannot be declared unlawful unless it is known that what they give is unlawful for them to give, nor can they be declared lawful unless it is known that what they have given comes from lawful funds. If lawful wealth predominates, dealings with them are not ruled unlawful. If unlawful wealth predominates, some hold that dealing with them is lawful, while others hold that it is unlawful.

As for someone who engages in usury, most of his wealth is lawful, unless some other ground for objection is known. This is because, if he sells one thousand for one thousand two hundred, only the excess is unlawful. If his wealth contains lawful and unlawful funds that have become mixed, the lawful portion does not become unlawful. Rather, he may take an amount corresponding to the lawful portion, just as when wealth belongs to two partners and the funds of one become mixed with those of the other: it is divided between them.

Likewise, someone whose lawful and unlawful wealth has become mixed must remove an amount corresponding to the unlawful portion; the remainder is lawful for him. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning what village headmen and neighborhood chiefs consume: is it lawful or not?

He replied:

If a headman wrongs people, whatever he takes from them unjustly is unlawful. Whatever belongs to him or has been acquired through a lawful means is permissible. If a neighborhood chief receives a reasonable fee for keeping watch and does not transgress against people, his fee is lawful.

---

## Page 14529

He was asked:

Concerning a provisions merchant from whom village headmen take supplies to offer hospitality to those stranded without means and others: they collect money from the poor and widows and give it to him. Is this lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

If they buy goods from such merchants and pay them with money that the merchants know has been seized—taken unjustly from its owners—the merchants may not benefit from it. However, if they use this money to purchase on the headmen’s behalf what the headmen demand of them, they incur no blame if they are compelled to do so.

Thus, someone who seeks to avoid both committing injustice and suffering it should, when compelled to undertake this work, use the oppressors’ money to purchase on their behalf what they demand of him, so that he neither wrongs anyone else nor is himself wronged.

Nevertheless, if the money they have collected from people cannot be returned to its owner, and they give it to the provisions merchant as compensation for what they took from him against his will, he has a stronger claim to it than someone who receives it without giving anything in exchange. The true wrongdoer is the one who took the money without right, not someone who receives compensation for his property from funds for which he knows of no specific rightful claimant. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 14530

He was asked:

Concerning dealings with the Tatars: are these permissible for those who transact with them?

He replied:

Dealings with the Tatars are permissible insofar as dealings with others like them are permissible, and prohibited insofar as dealings with others like them are prohibited. A man may buy their livestock, horses, and similar possessions, just as he may buy livestock and horses from the Turkmen, Bedouins, and Kurds. He may also sell them food, clothing, and similar goods that he may sell to others like them. But selling them—or anyone else—things that help them commit prohibited acts, such as horses and weapons for someone who will use them in unlawful fighting, is not permissible.

Allah Most High says:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression.”

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“cursed ten in connection with wine: he cursed wine itself, the one who presses it, the one who has it pressed, the one who carries it, the one to whom it is carried, its seller, its purchaser, the one who serves it, the one who drinks it, and the one who consumes its proceeds.”

Thus, he cursed the one who presses it, even though he merely presses grapes to produce juice. Juice is lawful and can be made into vinegar, syrup, and other things.

---

## Page 14531

If the property in their possession, or in the possession of others, is known to have been forcibly taken from a person protected by Islamic law, it is not permissible to purchase it in order to acquire it as one’s own. However, it is permissible to purchase it as a means of recovering it for its lawful disposal: returning it to its owners if possible, or otherwise using it for the benefit of the Muslims.

If it is known that their wealth includes something unlawful, but the particular property cannot be identified, this does not make dealings with them unlawful. The same applies when it is known that the marketplace contains property that has been forcibly taken or stolen, but that property cannot be identified. When the unlawful becomes mixed with the lawful, there are two cases:

The first concerns what is prohibited in itself, such as carrion or marriage to a sister through breastfeeding. If the prohibited item or person is indistinguishable among a number too large to enumerate, this does not make the others unlawful. For example, a man may know that he has a sister through breastfeeding in a particular town without knowing who she is, or that someone there sells carrion without knowing which meat it is. This does not make the women or meat of that town unlawful to him. However, if he cannot distinguish his sister from one unrelated woman, or lawfully slaughtered meat from carrion, both alternatives are prohibited.

The second concerns property that is unlawful because it was forcibly taken, or was received through unlawful transactions such as usury and gambling. If such property becomes indistinguishably mixed with other property, the whole does not become unlawful. Rather, the respective amounts must be distinguished, and each portion allocated to whoever is entitled to it. An example is a thief who takes people’s property and mixes it together, or takes their wheat or flour and mixes it together. It is then divided among them in proportion to their respective entitlements.

---

## Page 14532

If it is known that some property of this kind exists in a town, but the particular property cannot be identified, people are not prohibited from buying in that town. However, if most of a man’s wealth is unlawful, are dealings with him prohibited or merely disliked? There are two views. If most of his wealth is lawful, dealings with him are not prohibited. Nevertheless, it has been said that such dealings fall among the doubtful matters that it is recommended to avoid. And Allah knows best.

He also said:

Section

I have mentioned in more than one place that everything prohibited in Islamic law comes down to wrongdoing, whether concerning the rights of Allah Most High, the rights of the individual, or the rights of other people. Whenever a person wrongs others, he also wrongs himself, but the reverse is not true. Thus, all sins fall within a person’s wrongdoing against himself.

The first to acknowledge this was the father of mankind when he received the words from his Lord and said:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves, and if You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will surely be among the losers.”

These words contained his acknowledgment of his sin and his plea to his Lord, in utter need, for forgiveness and mercy. Forgiveness is the removal of evil deeds, while mercy is the bestowal of

---

## Page 14533

good things. Thus, he wronged himself without wronging anyone else. Moses, peace be upon him, said, in the account of the man who was his enemy:

“He killed him and said, ‘This is of Satan’s doing. He is indeed an enemy who clearly leads astray.’”

“He said, ‘My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me.’ So He forgave him.”

He thus acknowledged that he had wronged himself by harming another without having been commanded to do so. Jonah, peace be upon him, said:

“There is no deity except You. Glory be to You! Indeed, I have been among the wrongdoers.”

The Sahīh records the supplication that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught Abū Bakr to recite in his prayer:

“O Allah, I have greatly wronged myself, and none forgives sins except You. Grant me forgiveness from Yourself and have mercy upon me. You are indeed the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.”

This supplication corresponds to Adam’s supplication in acknowledging wrongdoing against oneself and asking for forgiveness and mercy.

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had settled on his mount and praised Allah, glorified Him, and proclaimed His greatness, he would say, ‘There is no deity except You. Glory be to You! I have wronged myself, so forgive me.’ Then he would laugh.”

This is preserved in a hadith narrated by Alī ibn Abī Tālib.

Accordingly, wrongdoing is of two kinds: neglecting what is due and overstepping the limits, as I have explained in several places. Failing to perform an obligation is wrongdoing, just as committing something prohibited is wrongdoing. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“For someone able to repay a debt to delay doing so is wrongdoing.”

Reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim. He thus declared that delaying repayment is wrongdoing—so what, then, of failing to repay altogether?

---

## Page 14534

I have established elsewhere that fulfilling an obligation is more important than refraining from a prohibited act, and that acts of obedience involving action are greater than those involving abstention. Thus, wrongdoing that consists in withholding obligatory rights is, as a category, graver than wrongdoing that consists in overstepping the limits. I have also established that religiously prescribed scrupulousness consists in fulfilling obligations and refraining from prohibitions, not merely refraining from prohibitions. Likewise, piety encompasses both fulfilling obligations and refraining from prohibitions, as Allah explained its scope in His words:

“Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west”

—through to His words—

“Those are the ones who are true, and it is they who are mindful of Allah.”

This is where many people go wrong: they consider the objectionable aspect of an action or some property that calls for avoiding it, but overlook the aspect involving a command that requires action.

An example is a question put to Ahmad concerning a man who died leaving property of doubtful lawfulness and an outstanding debt. The heir asked Ahmad whether he should scrupulously avoid that doubtful property. Ahmad replied, “Will you leave your father bound by his debt?” This was related by Abū Tālib [and Ibn Hāmid] (*).

This is sound understanding indeed. Repaying the debt is obligatory, and the creditor has a claim against the estate. If the heir does not repay the debt, the creditor is entitled to recover it from the estate. It is therefore impermissible to let the estate of doubtful lawfulness go to waste when the creditor has a claim against it. Nor is it permissible to harm the deceased by leaving him bound by his debt. Turning away from the estate thus harms both the deceased and the rightful claimant. These are two definite acts of wrongdoing resulting from neglecting two obligations. Taking the property of doubtful lawfulness

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 87, footnote):
> (*) Thus in the text; this is a textual corruption. The correct reading is “Abū Hāmid,” meaning al-Ghazālī, whom he explicitly names in this passage (10:644): “As Abū Tālib al-Makkī and Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī related from Imam Ahmad.”
> This text appears in al-Ta‘līq (Commentary), 10:644, and I have reproduced it here for the reader’s benefit.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Library).

---

## Page 14535

may involve harm to the wronged party.

Ahmad therefore told the heir, “Discharge your father’s liability.” This property of doubtful lawfulness is better than leaving him bound by worldly goods. Doing this is an individual obligation upon the heir if no one else undertakes it in his place, or a collective obligation, or strongly recommended—more strongly recommended than avoiding doubtful property—because of the greater benefit it brings.

Likewise, everyone has obligations, such as providing for themselves and their relatives, repaying their debts, and other duties. If they neglect these, their wrongdoing is certain. But if they fulfil them using property of doubtful lawfulness, their wrongdoing is not established. How, then, can a Muslim scrupulously avoid possible wrongdoing by committing definite wrongdoing?

For this reason, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib said:

“There is no good in someone who does not love wealth with which to worship his Lord, discharge his trusts, safeguard himself, and remain independent of others.”

In the Sunan collections, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“There are three whom Allah has undertaken to help: one who marries seeking chastity, a slave under a contract of emancipation who intends to make the required payment, and a debtor who intends to repay his debt.”

This hadith mentions what the believer needs: preserving his chastity, securing his freedom, and discharging his liabilities. He thus explained that these obligations—worshipping Allah, repaying debts, safeguarding oneself, and remaining independent of others—cannot be fully fulfilled without wealth. Whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory. There is no good in anyone who does not love to fulfil such a momentous obligation, without which religious life cannot be sustained. Such is this religious way, and it has many detailed applications. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14536

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said, after an earlier discussion:

The principle underlying this issue is that a prohibition indicates that the harm in what is prohibited outweighs its benefit. It is not prescribed for someone to commit himself to harm when he is enjoined to avert it. The foundation of this is that whenever Allah prohibits something in certain circumstances but permits it in others, its prohibited form cannot be valid and legally effective like its permissible form, producing the same legal consequences and achieving the same intended purpose.

This is what is meant by the statement, “Prohibition entails invalidity.” This is the position of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, the leading scholars of the Muslims, and their majority. Many speculative theologians among the Mu‘tazilīs and Ash‘arīs disagree, supposing that some prohibited acts are not invalid, such as an unlawful divorce or prayer in a usurped house, and similar cases.

Their argument is: “If prohibition entailed invalidity, these cases would constitute counterexamples to that causal principle. This shows that invalidity arises from some cause other than prohibition as such.” These men were not among the leading jurists versed in the detailed evidence of Islamic law.

They were therefore asked, “How can one know that an act of worship or a contract is invalid?”

They replied, “By the Lawgiver saying: ‘This is valid, and this is invalid.’” Yet these men were unfamiliar with the evidence of

---

## Page 14537

Islamic law as they actually occur. Rather, they posited things that might never occur, and others they imagined to be expressions used by the Lawgiver. But this is not how the matter works.

The Lawgiver never guided people through the expressions they mentioned. Nowhere in his words do we find: “The conditions of sale and marriage are such-and-such,” or “This act of worship or contract is valid,” or “It is invalid,” or similar expressions which they made the basis for determining validity and invalidity. Rather, all these expressions were introduced by those who introduced them among the proponents of juristic opinion and speculative theology.

Instead, the Lawgiver guided people through commands and prohibitions, through declaring things lawful and unlawful. From his statement concerning certain contracts, “This is not proper,” it was understood that they were invalid—as when he said of exchanging two mudds of dates for one mudd: “It is not proper.”

The Companions, the Successors, and the other leading scholars of the Muslims used prohibition alone as evidence that contracts were invalid. Thus, they cited the prohibition mentioned in the Quran as evidence that marriage to women within the prohibited degrees of kinship was invalid. The same applied to the invalidity of a contract uniting two sisters in marriage. Some of them imagined that two scriptural texts conflicted over the prohibition in this case and therefore withheld judgment. It has also been said that some of them permitted such a union.

Likewise, they cited the words of the Most High as evidence that remarrying a woman after divorcing her three times was invalid:

“If he divorces her, she is not lawful to him thereafter until she marries another husband.”

Similarly, the Companions cited the prohibition of shighār marriage as evidence of its invalidity. It is therefore a form of corruption, not soundness, for Allah does not love corruption, but loves soundness.

---

## Page 14538

And He does not forbid what He loves; He only forbids what He does not love. Thus they understood that what is prohibited is corrupt, not sound. Even if it contains some benefit, its harm outweighs that benefit. They knew that the purpose of Islamic law is to remove corruption and prevent it, not to bring it about and make it binding. If they were compelled to fulfil the obligations arising from unlawful contracts, they would be spreading corruption rather than setting things right. Allah does not set right the work of those who spread corruption.

His words:

“And when it is said to them, ‘Do not cause corruption on the earth’”

mean: do not act in disobedience to Allah Most High. Everyone who disobeys Allah spreads corruption, and prohibited acts constitute disobedience to Allah. The Lawgiver therefore prohibits them to prevent corruption and ward it off. There is not a single instance of something prohibited whose validity has been established by a scriptural text or scholarly consensus.

As for unlawful divorce and prayer in a usurped house, both are disputed, and there is no scriptural text establishing their validity that must be followed. Thus, anyone citing these two cases is left with no proof.

Some sales, however, are prohibited because they involve one party wronging the other, such as selling an animal made to appear more productive by leaving it unmilked, selling defective goods, intercepting goods before they reach the market, bidding up prices without intending to buy, and similar practices. Yet the Lawgiver has not made these sales binding like lawful sales. Rather, He has made them nonbinding, leaving the wronged party the choice: he may either annul them or approve them, for the right in this matter belongs to him. The Lawgiver did not prohibit them on account of a right belonging exclusively to Allah, as He prohibited acts of indecency. Rather, in these cases, if the wronged party knows the circumstances at the outset of the contract—for example, he knows of the defect, the deception, and the deliberate accumulation of milk in the udder, and knows the market price if he is arriving with goods—and consents to

---

## Page 14539

being underpaid by the buyer who intercepts him, that is permissible. Likewise, if he learns of this after the contract has been concluded, it is permissible if he consents; if he does not, he has the right to annul it. This shows that the contract is concluded as nonbinding, subject instead to ratification: the holder of the right may either approve or reject it.

This is agreed upon in cases such as the sale of defective goods, where consent is conditional upon freedom from defects. If that condition is unmet, the contract remains subject to ratification. Thus, it is binding under one set of circumstances and nonbinding under another. Where, however, a contract is nonbinding altogether and depends upon the consent of the person entitled to ratify it, there is disagreement. Most scholars recognize contracts whose effect is suspended pending ratification. This is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfa and others; it is also supported by most of Ahmad’s recorded statements and was adopted by his earlier followers, such as al-Khiraqī and others, as explained in detail elsewhere.

The point here is that some people consider this type to fall within the category of prohibited transactions. Then another group says, “Yet it is not invalid.” Prohibition must therefore entail invalidity.

Another group says, “Rather, this is invalid.” Some of them have therefore declared a sale involving artificial bidding invalid when the seller himself bids up the price or colludes in doing so. Others have declared invalid the marriage of a man who proposes after his brother has already proposed, as well as a sale in which he supplants his brother’s sale. Others have declared invalid the sale of defective goods whose defects have been concealed. Yet when confronted with the case of an animal whose milk has been allowed to accumulate in its udder, they suspended judgment. Others have upheld the validity of

---

## Page 14540

the marriage of a man who proposes after his brother has already proposed, without qualification, as well as a sale involving artificial bidding, with no right to annul it.

The correct understanding is that prohibition in such cases does not concern Allah’s rights, unlike marriage to women forbidden in marriage, marriage to a woman divorced three times, or transactions involving usury. Rather, it concerns a person’s rights. Thus, if the buyer knows that the owner of the goods is artificially bidding up their price and accepts this, the transaction is permissible. The same applies if he knows that someone else is doing so. Likewise, proposing to a woman already being courted is permissible whenever her first suitor gives permission.

Since the prohibition here concerns a person’s rights, the Lawgiver has not made the transaction valid and binding like a lawful transaction. Rather, He has upheld the wronged person’s right and granted him the choice: he may either ratify or annul it. Thus, the buyer in a sale involving artificial bidding may return the goods if he wishes, thereby achieving his purpose. Alternatively, he may accept them once he learns of the artificial bidding.

As for declaring the transaction invalid and requiring its reversal even if he accepts it, there is no basis for this. The same applies to returning defective goods, goods whose defects have been concealed, animals whose milk has been allowed to accumulate in their udders, and similar cases. Likewise, if the original suitor wishes to annul the marriage of the man who has wronged him and himself consents to marrying her, he may do so. If he wishes to ratify her marriage, he may do that instead. If he chooses to annul her marriage, matters return to their former state: she may marry him if she wishes, or decline if she wishes, since his purpose has been achieved by annulling the other suitor’s marriage.

If it is said, “He has turned the woman’s heart against me,” the reply is: “If you wish, we will punish him for this by preventing him from marrying that woman. This will be retribution for his wrong against you. Or, if you wish, you may forgive

---

## Page 14541

him, in which case we will uphold his marriage.”

The same applies to praying in a usurped house, slaughtering an animal with a usurped implement, cooking food with usurped firewood, and heating water with usurped fuel. All this is prohibited only because it involves wronging another person. That wrong is remedied by giving the wronged person his due.

If he compensates him for what he has taken, whether the use of his property or the property itself—paying him the rent for the house and the price of the firewood—and repents to Allah Most High for doing what He forbade, he has discharged his obligations towards both Allah and the person concerned. His prayer then becomes like prayer in a place one is permitted to use; the food becomes like food cooked with fuel one is permitted to use; and the slaughter becomes like slaughter performed with a knife one is permitted to use.

If he does not do so, the knife’s owner is entitled to payment for its use in slaughtering. The entire sheep does not become unlawful because of this questionable element. In this case, if he has eaten the food without paying him its full price, he is like someone who takes food in which another person has a share: his action is not unlawful, nor is it wholly lawful. If the food has been cooked, the owner of the fuel has a share in it.

The same applies to prayer: the sin of wrongdoing remains upon him, detracting from his prayer in proportion to it. His obligation is not discharged as fully as that of someone who has performed a complete prayer. Nor is he punished as someone who has not prayed; rather, he is punished in proportion to his sin. Likewise, the person who eats the food is punished in proportion to his sin.

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,
and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

---

## Page 14542

The reason it is said that someone who prays in an impure garment or place must repeat the prayer, unlike the case under discussion, is that in the former case he has no way to discharge his obligation except by repeating it. Here, however, he can do so by returning the wronged person’s land.

Praying in a silk garment, however, falls into the category in which the right involved belongs to Allah. Yet wearing silk is prohibited both during prayer and outside it; it is not prohibited only during prayer. Jurists have disagreed over cases of this kind.

Some say, “The prohibition here is due to a consideration external to the prohibited act.” They say the same about praying in a usurped house or garment, divorcing a woman during menstruation, selling at the time of the call to prayer, and similar cases. This claim has no real substance. If what is meant is that the sale itself involves neglecting prayer, and that the prayer itself involves wrongdoing, boasting, conceit, and other prohibited things, just as prayer in an impure garment involves contact with foul impurity, then this is incorrect. If, however, they mean that the consideration in question is not peculiar to prayer, but is common to prayer and other activities, then this is correct. Selling at the time of the call to prayer is prohibited only because it distracts from prayer, and this also applies to activities other than selling; it is not peculiar to selling.

But this distinction does not apply to divorcing a menstruating woman, since there is no such shared consideration in that case. They say, “It is prohibited only because it prolongs the waiting period, which is external to the divorce itself.”

The reply is: other prohibited acts are likewise prohibited only because they lead

---

## Page 14543

to harmful consequences external to them. Marrying two sisters at the same time is prohibited because it leads to the severing of kinship ties, which is external to marriage itself. Intoxicants and gambling were forbidden and declared filth of Satan’s handiwork because they lead to neglecting prayer and foster enmity and hatred—consequences external to intoxicants and gambling themselves. Usury is forbidden because it leads to the wrongful consumption of wealth, which is external to contracts involving gambling and usury.

Everything Allah has forbidden must therefore contain some feature that warrants its prohibition. He cannot prohibit something for no reason inherent in it whatsoever, but solely for a reason entirely foreign to it. That would be akin to punishing someone for another person’s sin, and Islamic law is free of such injustice. Some things, however, are prohibited to block the means to harm: apart from their capacity to lead to harm, they would contain no harm in themselves. An example is the prohibition of prayer at the forbidden times before sunrise and sunset, and similar cases. This is because the act involves harm by leading to resemblance to the polytheists. This is a feature inherent in it.

Then, among those who say that a prohibition may arise from a consideration inherent in the prohibited act or from a consideration external to it, some say that it may concern an attribute of the act rather than its essential nature, and thus indicate that the act is valid. An example is the prohibition of fasting on the two Eid days. They say: “It is prohibited because of the particular character of the two Eid days, not because of fasting as such. Thus, if someone fasts, the fast is valid, because it is termed ‘fasting.’”

---

## Page 14544

The reply to them is:

Fasting during menstruation, and praying without ritual purification or facing a direction other than the qiblah, are likewise acts of a prescribed kind. The prohibition concerns only a particular attribute: menstruation, ritual impurity, or facing a direction other than the qiblah. No intelligible distinction lacking any bearing on Islamic law is known between these cases.

If it is said that menstruation and ritual impurity are attributes of the menstruating woman and the person in a state of ritual impurity, whereas the other attribute pertains to the time, the reply is: An attribute of the circumstances in which an act takes place—its time and place—is like an attribute of the person performing it. If someone were to stand at ʿArafah outside its appointed time, or somewhere other than ʿArafah, that would not be valid, although the attribute here pertains to time and place. The same applies if someone were to stone the pillars outside the days of Minā or at somewhere other than the designated location: the attribute pertains to time and place. Facing a direction other than the qiblah concerns an attribute of the direction, not of the person, yet it is not permissible. Likewise, fasting at night would not be valid, even though this concerns time.

If it is said that night is not a legally prescribed time for fasting, the reply is: Neither is the day of Eid a legally prescribed time for fasting, just as the period of menstruation is not a legally prescribed time for fasting. The distinction must therefore be one recognized by Islamic law: it must be intelligible, and the Lawgiver must have made it operative in determining the ruling, making it the basis for the permissibility or prohibition that applies specifically to one of the two acts.

Many people invoke distinctions that have no reality and no bearing on Islamic law, or deny that an attribute has any effect on the ruling in the original case. A person may cite an attribute as the common ground between the original case and the case being compared with it, although that attribute is not shared by them; rather,

---

## Page 14545

it may be absent from both or from one of them.

Likewise, someone drawing a distinction may invoke an attribute that he claims fails to hold in one of the two cases, although it is not exclusive to that case but is shared by both. For example, they say: “The prohibition here concerns something inherent in the prohibited act, whereas there it concerns something external to it,” or, “There it concerns an attribute of the act rather than its essence.”

Yet a prohibition may concern something specific to an act of worship or a contract, or something common to it and other acts. Thus, a pilgrim in ihrām (ritual consecration) is forbidden certain things specifically because of his ihrām, such as shaving his head, wearing a turban or other prohibited clothing, having intercourse with his wife, and hunting land game. At the same time, he is forbidden unlawful sexual intercourse and wronging people with respect to the game they own. In such cases, a prohibition based on a reason common to both situations is more serious. Hence, if a pilgrim in ihrām kills game belonging to someone else, he must provide compensation to fulfil Allah’s right and reimburse the owner to fulfil the owner’s right. If he engages in unlawful sexual intercourse, that would invalidate his ihrām just as intercourse with his wife does, and he would also incur the prescribed punishment for unlawful sexual intercourse.

Accordingly, someone who wears during prayer something forbidden both within and outside prayer—such as clothing associated with arrogance and ostentation, including trailing garments and silk—has even stronger grounds for his prayer to be invalid than someone wearing an impure garment. The hadith recorded in the Sunan collections states:

“Allah does not accept the prayer of one who lets his garment trail.”

There is disagreement concerning impure clothing, as well as the amount of impurity. For men, however, praying in silk without a need is forbidden by explicit scriptural text and scholarly consensus. Likewise, selling after the call to prayer: if it has been prohibited, and other activities also distract from

---

## Page 14546

the Friday prayer, that makes the prohibition all the more emphatic. Everything that distracts from it is evil and corrupt, with no good in it. Ownership acquired in this way is like ownership obtained only through disobeying Allah, incurring His anger and defying Him—like that obtained only through other sins, such as disbelief, sorcery, soothsaying and sexual immorality. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A soothsayer’s fee is illicit, and a prostitute’s earnings are illicit.”

If a commodity cannot be acquired without abandoning the obligatory prayer, then ownership is obtained by abandoning prayer, just as the fee and earnings are obtained through soothsaying and prostitution. It is also like telling someone: “If you abandon prayer today, we will give you ten dirhams.” Whatever he receives for abandoning prayer is illicit; likewise, whatever is acquired through an exchange that entails abandoning prayer is illicit.

If someone hires a worker on condition that he does not pray, that condition is invalid, and whatever the worker receives for work performed during the time needed for prayer is illicit, even though paid work is permissible in principle. Exchanges are likewise permissible in principle, provided they do not encroach upon the duties Allah has prescribed.

If a sale takes place at this time and reversing it proves impossible, the buyer is entitled to the equivalent of the price he paid and must give the profit in charity. The seller is entitled to the equivalent of his commodity and must give any profit he has made in charity. Their mutual consent to this after prayer would be of no avail, for the prohibition here concerns Allah’s right, Exalted is He. It is therefore like their mutually agreeing to a prostitute’s payment. In that case, the payment is to be given in charity, according to

---

## Page 14547

the sounder of the two views; it is not to be given to the man who committed unlawful intercourse. The same applies to wine and similar cases in which someone has obtained an unlawful benefit: he must not be allowed to retain both the payment and what he received in exchange, for that is a greater sin than selling it. If wine may not lawfully be sold for a price, how much worse if someone is given both the wine and the price? Likewise, if a man may not commit unlawful intercourse even when he pays, how much worse if he receives both the money and the illicit intercourse? Rather, this money must be disbursed like other funds intended for the common good.

The same applies here: if someone sold a commodity at a profit during the call to prayer, he takes back his commodity. If it is no longer recoverable, he gives the profit in charity rather than giving it to the buyer, since that would have helped him make the purchase. The buyer recovers the price he paid and returns the commodity. If he has sold it at a profit, he gives that profit in charity rather than giving it to the seller, since that would give the seller two profits.

The jurists have differed over whether possession acquired under an invalid contract confers ownership, does not confer ownership, or requires a distinction according to whether the property remains recoverable. This is discussed at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14548

He was asked:

A man purchases an item with lawfully acquired money, without knowing whether it was lawfully or unlawfully obtained. If it was in fact unlawfully obtained, does he incur sin?

He answered:

If the buyer believes that the item in the seller’s possession belongs to him and purchases it from him on the basis of outward appearances, he incurs no sin. Even if the seller had actually stolen it, the buyer incurs neither sin nor punishment, whether in this world or the Hereafter. Liability and the obligation to reimburse rest with the person who deceived him and sold it to him.

If the item’s rightful owner subsequently comes forward, his item is returned to him, the buyer is refunded the price he paid, and the wrongdoing seller is punished. Whoever distinguishes between someone who knows and someone who does not know is correct; whoever fails to make this distinction is mistaken. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14549

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

The report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “ordered them to cut open the wine containers and break the wine jars” supports one of the two reported opinions permitting their destruction when putting a stop to wrongdoing, and establishes that a container takes the same ruling as its contents.

Similarly, it is established that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb and ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib ordered the burning of premises where wine was sold. Ahmad also expressly endorsed this. The same applies to destroying instruments used to produce prohibited musical compositions, namely musical instruments. Such penalties involving property are established by the Sunnah and the practice of the caliphs. Whoever claims that they have been abrogated has no evidence for that claim.

Some have argued on the basis of the following report:

“When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, learned that they had cooked donkey meat, he told them: ‘Pour it out and break the pots.’ They asked: ‘Could we not pour it out and wash the pots?’ He replied: ‘Do so.’”

They argue: “Perhaps, had they asked his permission regarding the wine vessels, he would have said the same.”

This was answered in two ways:

---

## Page 14550

The first response is that it is impermissible to set aside the revealed law on the basis of such suppositions. If we allowed the claim, concerning anything he commanded or prohibited, that he would have abrogated it had he been asked to reconsider, much of the revealed law could be set aside through such imaginings. One might, for example, say: “Had the Lord been asked to reduce the prayers below five, He would have reduced them”—and if this, and if that…

Such reasoning is invalid on two grounds:

First, we do not know that he would have done so had he been asked to reconsider. The fact that this happened in one instance does not establish that it would happen in every other instance, unless the circumstances were identical in every respect and no impediments existed. This is not known to be the case.

Second, even supposing that he would have done so had they asked, they did not ask. If abrogation depended on their asking, and they did not ask, then no abrogation occurred. Likewise, the initial imposition of an obligation or prohibition may depend on their asking. As Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, do not ask about things which, if made known to you, would distress you.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Those before you were destroyed only because of their excessive questioning and their disagreement with their prophets.”

He also said:

“The Muslim whose offense against other Muslims is greatest is the one who asks about something that was not forbidden, only for it to become forbidden because of his question.”

Concerning Hajj, when they asked him, “Every year?” he replied:

“Had I said yes, it would have become obligatory; and had it become obligatory, you would not have been able to fulfil it.”

Concerning the Ramadan night prayers, he said:

“The only thing that kept me from coming out to you was my fear that it would be made obligatory upon you and you would be unable to observe it.”

It has thus been made clear by

---

## Page 14551

the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, that asking questions and taking action may occasion the initial establishment of an obligation or prohibition. If that cause did not occur, and thus no obligation or prohibition arose, neither becomes established after his death, peace and blessings be upon him. Likewise, such questioning or action may occasion the lifting of an obligation or prohibition; but if the cause did not occur, the ruling is not lifted after his death.

The following statement of ʿĀ’ishah does not fall into this category:

“Had the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, seen what women did after him, he would have barred them from the mosque, just as the women of the Children of Israel were barred.”

ʿĀ’ishah was far too mindful of Allah to sanction setting aside the revealed law after the Prophet’s death. She meant only that, had the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, seen the misconduct involved in some women’s going out, he would have prevented them from going out. Her point was that his saying:

“Do not prevent Allah’s female servants from attending Allah’s mosques,”

although expressed in general terms, is qualified by excluding outings that involve misconduct. Thus, most jurists have said that young women whose going out involves misconduct are to be prevented from doing so. The intent, then, was to qualify the general wording. Evidently, she knew from the Prophet’s conduct, peace and blessings be upon him, that he would not permit outings of this kind; she did not intend to prohibit women from going out altogether. Not all women had introduced such conduct; she intended only to prevent those who had done so.

The second response:

This hadith concerning the vessels containing donkey meat also provides evidence in the matter under discussion. He initially ordered the vessels to be broken; then, when they asked his permission

---

## Page 14552

to wash them, he permitted it. This shows that breaking them is not obligatory, but does not show that it is impermissible. Rather, both options are permissible: breaking them and washing them. The same applies to wine vessels: they may be destroyed or purified. If destroying them better serves the public good, they are to be destroyed. If the owner of wine vessels and musical instruments purified the vessels and washed the instruments, that would be permissible by agreement. However, if he openly commits the wrongdoing and is consequently rebuked for it, he deserves to be punished by their destruction. The Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, had not known of the prohibition, so they were excused from having to destroy them. And Allah knows best.

He was asked about a man who trades in caps:

Is he permitted to buy and sell mir'izzī caps and earn money from them, and likewise comparable articles of sāmit silk? Or is this forbidden to him because caps are worn by men rather than women? Is wearing them permissible for soldiers, boys who have not reached puberty, Jews, Christians and others in a similar position, or is all of that forbidden?

May someone trading in these and other goods sell an item worth one dirham for two dirhams, or thereabouts, to desert dwellers, women or children who do not know its value, despite knowing that, if the buyer

---

## Page 14553

needed to sell it for cash later that day, he would not obtain even the dirham that was its original value, but less than that—or is this forbidden to him? What amount of profit is permissible on goods sold through bargaining? Is it one-third, less, or more?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

As for silk caps, both men and women are forbidden to wear them. For men, this is because they are silk, and wearing silk is forbidden to men according to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and scholarly consensus—even if it is lined with cotton or linen. For women, this is because caps are men’s attire. Moreover:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed women who imitate men and men who imitate women.”

As for boys who have not reached puberty wearing silk, scholars hold two well-known views. The stronger view, however, is that it is not permissible. Whatever a man is forbidden to do, he is also forbidden to allow a young child to do. Rather, he must instruct the child to pray when he reaches seven and beat him for neglecting it when he reaches ten. How, then, could it be lawful for him to dress the child in forbidden clothing?

Umar ibn al-Khattāb saw a silk garment on a young son of al-Zubayr, tore it, and said: “Do not dress them in silk.” Likewise, Ibn Umar tore a silk garment that his son was wearing. Whatever is forbidden to wear may not be manufactured or sold to anyone who will wear it from among

---

## Page 14554

those for whom wearing it is forbidden. There is no distinction in this regard between soldiers and others. A man may not earn his living by sewing silk garments for someone who is forbidden to wear them, for this is cooperation in sin and transgression, like assisting in acts of indecency and the like. Likewise, silk may not be sold to a man who will wear it when he is forbidden to do so. Selling silk to women, however, is permissible, as is selling it to a disbeliever. Umar ibn al-Khattāb sent some silk that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had given him to a polytheist.

As for sales, a trusting buyer may only be charged the same price as other buyers. No one may exploit his trust by making an abnormally excessive profit at his expense. Some scholars set this at one-third, and others at one-sixth. Others said that the matter should be determined by customary practice: the profit normally made from those who bargain should also be the profit made from a trusting buyer.

A trusting buyer has been defined as someone who does not bargain, but simply says, “Take the money and give me the goods.” It has also been defined as someone who does not know the value of the goods. Neither may be grossly overcharged. A hadith states:

“Overcharging a trusting buyer is usury.”

Anyone known to overcharge such buyers deserves punishment. Indeed, he should be barred from trading in the Muslims’ marketplace until he is made to obey Allah and His Messenger. The buyer who has been overcharged has the right to rescind the sale, return the goods to the seller, and recover the price he paid. If this seller who has overcharged repents

---

## Page 14555

and is unable to restore to those he has wronged what is rightfully theirs, he should give charity on their behalf equal to the amount he wrongfully took from them, so that he is discharged of that liability.

In negotiated sales, when buyers are familiar with the prices at which goods are generally purchased, other buyers should be charged the same prices as they are. Thus, the seller must not make a greater profit from a trusting buyer than from anyone else. Likewise, when someone in desperate need can find what he needs only with this seller, the seller should make the same profit from him as from someone who is not in such need. It is reported in the Sunan collections:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited sales involving a person in desperate need.”

If the need concerns something indispensable—for example, if people desperately need the food or clothing he possesses—he must sell it to them only at the customary price, whether he agrees or not, and they must not pay him more than that. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man sold wheat for a deferred payment. When payment fell due, the debtor had nothing but wheat. May the seller accept wheat from him?

He replied:

Yes, he may accept wheat from him, and this is not usury according to

---

## Page 14556

the majority of scholars, such as Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī, and a group of Ahmad’s followers. If accepting wheat is easier for the debtor than requiring him to sell it and pay in dirhams, it is preferable for the creditor to accept the wheat. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man bought grain for a specified price in dirhams, payable at a later date. When payment fell due, the creditor sought to collect what he was owed, but found nothing except grain, valued at the price prevailing at that time and assessed in dirhams toward discharging the debt. May the creditor accept the grain at the prevailing price?

He replied:

There are two opinions on this matter. An example is someone selling wheat on deferred payment and then accepting wheat in place of the price owed. According to Mālik and Ahmad, this is not valid. According to Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, there is nothing wrong with it; this is also the view of some of Ahmad’s followers.

He was asked:

A man is owed a debt by another man who has a son. The debtor’s son said to the creditor:

“Sell me a commodity on deferred payment, and I will sell it for immediate payment in dirhams

---

## Page 14557

and pay off what my father owes?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the buyer’s aim is to obtain dirhams, and he intends to purchase a commodity on deferred payment so that he can sell it and receive its price, this is called “tawarruq” (obtaining cash through purchase and resale), because his aim is silver money, not the commodity itself.

The scholars have differed over whether this is blameworthy. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and a group of scholars of Medina, including Mālikīs and others, disapproved of it. This is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. Others permitted it, but the stronger view is that it is blameworthy.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has an outstanding debt and needs goods or an animal for his own use or for trade. He asks someone to sell it to him on credit, but that person does not have it. May the person approached buy it and then sell it to him on deferred payment? May he appoint the prospective buyer as his agent to purchase it, then sell it to him afterward at a profit they agreed upon before the purchase?

He replied:

If a debtor has the means to repay his debt, he must do so. If he is in financial hardship, he must be granted more time. It is not permissible to replace his debt with a new debt against him through a transaction or otherwise. As for a deferred-payment sale undertaken independently, if the buyer’s intention is to benefit from the commodity

---

## Page 14558

and to trade in it, the sale is permissible if conducted in a lawful manner. But if his aim is to obtain dirhams, so he buys it for one hundred payable later and sells it in the market for seventy paid immediately, this is blameworthy and prohibited according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views. This is called “tawarruq.” ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz—may Allah be pleased with him—said:

“Tawarruq is the sister of usury.”

He was asked:

A man asks someone for a commodity worth fifteen, and the latter says that he will only sell it for twenty-eight. Is it permissible for the buyer to purchase it, knowing of the increase?

He replied:

If the buyer needs dirhams and purchases the commodity in order to sell it and receive its price, this is called “tawarruq.” If the buyer’s purpose is to obtain silver money, this is blameworthy according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views, as ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said:

“Tawarruq is the sister of usury.”

Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“If you value it at a cash price and then sell it on deferred payment, that is dirhams exchanged for dirhams.”

This is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

---

## Page 14559

He was asked:

Two merchants were offered a commodity for sale, and each wished to buy it. One said to the other, “I will buy it jointly for you and me.” His intention was to avoid being outbid by the other and having the other acquire it alone; this was why he sought the partnership. One of them then purchased it, paying its price equally from their funds. Is this sale valid under these circumstances, or does it involve deceiving the seller?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If there are others in the market bidding against the two of them, but one refrains specifically from bidding against his companion because they are buying jointly, this is not forbidden. Bidding remains open; one has merely refrained from bidding against the other.

This differs from a situation in which the market traders agree not to compete in bidding for goods they need, so that the owner will sell them below their value and they can divide them among themselves. This may harm the owner more than intercepting his goods before they reach the market when he sells them at a negotiated price. Such conduct plainly involves depriving people of their due. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14560

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Among the brokers in a caravanserai are three who buy from one another on each other’s behalf, then raise their bids and divide the profit. Is this permissible?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. An auctioneer acting as the seller’s agent in calling for bids may not enter into partnership with a bidder without the seller’s knowledge, for he would then effectively be bidding and buying himself. This is a betrayal of the seller. When someone acts in this way, no one is obliged to outbid him, and he does not faithfully serve the seller’s interests in seeking higher bids and concluding the auction.

If a group colludes in this, they deserve a severe discretionary punishment that deters them and others like them from such dishonesty. Their punishment may include barring them from conducting auctions until their repentance becomes evident. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14561

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An insolvent man obtained wheat on credit from another man at several times its value. Its price remained unchanged from the time he obtained it until payment fell due. The creditor supplied it on credit, described its qualities, and stated that each irdabb (a measure of grain) was worth sixteen. He drew up a deed, and they agreed on a price of thirty-two per irdabb. The debtor sold it, with supporting evidence and witnesses, for twelve dirhams per irdabb, contrary to the description given by the borrower. Payment has now fallen due, and the debtor is unable to meet the demand for payment. Should he be required to pay its fair market value, the amount recorded against him, or wheat equivalent to the wheat supplied?

He replied:

An insolvent person may not be pressed to pay what he cannot afford. Even if the debt is rightfully due, he must be granted respite. If the transaction is usurious, only the principal may be demanded. Selling a specific item that is not present, without describing it, is an invalid sale: the item or its equivalent must be returned, and the seller is not entitled to the stipulated price.

How much more so if he says, “This is currently worth such-and-such, and I will sell it to you on credit for such-and-such a higher amount”? This is usury. As Ibn ‘Abbās—may Allah be pleased with him—said:

“If you value it at the cash price and sell it for cash, there is no harm; but if you value it at the cash price and sell it on credit

---

## Page 14562

then that is merely dirhams exchanged for dirhams.” This seller valued the goods at their cash price and sold them on credit. If the buyer rescinded the sale because the goods did not meet the stated description, and could neither return the goods themselves to the seller nor leave them in anyone’s safekeeping, so he sold them and kept the proceeds for the seller, he owes nothing beyond those proceeds, provided he sold them at their fair market value. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man who engaged in usury died leaving wealth and a son who knew of his dealings. Does inheriting this wealth make it lawful for the son, or not?

He replied:

Whatever portion the son knows to be usurious gain, he must relinquish, returning it to its rightful owners if possible; otherwise, he must give it in charity. The remainder is not unlawful for him. However, it is recommended that he forgo the doubtful portion, provided he is not obliged to use it to repay a debt or support his dependants. If the father acquired it through usurious transactions that some jurists permit, the heir may benefit from it. If lawful and unlawful wealth are mixed together and he does not know the amount of either, he should divide it into two equal shares.

---

## Page 14563

He was asked:

What should a man do when his lawful wealth becomes mixed with unlawful wealth?

He replied:

He must weigh out the unlawful portion and return it to its owner. The lawful portion belongs to him. If he does not know the owner and cannot ascertain his identity, he should give it in charity on his behalf.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman used to be a singer and, in her ignorance, amassed considerable wealth. She has since repented, performed Hajj to the House of Allah Most High, and remained steadfast in obeying Allah. If she uses the wealth she acquired, whether lawfully or otherwise, for food and charity, will she be rewarded for doing so?

He replied:

If the money is earned in exchange for an item or service that is lawful in itself, but becomes unlawful because of the intended purpose—for example, selling grapes to someone who will make wine from them, or being hired to press grapes for wine or to transport wine—then the person does this in return for payment, but it is not lawful for him to consume that payment.

---

## Page 14564

But if the item or service is itself unlawful, as with a prostitute’s fee or the price of wine, no judgment is issued entitling the recipient to payment before he has received it. If the payer has already handed it over, however, its return is not ordered, for that would assist them in sin by allowing them to retain both the payment and what they received in exchange. This money is not lawful for the prostitute, the wine seller, or others like them; rather, it is to be spent for the benefit of the Muslims.

If the prostitute and the wine seller repent and are poor, they may be given enough of this money to meet their needs. If a person can engage in trade or practise a craft, such as weaving or spinning, he is given enough to serve as working capital. If they borrow some of it to earn a living and do not repay the loan, that is better.

As for someone who gives it in charity because he believes that it is not lawful for him to give it in charity, he is rewarded for doing so. But if he gives it in charity as an owner gives from his own property, Allah does not accept it—for Allah accepts only what is good and lawful. This money is impure, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A prostitute’s fee is impure.”

He was also asked:

About sources of revenue involving zakat and damān arrangements in markets and elsewhere, when payments to them are made by

---

## Page 14565

the sultan through the troops’ revenue allotments: is this lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

If a man is in need and the source of revenue contains both lawful and unlawful funds, or is of doubtful lawfulness, then, if he has no alternative but to accept it, he should spend it on external expenses, such as fodder for his mount, government levies, and similar expenses. If he gives it in charity to the poor, or intends it as charity on behalf of those entitled to it, that is good. Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

About a man who holds a concession involving atrūn. The customary practice among Muslims was for poor men to buy atrūn and sell it for three copper coins per ratl. During the reign of Baybars, someone took over the atrūn concession on the condition that no one could sell or buy except through the concession holder, at twenty-three dirhams per qintār. Is this lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

Where someone has acquired atrūn lawfully, no one may compel anyone to buy from him or prevent anyone from buying from someone else. Rather, if he acquires it lawfully and sells it as other Muslim merchandise is sold, that is permissible.

---

## Page 14566

The shaykh and imam, the scholar who practised what he knew, Shaykh al-Islām and pillar of the eminent imams, whose blessings reached the people of the two Iraqs and Syria, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-'Abbās Ahmad ibn 'Abd al-Halīm ibn 'Abd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī, later al-Dimashqī—may Allah grant the Muslims benefit from his blessings—was asked while in Egypt:

About a man who quoted an earlier jurist as saying:

“It is impossible to eat what is lawful; nothing lawful can be found in this age.”

He was asked, “Why is that?”

He explained, “The spoils from the Battle of al-Mansūrah were not distributed, and wealth became intermingled through transactions involving them.”

He was told, “A man may hire himself out for permissible work and receive his wages lawfully.” But he maintained that the dirham itself was unlawful.

He was then asked, “How could the dirham’s status change initially, becoming unlawful through a prohibited means, yet not change so as to become lawful through a legitimate means?” What is the ruling on this?

He replied—may Allah be pleased with him:

Praise be to Allah.

The person who says, “It is impossible to eat what is lawful; nothing lawful can be found in this age,” is mistaken and wrong in his assertion, by the agreement of the imams of Islam. Statements of this kind were made by some adherents of

---

## Page 14567

religious innovation, as well as some proponents of unsound jurisprudence and some practitioners of misguided devotion. The imams condemned this. Even Imam Ahmad, renowned for his scrupulousness, would condemn such a claim.

A devout worshipper came and mentioned something of this kind to him. He said:

“Look at this vile fellow, declaring the Muslims’ wealth unlawful!”

He also said:

“I have heard that some of these people say, ‘A thief should not have his hand cut off, because the property is not legally protected.’”

Some of those claiming affiliation with religious scholarship in our own time have made similar statements, based on this specious argument: that unlawful wealth has come to predominate because of widespread usurpation and invalid contracts, and that lawful wealth has not been distinguished from unlawful wealth. This misconception also affected a group of jurists who authored books, so they ruled that a person may take no more than necessity requires. Another group, seeing the hardship this entailed, closed the door to scrupulousness altogether.

They thus fell into two groups: the permissivists make no distinction between the lawful and the unlawful. Rather, the lawful is whatever falls into their hands, and the unlawful is whatever they themselves forbid. They adopted this same false assumption—that unlawful wealth had spread throughout the earth—and recognized that people cannot do without food and clothing. They therefore began obtaining these by whatever means they could.

Let the sensible person consider the consequences of that misguided scrupulousness: how it led to casting off the restraints of the religion of Islam.

---

## Page 14568

These people recount stories of misguided scrupulousness, some falsely attributed to the people concerned and others mistaken.

For example, they relate that when Imam Ahmad’s son Sālih became a judge, Ahmad would not have bread baked in his son’s house. When his family baked bread in his son’s oven, he refused to eat it, so they threw it into the Tigris; thereafter, he would not eat anything caught in the Tigris.

This is among the most outrageous lies and fabrications against such an imam. Only someone utterly ignorant, or exceptionally cunning in deceiving people and scheming to obtain their wealth, would behave like this. Allah kept him free of both. Every scholar knows that his son did not become a judge during his lifetime, but only after his death. Rather, the caliph al-Mutawakkil had awarded his children and household grants from the public treasury. Abū Abd Allāh instructed them not to accept the ruler’s grants, but they pleaded their need, and some of them accepted.
