He therefore refrained from eating food purchased with their wealth or using their fires to bake bread or heat water, because they had accepted the ruler’s grants.

They asked him about this money: “Is it unlawful?”

“No,” he replied.

“May we use it to perform Hajj?” they asked.

“Yes,” he replied. He explained that he abstained from it only lest it lead him to become involved with the caliph in pursuing the latter’s wishes. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Accept a grant so long as it is simply a grant. But if it becomes payment in exchange for your religion, do not accept it.”

Even if blood, carrion, pork, and every unlawful thing in existence were thrown into the Tigris, neither its catch nor the river itself would become unlawful. Some people have been led by excessive scrupulousness to adopt a course of action based on their own earnest judgment

---

## Page 14569

and are rewarded for their good intentions, even though the prescribed course differs from what they have done. Examples include someone who refuses to eat food sold in the markets, eating only what grows in the wilderness, or who refuses to eat from the wealth of Muslims, eating only from that of cultivators, and similar practices whose adherents mean well but have no interpretive justification for their actions. Yet the correct, prescribed course is otherwise.

Allah, glorified be He, created His creatures to worship Him and commanded them to do so. It is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has commanded the believers to do what He commanded the messengers to do. He said: ‘O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds.’ And He said: ‘O you who believe, eat of the good things We have provided for you.’ Then he mentioned a man who travels a long way, dishevelled and dusty, stretching his hand towards heaven: ‘My Lord! My Lord!’ Yet his food is unlawful, his clothing is unlawful, and he has been nourished with what is unlawful. How, then, can such a man’s supplication be answered?”

Thus the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that Allah commanded the believers, just as He commanded the messengers, to eat good things, just as He commanded them to do righteous deeds. Righteous deeds are not possible without food, drink, clothing, and whatever else a servant needs: a dwelling, a mount, weapons with which to fight, warhorses on which to fight, books from which to learn, and similar things without which Allah’s commands cannot be fulfilled. Whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory.

Since fulfilling these obligations is a duty upon all servants, and they cannot be fulfilled without such wealth, how can it be said that it is scarce? Rather, it is abundant and prevalent; indeed, it is

---

## Page 14570

what predominates in people’s wealth.

If unlawful wealth were predominant, and religious life among the general population could not be sustained without it, one of two things would necessarily follow: either most people would abandon their obligations, or what is unlawful would become permissible for most people. Both conclusions are false.

Scrupulousness is one of the foundations of religion. An authentic hadith relates from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“What is lawful is clear, and what is unlawful is clear. Between them are doubtful matters about which many people have no knowledge. Whoever avoids doubtful matters safeguards his honour and religion, while whoever falls into doubtful matters falls into what is unlawful, like a shepherd grazing his flock around a protected pasture, which he is on the verge of entering. Indeed, every king has a protected domain, and Allah’s protected domain is what He has forbidden. Indeed, within the body is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the whole body is sound; if it is corrupt, the rest of the body becomes corrupt because of it. It is the heart.”

Another hadith states:

“Leave what makes you doubt for what does not make you doubt.”

He also saw a date lying on the ground and said:

“Were it not that I feared it might be from charity, I would have eaten it.”

This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The matter becomes clear by setting out several principles.

First: Not everything a particular jurist believes to be unlawful is actually unlawful. Rather, what is unlawful is that whose prohibition is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, consensus, or analogical reasoning that weighs in favour of its prohibition. Matters over which scholars disagree must be referred back to these foundations. Among people are

---

## Page 14571

those who have grown up following the school of a particular imam, sought a ruling from a particular jurist, or heard an account related from certain shaykhs, and then wish to make all Muslims follow that view. This is a mistake, and there are comparable examples.

One is the question of war booty. The Sunnah is for it to be collected, a fifth set aside, and the remainder distributed equitably among the combatants. May the ruler grant extra allotments from its four-fifths? There are two opinions on this.

The jurists of the frontier regions, Abū Hanīfa, Ahmad, and the scholars of hadith hold that this is permissible, on the basis of the report in the Sunan collections:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, awarded a quarter as extra allotments on the outward journey, after setting aside the fifth, and a third on the return journey, after setting aside the fifth.”

Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib, Mālik, and al-Shāfiʿī held that this was not permissible. Rather, Mālik permitted extra allotments from the fifth, while al-Shāfiʿī permitted them only from one-fifth of that fifth. Ahmad used to marvel that this Sunnah had not reached Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib and Mālik despite their extensive knowledge.

It is authentically reported in the two Sahīh collections that Ibn ʿUmar said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent us on an expedition towards Najd. Our shares amounted to twelve camels each, and he awarded each of us one additional camel.”

It is clear that, if each share was twelve camels, one-fifth of the fifth would not suffice to provide an additional camel for each person. That would only be possible if each share was twenty-four camels.

Likewise, if the ruler grants some combatants more than others for an overriding benefit, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave

---

## Page 14572

Salama ibn al-Akwaʿ the shares of both a foot soldier and a horseman during the expedition of Dhū Qarad, then this is permissible according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. Some scholars do not permit it, as noted above.

Likewise, if the ruler says, “Whatever anyone takes is his,” and the spoils are not divided, this is permissible according to one of the scholars’ two opinions. This is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. According to the other opinion, it is not permissible; this is the well-known position of al-Shāfiʿī’s school. There is disagreement within both schools.

The question of “war booty in later times” rests on a principle of this kind. Examples include the spoils taken by the Seljuk Turks and those the Muslims took from the Christians in the frontier regions of Syria and Egypt. Regarding these spoils, some jurists—such as Abū Muhammad al-Juwaynī and al-Nawawī—ruled that a Muslim may neither purchase anything from them, nor have sexual relations with any female captive taken among them, nor acquire ownership of any of their wealth. This opinion led to harmful consequences whose extent Allah knows.

Abū Muhammad ibn Sibāʿ al-Shāfiʿī opposed them, ruling that the ruler is under no obligation whatsoever to distribute the spoils or set aside their fifth, and that he may give preference to a foot soldier, deprive some combatants of a share, and favor others. He claimed that the conduct of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, supported this. This opinion contradicts scholarly consensus, while the preceding opinion is likewise invalid and objectionable. Both are deviations.

The correct position in such cases is that if the ruler says, “Whatever anyone takes is his,”

---

## Page 14573

then, if this is held to be permissible, whoever takes anything acquires ownership of it and must set aside its fifth. If, however, the ruler has not said this or granted them the spoils, but instead seeks something from them that is unanimously deemed impermissible—or if it is held that he must distribute them justly and may not authorize indiscriminate seizure—then the spoils are property jointly owned by those who captured them, and no one else has any entitlement to them. Whoever takes an amount corresponding to his rightful share may therefore do so.

If he is uncertain about this, he may either exercise caution and observe the recommended degree of scrupulousness, or act upon what he considers most likely. Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.

The same applies to “sharecropping” in which the cultivator supplies the seed, which some people call mukhābara. The jurists have disagreed about it, but its permissibility is established by the authentic Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He engaged the people of Khaybar to cultivate the land at their own expense in return for half its yield of fruit and crops.

As for his prohibition of mukhābara, its meaning is explained in an authentic hadith: it refers to stipulating that the landowner receive the crops from a particular plot. Likewise, leasing land in return for a share of its yield was permitted by Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad according to the well-known report from him, but prohibited by Mālik and, according to another report, Ahmad. There are many similar examples. This, then, is clear.

The second principle:

If a Muslim enters into a transaction that he believes to be permissible and receives the proceeds, other Muslims may transact with him concerning such wealth, even if they do not consider that transaction permissible. For it is established that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb

---

## Page 14574

—may Allah be pleased with him—was informed that one of his officials was accepting wine from non-Muslims under Muslim protection in payment of the jizya. He said, “May Allah curse so-and-so! Did he not know that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘May Allah curse the Jews! Animal fat was forbidden to them, so they melted it down, sold it, and consumed its proceeds’?”

Then ʿUmar said, “Let them undertake its sale, and take the proceeds from them.” Thus, ʿUmar instructed them to accept from those non-Muslims the dirhams for which they had sold the wine, because they believed this to be permissible in their religion.

For this reason, the scholars said that if unbelievers conduct transactions among themselves which they believe to be permissible, receive the wealth exchanged, and subsequently embrace Islam, that wealth remains lawful for them. If they bring their case before us, we allow them to retain it, whether they seek our judgment before or after embracing Islam.

Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains due to you of usury, if you are believers.”

He commanded them to relinquish the usury still owed to them, but did not command them to return what they had already received, because they had regarded it as lawful.

If a Muslim conducts transactions which he believes to be permissible—such as the usurious legal devices that some followers of Abū Hanīfa permit in their fatwas—and receives the proceeds, or enters into a sharecropping agreement in which the cultivator supplies the seed, or leases land in return for a share of its yield, or undertakes similar transactions and receives the wealth, then other Muslims may, with even greater reason, transact with him concerning that wealth, even if they do not believe the original transaction to be permissible. Even if it later became clear to him that the stronger view was that it was forbidden, he would not be required to divest himself of the wealth he had earned

---

## Page 14575

on the basis of a defensible interpretation; for such a person has an even stronger claim to pardon and excuse than an unbeliever acting on an interpretation. When some jurists imposed restrictions in this matter upon a scrupulous person, they drove him to transact with unbelievers and abandon dealings with Muslims. Yet it is well known that Allah and His Messenger do not command a Muslim to partake of the wealth of unbelievers while shunning the wealth of Muslims. Rather, Muslims have a greater claim to every good, and unbelievers to every evil.

The Third Principle

What is unlawful falls into two categories. The first is what is unlawful because of its nature, such as carrion, blood, and pork. If any of these mixes with water, another liquid, or other food and changes its taste, colour, or smell, it renders it unlawful. If it does not change it, there is disagreement, which is not our subject here.

The second category is what is unlawful because of the manner in which it was acquired, such as property taken by usurpation or through an invalid contract. When this is mixed with lawful property, it does not render the lawful property unlawful. Thus, if a man usurps dirhams, dinars, flour, wheat, or bread and mixes it with his own property, this does not render the whole unlawful for either party. Rather, if the two portions are alike, they can divide the mixture, each taking the amount to which he is entitled, even if each receives some of the other’s actual property in exchange for the equivalent received by the other.

Is mixing to be treated as destruction? There are two views on this within the schools of al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, and others.

The first is that it is treated as destruction, so he gives the owner an equivalent of what he is owed, from whatever source he chooses.

---

## Page 14576

The second is that his right remains attached to the mixture, so the owner may claim his due from it. This is a useful principle, for many people imagine that when unlawfully acquired dirhams are mixed with lawful dirhams, the whole becomes unlawful. This is mistaken. Some scholars exercised scrupulous caution only when the sums were small; where they were large, however, I know of no disagreement.

The Fourth Principle

When the owner of property cannot be identified, it is to be used for the benefit of Muslims, according to the great majority of scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad, and others. Thus, if someone holds usurped property, borrowed items, deposits entrusted for safekeeping, or items held as collateral, and has lost hope of identifying their owners, he should give them in charity on their behalf, use them for the benefit of Muslims, or hand them over to a just distributor who will use them for the benefit of Muslims in ways recognized by Islamic law.

Some jurists say that the property must be held indefinitely until its owners are identified. The first view, however, is correct. Keeping property indefinitely for someone whom there is no hope of finding serves no purpose; rather, it exposes the property to loss or seizure by wrongdoers.

‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd once bought a slave girl and went into his house to fetch the payment. When he came out, he could not find the seller. He therefore began going around among the poor, distributing the purchase money to them in charity and saying:

“O Allah, this is on behalf of the slave girl’s owner. If it is accepted, well and good; if it is not accepted, its reward is mine, and I shall owe him its equivalent on the Day of Resurrection.”

Likewise, some of the Successors ruled that someone who had misappropriated spoils of war and then repented after those entitled to them had dispersed should give that property in charity on their behalf. This ruling was approved by the Companions and Successors whom it reached, including Mu‘āwiyah and others among the people of Syria. This makes clear:

---

## Page 14577

the fifth principle:

This principle reveals the heart of the matter: in Islamic law, what is unknown is treated as nonexistent or beyond one’s capacity. Allah, glorified and exalted, says:

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.”

And He, exalted, says:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

Thus, whenever Allah commands us to do something, that command is conditional upon our having the ability and opportunity to carry it out. Whatever we are unable to ascertain or carry out is therefore no longer required of us. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said concerning lost property:

“If its owner comes, return it to him; otherwise, it is Allah’s property, which He gives to whomever He wills.”

This lost property belonged to someone who had lost it. When identifying its owner proved impossible, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is Allah’s property, which He gives to whomever He wills.”

This indicates that Allah willed to remove that owner’s ownership of it and give it to the finder who had publicly announced it for a year. The imams agree that, after announcing it for a year, the finder may give it in charity. He may also take ownership of it if he is poor. But may he take ownership of it if he is wealthy? There are two well-known views on this. The position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad is that this is permissible, whereas Abū Hanīfah does not permit it.

If a man dies and no heir of his is known, his property is used for the benefit of Muslims, even if he actually has an unidentified heir. Indeed, if the heir is subsequently identified, his property is to be handed over to him, although, before he was identified, disbursing it to its recipient was permissible

---

## Page 14578

and its recipient’s accepting it was not unlawful, even though many people die leaving agnatic heirs who have not yet been identified.

Once this is clear, we may say:

If a Muslim knows that particular property has been seized unlawfully or acquired under contracts where taking possession does not render it lawful, he must avoid it. If I know that someone has stolen property, misappropriated property entrusted to him, or seized it from its owner by force and without right, I may not accept it from him—whether as a gift, in exchange for something, as payment of wages or the price of goods sold, or as repayment of a loan—for it is the very property belonging to the person who was wronged. If, however, he acquired that property on the basis of a tenable legal interpretation recognized in the school of one of the imams, I may accept it in payment for goods sold, wages, a loan, or other debts.

If its status is unknown, then what is unknown is treated as nonexistent. The presumption regarding property in a Muslim’s possession is that it belongs to him, if he claims ownership, or that he has authority over it—as the administrator of an endowment, the guardian of an orphan, or the administrator of the public treasury—or that he acts as an agent concerning it. A Muslim’s or a non-Muslim protected subject’s dealings with property in the capacity of owner or guardian are permissible.

Thus, if I do not know the status of the property in his possession, I proceed on the basis of this presumption. Then, even if he had in fact seized that dirham unlawfully, without my knowing it, I was ignorant of that fact, and what is unknown is treated as nonexistent. My accepting payment for goods sold, wages for work, or repayment of a loan is therefore no less permissible than my taking lost property, for I took the lost property without

---

## Page 14579

giving anything in return, and I did not subsequently discover its owner. Yet I know of no owner of this property other than this man, and I have taken it in settlement of what is owed to me. How, then, could this be unlawful for me? However, if that man is known to have unlawfully acquired property among his possessions, dealings with him are avoided as a matter of religious caution. If most of his property is unlawfully acquired, scholars disagree over dealing with him. As for a Muslim against whom nothing adverse is known, there is no reason at all to regard dealings with him as doubtful. Anyone who avoids dealing with him out of religious caution has introduced into the religion an innovation for which Allah has granted no authority.

This also clarifies the ruling concerning all other property. Someone making this mistake says:

“The meat and milk that people consume may originally have been plundered or seized unlawfully.”

The answer is:

What is unknown is treated as nonexistent. If we do not know of it, then, as far as we are concerned, it is as though it never happened. This is because Allah has forbidden invalid transactions only because of the injustice they involve. Allah Most High says in His mighty Book:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people might uphold justice. And We sent down iron, with its great might and benefits for people, so that Allah may know who supports Him and His messengers without seeing Him. Indeed, Allah is All-Powerful, Almighty.”

Forcible seizure in all its forms, theft, and betrayal of trust are all acts of injustice. That being so, consider this wronged person whose property was taken without right through a sale or as wages: it was taken from him without the purchaser knowing this, and is then transferred from the purchaser

---

## Page 14580

to someone else, and then to yet another person. He knows that these people have not wronged him; rather, the one who wronged him is the person who transgressed against him. But if he knew who they were, would he be entitled to claim compensation from them for property for which they had assumed no liability? Scholars hold two views, the sounder being that he would not.

For example, if the wrongdoer deposits the owner’s property with someone who does not know that he has seized it unlawfully, and the deposited property is lost, may the owner claim compensation from the person entrusted with it? There are two views, the sounder being that he may not.

Likewise, if he serves it as food to a guest who knows nothing of the wrongdoing, and the owner subsequently finds out, may he claim compensation from the guest? There are two views. “One view” is that he may not.

Those who say that he may claim compensation do not say that eating it was unlawful. Rather, they say that the guest incurs no sin by eating it, but must pay its price, just as if he had purchased it.

Those who hold the correct view say that the guest incurs neither sin by eating it nor any liability whatsoever to compensate its owner. Rather, liability rests with the wrongdoer who seized it from him without right.

Thus, if we consider particular property in someone’s possession, without knowing that it was seized unlawfully or acquired through a form of possession that confers no ownership, and we receive it from him, accept it as a gift, or take it as wages or repayment of a loan, there is unanimous agreement that we incur no sin—even if, in reality, he had marketed it or seized it unlawfully. If we subsequently learn that it was stolen, then, according to the sounder of the two views, we owe only what we undertook by contract. That is, our ultimate liability extends only to what we undertook by contract. We therefore bear no ultimate liability for anything he gave us as a present or gift, nor for anything beyond the purchase price. The same applies to wages

---

## Page 14581

and repayment of a loan: if we have already disposed of these funds, we bear no ultimate liability to replace them. Here, however, the jurists disagree over a “question”: may the owner demand compensation from the deceived person in whose possession the property was lost, who may then recover from the deceiver whatever he paid because of the deception? Or may the owner demand from the deceived person only what ultimately falls within his own liability? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad.

A similar case arises when a man unlawfully seizes a slave woman, and another man buys her from him—or receives her from him as a gift—and has children by her. The Companions and the imams agree that her children by the deceived man are free, because the man who had intercourse with her did not know that she belonged to someone else; rather, he believed that she belonged to him. They agree that a child follows the mother in freedom or slavery, and the father in lineage and patronage. Nevertheless, they ruled that his child was free because the father did not know, and what is unknown is treated as nonexistent.

They also awarded the slave woman’s master compensation for the child, because he would have been entitled to the child were it not for the deception. Since the children passed out of his ownership without right, he was entitled to compensation for them. They also awarded him the dower due for a slave woman.

According to the sounder of the two views, these payments are owed by the wrongdoing deceiver who unlawfully seized and sold the slave woman. The deceived purchaser owes only what he undertook by contract: the purchase price alone. But may her owner demand compensation for the child’s freedom and the dower from the deceived purchaser, who may then recover those payments from the wrongdoing deceiver? Or may he demand payment only from the wrongdoing deceiver? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad.

There is no disagreement within the Muslim community that his intercourse with her was not unlawful and that his child is legitimately born, not illegitimate. He is thus a legitimate child, not a child of

---

## Page 14582

fornication. Likewise, in all these cases, they did not dispute that no sin attaches to the person who eats, wears clothing, or has intercourse without knowing. Their disagreement concerns only liability for compensation, because such liability belongs to the justice required in matters of people’s rights and applies whether the act was deliberate or mistaken:

“It is not for a believer to kill another believer except by mistake. Whoever kills a believer by mistake must free a believing slave and pay blood money to the deceased’s family, unless they waive it as charity.”

Someone who kills a person by mistake incurs no sin and does not become an evildoer thereby, but must pay blood money. Likewise, someone who mistakenly destroys unlawfully seized property must compensate for it, although he incurs no sin. It is thus clear that there is no sin in the absence of knowledge.

Accordingly, it is undoubtedly permissible to transact with Muslims, Jews, and Christians concerning any property in their possession when neither evidence nor any indication establishes that it was unlawfully seized or taken into possession in a manner that makes transactions with its holder impermissible. I know of no disagreement among the imams concerning this.

It is well known that most people’s property falls into this category. Taking possession through outright wrongdoing does not confer ownership. As for property received under an invalid contract, such as usury, gambling, and the like, does taking possession confer ownership? The jurists hold three views:

The first is that it does confer ownership. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa.

The second is that it does not confer ownership. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and of Ahmad according to the well-known position

---

## Page 14583

of his school.

The third view is that taking possession confers ownership if restitution of the property is no longer possible. But if it can be returned to its owner and has undergone no change in its characteristics or price, taking possession does not confer ownership. This is the view reported from Mālik’s school.

We have discussed these matters and principles at length elsewhere, outside this response. Here, however, we have drawn attention to important principles that open up the question of ambiguity concerning this foundational principle, which is one of the foundations of Islam. As Imam Ahmad and others have said, the foundations of Islam revolve around three hadiths—the Prophet’s sayings:

“What is lawful is clear, and what is unlawful is clear.”

“Actions are judged only by intentions.”

“Whoever performs an act that is not in accordance with our teaching will have it rejected.”

Actions are either enjoined or forbidden. The first hadith addresses what is forbidden, while what is enjoined concerns either the heart’s purpose, namely intention, or the outward action, which must be prescribed and conform to the Sunnah. Thus, concerning Allah’s words,

“that He may test you as to which of you is best in deeds,”

al-Fudayl ibn ‘Iyād said, “That which is most sincere and most correct.” They asked, “Abū ‘Alī, what do you mean by ‘most sincere and most correct’?”

He replied, “If an action is sincere but not correct, it will not be accepted. If it is correct but not sincere, it will not be accepted until it is both sincere and correct. To be sincere is to be done for Allah, and to be correct is to conform to the Sunnah.”

It is thus clear that the claim made by the person who said, “Eating lawful food is impossible, and it cannot be found in this age,” is mistaken and contrary to scholarly consensus. Rather,

---

## Page 14584

the lawful predominates in people’s wealth and is more abundant than the unlawful. This claim is sometimes made by certain Sufis who study jurisprudence. I know senior shaykhs in Iraq who have made it, and perhaps it passed from them to some of the shaykhs of Egypt. The person who said this did not mean to rule out eating altogether. Rather, he said, “Religious scrupulousness is then unattainable.” He then discussed how one should proceed regarding what one does and refrains from doing; I cannot recall his remarks at present.

Let the discerning person reflect and understand that whoever departs from the prophetic law of Muhammad, established by the Quran and Sunnah and unanimously accepted by the early generations of this community and its imams, will need to devise another, self-contradictory code that both reason and religion reject. Yet whoever exercises independent judgment and is tested in obeying Allah and His Messenger, Allah rewards him for his effort and forgives his error:

“Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place no resentment in our hearts toward those who believe. Our Lord, You are indeed Most Kind, Most Merciful.”

As for his claim that doubt arose because the spoils from the battle of al-Mansūrah were not distributed and became mixed with other wealth, the answer consists of two points.

The first is to say: the wholly unlawful wealth mixed with people’s possessions—such as property forcibly seized by those with power, whether rulers, highway robbers, or participants in civil strife, together with wealth obtained through the dishonest dealings that fall under this heading—far exceeds those spoils;

---

## Page 14585

especially here in Egypt, where injustice is more prevalent than in Syria and the Maghrib. This includes people wronging one another in their dealings through dishonesty, fraud, and denial of others’ rights; the widespread wrongdoing of highway robbers, peasants, and Bedouins; and the many unjust exactions imposed by those in authority. It would therefore be more appropriate to attribute the unlawfulness to these matters than to the spoils of war.

The second point is that we have already discussed the jurists’ positions concerning those spoils and explained that the correct view is that, if the ruler permits people to take from them without a formal division, this is permissible. Where this is not permissible, anyone who takes the amount of his rightful share may do so. Anyone who takes more than his share and cannot return the excess to its rightful owners because their identities are unknown must give it in charity on their behalf. Even if he does not give it in charity on their behalf but disposes of it otherwise, whenever any of it reaches someone unaware of its circumstances, it is not unlawful for that recipient, nor does he incur any sin concerning it. This ruling also applies to all the aforementioned forms of usurped property.

What we have explained makes clear that someone who hires out his services, animals, real estate, or other belongings and receives a price or fee is not forbidden to accept it. This applies whether he knows that the price or fee lawfully belongs to its owner or does not know the owner’s circumstances because they are undisclosed. If, however, he knows that the payer usurped those dirhams, stole them, or acquired them in a manner that did not entitle him to take them, he may not accept them in payment of the price or fee owed to him. Nevertheless, the jurists disagree on this issue, and this paper is too brief to set out their disagreement in detail.

---

## Page 14586

As for the objection:

“How could the dirham undergo a change and become unlawful through a prohibited means, yet not undergo a change and become lawful through a legitimate means?”

The answer is: Rather, change does occur in what is prohibited because of its properties, not in what is prohibited because of how it was acquired.

The first is exemplified by wine: when it was juice, it did not become lawful and pure; once it fermented, it became unlawful and impure. If it then turns into vinegar by Allah’s action, without any deliberate attempt to turn it into vinegar, it becomes wine vinegar that is lawful and pure, by scholarly consensus. They disagree only when someone deliberately seeks to turn it into vinegar. They also disagree concerning other impure substances, such as a pig that turns into salt or an impure substance that turns into ash. One view is that it does not become pure. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and one of the two positions within the schools of Mālik and Ahmad.

The “second” is exemplified by usurped property. It is unlawful because it was taken unjustly; if it is taken rightfully, it becomes permissible. For example, the owner may consent to the usurper’s possession of it, give it to him as a gift, or sell it to him; or the owner, his guardian, or his agent may take possession of it. Furthermore, if the usurper gives it to someone who does not know that it was usurped, that person’s receipt of it is rightful, for Allah does not hold him responsible for what he does not know. Likewise, he explained that his receipt of it from the recipient was rightful. Liability has already been discussed. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14587

Chapter: Conditions in Sales

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A Muslim man purchased a slave woman from the People of the Book, and the seller stipulated that she was a good cook and could make wine and nabīdh. Is this valid?

He replied:

The stipulation that she can make wine and nabīdh is a void condition by Muslim consensus, and the contract containing it is also invalid.

This is clear according to those who hold that an invalid condition invalidates the contract, which is the well-known position in the schools of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī, and one of the two views reported from Ahmad. According to the other view, even if the seller sold her without stipulating this condition, it would not be permissible to purchase the slave woman because she makes wine. Likewise, it is not permissible to purchase anything in order to disobey Allah with it, such as buying juice to make wine or buying weapons to fight Muslims. This is the more correct of the two scholarly views, and it is the position of Mālik and Ahmad

---

## Page 14588

and others, as Allah Most High says:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and transgression.”

He was asked:

A man purchased a house from another man for one thousand dirhams, although it was worth two thousand. Immediately, on the same date, the purchaser leased the house back to the seller for a number of months at a specified rent, while their private understanding was that this was a sale subject to redemption. Is this contract valid on these terms? Is the original seller liable for the rent over the lease period or not?

It has been reported from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās—may Allah be pleased with them—that “the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade a loan that yields a benefit.” Does this transaction fall into that category or not?

Is it also reported in a hadith concerning the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that:

“He borrowed a young camel from a man. Then camels collected as obligatory charity came to him, and he instructed me to repay the man with a young camel. I said, ‘Among the camels I could find only a fine rubāʿī camel.’ The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Give it to him, for the best of you are those who are best in repaying their debts.’”

Is this an authentic hadith or not?

He replied:

If the intention is for one of them to take dirhams from the other

---

## Page 14589

and for the lender to benefit from the other’s property for as long as the dirhams remain owed to him, returning the property when the dirhams are repaid, then this is undoubtedly forbidden. This is an exchange of dirhams for an equal amount of dirhams plus the use of the house, which is manifest usury.

The scholars agree that whenever a lender stipulates an increase over his loan, this is forbidden. The same applies when there is a prior understanding to that effect, according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. It is authentically reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, to impose two conditions in one sale, to profit from something for which one has not assumed liability, or to sell what one does not possess.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade combining a loan with a sale because, when someone lends to another and also sells to him, he grants him favourable terms in the sale on account of the loan. The same applies when he combines a lease with a sale to him.

The transaction they outwardly present as a sale subject to redemption—in which they agree that the buyer will return the item sold whenever the seller brings him the price—is invalid by agreement of the imams, whether this is stipulated in the contract or agreed upon beforehand, according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. In such a case, the property must be returned to its owner and the money to its owner. Both parties must receive discretionary punishment if they knew that it was forbidden.

The prohibition of a loan that yields a benefit is established through reports from several of the Companions mentioned by the questioner, as well as others, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām and Anas ibn Mālik. A report to this effect has also been traced back to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and was transmitted by Ibn Mājah and others.

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām is reported to have said:

“You are in a land where usury is widespread. If you give a man a loan and he gives you a load of straw or a load of

---

## Page 14590

fodder, deduct its value from his loan.” A man said to Ibn ‘Abbās, “I lent a fishmonger twenty dirhams, and he gave me a fish which I valued at thirteen dirhams.” He replied, “Take only seven dirhams from him.”

The hadith concerning the young camel is authentic. If the lender is repaid with something better than what he lent, without any prior arrangement, that is permissible. If he is repaid a greater amount than he lent, the scholars hold two opinions concerning this. This is because the additional amount is given after the loan has been repaid. This differs from giving him a gift before repayment: unless he deducts its value from the loan, the loan remains owed, to be recovered along with the gift, which was given only because of the loan.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“What is the matter with a man whom we appoint to carry out some duty Allah has entrusted to us, who then says, ‘This is for you, and this was given to me as a gift’? Why did he not sit in his father’s or mother’s house and see whether he would be given gifts or not?”

He thus made clear that when a gift is given for a particular reason, its ruling follows that reason. Hence, the position transmitted from the Companions and the majority of the imams is that a gift given before repayment is credited to its giver against the loan, unlike repayment with something of better quality.

As for the particular arrangement in question, the two parties agree beforehand that one will buy the property from the other for a certain price, then lease it back to him for a specified period, and return the property to him when he brings back the purchase price. Here, the intended result is that the party who has transferred the property pays rent for as long as the money remains owed by him. There is no difference between receiving the use of the property and receiving payment for its use: both are forbidden.

---

## Page 14591

Although some jurists have permitted this on the grounds that it was not stipulated in the contract and that prior agreement and intention have no bearing on contracts, the correct view—supported by the Quran and Sunnah, agreed upon by the Companions, and held by most of the imams—is that such arrangements are forbidden and that intentions are taken into account in contracts. As the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person shall have only what he intended.”

A condition agreed beforehand has the same effect as one stipulated when the contract is concluded.

Allah has rebuked those who evade obligations and treat forbidden things as lawful through stratagems and deception, as He relates in Surah Nūn and in the account of the Sabbath-breakers. It is also reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Do not do as the Jews did, making lawful what Allah has forbidden through the simplest of stratagems.”

Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī said:

“They try to deceive Allah as they deceive children. Had they done it outright, I would have found it less objectionable.”

The evidence for this is set out at length in a substantial work.

---

## Page 14592

Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

Section: On the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to ʿĀʾishah:

“Buy her and stipulate that they shall have the rights of patronage, for the rights of patronage belong only to the one who sets a slave free.”

This has puzzled many people. Some have even said that Hishām alone transmitted it, whereas al-Zuhrī did not, and considered this a defect in the hadith. Yet the hadith is recorded in both Sahīh collections and has no defect.

Others have said that “stipulate in their favour” means “stipulate against them.” They cited as a parallel the words of Allah Most High, “For them is the curse,” meaning, “Upon them is the curse.” Harmalah transmitted this interpretation from al-Shāfiʿī, and it has also been transmitted from al-Muzanī. This interpretation is weak.

First, the words “stipulate in their favour” are explicit in their meaning: the preposition lām denotes a particular association. As for His words, “For them is the curse,” they resemble His words, “For them is punishment,” and “For them is disgrace.” This conveys a valid meaning; it does not mean that they own the curse;

---

## Page 14593

rather, when it is said here, “For them is the curse,” it means that they receive it as their recompense. When it is said, “Upon them,” the intention is to invoke a curse upon them. The two meanings are therefore distinct.

“Upon them” may also be intended as a statement of fact, meaning that the curse has fallen upon them. Thus the preposition “upon” changes the meaning conveyed by “for,” which denotes a particular association, although both expressions convey that those people are cursed.

His words, “Stipulate in their favour,” have a meaning distinct from “Stipulate against them.” How, then, can an expression be explained as meaning its opposite? Moreover, ʿĀʾishah had already stipulated this against them, saying: “If they wish, I will pay them the whole amount at once, and your rights of patronage will belong to me. But they refused.”

Furthermore, the rights of patronage belong to the one who sets a slave free without any need to stipulate this. Once he frees the slave, those rights belong to him, whether or not he stipulated this against the seller. This interpretation of the hadith would still suggest that the rights of patronage accrue to them only if she stipulates this, which is false. Anyone who reflects on the hadith will know with certainty that the Messenger did not intend this.

As for what the hadith actually indicates, they found it problematic on two counts. First, how could the Messenger command an invalid condition? Second, how could an invalid condition fail to invalidate the contract? A group of scholars offered a third answer, mentioned by Ahmad and others, namely that

---

## Page 14594

the people already knew that this condition was forbidden, yet proceeded with it after the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) had prohibited it. Their stipulation therefore had no more effect than if they had not made it. He explained to ʿĀ’ishah that stipulating the rights of patronage in their favour would not harm her. This was not a command to impose the condition, but permission for the purchaser to agree to it if the seller refused to sell without it, together with an assurance that it would not harm the purchaser. A person may enter into such a transaction. Thus, it was permission to purchase despite the seller’s stipulation, and permission to agree with them to that stipulation, since doing so caused no harm.

The hadith itself explicitly states that an invalid condition of this kind does not invalidate the contract. This is the correct view. It is the position of Ibn Abī Laylā and others, and Ahmad’s position according to the more evident of the two reports from him. Only those who assumed that an invalid condition invalidates the contract found the hadith problematic. That is not the case. Rather, if the person imposing the condition knows that it is forbidden and unlawful to stipulate—as was the case with these people—then imposing it has no more effect than not imposing it. The purchaser’s purchase is therefore valid, and he acquires ownership, while this condition—which the seller already knows to be forbidden and impermissible to fulfil—is of no effect.

As for those people, if they knew of the prohibition before ʿĀ’ishah sought a ruling, there is no difficulty. However, nothing in the hadith indicates this. Rather, it states:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) stood up one evening and said: ‘What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions that are not in the Book of Allah? Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in the Book of Allah, it is invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions.’”

This took place immediately after ʿĀ’ishah sought a ruling, and those people had learned

---

## Page 14595

of this beyond doubt. ‘Ā’ishah concluded her contract with them after this announcement by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him). Thus, either they had repented of imposing this condition, or they proceeded with it knowing that it was forbidden. In that case, stipulating it causes no harm. This is what the hadith and its context indicate. There is no difficulty here, and to Allah belong all praise and gratitude.

If, however, someone stipulates such an invalid condition without knowing that it is forbidden, believing it to be binding, the sale is neither binding upon him nor invalid. This is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. Rather, he may rescind the sale if he did not know that this condition need not be fulfilled. He consented to relinquish ownership only on this condition; if it is not fulfilled, he may reclaim ownership if he wishes, or uphold the sale if he wishes. This is comparable to discovering a defect in the item purchased, or to valid conditions that are not fulfilled: if someone sells on condition that collateral or a guarantor be provided, but it is not provided, he may either rescind or uphold the sale.

The view that the sale is invalid in such a case is weak and contrary to established principles. Rather, it is nonbinding, and the purchaser has the right to rescind it, just like someone who purchases a defective item or an animal whose milk has been left to accumulate in its udder, and similar cases. His right is to be given a choice by being allowed to rescind.

It has also been said within Ahmad’s school that he is entitled to compensation for the shortfall in the price resulting from setting aside this condition, just as a similar view has been expressed concerning a defective item. This is the better-known of the two reports from him. According to the other report, he is entitled only to rescind; compensation is available only by mutual agreement or when returning the item is impossible, as is the position of the majority of jurists. This is the more correct view. For just as the party imposing the condition consented only

---

## Page 14596

on the stipulated condition, and is therefore not bound by the sale without it but has the option to rescind, so too the other party consented only to the stated price, even though he accepted that price together with the condition. If the condition is set aside and the right of patronage belongs to him, he has not thereby consented to pay more than the stated price. Rather, he may rescind the sale if he wishes; he cannot be compelled to pay an additional amount. If he pays the price, the other party may either accept it and uphold the sale or rescind it. If they mutually agree on compensation, that is permissible, but neither may be compelled to accept it without his consent, for it constitutes payment in exchange for the missing component.

The same applies to comparable cases, such as when a transaction is divided. It has been said that the sale remains valid for the lawful portion at its proportional share of the price, as is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. The party whose transaction has been divided may rescind it if he agreed to sell this portion at its proportional price only together with the other portion.

The fundamental principle governing contracts is that a person is bound only by an obligation he has undertaken himself or one imposed upon him by the Lawgiver. What he has undertaken is what he has pledged to do, so he must neither break his pledge nor act treacherously. What the Lawgiver has commanded him to do is something Allah has obliged him to observe, even if he has not undertaken it himself. Thus, Allah has obliged him to maintain what He has commanded to be maintained, including faith in the Scriptures and the messengers, and the ties of kinship.

For this reason, Allah mentions both in His Book, as when He says:

“Those who fulfill the covenant of Allah and do not break the contract.”

“And those who join that which Allah has ordered to be joined.”

---

## Page 14597

Thus, whatever Allah has commanded to be maintained is an obligation He has imposed. Whatever a person has pledged to do is an obligation he has undertaken himself; he must therefore fulfill the covenant of Allah and not break his pledge, provided that it does not contradict the Book of Allah.

A person may stipulate a condition contrary to the Book of Allah, intending thereby to make lawful what Allah has forbidden. For example, he may sell or emancipate a slave woman while stipulating that he may continue to have intercourse with her after she leaves his ownership. Or another person may sell a slave and stipulate that the right of patronage shall belong to him rather than to the emancipator. Or he may marry off his slave woman or female relative and stipulate that any child be attributed to someone other than the father, or to himself. Yet Allah has commanded that a child be attributed to his father, and patronage is a bond like the bond of lineage. Whoever is attributed to someone other than his father, or affiliates himself with patrons other than his own, incurs the curse of Allah, the angels, and all mankind.

An authentic report from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, states:

“He prohibited selling or giving away the right of patronage.”

For this reason, according to the majority of scholars, patronage itself is not inherited; rather, like lineage, it provides a basis for inheritance. The right of patronage belongs to the emancipator’s nearest agnatic relatives. It is thus clear that the hadith is true as transmitted. And Allah knows best.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The conditions most deserving of fulfillment are those by which you have made marital intimacy lawful.”

This shows that fulfilling conditions in marriage is even more deserving of priority than fulfilling them in sales. Hence, many earlier and later scholars have said that if a person stipulates a condition contrary to the Book of Allah—for example, that he marry a woman without a dowry, or with an unlawful dowry—the marriage is invalid, as in marriages by exchange without dowries and other such cases. This is the position of Mālik and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him.

---

## Page 14598

Indeed, “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited marriages by exchange without dowries,” and the Companions declared them invalid, because those involved had left the marriages devoid of dowries. This is the rationale stated in Ahmad’s well-known pronouncements, and it is also the position of Mālik and others.

Some of Ahmad’s followers, however, hold that the rationale is what al-Shāfiʿī stated: shared conjugal rights. The first explanation is more correct. The latter makes no sense, for no sharing of conjugal rights has occurred. Rather, each husband has acquired exclusive conjugal rights over one woman, even though he has made conjugal rights over the other woman her dowry. The free woman has not acquired conjugal rights over another woman, nor could she do so, since a woman cannot marry another woman. Instead, she has assigned to her guardian the dowry to which she is entitled. Thus, it is her guardian who has acquired the conjugal rights: her dowry has been made to consist in her guardian’s acquiring those rights, while she herself has acquired nothing. This is why it is called shighār. A shāghir place is a vacant one, and shagharat hādhihi al-jiha means “this area has become vacant.”

If a woman is promised something as her dowry but does not receive what she was promised, the marriage is not binding upon her, and she is given a substitute, as in a sale—and with even greater reason. For:

“The conditions most deserving of fulfillment are those by which you have made marital intimacy lawful.”

If a woman is held bound by a marriage without receiving what she agreed to, she is being held bound by a marriage to which she did not consent. This contradicts the Book and the Sunnah. If such a thing is impermissible in a sale, it is even more clearly impermissible in marriage. The Lawgiver has not bound her to marriage on these terms, nor has she undertaken it herself. A person is bound only by what the Lawgiver has imposed or what he has undertaken himself. Neither applies here, so there is no basis

---

## Page 14599

for binding her to a marriage to which she did not consent.

The assertion that “the dowry is not an objective of marriage” has no substance. It is an integral part of marriage, and stipulating it carries greater force than stipulating the price in a sale, because the Prophet said:

“The conditions most deserving of fulfillment are those by which you have made marital intimacy lawful.”

Property may lawfully be acquired in exchange for compensation, whereas marital intimacy becomes lawful only through dowries. A marriage may be contracted without specifying and fixing the dowry, but not with an express exclusion of it. A marriage contracted without further specification entails the customary dowry for a woman of her standing. Likewise, according to the correct view—which is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad—a sale may be concluded at the going price. There is therefore no difference, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

What is established by the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus is that marriage may be contracted without specifying the dowry—that is, without determining its amount—not that it may be contracted with an express exclusion of it. Indeed, Allah Most High said:

“We certainly know what We have prescribed for them concerning their wives and those whom their right hands possess.”

When He permitted the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to marry without a dowry, He required the others not to marry without one. The Quran likewise indicates this in more than one passage. There must therefore be a dowry, whether expressly specified and fixed or left unspecified. If it is subsequently fixed, it is whatever the two agree upon; otherwise, she is entitled to the customary dowry of women comparable to her, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled in the case of Birwa‘ bint Wāshiq.

How different the two cases are! People regularly marry without specifying a dowry, having mutually accepted the dowry customary in such circumstances, namely, the customary dowry for a woman of her standing. Likewise, they regularly buy and sell, having mutually accepted the price the seller charges at

---

## Page 14600

such times, as when they buy bread, accompaniments, fruit, meat, and other goods from bakers, butchers, provision merchants, and others, having agreed on the customary price. This is the price the seller charges people—the going rate for comparable goods in that place and time. Such a sale is valid, as Ahmad expressly stated, although there is disagreement within his school concerning it.

Section

The foundation of religion is that nothing is obligatory except what Allah and His Messenger have made obligatory; nothing is forbidden except what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; nothing is disliked except what Allah and His Messenger dislike; nothing is lawful except what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; and nothing is recommended except what Allah and His Messenger love.

Thus, the lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful, the forbidden is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, and religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed. Hence Allah condemned the polytheists and others for what they declared lawful or forbidden, or instituted as religion, without His permission.

Allah may impose an obligation upon His servant from the outset, as He obliges everyone to have faith and affirm His oneness. Or He may impose it because the servant has undertaken it and made it binding upon himself, whereas otherwise He would not have imposed it. Examples include fulfilling vows to perform recommended deeds and fulfilling commitments undertaken in permissible contracts, such as sale, marriage, divorce, and the like, when this is not

---

## Page 14601

obligatory. He may also impose it on both grounds, as with pledging allegiance to the Messenger to hear and obey him, pledging allegiance to the leaders of the Muslims, and people entering into agreements to act upon what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. The very commitment to observe the ordinances of Islam belongs to this category. The believer has undertaken to observe them through faith and the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. This testimony obliges him to fulfil its requirements: affirming the truth of what the Messenger brought from Allah and obeying him in what he made obligatory and commanded. For the Messenger conveyed from Allah that obedience to him is obedience to Allah, and disobedience to him is disobedience to Allah. These principles are discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is this: if the fundamental principle of the revealed law is that nothing binds a person unless the Lawgiver makes it binding upon him or he undertakes it himself, then whenever jurists disagree over a subsidiary question arising from this principle, it must be referred back to it. Some jurists apply this principle consistently, while others do not; rather, they depart from it in many questions, even though they generally adhere to it in most. Among these are “questions of marriage and its stipulations.”

Here too, the governing principle is that stipulations are presumed valid and binding unless evidence establishes otherwise.

It has also been said that, on the contrary, stipulations are presumed invalid unless evidence establishes their validity, on the basis of the hadith of ʿĀ’ishah. The first view, however, is correct, for the Book and the Sunnah both establish the duty to fulfil contracts and covenants and condemn treachery

---

## Page 14602

and the breaking of pledges; this obligation applies, however, only when what is stipulated does not conflict with Allah’s Book and His stipulation. If it does conflict with Allah’s Book and His stipulation, the stipulation is invalid. This is the meaning of the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Any stipulation a person lays down that is not in Allah’s Book is invalid, even if there are a hundred stipulations. Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s stipulation is more binding.”

His words, “lays down a stipulation,” refer to the matter stipulated. His words, “not in Allah’s Book,” mean that the matter stipulated is not in Allah’s Book—that is, it is not something Allah has permitted. Examples include stipulating that rights of patronage belong to someone other than the person who freed the slave, or that lineage be attributed to someone other than the father; likewise, sexual intercourse without either marriage or slave ownership, and other things that Allah has not permitted under any circumstances.

This also includes marrying a woman without a dower. Hence his words:

“Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s stipulation is more binding.”

This is said only when the matter stipulated contradicts Allah’s Book and His stipulation. Allah’s Book and His stipulation must then take precedence, and it is said:

“Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s stipulation is more binding.”

But if Allah has not expressly declared the stipulation itself or the matter stipulated lawful, and has instead remained silent about it, it does not thereby contradict Allah’s Book and His stipulation such that one could say:

“Allah’s Book has greater authority, and His stipulation is more binding.”

Thus, his words, “Any stipulation a person lays down that is not in Allah’s Book,” mean one that conflicts with Allah’s Book.

It makes no difference whether “stipulation” is understood to mean the act of stipulating or the matter stipulated: whenever either conflicts with Allah’s Book, the other does too. This is unlike a matter about which He has remained silent. This, then, is one fundamental principle.

---

## Page 14603

The second fundamental principle is this:

If the two parties consent only on the basis of a stipulation that conflicts with Allah’s Book, they have undertaken something Allah has forbidden. Their undertaking is therefore not binding, just as a vow to commit a sin is not binding. This applies whether they know of the prohibition or are unaware of it. If one party imposes such a stipulation on the other, believing it permissible, and consents only on that basis, the contract is not binding upon him—unless he undertook it for Allah’s sake. In that case, whatever was undertaken for Allah’s sake remains binding, but not what was not. This applies to vows, endowments, bequests, and other arrangements whose components can be treated separately.

If, however, he knows that the stipulation is forbidden and nevertheless imposes it, it is like the stipulation made by Barīrah’s owners: his stipulation is invalid, but the contract is not invalidated. In this respect, there is no distinction between marriage, sale, and other contracts.

Some jurists have invalidated many stipulations in marriage without evidence. Then, regarding an invalid stipulation in marriage, they say that the stipulation is invalid but the marriage remains valid without it—even though the party imposing the stipulation consented to the marriage only on that basis. Yet stipulations in marriage are more binding than those in a sale, because the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“The stipulations most deserving of fulfilment are those by which you have made sexual relations lawful.”

Their position has thus led them to contradict the scriptural texts in many instances and to bind people to something they neither undertook nor were bound to by Allah. They have therefore imposed upon people what Allah and His Messenger did not impose. They may then take a broad approach to divorce, which Allah detests, thereby forbidding people what Allah and His Messenger have not forbidden. Then they permit it through contracts containing invalid stipulations, thereby making lawful what Allah and His Messenger have not made lawful.

For example, stipulating in the marriage contract that the woman is to be made lawful again for her former husband is, by consensus, a forbidden and invalid stipulation.

---

## Page 14604

if it is stipulated that he is to divorce her once he has made her lawful again for her former husband. The same applies to stipulating divorce after a specified term. Thus, among those who hold temporary marriage to be forbidden, there is consensus that a stipulation requiring divorce when the term expires, or after making her lawful again for her former husband, is invalid. Allah has permitted neither marriage for a fixed term nor marriage contracted to make a woman lawful again for her former husband.

A group of jurists said that the contract is valid and binding, while the stipulation is invalid. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī, and of Ahmad according to one of the two narrations from him. Many of these jurists then distinguished between setting a time limit and imposing a stipulation.

They said: If he says, “I marry her for one month,” this is temporary marriage and is invalid. Some carried the analogy through consistently—this is Zufar’s position—and a corresponding view was derived within Ahmad’s school: the contract is valid, while the time limit is disregarded, just as they held that the stipulation is disregarded.

If, in a marriage intended to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, someone says, “On condition that, once you have made her lawful again, you divorce her,” this is a stipulation, just as if someone says in a temporary marriage, “On condition that, when the term expires, you divorce her.”

But if he says, “There will then be no marriage between you,” two views on this have been attributed to al-Shāfi‘ī and others. According to one, it is treated as an invalid stipulation, so the marriage is valid. According to the other, it is treated as setting a time limit, so the marriage is invalid.

If an option to rescind is stipulated in a marriage contract, there are three positions, corresponding to three narrations from Ahmad: that both the contract and the stipulation are valid; that both are invalid; or that the contract is valid but the stipulation is not.

The stronger view is that this stipulation is valid. Even if it is deemed invalid, the contract would not be binding without it, for the basic rule is that stipulations must be fulfilled, and stipulating an option to rescind serves a legitimate purpose, especially in marriage. This is based

---

## Page 14605

on a basic principle, namely: is a stipulated option to rescind a sale valid in principle, or is it invalid in principle, with a three-day option permitted as an exception?

The first is the position of the leading jurists Mālik, Ahmad, Ibn Abī Laylā, Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad. The second is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī. For this reason, the latter two deemed an option to rescind invalid in most contracts, including marriage and other contracts. Likewise, there are three positions on making a marriage conditional upon a future event, corresponding to three narrations from Ahmad.

The jurists of the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad distinguish, in marriage, between a stipulation that terminates the contract, such as divorce, and other stipulations, such as stipulating that there will be no dower, no intercourse or no allocation of nights. Within Ahmad’s school, there is disagreement concerning a stipulation excluding the dower and similar stipulations.

The correct view is that, if a stipulation is permissible, it is binding and must be fulfilled. If it is not fulfilled, the right to rescind is established—as when a particular kind of property or currency is stipulated as the dower. The marriage may not be deemed binding despite failure to fulfil the stipulation. Rather, the party who imposed it must be given the choice between allowing the marriage to stand and rescinding it, just as with stipulations in a sale and with defects. There is consensus that a purchase may be returned on account of a defect; according to the majority, the same applies to rescinding a marriage.

A group of Medinan jurists and others said that a marriage to a free woman cannot be rescinded on account of a defect. They maintained that marriage does not admit rescission, and therefore allowed it to be rescinded neither on account of a defect nor on account of a stipulation. Yet they, together with all other Muslims, require a husband who has sworn to abstain from marital relations either to resume those relations or to divorce his wife. They say:

---

## Page 14606

Divorce takes effect immediately upon expiry of the period if he has not resumed marital relations. If the husband is impotent or his penis has been amputated, most of them hold that she has the right to rescind the marriage. Yet they say that a woman cannot pronounce divorce. The majority uphold the right to choose whether to rescind on account of insanity, leprosy or vitiligo, as did ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb. Many of them, however, restricted rescission to defects that preclude marriage, just as they invalidated a marriage containing a stipulation that terminates the contract. A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the fundamental principles and scriptural texts establish that a stipulation is binding unless it contradicts the Book of Allah. If it is binding, the contract is not binding without its fulfilment.

All Muslims allow a specific item to be stipulated as the dower, such as this slave, this horse or this house. Yet they say that if delivery of the dower becomes impossible, a substitute becomes due, and the woman has no right to rescind, even if the impediment comes from the husband. This is a weak position that contradicts the fundamental principles. Even if one does not regard the contract as precluded, its performance may prove impossible. At the very least, therefore, the woman must be allowed to rescind, for she consented and permitted marital intercourse only in return for this. If it becomes unobtainable, she has the right to rescind.

They say: “The dower is not the primary purpose.”

The reply is that every stipulation serves an intended purpose, and the obligation to provide the dower is more firmly established than the obligation to pay the purchase price. Here, however, the spouses are both the subjects of the contract and the contracting parties, unlike a sale, in which the two parties conclude the contract but are not its subjects. This entails that, if the dower is unavailable, the woman has the choice between rescinding and demanding a substitute, as with defects in a sale. Since the subjects of the contract—the two spouses—remain, what is missing is only part of the subject matter of the contract. It is thus like a defect arising

---

## Page 14607

in the merchandise before possession can be taken: it gives grounds for rescission but does not invalidate the contract. This is what the fundamental principles, scriptural texts and analogical reasoning entail.

If a stipulation is invalid and the person who made it was unaware of its invalidity, the contract is not binding upon him. Rather, he may accept it without the stipulation; otherwise, he has the right to rescind. This is the governing principle. As for binding him to a contract to which he did not consent and which the Lawgiver did not oblige him to conclude, this contradicts the fundamental principles of Islamic law and the justice that Allah sent down the Book and sent the messengers to establish.

They have adopted the principle that a marriage to a free woman cannot be rescinded on account of a defect. They say: “Thus, marriage cannot be rescinded because a stipulation goes unfulfilled, since the two belong to the same category.” They also say: “Marriage is valid without specifying a dower, so it is valid when the dower is expressly excluded, and therefore valid with any invalid stipulations.”

That marriage is valid without a specified dower is established by the Book, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus. However, if he believes that no dower is obligatory, the dower due by default is the customary dower of comparable women. As for expressly excluding it, there are two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. The view that the marriage is invalid is that of most of the early generations, as reflected in the school of Mālik and elsewhere. This is the correct view, as indicated by the Book, the Sunnah and the hadith on exchange marriage.

They say: “Thus, the distinction between marriage and sale is established in these two respects: there is no rescission when a valid stipulation goes unfulfilled, and the contract remains valid despite an invalid stipulation.”

The reply is:

---

## Page 14608

As for denying rescission when a valid stipulation goes unfulfilled, and the assertion that marriage to a free woman cannot be rescinded on account of a defect, this has absolutely no basis in the words of the Lawgiver. Rather, whenever a valid stipulation goes unfulfilled, the person who made it has the right to rescind.

Is a stipulation made before the contract equivalent to one made when the contract is concluded? There are two views. The correct view is that it is equivalent. This is the apparent position of the schools of Ahmad and Mālik, and a view within the school of al-Shāfi‘ī, inferred from its rulings on private agreements and public declarations. Ahmad makes the publicly specified amount binding, even if it is less than what was privately agreed. However, he makes that amount binding for purposes of outward judgment, while instructing them to fulfil what they stipulated for him. On this understanding, the private agreement is not enforced because it has not been established by evidence; if it is established, judgment is given accordingly.

If, however, it is said that the private agreement is never enforced, this is because they publicly declared something contrary to what they privately intended, and marriage is founded on public declaration, not secrecy. This differs from a stipulation that they have not publicly contradicted, whether in marriage, sale or another contract. According to him, such a stipulation must be fulfilled and has an effect on the contract. If al-Shāfi‘ī holds that the private agreement is given effect in marriage, then this applies all the more to other contracts.

As for the validity of marriage despite an invalid stipulation, the basis for this argument is marriage without specifying a dower. Yet this is not an invalid stipulation: an invalid stipulation is unlawful to make, whereas this marriage is lawful. Thus, if he marries her without specifying a dower, but in accordance with the customary understanding that she must receive one—either an amount upon which they mutually agree or the customary dower of comparable women—then this marriage is lawful and contains no invalid stipulation. Thus, on the basis of those two analogies

---

## Page 14609

—both invalid—they distinguished between marriage and sale, and bound people to marriages to which they had not consented, even when they had made a valid stipulation, just as they bound a man to marriage with a woman who had a defect, although he had not consented to marrying a woman with a defect.

If it is asked, “Why, then, did he distinguish between genital defects and other defects?” the answer is:

It is well known that genital defects preventing intercourse are not ordinarily accepted, since intercourse is an intended purpose of marriage. This differs from complexion, tallness, shortness and similar characteristics on account of which a slave woman may be returned to her seller. A free woman is not inspected as a slave woman is, and the husband gave his consent without qualification; he had not stipulated a particular quality which she then proved to lack. If he did make such a stipulation, there are two views within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. The correct view is that he has the right to rescind the marriage. The same applies when the roles are reversed. This is Mālik’s position.

A stipulation is established either expressly or by custom. In a sale, custom indicates that the buyer has consented only to something free of defects. Likewise, in marriage, the husband has not consented to marrying someone with whom intercourse is impossible. Regarding a defect that impairs the fullness of intercourse without preventing intercourse itself, there are two views within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. As for conditions that allow intercourse and its full enjoyment, people’s preferences cannot be defined by a fixed standard.

The Lawgiver has permitted—indeed, encouraged—a man to look at the woman he intends to marry, saying:

“When Allah places in the heart of any of you the desire to propose to a woman, let him look at her, for that is more likely to foster affection between them.”

He also said to a man who had proposed to a woman of the Ansār: “Look at her, for there is something about the eyes of the Ansār.”

His words, “more likely to foster affection between them,” indicate that if

---

## Page 14610

he knows her before marriage, affection will endure. It also indicates that the marriage is valid even if he has not seen her, since he did not explain the purpose of looking at her as making the marriage valid.
