This shows that looking is not obligatory and that marriage is valid without it. It is not customary among Muslims or others to describe the woman being married in that way. A sale is different: either it is invalid, or the buyer has the option to cancel upon inspection. There is, however, a weakly supported view reported within Ahmad’s school that a sale is valid without inspection or description, and that no option to cancel is established.

This distinction rests on the difference between women and property: women are ordinarily accepted as wives despite their differing attributes, whereas property is not accepted irrespective of its attributes. Property is sought as wealth, and its value varies according to its attributes. Marriage, however, is intended to establish ties by marriage and provide intimate enjoyment, both of which can be attained despite differences in attributes. This is a distinction recognized by Islamic law and intelligible in light of people’s customs. But when it is known that a man did not consent to what he received, because he stipulated a particular attribute and she proved otherwise—and likewise when the roles are reversed—binding him to something to which he did not consent runs contrary to the established principles.

If he were to say, “I thought she was more beautiful than she is,” or “I did not think she had this characteristic,” or something similar, then he himself was negligent: he neither asked about it, nor saw her, nor sent someone to see her. It is neither prescribed by Islamic law nor customary for her to be described to him in the marriage contract as slave women are described in advance-payment sales. Allah has safeguarded free women against this and loves that their privacy be preserved. This is why a woman is prohibited from concluding a marriage contract. If women do not conclude the contract themselves, how, then, should they be described in it? As for the man, his attributes are readily apparent

---

## Page 14611

and anyone who wishes may see him, so there is no defect in him warranting rejection. As for the woman, if the husband has been negligent, divorce is in his hands.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy upon him:

“A man bought a slave on condition that the seller be exempt from liability for all defects except running away. After the purchase, the slave ran away from him. What is the seller liable for?”

He replied:

“If the seller acknowledges that the slave had run away before the sale, this is a defect entitling the buyer to return him. If the seller concealed this defect and the slave subsequently ran away while in the buyer’s possession, then, according to one of the two views, the buyer may demand repayment of the full price. This is the position of Mālik and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him; indeed, it is the ruling he expressly stated.”

---

## Page 14612

Chapter on the Option in Sales

He was asked—may Allah have mercy upon him:

“Two men concluded a sale of a specific item, stipulating that each would have the right to rescind or affirm the sale within a period recognized as valid under Islamic law. Does this option apply to both affirmation and rescission, or only to rescission, making the mention of affirmation legally redundant? Or are the two not given effect together?

“If mentioning affirmation is legally redundant, there is nothing further to discuss. But if both are given effect, and each expression has a legal consequence, then, if one party chooses affirmation and the other rescission, does the choice of the party seeking rescission prevail, or that of whichever party acted first? Please give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the circumstances are as described and one party chooses to rescind the sale, he may do so without the other’s consent, even if the other was first to affirm it.

“Affirmation, when mentioned alongside rescission, means refraining from rescission: that is, each party may either rescind the sale or refrain from doing so. For, if neither rescinds it before the period expires, affirmation is not intended to bind the other party to the contract, since interpreting it in this way would be incompatible with

---

## Page 14613

the other party’s right to rescind, when each has been granted that right.

If, however, by affirming the contract he means affirming it on his own part by relinquishing his right of option, that is valid. Yet the lapse of his option does not cause the other party’s option to lapse.

The usual meaning of such wording, however, is that each party may rescind the contract or refrain from doing so. If he does not rescind it, he has thereby affirmed it. A parallel is Allah’s statement:

“When you divorce women and they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honorably or release them honorably.”

Releasing her means refraining from retaining her, so that he no longer holds her in marriage. Releasing her does not require pronouncing a further divorce. Likewise, affirming the contract does not require a fresh act of affirmation. And Allah knows best.”

He was asked:

“A man entrusted a leather mat to an auctioneer to sell. The auctioneer offered it for bidding, and the man raised the bid by half a dirham. The auctioneer then went to the deputy official responsible for public oversight and told him, ‘The owner of this leather mat raised the bid by half a dirham.’ The official summoned him, and he was informed of the allegation. He denied it and swore an oath of divorce—out of fear for his own safety and to dispel the suspicions of those who had heard him—swearing that he had not done it. Does divorce take effect as a result?”

He replied:

“When the owner raises the bid on his own goods, he is doing wrong and engaging in sham bidding, and he is worse

---

## Page 14614

than a trader who does not own the goods and who bids up their price without intending to buy them. Thus, if an outsider engages in sham bidding, the sale is not invalidated. If, however, the seller himself engages in sham bidding or colludes with someone who does, there are two opinions concerning the validity of the sale in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

Such a person should receive discretionary punishment for two things: his sham bidding and his knowingly false oath of divorce. His unlawful conduct does not excuse him for swearing a false oath.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“What is the ruling on someone who asks a high price for goods and sells them for more than their usual value, when the buyer may be unaware of their value? Is this permissible or not?”

He replied:

“If the buyer is uninformed—that is, unaware of the value of the goods—the seller may not overcharge him beyond customary bounds. Rather, he must sell to him at the usual value or close to it. If he grossly overcharges him, the buyer has the option of rescinding the sale or allowing it to stand.

It has been reported in a hadith:

‘Overcharging an uninformed buyer is usury.’

It is also established in the authentic hadith collections:

‘The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade intercepting those bringing goods for sale before they reached the market, and granted the seller the option to rescind upon reaching it.’

This is because, before reaching the market, the seller is unaware of the value of the goods. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore forbade the buyer from going out to meet him and purchasing from him, because this involves misleading and deceiving him. He granted the seller the option to rescind once he learned the true state of affairs.

---

## Page 14615

Likewise, anyone who is unaware of the value of goods must not be misled or deceived—for example, by being quoted an unusually high price so that he will offer something close to it. Rather, he should be sold the goods in accordance with customary, acceptable practice. And Allah knows best.”

He was asked:

“What is the ruling on selling to a trusting buyer?”

He replied:

“A trusting buyer must be charged only the price charged to others. No one in whom a buyer places his trust may overcharge him by taking a profit beyond customary bounds. Some scholars have set this threshold at one third, and others at one sixth.

Others have said that customary practice is the standard: whatever profit sellers normally make from buyers who bargain, they may also make from a trusting buyer.

A trusting buyer has been defined as someone who does not bargain, but simply says, ‘Take your payment and give me the goods.’ He has also been defined as someone who is unaware of the value of the goods. Neither may be grossly overcharged. A hadith states:

‘Overcharging a trusting buyer is usury.’

Anyone known to overcharge such buyers deserves punishment. Indeed, he must be barred from trading in the Muslims’ marketplace until he commits himself to obeying Allah and His Messenger. The overcharged buyer may rescind the sale, return the goods, and recover the price paid. If this unjust seller who overcharges people repents but cannot restore to those he wronged what is rightfully theirs, he should give in charity an amount equal to what he took from them through wrongdoing and overcharging;

---

## Page 14616

so that he may thereby discharge his liability. In ‘sales by bargaining,’ when buyers are familiar with the prices at which goods are ordinarily purchased, others should be charged the same prices as they are. No greater profit should therefore be made from a trusting buyer than from anyone else. Likewise, someone in pressing need who can obtain what he needs only from a particular seller should be charged the same profit as someone who is not in such need. For it is reported in al-Sāq:

‘The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited sales to those in pressing need.’

If the need concerns something indispensable—for example, if people urgently need the food or clothing that he possesses—he must sell it to them only at its customary value. They are entitled to take it from him at that value without his consent, and need not pay him anything beyond it. And Allah knows best.”

He also said:

Section

Selling adulterated goods whose degree of adulteration is known is permissible, provided the buyer knows this and does not pass them on deceptively to someone else. An example is trading with our debased dirhams.

But when the degree of adulteration is unknown—as with milk mixed with water when the amount of water is not determined—this is prohibited, even if the buyer knows that it is adulterated.

---

## Page 14617

And whoever sells adulterated goods is forbidden to retain only the portion of the price attributable to the adulteration. He must return it to its owner or, if returning it proves impossible, give it in charity on his behalf. For example, someone sells a defective, adulterated item for ten dirhams; it would be worth ten if sound, but with the defect it is worth eight. If he knows the buyer, he must pay him the two dirhams if the buyer chooses this; otherwise, the buyer returns the purchased item to him. If he does not know the buyer, he must give the two dirhams in charity on his behalf. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Some people make cloaks using unusable wool that they call ‘sallāqa.’ They fraudulently mix it with flax tow and sell it as good-quality wool. A merchant may know about this, but conceal it from the buyer. What must the manufacturer do? Is it permissible to trade in such goods while concealing this from the buyer? What is the ruling on manufacturing them in the first place? What must Muslims who have done this do? And what must those in authority do about people who mix flax tow into white wool and repeatedly return to this practice despite having been forbidden to do so more than once?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The manufacturer may not make this, nor may the seller sell it, even if the buyer knows that it has a defect, because the degree of adulteration is unknown. It is reported of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that “he prohibited mixing

---

## Page 14618

milk with water for sale,” as distinct from mixing it for drinking. Mixing milk with water for drinking is permissible, but doing so for sale is not, even if the buyer knows that it has been mixed with water. The buyer does not know the proportion of the mixture, so what is being sold remains undetermined, introducing uncertainty into the transaction. The same applies to all adulterated goods whose degree of adulteration is unknown: selling them, or manufacturing them for someone who will sell them, is prohibited. This also applies to mixing flax tow with white wool, and to any adulteration of food, clothing, or other goods when the degree of adulteration is unknown.

A number of scholars among the followers of Mālik, Ahmad, and others have ruled that someone who does this may be punished by having the adulterated garment torn apart or the adulterated food given away in charity. Similarly, the Prophet slit open wine containers and broke its jars, and ‘Umar and ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with them both—ordered that premises where wine was sold be burned down. Ahmad and others expressly stated this ruling. Likewise,

“the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar to burn the two garments dyed with safflower.”

Muslim narrated this in his Sahīh. Similarly, Moses, peace be upon him, burned the calf rather than returning it to its owners, and instruments of amusement are broken. There are numerous comparable examples. These rest on the principle that penalties affecting property are to be applied wherever Islamic law prescribes them, just like corporal punishments.

Some scholars have claimed that this has been abrogated, but they have no evidence whatsoever for that claim. Just as a person may be put to death for his wickedness, so too property in which there exists

---

## Page 14619

workmanship serving wicked purposes—such as carved idols—may be smashed and burned, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, burned idols. The same applies to anyone who produces food, clothing, or similar goods in a prohibited manner. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man owned a house and sold one-third of it to Zayd. He then sold the remainder to ‘Amr—one-third from his own property and two-thirds as Zayd’s agent—and Zayd died. Among the rights attached to the house was a water channel which proved to require relocation and removal because the harm it caused extended to others. Returning the property was impossible because substantial additional construction had been undertaken. Must the seller pay ‘Amr compensation for the channel? If so, may compensation be claimed from him for the share he sold as agent for the person who had purchased it from him, or is the claim confined to what he sold on his own behalf?

He answered:

Regarding compensation due because of a defect in the price: if the buyer has not received the price, an amount equal to the compensation is deducted from it. If he has received it on behalf of the seller or his agent, he may claim compensation from the seller.

If the agent guaranteed liability arising from the sale, or did not name his principal in the contract, he is liable for the compensation and may be required to pay it. If he named his principal in the contract but did not guarantee that liability, is he nevertheless liable? Scholars within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold two opinions on this matter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14620

He was asked—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave:

A house was jointly owned by two people. One of them sold it to another person, acting on his own behalf and as his partner’s agent. The buyer then erected a substantial structure above the property he had purchased. Among its appurtenant rights was a water channel adjoining the wall of a burial site. It made the wall damp, and the dampness spread to the grave. The owners of the burial site brought a complaint against the buyer before the public oversight authority. Expert witnesses testified that the channel was causing dampness in the wall which reached the grave, that the channel had been added against the wall, and that it caused harm requiring its removal from that location. The buyer was therefore required to relocate it.

Does the construction and demolition undertaken by the buyer prevent him from returning the property, or not? If it does, is he entitled to compensation for the defect?

If such an entitlement is established, must it be claimed immediately, so that it lapses through delay, or may it be claimed later without lapsing? As for what he was required to demolish and did demolish, does his entitlement to compensation for it lapse or not?

If it is held that compensation must be claimed immediately, and he has witnesses attest that he is claiming it, but then deals with the property after that attestation, does his entitlement lapse or not? If he retains that entitlement, may he claim against the agent for both the share sold from the agent’s own property and the share belonging to his principal, or only for the agent’s own share?

He answered:

If the water channel was installed somewhere it was not permissible to install it, the person who installed it must remove what he was not entitled to install. If the buyer was unaware of this, but rather

---

## Page 14621

believed that this was a right attached to the property which could not lawfully be removed, and matters then proved otherwise, this constitutes a defect.

If he built on the property before learning of the defect, and subsequently discovered it, he is entitled only to compensation for the defect, not to return the property, according to one of the two scholarly views. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to the more authoritative of the two reports from him. According to the other report—which is also Mālik’s view—he likewise may not return it, and he becomes a co-owner with the seller to the extent of the improvements he has made to it. He cannot be required to demolish them without compensation, since he built them lawfully.

According to the majority of scholars, the option to return property because of a defect need not be exercised immediately. This includes Mālik and, according to the apparent position of their respective schools, Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad. The latter two also have another view, corresponding to al-Shāfiʿī’s position, that it must be exercised immediately. If, however, the buyer says or does anything indicating acceptance, his option lapses by agreement. Thus, if he builds after learning of the defect, his option lapses.

If, however, he has witnesses attest to his claim for compensation, he is entitled to it and may demand it later. His dealings with the property do not extinguish his entitlement to compensation.

The seller may be held liable for compensation for the defect or for taking back the property insofar as he sold property of his own. As for property he sold on behalf of his principal, if he did not name his principal in the sale, he may likewise be held liable for the property sold. If he did name him, may a claim be brought against the agent, and is he responsible for the warranty obligations relating to the property sold? Scholars hold two views on this, both reported from Ahmad.

---

## Page 14622

If, however, the buyer has been required to pay compensation because of the newly constructed channel, which it was impermissible to construct, he may recover from the seller who misled him the compensation he was obliged to pay as a result of that deception.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some people produce distilled waters, such as rosewater and others. They then take the burnt rose material, soak it, and extract water from it in a manner departing from the usual practice. Likewise, they soak dried nīnūfar. Is it permissible for them to do this and sell the resulting water?

He replied:

Anyone intending to sell it may not mix the water from the first extraction with that from the second, even if the buyers know about this, as it is reported:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade mixing milk with water for sale, though there is no harm in doing so for drinking.”

When these liquids are mixed, the extent of their adulteration cannot be determined. The ruler must punish anyone who does this and adopt measures that will deter them from adulteration.

---

## Page 14623

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

Regarding the practice of “alchemy”: is it rationally possible, or permissible under Islamic law?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Whatever human beings manufacture of gold, silver, other precious substances, perfumes, and the like, in imitation of what Allah has created—such as the pearls, rubies, musk, ambergris, rosewater, and other things they produce—is not the same as what Allah creates. Rather, it resembles it in certain respects, but is not identical to it in definition or true nature. All of this is forbidden under Islamic law, without dispute among Muslim scholars who understand its true nature.

Whoever claims that manufactured gold is the same as naturally created gold makes a claim that is false according to both reason and religion. The reality of “alchemy” is merely the imitation of created things, and this is unsound according to reason. There is nothing like Allah Most High, whether in His essence, His attributes, or His acts. He, glorified be He, has created nothing whose like His servants can produce; and whatever

---

## Page 14624

they manufacture, He has not created its like for them. For He, glorified be He, has enabled them to prepare cooked food, weave clothing, and build houses, yet He has not created for them the like of the cooked foods, woven fabrics, and buildings they manufacture.

As for the kinds of animals, plants, and minerals that Allah, glorified be He, has created—such as human beings, horses, donkeys, livestock, birds, and fish—the children of Adam cannot manufacture the like of these living creatures. Likewise, with wheat, barley, broad beans, beans, lentils, grapes, fresh dates, and the various kinds of grains and fruits, human beings cannot manufacture the like of what Allah, glorified and exalted be He, creates. They can only make things resemble some of these fruits, just as they may manufacture something resembling an animal, fashioning an image that looks like an animal.

The same applies to metals such as gold, silver, iron, copper, and lead: the children of Adam cannot manufacture the like of what Allah creates. At most, they can produce a resemblance in certain respects, imparting a yellow colour and making alterations, while the underlying substances remain different. Hence they say, “Can you make it yellow?” And they say, “We are colourists.”

This “principle,” supported by a survey of existing things—that naturally created things are not manufactured, and manufactured things are not naturally created—is established among Muslims and among the early philosophers who discussed natural properties, alchemy, and other subjects. For Allah says in His Book:

“Or have they attributed to Allah partners who created like His creation

---

## Page 14625

so that the two creations seemed alike to them? Say, ‘Allah is the Creator of all things.’” In an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, relates that Allah said:

“Who is more unjust than one who sets out to create as I create? Let them create a tiny ant! Let them create a mosquito!”

It is also established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed image-makers. He said:

“Whoever makes an image will be required to breathe a soul into it, but he will never be able to do so.”

He also said:

“Those who will receive the severest punishment on the Day of Resurrection are those who imitate Allah’s creation.”

Such image-making involves neither deception nor fraud, for everyone knows that an image depicting an animal is not an animal. Hence, in image-making, a distinction is drawn between animals and other things: it is permissible to depict trees and minerals on clothing, walls, and the like. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever makes an image will be required to breathe a soul into it, but he will never be able to do so.”

For this reason, Ibn ‘Abbās told the man who sought his legal opinion: “Depict trees and things that have no soul.” In the Sunan collections, it is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Gabriel said to him concerning an image: “Order that its head be cut off.”

The imams therefore expressly stated this, saying: “The head is what constitutes the image; without it, no soul remains in it, and it becomes like an inanimate object.” Such image-making involves neither fraud nor deception, for everyone can distinguish between a depiction and a created thing.

As for alchemy, manufactured things are made to resemble created things, and its practitioners’ aim is either

---

## Page 14626

to make the manufactured material resemble the natural one, then spend it and use it in their dealings with people. This is among the gravest forms of fraud. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, passed by a man selling food. He put his hand into it and found it wet. He asked:

“What is this, O seller of food?”

The man replied: “O Messenger of Allah, it was exposed to the elements”—meaning rain.

He said: “Why did you not put this on top? Whoever cheats us is not one of us.”

His statement, “Whoever cheats us is not one of us,” is comprehensive, encompassing everyone who cheats. Alchemists are among the worst perpetrators of fraud. Hence, when dealing with people, they do not disclose that their product is made through alchemy. Were they to disclose this, no one would buy it from them except someone intending to use it to defraud people.

The imams have stated that it is impermissible to sell adulterated goods whose degree of adulteration is unknown, even if the buyer is told that they are adulterated. It has also been reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “forbade mixing milk with water for sale, but permitted doing so for drinking.” Selling adulterated goods to someone who is unaware of their adulteration is forbidden by consensus. The degree of adulteration in alchemical products is unknown, so neither making nor selling them is permissible under any circumstances.

Moreover, if people knew that gold and silver were produced through alchemy, they would not buy them, even if they were told that they would remain unchanged on the rūbās or the like. Indeed, hearts are naturally disposed to reject this. The authorities condemn anyone they find practising it. Even if one of them were to practise it secretly, he would still have to condemn it publicly;

---

## Page 14627

because human nature rejects it. You will find no one practising it except in secret or with the support of someone influential. Its practitioners bear the same humiliation, abasement, and darkened faces as those guilty of fabrication, lying, and deception. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who took the calf for worship will incur wrath from their Lord and humiliation in the life of this world. Thus do We recompense those who invent falsehood.”

Abū Qilāba said: “This applies to everyone in this community who invents falsehood, until the Day of Resurrection.” These people engage in fabrication, fraud, and deception in religion, and both are among those who invent falsehood.

As for the ancients, they stated that artifice cannot accomplish what nature does, and explained that what is manufactured cannot be like what is naturally produced. Thus, when their authors gave a precise account of alchemy, they would say: “Since its sole purpose is imitation, the way to achieve that imitation is such and such.”

They therefore follow methods that produce this imitation. Yet despite the variety and abundance of these methods, and despite the many groups who have mastered them and agree about them, they remain difficult for most people and fraught with pitfalls. Those who fall short outnumber many times over those who succeed, numerous though the latter are. The vast majority of those who pursue alchemy never attain even the manufactured product, which is adulterated, spurious by nature, and forbidden by Islamic law. Rather, they pursue and yearn for what is false and forbidden, exchange stories about it, study books devoted to it, and recite poetry about it, yet never attain the actual product of alchemy—which is adulterated material. They are like the followers of the awaited one who is in the cellar, and the followers of the men of the unseen, who are not seen

---

## Page 14628

by anyone, and others like them who seek what has no reality, believing that it exists. They die without ever reaching it, even if they find liars who claim to have encountered it.

Likewise, those pursuing alchemy are called “hunchbacks” because they spend so much time bending over to blow the bellows. Most never attain what is forbidden or obtain the adulterated product. Their most accomplished practitioners do attain the product of alchemy, which is forbidden and spurious, though it comes in different grades. Some of it changes after a few years, and some after a longer period. Yet the manufactured product changes and deteriorates, however long that may take, unlike natural gold created in the earth, which neither deteriorates nor changes.

Thus, it is related that Muhammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī, the physician—who upheld the validity of alchemy—made gold and sold it to some Christians. When they reached their own lands, it changed, so they returned it to him. I know of no physician who was more proficient in the craft of alchemy than he was. As for philosophers more skilled in philosophy than he, such as Ya‘qūb ibn Ishāq al-Kindī and others, they refuted alchemy, demonstrated its falsity, and exposed its tricks.

Among the practitioners of alchemy there were none of the prophets, religious scholars, shaykhs of the Muslims, Companions, or those who followed them

---

## Page 14629

in righteousness. The earliest person to whom we have found anything concerning alchemy attributed is Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Mu‘āwiya. He is not someone whom Muslims follow in their religion or whose judgment they consult. If the reports about him are authentic, then he was deceived, just as others were.

As for Jābir ibn Hayyān, the author of the works renowned among alchemists, he is an unknown figure, with no recognized standing among scholars or people of religion. Such men can only be in one of two positions: either they believe, out of ignorance and error, that manufactured gold is like naturally occurring gold, as others have supposed; or they know that it is not the same, but concealment and deception are involved. How many lay claim to mastery of alchemy because of people’s love of gold and silver! One of them even says:

“If a singer were to sing of it, the whole universe would dance.”

Most of them consume people’s wealth unjustly and turn others away from Allah’s path. They give the covetous the impression that they practise alchemy, only to consume their wealth and ruin their circumstances. Their stories in this regard are too well known among people to require a firmly established report. They show that practitioners of alchemy are punished with the opposite of what they intended: their wealth disappears. Having sought to increase their wealth through what Allah has forbidden, they suffer its diminution. As Allah Most High says:

“Allah destroys usury and causes charity to grow.”

Alchemy is even more strictly forbidden than usury. Judge Abū Yūsuf said: “Whoever seeks wealth through alchemy becomes bankrupt; whoever seeks religion through speculative theology becomes a heretic; and whoever pursues rare hadith reports ends up lying.” These words are also reported from Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī

---

## Page 14630

—may Allah be pleased with them all.

When I forbade one of their leading figures from practising alchemy and explained its unsoundness and prohibition to him, the evidence became conclusive against him. He began asking to be excused from further debate, acknowledging that he had been defeated in argument. Among other things, he said to me: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, knew alchemy.” I replied that this was a lie—indeed, that it entailed disbelief—for Allah says in His Book:

“Nor is there blame on those who, when they came to you for mounts and you said, ‘I can find no mounts for you,’ turned away with their eyes overflowing with tears, grieving that they had nothing to spend.”

There is unanimous agreement that this verse was revealed concerning the expedition to Tabūk. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had urged people to give charity for that expedition, until “a man brought a she-camel fitted with a bridle and reins, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘For it you shall have seven hundred she-camels fitted with bridles and reins.’”

Abū ‘Aqīl brought a sā‘ (a dry measure), but some of the hypocrites criticized his offering, saying: “Allah had no need of this man’s sā‘.” Another man brought a pouch so heavy that his hand could scarcely carry it, and they said: “He is showing off.” Then Allah Most High revealed:

“Those who criticize the believers who give charity voluntarily, as well as those who can give only what little they have, and ridicule them—Allah ridicules them, and theirs is a painful punishment.”

“‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān undertook to provide a thousand she-camels. He fell fifty short, so he completed the number with fifty horses. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘Nothing ‘Uthmān does after today will harm him.’”

This became one of his celebrated merits, and he is therefore called ‘the outfitter of the Army of Hardship.’

---

## Page 14631

Allah has also said:

“There is no blame on the weak or the sick,”

up to His words:

“Nor is there blame on those who, when they came to you for mounts and you said, ‘I can find no mounts for you,’ turned away with their eyes overflowing with tears, grieving that they had nothing to spend.”

It has been said that they asked him to supply them with ni‘āl (sandals). Whether ni‘āl here means sandals worn on the feet or animals used as mounts, Allah has reported that His Prophet said to them:

“I can find no mounts for you.”

Yet he was urging people in the strongest terms to contribute. Had alchemy been real and lawful, and had he known it, he would have been obliged to practise it to obtain the means to equip the army. For whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory. Whoever attributes this to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, attributes to him something from which Allah has declared him free.

Furthermore, none of the scholars of the Muslim community has prescribed any financial due on alchemy—neither a fifth, nor zakat, nor anything else. They have, however, agreed that a fifth is due on rikāz (buried wealth), as established in an authentic hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. What unquestionably constitutes rikāz is treasure buried in pre-Islamic times: treasures buried in the earth, like mineral deposits. The scholars of the Hijaz do not include mineral deposits within rikāz; this is also the position of Ahmad and others. The scholars of Iraq do include them. Some scholars distinguish between cases where the wealth is found all at once and cases where it is not. Al-Shāfi‘ī has several well-known positions on the matter. The majority of scholars require a due on mineral deposits: either zakat or a fifth.

---

## Page 14632

And if alchemy were real and lawful, the due payable on it would be greater than a fifth and greater than zakat, for it would yield vast quantities of gold with little effort—less than is needed to extract minerals or recover buried treasure. Yet religious scholars regard it as fraudulent, false and forbidden: neither practising it nor treating its products as wealth is lawful, let alone requiring the dues payable on lawful wealth.

The man with whom I was discussing it said to me, “But Moses, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, practised alchemy.”

I replied, “That is a lie. No scholar among the Muslims or the People of the Book has reported this about Moses. Rather, reports transmitted from the People of the Book state that Moses was entitled to a due from them on which he lived. Had he practised alchemy, he would have supported himself from it.”

He said, “But Qārūn practised alchemy.”

I replied, “This too is false. No recognized scholar has said it. It is merely related by the likes of al-Tha‘labī in his Quran commentary, on the authority of someone he does not name. Al-Tha‘labī’s commentary contains both worthless and valuable material, for he collects reports indiscriminately. Had Qārūn’s wealth come from alchemy, he would not have been exceptional in this regard, since countless people have practised alchemy. Yet Allah, glorified be He, said:

‘We gave him such treasures that even their keys would have burdened a group of strong men.’

“He thus states that He gave him treasures whose very keys would have burdened a group of strong men. These treasures were either ones he had amassed himself, as Allah says:

‘And those who hoard gold and silver,’

and the rest of the verse;

---

## Page 14633

or buried hoards he had discovered, which constitute buried treasure. There is no doubt that such treasure exists.”

That man later died. He had been a preacher at a congregational mosque, yet fewer than ten Muslims, including his neighbours, attended his funeral. He practised sorcery and sīmiyā (an occult art), and used to buy many scholarly books. I therefore attended the sale of his books. The auctioneer began calling out “books of the craft”—meaning books on alchemy—which were numerous. Its practitioners call it “the science of the noble stone” and “the science of wisdom,” describing it in various terms.

The official overseeing the sale was a military officer, and members of the administration were present. I told the official in charge, “It is not lawful to sell these books. People buy them and put their contents into practice; then the authorities call them ‘counterfeiters’ and cut off their hands. By selling these books, you will have enabled them to do that.” I instructed the auctioneer to throw them into a pool there, and they were left in it until the water ruined them and their contents were no longer legible.

Another point that makes this clear is that alchemy has never been practised by anyone enjoying an honourable reputation among the Muslim community: neither a scholar whose guidance is followed, nor a shaykh regarded as an example, nor a just king, nor a conscientious minister. Rather, it is practised by misguided shaykhs devoted to falsehood, such as Ibn Sab‘īn and his like, or the Banū ‘Ubayd, or by a tyrannical king or a wicked man. Although its true nature may be unclear to some people of intelligence and religious commitment, most of them eventually recognize it, and they do not

---

## Page 14634

succeed in practising it, as Allah protects them because of their good intentions. I know of no man among the best of the Muslims who has spent any of its proceeds or derived his sustenance from it.

As for the claim some people make that the friends of Allah practise it, this can only be one of two things: either it is a lie, or someone practising it has imagined himself to be one of Allah’s friends singled out for such a miraculous favour. This is ignorance, for alchemy is practised by polytheists, Jews, Christians, evildoers and religious innovators; it is not exclusive to the friends of Allah. Indeed, no one whose standing as a friend of Allah is established is known to practise it.

As for those who have spoken of it while claiming to be among Allah’s friends, such as the author of al-Fusūs and others like him, their statements about religion contain errors and misguidance far greater than those found in their statements about alchemy. If their statements about Allah’s oneness, prophethood and the Last Day contain misguidance contrary to the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger and the consensus of the Muslims—indeed, things that even the Jews and Christians have not said—then what must their statements about alchemy be like?

Furthermore, as for anyone misled by what the author of Kitāb al-Saʿāda (The Book of Happiness) says in that work, in Jawāhir al-Qurʾān (The Jewels of the Quran), and in similar books, these books contain objectionable statements contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community’s early generations and leading scholars, as is plain to anyone knowledgeable about these matters. Muslim scholars have refuted the philosophical assertions contained in these books and similar forms of misguidance that contradict the Quran and the Sunnah.

Some people challenge the transmission of these books from the person to whom they are attributed, saying that it is a lie

---

## Page 14635

against him to attribute these books to him.

Others say that he in fact retracted those views, since it is firmly established that elsewhere he expressed the opposite of what he says in these books, and that he died while studying al-Bukhārī and Muslim.

Certainly, extraordinary events may occur for the friends of Allah, such as a plant turning into gold. But this does not occur through the methods of alchemy involving natural processes. The difference between the two is like the difference between Moses’ staff and the magicians’ staffs: the former was a serpent moving swiftly, whereas the latter merely appeared to him, through their magic, to be moving.

In short, the fact that some people associated with learning and worship have believed alchemy to be genuine and lawful proves nothing. Only a fool would invoke the opinion of a group of scholars and worshippers when they are opposed by others whom the Muslim community regards as greater and more eminent. If following scholarly authority is itself proof, then those who are greater, more learned and more devoted to worship have a stronger claim to be followed. If it is not proof, then citing these people is of no benefit. Either way, this proves nothing.

It should suffice him that the best generations of this community—those among whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent, and those who followed them—never engaged in any of this. Had it been lawful, they would have engaged in it just as they engaged in other permissible activities. They earned wealth through various means, and earning wealth and spending it in obedience to Allah is a righteous deed.

It is authentically reported in al-Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every Muslim must give charity.
They asked, ‘What if someone has nothing to give?’ He replied:

---

## Page 14636

‘He should work with his own hands, benefiting himself and giving in charity.’
They asked, ‘What if he cannot?’ He replied, ‘He should assist a craftsman or do the work for someone who lacks the skill.’
They asked, ‘What if he cannot?’ He replied, ‘He should restrain himself from evil, for that is charity he gives to himself.’”

What further clarifies this matter is that Allah, glorified and exalted, created things in genera, classes and species, sharing certain features while differing from one another in others. Animals, for example, share the capacity for sensation and voluntary movement; these are inseparable characteristics of them all. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The truest names are Hārith and Hammām.”

For every person necessarily engages in labour—his earning and work—and necessarily has an intention, which is the starting point of his will. Animals are distinguished from one another by features that set each apart from the others. These distinguishing characteristics are particular to each kind, just as their shared attributes are common to them all. Examples include human speech, the neighing of horses, the grunting of camels, the braying of donkeys, and the like.

Likewise, plants share with animals the characteristics of growth and nourishment. Plants, however, have neither sensation nor a will by which they move. Minerals also share some of these characteristics.

It is known that these classes—which are called species and are distinguished from one another by these particular characteristics—once constituted by these specific distinguishing properties, cannot be turned by any human being into other species. Nor can a human being produce that distinguishing property and invest them with another. Thus, he cannot turn wheat into barley, nor

---

## Page 14637

a horse into a donkey, or a donkey into an ox. Likewise, he cannot turn silver into gold or copper into silver, and so forth. All he can do is imitate its appearance and pass it off deceptively. Any alchemist who claims that silver is gold that has not fully matured has spoken falsely. Rather, each has its own mineral deposit, as is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He was asked, ‘Who are the noblest people?’
He replied, ‘Those most mindful of Allah.’
They said, ‘That is not what we are asking you about.’
He replied, ‘Joseph, Allah’s prophet, son of Jacob, Allah’s prophet, son of Isaac, Allah’s prophet, son of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend.’
They said, ‘That is not what we are asking you about.’
He replied, ‘Are you asking me about the ancestral origins of the Arabs? People are mines, like mines of gold and silver.’”

Just as Quraysh does not have the same origin as Tamīm, nor ʿAdnān the same origin as Qahtān, nor the Arabs the same origin as non-Arabs, so gold does not have the same origin as silver, nor silver the same origin as gold. Even supposing that a gold deposit contains silver, just as a silver deposit contains copper, the same applies to the dross in mineral deposits. It is well known that whatever is extracted from a mineral deposit must be purified of its dross. People know of many silver deposits—as many as Allah has willed—from which no gold is extracted. If silver became gold once it had been heated sufficiently, gold would emerge from silver deposits—unless it is said:

“Such a deposit does not naturally possess the heat required to bring it to maturity.”

The reply is that this too refutes the alchemists’ claim, for Allah, glorified is He, creates

---

## Page 14638

gold in mineral deposits through heat and moisture, bringing it into being there just as He creates embryos in wombs, and trees and crops in cultivated land, through heat that He creates. The heat He has placed within those bodies, through which He creates, cannot be replaced by the heat of the fires we make.

In short, these two principles—that what is naturally created cannot be manufactured, and what is manufactured cannot be naturally created, and that kinds distinguished by their particular properties cannot be transformed from one kind into another—are borne out by systematic observation, reason, and religious evidence derived from the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Moreover, Allah has instilled rejection of this in His servants, making it common throughout His lands. The punishment of its practitioners is generally evident, even though some practise it secretly.

Furthermore, those who practise alchemy and claim that it is genuine and lawful would not buy gold offered to them as they would mined gold if they were told, “It was produced by alchemy,” even if it underwent the same tests they apply to gold they know to be genuine. Rather, people are naturally disposed to accuse anyone who does this of fraud, deceit, and deceptive imitation. People are Allah’s witnesses on earth.

Moreover, the leading practitioners of “alchemy” combine it with what they call sīmiyā (a form of magic), as was practised by Ibn Sabʿīn, al-Suhrawardī, who was put to death, and al-Hallāj

---

## Page 14639

and others like them. It is well known that such magic is forbidden by the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the consensus of the Muslim community. Indeed, most scholars hold that a sorcerer is a disbeliever who must be put to death. The execution of sorcerers is established in reports from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, Hafsah bint ʿUmar, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, and Jundub ibn ʿAbd Allāh. A report to this effect has also been transmitted through the latter and attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Allah Most High says:

“The sorcerer will never succeed, wherever he may go.”

He also says:

“They followed what the devils recited during the reign of Solomon. Solomon did not disbelieve, but the devils disbelieved, teaching people sorcery and what was revealed to the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. Yet the two would teach no one without saying, ‘We are only a trial, so do not disbelieve.’ They learned from them what would cause separation between a man and his wife, but they could not harm anyone through it except by Allah’s permission. They learned what harmed them and did not benefit them. They certainly knew that whoever purchased it would have no share in the Hereafter. How wretched was that for which they sold themselves, if only they knew!”

Thus, He, glorified be He, makes clear that those who seek sorcery know that its practitioner will have no share in the Hereafter. Nevertheless, they seek worldly gains through it: power and wealth. “If they had believed and feared Allah,” they would have received a reward from Allah in this world and the Hereafter better for them than what they seek. This is why you find those who engage in sorcery and the invocation of the heavenly bodies

---

## Page 14640

and hymns in their praise, addressing them and prostrating before them, seeking nothing but wealth and power. Such a person disbelieves and associates partners with Allah for the sake of the power and wealth he imagines he will obtain. Yet he obtains only what harms him and does not benefit him, as a survey of people’s circumstances shows.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, counted among the major sins associating partners with Allah, sorcery, taking a life, usury, fleeing the battlefield, and accusing chaste, unsuspecting believing women of unchastity, as well as other offences, including other kinds of sorcery. Its forms are diverse.

The point here is simply that “sīmiyā,” which is a form of sorcery, is very often associated with alchemy. It is necessarily known in Islam that sorcery is among the gravest prohibitions. Since alchemy is frequently associated with it, rather than with people of knowledge and faith, it is clear that alchemy is not among the practices of people of knowledge and faith, but among those of people of disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience.

All this concerns someone who succeeds in practising alchemy and can spend what he obtains from it without objection. Most of those who pursue it have neither achieved this nor been able to do so. Even those who achieve it once are generally unable to repeat it, quite apart from the harmful consequences that arise.

Anyone who surveys the circumstances of those who pursue it will find confirmation of what the imams said:

---

## Page 14641

“Whoever seeks wealth through alchemy becomes bankrupt; whoever seeks knowledge through speculative theology falls into heresy; and whoever seeks out rare hadith reports falls into lying.”

How much money they have spent on it! How many men they have associated with in its pursuit! How much idle talk they have indulged in, how many hopes and ambitions they have pinned upon it, and how many nights they have stayed awake over it! Yet they have gained nothing but worldly and religious ruin, diminished reason and knowledge, exposure of their honour to attack, abasement and humiliation, need and poverty, abundant worries and sorrows, and the company of the worst companions. They have been distracted from what benefits them in their livelihood and their ultimate return, and have turned away from gathering provisions that would benefit them in the Hereafter.

This is especially so because it so often leads its practitioners into various kinds of disobedience and wickedness: its seeker pursues it as a lover pursues the beloved. Indeed, it may even lead to disbelief in the Most Merciful, turning away from faith and the Quran, and embracing the errors of those who associate partners with Allah and worship idols. This is worldly and religious ruin: he has gained nothing but a false hope, like lightning that promises rain but brings none, or the mirage that

“the thirsty person mistakes for water, until, when he reaches it, he finds nothing there, but finds Allah there, who pays him his account in full. And Allah is swift in reckoning.”

In this, they are like someone who imagines that disbelieving priests and monks are among Allah’s believing servants, or someone who imagines that people of innovation, falsehood, and deception are among the friends of Allah, those who

“will have no fear, nor will they grieve,”

“who believe and are mindful of Allah,”

“for whom there are glad tidings in the life of this world

---

## Page 14642

and in the Hereafter. There is no changing the words of Allah. That is the supreme triumph.”

Indeed, many of them suppose that lying claimants to prophethood, such as Musaylimah, al-Ansī, and their like, rank alongside the truthful prophets, such as Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and Muhammad—may Allah’s blessings be upon them all.

Confusing truth with falsehood, a prophet with a pretender to prophethood, someone who speaks with knowledge with someone who speaks in ignorance, or a true friend of Allah with a lying hypocrite is like confusing naturally occurring gold with artificially produced gold. Allah has distinguished truth from falsehood, but only those endowed with knowledge and faith among Muhammad’s community—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—have been guided to distinguish fully between them. They are those of whom Allah says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and as warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning their differences. None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of rivalry among themselves. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they had differed. And Allah guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

They are the justly balanced community, Allah’s witnesses on earth who bear truthful witness concerning His creation. They are those who enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong. May Allah, through His grace and bounty, make us and all our brethren their followers, enable us to follow their example, and gather us in their company.

Among the strongest arguments advanced by the “alchemists” is their appeal to glass as evidence. They say:

---

## Page 14643

“For glass is made from sand, pebbles, and the like.” They thus draw an analogy between this and what they produce through alchemy. This argument is invalid. For Allah, glorified and exalted be He, creates glass for people neither in mineral deposits nor elsewhere; rather, glass belongs to the category of manufactured things, like bricks, pottery, and similar things fired in a furnace.

It has already been explained that Allah, glorified and exalted be He, has given human beings the ability to make various kinds of food, clothing, and dwellings. Likewise, He has given them the ability to manufacture vessels of pottery, glass, and similar materials. Yet He has provided them with no means of making the like of what He has created.

Once it becomes clear that glass belongs to the category of manufactured things rather than naturally created things, and offers no instance in which the manufactured might be confused with the naturally created, the argument for alchemy collapses. For human beings cannot manufacture the like of the fundamental things Allah has created, nor can they transform one created kind of animal, plant, or mineral into another created kind. This holds invariably, without exception. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who sold property and real estate that subsequently proved to belong to someone else: Is the purchaser to be credited with the purchase price against the rent due for the property, if it has a rental value? And does entitlement to

---

## Page 14644

rent depend upon the purchaser’s benefiting from the sale, or not? Can the purchaser recover from the seller the rent legally payable for the property during the period it remained in his possession, or the purchase price he paid?

He replied:

If the purchaser knew that the property had been wrongfully seized, he is a wrongdoer and is liable for its rental value, whether he benefited from it or not. If he did not know, ultimate liability rests with the wrongdoing seller. If the property is taken from the purchaser’s possession, he may demand repayment of the price received by the seller. If rent is collected from him when he had been deceived, he may recover it from the seller who deceived him. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

What of someone who says that “sīmiyā” and “alchemy” are two sciences belonging to the knowledge of the prophets and the allies of Allah? Some of them transmit concerning alchemy—that is, silver, al-khadmā or al-khidma—the saying, “Whoever asfāhā eats what is lawful,” and similar sayings?

He replied:

Whoever says that “sīmiyā and alchemy” belong to the knowledge of the prophets and the allies of Allah is a liar and a fabricator. No prophet is known to have spoken about either of them, nor any ally of Allah recognized as such by the Muslim community. As for sīmiyā, it is a form of magic:

“The magician will never succeed, wherever he may go.”

---

## Page 14645

And there is no doubt that sorcerers may be mistaken for prophets and allies of Allah, and perform feats thought to rival those of the prophets, just as Pharaoh’s sorcerers sought to rival the miracle of Moses:

“Then Moses threw down his staff, and behold, it swallowed what they had fabricated”—through to His words—“prostrate.”

As for “alchemy,” its product is an imitation of naturally created gold and silver. Alchemy is not confined to these two; imitations are also made of gems, such as pearls and peridot; fragrant substances, such as musk, ambergris and roses; and foodstuffs.

Alchemy is invalid on natural grounds and forbidden by religious law. It is established in an authentic report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever deceives us is not one of us.”

Alchemy is a form of deception. Allah has created nothing except according to a determined measure, and creatures cannot manufacture the like of what Allah Most High has created. Allah Most High says:

“Or have they assigned to Allah partners who created the like of His creation?”

In an authentic hadith, Allah Most High says:

“Who is more unjust than someone who sets out to create as I create? Let them create a tiny ant, or let them create a grain of barley.”

The philosophers say, “Artifice cannot do what nature does.” This means that manufactured gold, silver and other substances cannot be like their natural counterparts, which are created by the natural force permeating physical bodies. Thus, among created things, there is nothing whose like creatures have manufactured; and He has not created for them anything like what they manufacture. Thus, they grind

---

## Page 14646

food, weave clothes and build houses, yet He has not created the like of these for them. Likewise, they manufacture glass from sand and pebbles, yet He has not created its like.

This is among the arguments alchemists have advanced to establish the validity of alchemy. It is an invalid argument for the reason already mentioned: if glass had been created naturally and its like had then been manufactured, this would constitute evidence. But that is not the case. The vast majority of rational thinkers, past and present, who have spoken knowledgeably on this subject know that alchemy produces an imitation, and that the like of gold created naturally in mines cannot be manufactured; indeed, gold itself cannot be manufactured. Every imitation is exposed sooner or later, though some resemble the original very closely and others less so. This subject has been discussed at length elsewhere.

He was asked:

A man bought a slave who was free of defects, then sold him as such. The slave subsequently stole a sum of money from the second purchaser and absconded. May the purchaser recover the purchase price from the first seller or the second, or claim compensation for the defect, or not?

He replied:

The purchaser may demand compensation for the defect, without any disagreement among the scholars. The meaning of

---

## Page 14647

this is that the slave is valued both without any defect and with these two defects. The purchase price is then reduced in proportion to the reduction in value. Thus, if his value without defects is four hundred and his value with the defects is two hundred, half the purchase price is deducted.

May he recover the entire purchase price from the master who concealed the defect? There is a well-known disagreement among the scholars concerning this. The position of Mālik and Ahmad, according to the more explicit of the two reports from him, is that he may recover the entire purchase price from him. Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, in his other opinion, held that he may recover that from him.

He was asked:

A man bought a slave woman, and it became apparent that she was in love with the master who had sold her. The second owner then sold her to a third. May the third return her to the second, and may the second return her to the first, or not?

He replied:

Yes. This is a defect that ordinarily reduces her value considerably. If it is established and the purchaser was unaware of it, he may return her to his seller, the second purchaser. If the second purchaser was unaware of the defect, he may return her to the first seller. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14648

He was asked:

A man bought a slave woman who was healthy and free of defects, but she ran away on the very day he bought her, without having been beaten or mistreated. May the purchaser recover the purchase price from the seller before she is found and brought back?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the slave woman had previously been known to run away, the seller concealed this defect, and she then ran away from the purchaser, the purchaser may demand repayment of the purchase price from the seller, according to the more correct of the scholars’ two opinions. This is the position of Mālik and the explicitly reported one of Ahmad’s two opinions. According to the other opinion, the seller may be required to pay compensation for the defect.

If, however, she had not run away before, but ran away because of something the purchaser did to her, the seller bears no liability. If the defect of running away, or any other defect, arises after possession has been taken, the purchaser has no right to return the slave according to Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad.

Mālik, however, says:

He may return the slave on that account until three full days have elapsed, and beyond that up to one year; he may return the slave for insanity, leprosy, or vitiligo. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14649

He was asked:

Neither party to the sale knew of any defect in an animal. It remained with the purchaser for about a month, after which he discovered a defect in it. What is the ruling?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If a defect is discovered that existed before the sale and of which the purchaser was unaware, he may return the animal on account of that defect, provided he has done nothing indicating his acceptance of it.

He was asked:

A man sold some wheat, which the purchaser sowed, but it perished. The purchaser then demanded that the seller pay the land tax. Is the seller obliged to do so? Is the purchaser entitled to demand this from him? What if the purchaser claims that the defect was present while the wheat was with the seller?

He replied:

If he sold it and delivered it to the purchaser, and it subsequently perished while in the purchaser’s possession, or the purchaser sowed it and it perished, the seller bears no liability. Rather, he is entitled to the full price, unless there was a defect, deception, or something similar.

---

## Page 14650

If the purchaser claims that the wheat perished because of a defect it already had, but others who bought some of the same wheat testify that it was free of defects, the purchaser’s claim is not accepted. Even if the seller has no evidence, his word is accepted upon his oath, unless the purchaser produces evidence.

Furthermore, if experts state that defective wheat does not grow normally, whereas this wheat grew normally and subsequently failed, that constitutes evidence in the seller’s favour.

He was asked:

A man sold his wife a house for four hundred dirhams in a sale subject to redemption. She has already recovered the money through rental income. May she take anything further, having received the four hundred? Is that forbidden to her?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah alone. The purpose of this and similar arrangements is for one party to advance money to another and receive the income from the property in return for the use of that money. As long as the recipient remains liable for the money, the other party receives the property’s income; when the recipient repays the money, he takes back the property. An arrangement of this kind is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims. If this is their intention, but they give the transaction the outward form of a sale, it is likewise impermissible according to the more correct of the two scholarly opinions. Even according to those who regard it as valid, the sale must satisfy the requirements of Islamic law. Thus, if he stipulates that

---

## Page 14651

the property must be returned to him when he repays the purchase price, the sale is invalid. According to the more correct of the two scholarly opinions, a condition agreed upon before the contract has the same force as one stipulated when the contract is concluded.

Accordingly, any rental income the woman received after learning that this arrangement was forbidden must be counted towards repayment of the principal. Whatever she received before that is subject to the disagreement already mentioned. If the two reach an amicable settlement concerning this, that is better. According to the more correct of the two opinions, she is not obliged to return what she received under a contract whose validity is disputed if she believed it to be valid.

He was asked:

A man who owned a vineyard asked someone to lend him some dirhams. The latter refused unless the man sold him the vineyard for one hundred dirhams, on condition that he would return the vineyard when the man brought back the money. The man sold him the vineyard on this condition, but the condition was not mentioned in the contract. After the contract was concluded, the purchaser said to a group of witnesses: “Bear witness that whenever this man brings back my dirhams, I will return his vineyard to him.” Is this sale valid or not? Must the purchaser fulfil the obligation he undertook to return the vineyard? Is it permissible for the purchaser to deceive the seller?

He replied:

This is not a binding sale. Rather, he must return the man’s vineyard when the man gives him back his dirhams. It is not lawful for him to deceive him.

---

## Page 14652

He was asked:

A woman bought a piece of cloth to sew, but later found that it was unfinished and had tears in it. Must the merchant accept it if she returns it to him?

He replied:

She may demand compensation from him for the pre-existing defect. If its value has decreased because of a defect she herself caused, she may return it while compensating him for the newly caused defect, according to the more correct of the two scholarly opinions. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man sold property belonging to a daughter under his guardianship for one thousand and eighty dirhams—under an arrangement requiring its return upon repayment of the price—although it was worth four thousand dirhams. The witnesses testified and stated in the written document that the seller’s daughter had authorized the sale, although they had not been in her presence and had no clear knowledge of her circumstances. Is this sale valid?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Such a sale is invalid, and the purchase price must be returned. It is not permissible for a father to sell at such a gross undervalue. In the case of a woman under legal guardianship, no validity attaches to

---

## Page 14653

her authorization or the testimony of witnesses that she authorized such a transaction. Rather, once this becomes known, the sale must be annulled in all circumstances.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Has any scholar stated that if the first purchaser is not permitted to dispose of the property before taking possession of it, and it is destroyed, he nevertheless bears liability for it? Or that permission to dispose of it and liability necessarily entail one another in both directions? Is there not well-known disagreement on this matter?

He replied:

There are two views within Ahmad’s school:

The first is the approach of al-Qādī and his followers. The later scholars of Ahmad’s school, together with Abū Hanīfah and the Shāfiʿīs, hold that permission to dispose of property and liability necessarily entail one another. According to them, whatever comes under the purchaser’s liability may be disposed of by him, while whatever has not come under his liability may not. Accordingly, al-Shāfiʿī applied this principle consistently to the sale of fruit still on the trees and did not hold that relief should be granted for losses caused by calamities. This was based on the premise that once the purchaser takes possession of the fruit and is permitted to dispose of it, liability for it passes to him.

The second view within Ahmad’s school, mentioned by al-Khiraqī and other early scholars and supported by Ahmad’s fundamental principles, is that liability and permission to dispose of property do not necessarily entail one another. Hence, the apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that if the fruit is destroyed before the purchaser is able

---

## Page 14654

to harvest it, liability for it rests with the seller, even though the apparent position of his school permits the purchaser to dispose of it by sale or otherwise. Thus, he permits the purchaser to dispose of it while liability remains with the seller.

The Sunnah establishes that liability for the fruit rests with the seller. In Sahīh Muslim, Jābir reports that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity strikes it, it is not lawful for you to take any of its price. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s wealth without right?”

However, the other report from Ahmad, prohibiting disposal of this fruit, accords with the first approach.

Among the arguments supporting this approach is that the lessor bears liability for the benefits stipulated in a lease before they are received. This means that if they are lost through a calamity beyond human control, such as the death of the animal, and the benefits become unavailable, liability rests with the lessor, because they were lost before it was possible to receive them. Nevertheless, the lessee may dispose of those benefits, even by selling them, according to the apparent position of the school. There is, however, another report from Ahmad that the lessee may not sublet the property for more than the rent he pays if no enhancement has been made to it, lest he profit from something for which he has borne no liability. This is also Abū Hanīfah’s position. Abū Hanīfah holds that ownership of the benefits is not acquired through the contract, but only gradually as they are received.

According to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, he and other scholars permit subletting for more than the original rent. They say:

“This is not profit from something for which one bears no liability, because liability for these benefits rests with

---

## Page 14655

the purchaser, in the sense that if he leaves them unused despite being able to receive them, he bears liability for them. Liability rests with the seller only if they are lost before it becomes possible to receive them.” Accordingly, the apparent position of Ahmad’s school concerning contractual liability distinguishes between what one is able to take possession of and what one is not, rather than between what has actually been received and what has not.

An example is the position stated by al-Khiraqī and others: liability for a specifically identified heap of grain sold in bulk without measurement passes to the purchaser upon conclusion of the contract. Yet they do not permit the purchaser to sell it until he moves it, on account of the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar. Ibn ‘Umar reported both rulings. He said:

“It is part of the Sunnah that whatever is alive and intact when the transaction is concluded belongs to the purchaser.”

He also said, as al-Bukhārī narrated from him:

“We used to purchase food in bulk without measurement, but we were forbidden to sell it where it lay until we had moved it to our quarters.”

Thus, disposal is permitted where liability rests with the seller, as with fruit, while disposal is prohibited where liability rests with the purchaser, as with a heap of food. This establishes that the two do not necessarily go together.

Another argument supporting this position is that the purchaser may not dispose of everything for which he bears liability, as shown by property taken into possession invalidly and property received in an invalid taking of possession.

As for the first case, suppose someone purchases a qafīz (a measure of capacity) from a heap of grain, or a ratl (a unit of weight) from a lump of metal, or something similar whose delivery requires measuring or weighing, but then takes possession of the entire heap or lump. This is an invalid taking of possession and does not permit him to dispose of it unless his property is distinguished

---

## Page 14656

from the seller’s property. Nevertheless, if it perished while in his possession, he would be liable for it. Moreover, the purchaser is not prohibited from every form of disposal; the Sunnah addresses sale specifically. If he emancipated the slave he had purchased before taking possession, the emancipation would be valid by consensus. Scholars have differed concerning gifts and other forms of disposal. They have also differed concerning the income from food sold before the prohibition against taking possession of it, for that is what is established in the texts and agreed upon by the scholars. They have likewise differed over the subsidiary rulings derived from this principle.

The fundamental principles of Islamic law support this approach. Not everything for which a person bears liability is something he may dispose of, as with usurped property and property borrowed for use. Nor does everyone entitled to dispose of something necessarily bear liability for it. An owner, for example, may dispose of his property that has been usurped or lent for use: he may sell the usurped property to the usurper or to someone able to recover it from him, even though liability rests with the usurper.

Likewise, the principle that liability accompanies entitlement to revenue applies only where ownership and possession are united in the same person. When ownership belongs to one person and possession to another, the revenue may belong to the owner while liability rests with the person holding the property. Furthermore, selling a debt to the debtor is permissible according to the apparent position of the schools of Ahmad and al-Shāfi‘ī; Abū Hanīfah holds the same view. Mālik permits selling it to someone other than the debtor, and this is also one position reported from Ahmad, even though the owner does not bear liability for the debt.

Moreover, once the seller has enabled the purchaser to take possession, he has fulfilled his obligation;

---

## Page 14657

and it is the purchaser who is negligent in failing to take possession, so liability rests with him. This differs from the case in which the seller has not enabled him to take possession by failing to deliver the full amount due—by not measuring, weighing, or counting it. In that case, it is like a debt that he has not paid him. If the seller fails to fulfil his obligation to deliver the full amount, he is the negligent party and therefore bears liability, for negligence is grounds for liability.

Whether disposal is lawful or prohibited, however, depends on other considerations. One such consideration may be the ability to deliver the property, so that the transaction does not resemble a sale involving uncertainty. If the purchaser has not moved it from its location, the seller may deny having sold it, leading to a dispute. The seller may also prevent him from delivering it.

Similarly, taking possession is stipulated in the case of a pledge because its purpose is to recover the debt from the pledged property when it cannot be recovered from the pledgor. This purpose can be achieved only if the pledgee holds the pledged property. If it remains in the pledgor’s hands, by contrast, he can prevent the pledgee from gaining access to it.

Another reason may be that the purpose of contracts is for the parties to take possession of what is due to them: taking possession completes the contract and fulfils its purpose. Thus, if unbelievers embrace Islam and bring their disputes before us, having concluded contracts that they consider permissible and having exchanged possession under them, we do not rescind those contracts, even if they are prohibited in the religion of the Muslims. If, however, possession has not yet been exchanged, we annul them, lest this lead to authorizing a prohibited taking of possession after their conversion to Islam. Thus, when property is sold before possession is taken, the purchaser’s ownership of it is not yet complete;

---

## Page 14658

rather, it remains vulnerable to adverse eventualities, both legally and physically. Selling it before taking possession therefore falls within the category of sales involving uncertainty. Hence the prohibition on selling spoils before taking possession. For the same reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling what one does not have, because one is unable to effect the transfer of possession required by the contract.
