Some understand the hadith to cover both debts and specifically identified property, and regard delivery as an exception to it. Others restrict it to specifically identified property, interpreting it as prohibiting the sale of a particular item that one does not own. They understand “what you do not have” to mean “what you do not own.”

Others understand it to require both ownership and possession, or stipulate that the property sold must be owned and possessed. Thus, one may not sell property that one owns but cannot deliver, since this constitutes a sale involving uncertainty, such as selling a runaway slave or a horse that has strayed. This is the argument of those who prohibit selling a debt to someone other than the debtor. They argue: “It involves uncertainty and has not been taken into possession.” Those who permit it say: “Selling it is like referring someone to the debtor for payment, or selling property held on deposit or lent for use: it is constructively possessed. For this reason, we permit the sale of fruit.”

The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that, when fruit whose ripeness has become apparent is purchased and its price has been received, it remains at the seller’s risk. This is because he remains responsible for delivering possession until harvesting is complete, while the purchaser has not yet been able to harvest it. Nevertheless, the purchaser may be given control of it when the seller grants him free access to it: free access is treated as possession for the purposes of disposal, whereas, for the purposes of liability, possession is understood as the ability to obtain the benefit intended by the contract.

Because the underlying grounds of these questions are obscure, jurists have disputed them extensively, and no analogy has proved consistently applicable to them, even conjecturally, as you can see.

---

## Page 14659

Many of them discern no underlying rationale in these matters, but instead adhere to the apparent meaning of the texts. Those following either approach may be inconsistent in these matters; nevertheless, efforts have been directed toward upholding the schools’ positions on them. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man purchased a heap of goods in bulk, without measuring it. It then perished while in his ownership, before he had taken possession of it. He subsequently sold it before taking possession, unaware that it had perished.

If we hold that the first purchaser was not permitted to sell it before taking possession, and it perished, does he bear the loss, or does the original seller?

Has any scholar stated that the first purchaser bears the loss if the goods perish, even though he was not permitted to dispose of them before taking possession? Or do the right to dispose of the goods and liability for their loss necessarily go together, each implying the other?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. In this case, the sale is invalid by agreement if the goods had already perished when the contract was concluded, whether he sold them on the basis of a description, without a description, or on the basis of an inspection made before the contract. Indeed, in such a case, even if they perished after the contract but before they were found to conform to the description or their previously observed condition, the sale would not be rescinded. But if it becomes clear that they had already perished when the contract was concluded, the sale is undoubtedly invalid.

As for liability for their loss, the apparent position of the schools of Mālik and Ahmad is that the loss is borne by

---

## Page 14660

the purchaser, on the basis of the report cited as evidence, narrated by al-Awzāʿī from al-Zuhrī, from Sālim, from Ibn ʿUmar, who said:

“The established Sunnah is that whatever is still in existence and intact when the sale is concluded is at the purchaser’s risk.”

For the apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that goods specifically identified in the contract, whose delivery does not require measuring, weighing, or the like, are at the purchaser’s risk provided he has been able to take possession of them, whether or not he has actually done so.

According to the schools of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, however, the seller bears the loss. This is also the other position reported from Ahmad, and it was preferred by Abū Muhammad. The correct ruling, however, varies according to the case.

According to Abū Hanīfa, purchased property does not pass to the purchaser’s risk until he takes possession, except in the case of real estate. Al-Shāfiʿī makes no distinction between real estate and other property; this is also one position reported from Ahmad. Another position reported from Ahmad distinguishes between goods measured or weighed and other goods. Another distinguishes between food and other goods, while yet another distinguishes between foodstuffs measured or weighed and other goods. These positions reported from him concerning possession parallel those reported from him concerning ribā (usury).

---

## Page 14661

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section: Property Received under an Invalid Contract

The underlying principle is that a valid contract obliges each contracting party to fulfil its requirements. Thus, sales, leases, marriage contracts, and similar binding contracts of exchange require each party to deliver what is due to the other. Their binding nature entails the obligation to fulfil them and the prohibition against breaking them.

As for “revocable contracts,” including all forms of agency and partnership, they do not entail an unconditional obligation of fulfilment. Such a contract is not binding such that it must be fulfilled; rather, it is revocable and permissible, and the party concerned may choose either to maintain it or to terminate it. Terminating it brings it to an end. Yet, as long as the contract remains in force, he must fulfil its requirement to safeguard the property, for it is a contract of trust.

As for the prohibition of wrongdoing, such as betrayal of trust, that obligation is imposed by the revealed law, not by reason: he is forbidden to wrongfully encroach upon the property of anyone, whether that person has entrusted him with it or not. Nevertheless, the contract also imposes this obligation and further reinforces it.

---

## Page 14662

As for the obligation to carry out the work, the more evident view is that the worker in a profit-sharing investment, sharecropping, or orchard-tending contract is negligent if he fails to perform the work required by the contract. Although the contract is revocable, as long as it remains in force it imposes two obligations: safeguarding the property, as with a deposit entrusted for safekeeping, and performing the work required by the contract.

This follows by analogy from the principles of our school, since we require either co-owner to participate in sales and repairs to the extent customarily needed by the other, such as repairing what has fallen into disrepair. This applies to co-ownership, and the same therefore applies to contractual partnerships, whose purpose is to conduct the agreed activities. Failing to act under profit-sharing investment, orchard-tending, and sharecropping contracts may cause greater harm than failing to repair a dilapidated property held in co-ownership, and than failing to sell a jointly owned asset or its use. In the latter cases, the co-owner can sell his share, whereas here the worker has misled him and deprived him of the benefit of his property.

If the contract is invalid, not all its usual consequences are established, such as the obligation to exchange possession and carry out the agreed activities, the permissibility of dealing with and benefiting from the property, and similar matters. Nevertheless, if possession is taken under it, this is possession authorized by a contract, and is therefore unlike a usurper’s taking possession without permission.

For this reason, the jurists said: Whatever entails liability upon taking possession under a valid contract also entails liability upon taking possession under an invalid contract, such as property sold or leased. Whatever does not entail liability upon taking possession under a valid contract likewise does not entail liability upon taking possession under an invalid contract, such as property held in trust under profit-sharing investment contracts, partnerships, and similar arrangements, because permission has been given. Hence the scholars disagreed over whether ownership is acquired by taking possession under such a contract, and over what one is entitled to

---

## Page 14663

by way of compensation—whether it is the stipulated consideration, its customary equivalent, or something similar.

The distinction between the two forms of taking possession rests on two points:

First, in the former case, possession is taken without the owner’s permission, whereas in the latter it is taken with his permission.

Second, in the latter case, possession is required by a contract, albeit a defective one, whereas in the former no contract whatsoever requires it. For this reason, the apparent position of our school requires the stipulated dower in an invalid marriage contract; one of the two views likewise requires the stipulated consideration in an invalid profit-sharing investment contract and similar arrangements.

If the property taken into possession under the contract still exists and the recipient wishes to return it, he returns it. If it is no longer available, he returns an equivalent in kind, where possible. When neither the property itself nor an equivalent can be returned, compensation must be given. This applies, for example, when the item sold is not replaceable in kind but must instead be assessed by value; when benefits have already been enjoyed under an invalid lease; or when a worker has performed work under an invalid partnership arrangement, such as an orchard-tending or profit-sharing investment contract.

Some jurists of our school, in agreement with al-Shāfiʿī, require payment of the value in such cases. Their reasoning is that what is owed is restitution of the property or benefit itself; since neither it nor its equivalent can be restored, its value becomes payable, just as when liability arises through destruction or usurpation.

Al-Shāfiʿī extended this principle to a stipulated dower in marriage that is invalid or consists of usurped property, requiring instead a dower commensurate with that of comparable women. His reasoning was that the right to marital intimacy ought to have been restored because the stipulation was invalid; since it could not be restored, its substitute became payable, namely, a dower commensurate with that of comparable women. Some jurists of his own school disagreed with him.

---

## Page 14664

The majority of our school’s jurists and of scholars generally require a substitute for the stipulated dower—its equivalent in kind or its value—not compensation for marital intimacy. This is unquestionably correct, because the marriage contract here is not invalid, so restitution of the entitlement it confers—marital intimacy—is not required. Since restitution of marital intimacy is not required, neither is compensation for it. Rather, what is required is payment of the stipulated dower if possible, or otherwise its substitute. Thus, it is the substitute for the stipulated dower that is owed, and this is closer to what the parties agreed upon than compensation for marital intimacy. In other contracts, however, invalidity requires restitution of the property itself or its substitute.

The apparent meaning of Ahmad’s statements is that, in partnership arrangements—such as profit-sharing investment contracts and similar arrangements—the stipulated consideration is likewise owed, just as in an invalid marriage contract according to the apparent position of our school. This view is stronger. Indeed, the correct position is that the value of the property or benefit is not invariably payable under an invalid contract. For if the property itself or its equivalent in kind could be returned, that would be obligatory: once the contract is rendered ineffective, every entitlement must be restored to its rightful holder, and an equivalent in kind takes the place of the property itself.

But where the entitlement can no longer be restored—as with intercourse in an invalid marriage, work performed under hiring or profit-sharing investment contracts, or loss through unfair pricing in a sale—its monetary value is not an equivalent in kind. Payment of value is required only in certain cases, such as destroyed or usurped property for which an equivalent in kind cannot be obtained. This is a matter of necessity, since nothing closer to the entitlement than its value is available. That, therefore, is the justice that can be achieved, just as we have made a similar point concerning retribution in kind

---

## Page 14665

and blood money for accidental killing and compensation for wounds. We determine value according to people’s appraisals, since in those cases there are no two contracting parties who have mutually agreed upon anything. Here, however, the parties have agreed that the stipulated consideration should be given in exchange for the property or benefit, whereas others would accept a different consideration for it. Giving weight to the parties’ mutual consent is therefore more appropriate than giving weight to what others would accept.

If it is objected:

“They consented to this consideration only on the basis that the contract would be valid and its legal consequences binding, neither of which applies here.”

The answer is:

“Others likewise assign this value only within a valid contract carrying its own legal consequences. Since such a contract is unavailable here, we posit a contract through which the consideration payable can be determined. It is therefore more appropriate to use the contract they actually concluded as this hypothetical basis than to posit one that never existed at all, to which they never consented, and which no one else concluded either. If hypothetical assumptions and approximations are unavoidable, whatever most closely resembles what actually occurred is the more appropriate assumption and nearer to what is correct.”

This makes clear that requiring the customary dower for a comparable woman in an invalid marriage rests on likening her to comparable women who enter into valid, binding marriages. This requires two hypothetical assumptions: positing a woman comparable to her, and positing a valid marriage with a stipulated dower. We thus compare her with comparable women, and her invalid marriage with their valid marriages. This is an exceedingly remote analogy. If, however, we require the stipulated dower in the invalid marriage, we compare her invalid marriage with her own marriage supposing it were valid; and she is closer to herself than anyone else is to her. Moreover, the parties’ invalid contract is closer to their own contract supposing it were valid than it is to someone else’s valid contract. Where, however, intercourse takes place under a semblance of lawfulness without a marriage contract, the customary dower for a comparable woman is payable.

---

## Page 14666

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section: A Principle Concerning Property Received under an Invalid Contract

There are two possibilities: either the contracting party considers the contract invalid and knows it to be so, or he does not consider it invalid.

In the first case, he is like someone who wrongfully seizes another’s property, since he has taken possession of something he knows he does not own. Yet, because the contract provides a semblance of entitlement and possession was taken by mutual consent, does he acquire ownership by taking possession, or not? Or should a distinction be drawn according to whether or not he has undertaken further transactions involving it? This is the well-known disagreement concerning ownership: is it acquired by taking possession under an invalid contract?

As for a contracting party who considers the contract valid, an example is that of non-Muslims under Muslim protection who conclude contracts among themselves that are forbidden in Islam, such as sales of wine and pigs, and usurious transactions. If possession is taken under these contracts before they embrace Islam or seek adjudication from us, the contracts are upheld for them, and they own what they have received under them, without dispute. This is based on the words of Allah Most High:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are

---

## Page 14667

believers.” Thus, He commanded them to give up what remained. If they embrace Islam or seek adjudication before taking possession, the contract is annulled, and the property must be returned if it still exists, or its equivalent if it no longer does. The basis for this is the saying of Allah Most High:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are believers,”

through to His words:

“But if you repent, you may have your principal.”

Allah Most High commanded them to relinquish the usury still owed, but did not command them to return what they had received before embracing Islam. He entitled them to their principal in addition to what they had received before embracing Islam. It is therefore clear that whatever a person received under such a contract before embracing Islam becomes his property.

If, however, they embrace Islam while a usurious contract remains in force between them, it is annulled. Once it is annulled upon their embracing Islam, the creditor becomes entitled to the principal he advanced, but not to the usurious increment that he has not yet received. Nor must what he received before embracing Islam be charged against his principal, because he acquired ownership of it by taking possession under a contract he believed to be valid, and that contract entitled him to receive it. Were we to make him liable for it, we would effectively require him to return it and deduct it from the principal he is entitled to claim. That would contradict what has already been established.

The same applies to every contract that a Muslim believed valid on an interpretive basis arising from juristic reasoning or approval. Examples include usurious transactions permitted by those who allow legal stratagems; the sale of the disputed kind of nabīdh (an infused drink) by those who consider such sales valid; and prohibited sales involving uncertainty by those who permit some of them. For when both parties have taken possession under these contracts in the belief that they are valid,

---

## Page 14668

they are not subsequently annulled, whether by a judicial ruling or by a departure from that juristic opinion. If, however, before both parties have taken possession, they seek adjudication from someone who knows these contracts to be invalid, or consult him for a fatwa when their error becomes apparent and the earlier opinion is abandoned, whatever was received on the basis of the earlier belief is allowed to stand.

If the principal and a usurious increment remain owed, the increment is cancelled and the claim is restricted to the principal. The recipient need not return what he previously received on the basis of his earlier belief, just as with those under Muslim protection—and with even greater reason, since the belief held by the latter is certainly false.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

If, in an invalid transaction in which restitution—returning what was received or its equivalent—is impossible, requiring the stipulated consideration or its equivalent is closer to equity than requiring an equivalent of the consideration specified in the contract, by comparison with this property for which compensation is owed, then we say:

Where something is invalid, its equivalent is likewise invalid. A deferred payment is not equivalent to an immediate one, nor is one kind equivalent to the other. Thus, if someone advanced dirhams to another for an item to be delivered later, and it did not

---

## Page 14669

change in price, and we hold that this is a salam contract—an advance payment for future delivery—then, if he returns the principal immediately, or its equivalent, this is what is required.

But if he delays repayment until delivery under the salam contract falls due, then seeks to return an amount equivalent to the principal, this is not truly equivalent to it. Requiring delivery of the stipulated commodity according to its value when the advance was made would therefore be closer to justice. For they mutually agreed that these dirhams would purchase a quantity of that commodity, not something else, but they did not agree upon the quantity. Referring them to a fair valuation is therefore what legal analogy requires, since receipt of the price precedes receipt of the goods.

Likewise, if someone purchases goods without fixing their price, and we hold that the sale is invalid, then, if neither the goods themselves nor their equivalent can be returned, their value must be repaid according to the price prevailing when possession was taken. Just as we require here the value of the goods received, there we require the value of the dirhams received.

An exact parallel is where the goods sold are measured by volume or weight, their price has not been fixed, and payment is deferred for a year. When payment falls due, if he returns wheat, for example, it is not equivalent to the wheat originally received, because its value differs. Paying the value of the goods as assessed when possession was taken, with payment deferred until the time for receiving the price, is therefore more in keeping with justice. This applies equally to the price and the goods sold.

The underlying principle is that whatever comes closer to what the two parties contracted for and mutually agreed upon has a stronger claim to being due than what they neither contracted for nor mutually agreed upon, and that

---

## Page 14670

property for which compensation is due because of usurpation or destruction, if it has no equivalent, is assessed by its value, not by contracts. Assessing the compensation according to that same contract is therefore more appropriate than assessing it according to liability arising under another contract. However, this question—“the question of immediate and deferred payment”—rests on another principle: that differences in prices affect equivalence. This is discussed in its proper place. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man concluded a contract with another man in the port of Alexandria for produce which the latter said was stored in a district of Beirut. The buyer paid him the price, and he sent his agent to deliver the produce to the buyer. The agent did not find the produce available; rather, he found it held under restraint. All this occurred after the buyer had formally acknowledged before witnesses that he had received the produce in question. May this seller delay returning the price he received? Does he have any plausible justification for doing so, and is the seller to be granted a delay in returning the price to the person who paid it, even after repayment has been demanded, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the buyer does not find the goods sold to him at a distant location, or finds them but cannot take possession of them, he may rescind the sale if the property has been usurped. If it has perished, the sale is dissolved, and the seller must return the price when the buyer requests it. The buyer’s witnessed acknowledgment of receipt does not benefit the seller if the buyer had made that acknowledgment

---

## Page 14671

before taking possession. Even if evidence establishes that he made the acknowledgment and that acknowledgment was legally valid, he is entitled, according to the more correct of the scholars’ two views, to require the seller to swear that the acknowledgment reflects the actual facts. If, however, the acknowledgment is known to be false because he acknowledged receipt before he could take possession, that acknowledgment is wholly invalid—all this being on the assumption that the sale of absent goods is valid, as when they are sold by description, according to Mālik’s school and Ahmad’s well-known position.

As for those who regard such a sale as invalid in all cases, such as al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad according to one report, the sale is invalid from the outset. Abū Hanīfa considers it valid in all cases, as does Ahmad according to another report; however, the buyer always has the option to rescind upon seeing the goods. In any event, the imams agree that, under these circumstances, the seller must return the price when the buyer requests it. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who concluded a sale, then denied it and had the buyer formally acknowledge its rescission?

He replied:

If the seller denied the sale and the buyer accordingly rescinded it, the seller may not subsequently compel the buyer to take possession. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14672

He was also asked:

A man purchased a property for a specified price and paid it in the presence of the witnesses to the deed of sale. The deed was authenticated and upheld by the four Muslim judges. Subsequently, someone other than the seller established a rightful claim to the purchased property under Islamic law and dispossessed the buyer of what he had purchased. At that time, the seller was absent, beyond the distance permitting the shortening of prayers, but had property and funds locally. In these circumstances, if the aforementioned buyer asks the judge in his town to award him judgment against the absent seller for an amount equal to the sale price that the seller received from him, should the judge grant his request?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes. If the property sold proves to belong rightfully to someone else, the buyer is entitled to recover the price, or its equivalent, from the person who received it from him. If that person is absent, judgment is to be entered against him once the evidence has been established, and the successful claimant is to receive his due from the absent person’s assets, while the absent person retains his right to present his case. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14673

Chapter on Usury

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

About the prohibition of usury and the transactions people engage in today as a means of practising it; about an insolvent debtor who, when his debt falls due, rolls it over into another transaction for an increased amount; about the duties of those in authority in this regard; and whether the owner of the money should receive only his principal, without the increase arising from the usurious transaction.

He replied:

Engaging in usury is forbidden by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, cursed the recipient of usury, its payer, its scribe, and its two witnesses. He also cursed the man who marries a woman to make her lawful again for her former husband, and the man for whom this is done.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is an authentic hadith.” Thus, both are accursed.

Even if the original form of usury in the pre-Islamic era was as follows: a man was owed money by another man, payable at an appointed time. When payment fell due, he would ask him, “Will you repay, or will you pay an increase?” If the debtor paid him in full, that settled it; otherwise, the creditor extended the term and the debtor increased the amount owed, so that the debt kept multiplying.

---

## Page 14674

while the principal remained the same. This usury is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims. When the same outcome is intended but another transaction is used to achieve it, later Muslim scholars have differed over this. The Companions, however, did not disagree that it was forbidden, for actions are judged only by intentions. Numerous well-known reports from them establish this. Allah Most High prohibited usury because it harms those in need and consumes wealth unjustly; these harms are also present in usurious transactions.

When a debt falls due and the debtor is insolvent, the consensus of the Muslims is that it is impermissible to roll the debt over, whether through another transaction or otherwise. Rather, he must be granted respite. If he is solvent, he must repay. Thus, there is no need to roll the debt over, whether he is solvent or insolvent.

After imposing discretionary punishment on those who engage in usurious transactions, those in authority must order the debtor to repay the principal and cancel the usurious increase. If he is insolvent but has income-producing assets from which repayment can be made, his debt should be repaid from their proceeds to the extent possible. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14675

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section: Those Who Enter into Forbidden Contracts and Then Repent

Allah Most High says concerning usury:

“But if you repent, you may retain your principal—neither wronging nor being wronged.”

This has been discussed at length in its proper place.

When Allah Most High mentioned khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation) and divorce, He said concerning khulʿ:

“It is not lawful for you to take back anything you have given them, unless both fear that they cannot observe the limits set by Allah. If you fear that they cannot observe the limits set by Allah, there is no blame on either of them concerning what she gives to secure her release. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not overstep them. Whoever oversteps the limits set by Allah—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

The passage continues to His words:

“When you divorce women and they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honorably or release them honorably. Do not retain them to harm them and thereby transgress. Whoever does so has wronged himself.”

And Allah Most High says:

“When you divorce women, divorce them with due regard for their waiting period, and keep count of the waiting period. Fear Allah, your Lord. Do not expel them from their homes, nor should they leave, unless they commit a clear indecency. These are the limits set by Allah. And whoever oversteps

---

## Page 14676

the limits set by Allah has wronged himself. You never know: perhaps Allah will bring about something new thereafter.”

“When they approach the end of their waiting period, either retain them honorably or part with them honorably. Call two just men from among you as witnesses, and bear witness faithfully for Allah. This is an admonition for whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day. Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him.”

“And He will provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him. Allah will surely accomplish His purpose. Allah has set a measure for all things.”

Forms of forbidden divorce include divorcing a wife during menstruation or during a period of purity in which he has had intercourse with her—both prohibited by explicit scriptural evidence and scholarly consensus—and triple divorce, according to the majority of scholars. Such divorce transgresses the limits set by Allah, and whoever does this wrongs himself, as Allah Most High has stated:

“Whoever oversteps the limits set by Allah has wronged himself.”

When someone who has wronged himself repents, Allah accepts his repentance, for He says:

“Whoever does evil or wrongs himself, then seeks Allah’s forgiveness, will find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

Thus, if he seeks Allah’s forgiveness, Allah forgives him and has mercy on him. He then becomes one of those who fear Allah and is included in His words:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

Those whom Umar and those who agreed with him held to their forbidden divorces knew that this was prohibited. They had been forbidden to do it, yet did not desist. They were therefore not among those who feared Allah; rather, they were wrongdoers who had transgressed His limits and deserved punishment.

For this reason, Ibn Abbās said to one of those seeking a ruling:

“Your uncle did not fear Allah, so He did not grant him relief or a way out. Had he feared Allah

---

## Page 14677

He would have granted him relief and a way out.” This can only be said to someone who knew that the act was forbidden and nevertheless committed it. Someone unaware of its prohibition, however, deserves no punishment and is not considered a transgressor, for once he learns that it is forbidden, he repents, turns away from repeating it, and commits himself not to do it.

Those whose triple divorces the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, counted as one during his lifetime used to repent. Likewise, those who divorced during menstruation, as Ibn Umar did, used to repent and thus become among those who fear Allah. Whoever does not repent wrongs himself, as Allah says:

“How evil is the name of disobedience after faith! And whoever does not repent—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

He thus confines wrongdoing to those who do not repent. Whoever repents is not a wrongdoer and therefore is not to be regarded as transgressing Allah’s limits. Rather, his pronouncement is as though it had never been made. As for someone who does not repent, his case is open to scholarly judgment. Umar punished them by holding them to their divorces, and there was no resort to marriages contrived to make their former wives lawful to them again. Believing that their wives would become forbidden to them, they refrained from forbidden divorce and were thereby deterred from transgressing Allah’s limits.

But if they begin pronouncing forbidden divorces and then taking their wives back through forbidden marriages contrived to make them lawful again, they commit what is forbidden twice and transgress Allah’s limits twice—indeed, three times, even four. For the first husband’s divorce was a transgression of Allah’s limits, as were the marriage of the man who makes her lawful again and his intercourse with her. Through this, the curse has fallen upon him

---

## Page 14678

and upon the first husband. Both have thus transgressed Allah’s limits: one once more, and the other once. The woman and her guardian, having knowingly participated in this, have also transgressed Allah’s limits. Holding them to their divorces in these circumstances has therefore not restrained them from transgressing Allah’s limits; rather, it has increased such transgression. Not holding them to their divorces—even though they are wrongdoers who have not repented—is thus better than holding them to them. For that fornication leads to transgressing Allah’s limits time after time.

It may be objected: Had the man who sought a ruling from Ibn Abbās or someone like him been told, “Repent,” he would have repented. This is why Ibn Abbās sometimes ruled that such divorces were not binding, as Ikrima and others reported from him. Umar counted the expressions “You are free” and “You are released” as only a single revocable divorce. And when he said … Umar said (1):

“If they had done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them and given them greater steadfastness.”

When holding people to their divorces is a general, publicly established policy, singling some out for assistance would undermine it, and the individual’s repentance could not be trusted.

There are thus four cases. If they fear Allah and repent, there is no doubt that not holding them to their divorces—as was the practice during the time of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and Abū Bakr—is better. If, however, they desist only when held to their divorces, so that they then desist and do not pronounce forbidden divorces

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 224):
> There appears to be some textual disorder here owing to an omission. The blank may have contained the account of Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—and al-Muttalib ibn Hantab, as narrated by Abd al-Razzāq (6/356), al-Bayhaqī (7/343), and others:
> “Al-Muttalib ibn Hantab came to Umar and said, ‘I told my wife: You are divorced irrevocably.’ Umar asked, ‘What prompted you to do that?’ He replied, ‘The divine decree.’ Umar then recited, ‘O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them with due regard to their waiting period,’ and this verse: ‘Had they done what they were admonished to do, it would have been better for them.’ He then said, ‘A single divorce severs the marriage bond. Take your wife back; it counts as one divorce.’”

---

## Page 14679

and have no need of a marriage arranged to make their former wives lawful to them again. This is the second case, in which Umar applied this measure to them.

The third case is when they would need to resort to such an unlawful marriage. Here, not holding them to their divorces is better.

The fourth case is when they do not desist, but pronounce forbidden divorces which are enforced without resorting to such an intervening marriage. Here, holding them to their divorces serves no purpose: it merely imposes burdens and shackles that have not led them to fear Allah and observe His limits. All that has happened is that their wives have become unlawful to them and their homes have been ruined. The Lawgiver has not prescribed anything that entails making wives unlawful and ruining homes. Rather, not holding them to these divorces causes less harm. Even though they have sinned, they remain sinful in either case; but ruining homes causes greater harm, and Allah does not love corruption. As for not holding a man to his divorce, all this entails is that he has sinned through his utterance and has not repented of it. This causes less harm than the harm the Lawgiver intended to ward off and prevent by every means.

He was asked about exchanging wheat for wheat.

He replied:

If wheat is exchanged for wheat, measure for measure and like for like, it is permissible. If there is an excess on either side, it is not permissible.

---

## Page 14680

He was asked:

A woman sold gold bracelets for a specified price, payable at a specified future date. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

If they were sold for gold or silver with deferred payment, this is impermissible by agreement of the imams. The bracelets must be returned if they are still available; otherwise, their equivalent must be returned. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is it permissible to sell a hiyāsa on deferred payment at an increased price?

He replied:

A hiyāsa containing gold or silver may not be sold for silver or gold with deferred payment. It may, however, be sold in exchange for goods to be delivered at a specified future date. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14681

He was asked:

Regarding the hadith:

“He granted a concession allowing ‘arāyā sales on the basis of their estimated yield.”

What is meant by their estimated yield? And what of the hadith:

“He prohibited the sale of musarrāh and muhaffalah animals”?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. The terms “musarrāh and muhaffalah” refer to an animal—whether a camel, sheep, goat or another kind—that is left unmilked for several days so that milk accumulates in its udder. It is then sold, leading the buyer to think that it produces that much milk every day. This constitutes deception and fraud, which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited both generally and specifically. He granted the buyer a three-day option after milking it: if satisfied, he may keep it; if dissatisfied, he may return it, together with compensation for the milk present at the time of the sale. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fixed this compensation at one sā‘ of dates.

As for sales involving uncertainty, where the seller cannot deliver the item—such as selling his runaway slave, his camel or horse that has strayed, or his bird that has escaped its cage or tether, and similar cases—selling such things falls under “risk-taking and gambling.” If the buyer manages to obtain the item, he has won the gamble against the seller, having acquired his property for less than its value; if he cannot obtain it, the seller has won the gamble against the buyer

---

## Page 14682

and in either case one consumes the other’s wealth unjustly. Worse still is selling him what is in an animal’s womb. Likewise, selling him fruit before it begins to ripen is a form of uncertainty in a sale. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited such sales both generally and specifically. All of this constitutes gambling, which Allah has forbidden in the Quran.

The same applies to a pebble sale, such as saying:

“I have sold you a portion of this land extending as far as this pebble reaches,” or, “I have sold you whichever of these garments, sheep, slave boys, or other items this pebble lands upon.”

Thus, the quantity, identity, or description of the item sold is unknown.

As for “‘arāyā” sales, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made them an exception to his prohibition of muzābanah. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited both muzābanah and muhāqalah.

“Muzābanah” means buying fresh dates still on the trees in exchange for their estimated equivalent in dried dates. “Muhāqalah” means buying wheat still in its ears in exchange for its estimated equivalent in wheat. Khar s means estimation and appraisal.

Thus, someone asks, “How much is on this date palm?” The answer is, “Five wasqs,” whereupon he says, “I have bought it for five wasqs.” Or someone asks, “How much wheat is in this field?” The answer is, “Five wasqs,” whereupon he says, “I have bought it for five wasqs.”

This ruling applies generally to everything that may only be sold for an equivalent quantity, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sell gold for gold except in equal quantities, and do not sell

---

## Page 14683

silver for silver except in equal quantities; do not sell wheat for wheat except in equal quantities; do not sell barley for barley except in equal quantities; do not sell dates for dates except in equal quantities; and do not sell salt for salt except in equal quantities.”

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also prohibited selling a heap of foodstuffs of unknown volume in exchange for a specified measure of foodstuffs. Thus, these commodities may not be exchanged for others of the same kind without measuring their quantities, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited exchanging them except in equal quantities. If their equality is not established, the sale is impermissible. Hence the jurists say:

“Not knowing whether the quantities are equal is like knowing that they are unequal.”

Equality is established by measuring volume or weight. Estimation, by contrast, is a matter of conjecture and approximation, used when there is need or necessity, but not when measurement by volume or weight is possible. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore prohibited muhāqalah and muzābanah because people were estimating quantities without any need to do so. He permitted this in ‘arāyā sales because of need: the buyer needs fresh dates to eat and exchanges dried dates for them on the basis of an estimate to meet that need. He allowed this concession for the small quantity needed, below the prescribed threshold—that is, less than five wasqs. It is likewise permissible when the seller needs to sell, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The linguistic meaning of “‘arāyā” is date palms that a man lends to someone else: he gives them to him so that he may eat their fruit, then return them to him, as the poet said in praise of a man’s generosity:

---

## Page 14684

“They are neither drought-stricken palms nor palms propped up,
But palms whose fruit is given freely in years of blight.”

This resembles what is called a manīhah—a loan of livestock—as when someone gives another a she-camel or sheep so that he may drink its milk and then return it. It is a form of lending for use, as when someone lends another his house to live in and then have it returned to him.

Another example is lending a mount: someone gives another his riding animal so that he may ride on its back and then return it. This, then, is the original meaning of the term. But does the ruling on ‘arāyā sales apply only to fruit that has been given to the buyer, or does it apply both to that and to other cases? Scholars hold two opinions. The first is Mālik’s view, and the second is al-Shāfi‘ī’s. Both opinions are found within Ahmad’s school. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man purchased wheat for a specified price payable at a specified time, but the owner of the wheat received nothing. He then recast the arrangement through a contract and took a property as security for it, treating the transaction as a sale and purchase under that contract. Is the sale permissible?

He replied:

If someone purchases wheat for a price payable at a later date, and the seller then takes, in place of that price, a commodity to be delivered at a later date, this is impermissible, for it is an exchange of one debt for another. Likewise,

---

## Page 14685

if he resorts to a stratagem whereby the amount owed is increased in return for an extension of the term, in a form whose usurious nature is evident, this is impermissible, and he is entitled only to the original debt. This is the very usury concerning which Allah revealed the Quran: when the debt fell due, a man would say to his debtor, “Will you pay, or increase the debt?” If the debtor paid, that settled it; otherwise, the creditor increased the debt and extended the term. Allah and His Messenger prohibited this, and commanded that those who would not desist be fought. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man needed to borrow some dirhams but could find no lender except someone who charges interest. The lender goes to the market, buys goods for him for fifty dirhams, and sells them to him at a specified profit, payable after a specified period. Is this a bridge to usury?

He replied:

If he purchases goods for him and sells them to him, then buys them back from him, or if their original owner—from whom the lender purchased them—sells them to a third party, this is usury.

There are numerous reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Companions, and the Successors prohibiting this.

One example is the report concerning ‘Ā’ishah and the bondwoman who had borne Zayd ibn Arqam a child. The latter said to her, “Mother of the Believers, I bought a slave boy from Zayd ibn Arqam for eight hundred dirhams on credit, payable when the stipend was distributed. Then I bought him from him for six hundred in cash.”

‘Ā’ishah said:

“How wretched

---

## Page 14686

was what you sold, and how wretched was what you bought! Tell Zayd that he has nullified his striving alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unless he repents.”

She said, “Mother of the Believers, what if I recover only my principal?”

‘Ā’ishah replied:

“Whoever receives an admonition from his Lord and desists may keep what is past, and his case rests with Allah. But whoever returns—those are the inhabitants of the Fire; they will abide therein forever.”

It is also reported that Anas ibn Mālik was asked about a similar transaction. He said, “This is what Allah has forbidden.”

As for a case in which the goods do not return to the seller at all, but the buyer instead sells them elsewhere to his neighbor, this is called “tawarruq.” There is disagreement over whether it is disliked. ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz disliked it, as did Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal—may Allah be pleased with him—according to one of the two reports from him. ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said, “Tawarruq is the tethering point of usury”—that is, the root of usury. This view is stronger.

He was asked:

A man asked someone for one thousand dirhams for a year in return for twelve hundred dirhams. He then sold him a horse or some cloth for one thousand dirhams and bought it from him for twelve hundred dirhams, payable at a specified time. Is that permissible?

He replied:

That is not lawful for him. Rather, it is usury according to the unanimous agreement of the Companions and the majority of

---

## Page 14687

scholars, as indicated by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him, was asked about a man who sold a piece of silk and then bought it back in a transaction involving an extra dirham. He replied, “Dirhams for dirhams, with a piece of silk interposed between them.” Anas ibn Mālik was asked about this and said, “This is among the things Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.” Concerning a similar transaction, ‘Ā’ishah said to the slave woman who had borne Zayd ibn Arqam a child, “How wretched was what you sold, and how wretched was what you bought! Tell Zayd that he has nullified his striving alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unless he repents.”

Whenever a party’s purpose in a transaction is to exchange dirhams for dirhams with deferred payment—for deeds are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended—it makes no difference whether the seller is the one granting the deferment or the one receiving it, followed by the recovery of the commodity.

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever conducts two sales in one transaction is entitled only to the lower of the two prices; otherwise, it is usury.”

They also report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you engage in buyback sales, follow the tails of cattle, and abandon jihad in the cause of Allah, Allah will inflict humiliation upon you which He will not remove until you return to your religion.”

All of this concerns buyback sales, which constitute two sales in one transaction. He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, to impose two conditions in a sale, to profit from something for which one has borne no liability, or to sell what one does not possess.”

Al-Tirmidhī said, “An authentic hadith.” Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade a man to sell

---

## Page 14688

something while also lending him money; for he would give him preferential treatment in the sale on account of the loan, thereby benefiting him, and this is usury. These and other hadiths make clear that any arrangement between two men intended to exchange dirhams for a greater number of dirhams payable later constitutes usury, whether it involves selling and then buying back, selling alongside lending, or something similar. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man incurred a debt, went with the other man to the market, purchased something in his presence, and then sold it to him at a profit. Is this permissible or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

This may take three forms:

The first is that the parties have an agreement, whether expressed verbally or understood by custom, that the commodity is to be purchased from the shopkeeper. This is not permissible.

The second is that he purchases it from him on condition that he returns it to him. This, too, is not permissible. The slave woman who had borne Zayd ibn Arqam a child came to ‘Ā’ishah and said, “Mother of the Believers, I purchased a slave boy from Zayd ibn Arqam for eight hundred dirhams, with payment deferred until the stipend was paid. Then I purchased him from him for six hundred in cash.” ‘Ā’ishah said to her, “How wretched was what you sold, and how wretched

---

## Page 14689

was what you purchased! Tell Zayd that he has nullified his jihad alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unless he repents.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever combines two sales in one transaction must accept the lower of the two prices, or else it is usury.”

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about this and said, “Dirhams for dirhams, with a piece of silk inserted between them.” Anas ibn Mālik said, “This is among the things Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.”

The third form is that he purchases the commodity privately, then sells it openly to the person seeking to borrow, after which one of them sells it. This is called tawarruq, because the buyer’s aim is neither trade nor selling; rather, he needs dirhams. Thus, he receives one hundred while remaining liable for one hundred and twenty, for example. The early generations and the scholars differed over this.

Here, too, the stronger view is that it is prohibited. ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said, in substance, that tawarruq is the root of usury. Allah has forbidden receiving dirhams in exchange for a greater amount payable later because this harms the person in need and consumes his wealth unjustly. The same consideration applies to this arrangement. Deeds are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended. What Allah has permitted is selling and trading, where the buyer’s purpose is to trade in the goods.

But if his sole intention is to exchange dirhams for a greater number of dirhams, there is no good in this. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14690

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

About a man who extends credit to people at one hundred and forty for every hundred, treating it as an advance payment for silk. When payment falls due and the debtor cannot repay him, he says to him, “Enter into another transaction with me.” The owner of the silk then takes some from him and says to the debtor, “You have bought some of this silk for one hundred and ninety,” though he charges him at the rate of one hundred and forty for every hundred.

Once the debtor has taken possession of it, he says, “Give me this silk in settlement of the advance payment I made to you.” When the second year comes, he demands the aforementioned dirhams. If the debtor cannot pay all or part of them, he says, “Enter into another transaction with me.” He then calculates the outstanding balance and the principal, treating that as an advance payment for silk. What must this man do?

He replied:

This is precisely the usury concerning which the Quran was revealed. A man would be owed a debt by another. When it fell due, he would approach him and say, “Either repay or increase the debt.” If he repaid, well and good; otherwise, the debtor would increase the amount owed, and the creditor would extend the repayment period, until the sum multiplied.

Allah Most High therefore revealed:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are believers.”

“But if you do not, then be warned of war from Allah and His Messenger. But if you repent, you shall have

---

## Page 14691

your principal, neither wronging nor being wronged.”

“If the debtor is in hardship, grant him respite until a time of ease.”

The transaction conducted by a usurer of this kind has the same purpose as the transactions of those polytheistic usurers. However, this man gives it the outward form of a commercial transaction. This does him no good, according to the unanimous agreement of the Companions of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This usurer sells the debtor that silk on deferred payment so that the debtor may return it to him in settlement of his debt. This is equivalent to selling it to him on deferred payment in order to buy it back for less.

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about something like this and said, “This is forbidden; Allah and His Messenger have forbidden it.”

Zayd ibn Arqam’s concubine, who had borne him a child, asked ‘Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers, about something like this. She said, “I sold Zayd a slave boy for eight hundred dirhams, payable when his stipend was disbursed, then bought him back for six hundred.”

‘Ā’ishah told her, “What an evil purchase you made, and what an evil sale! Tell Zayd that he has nullified his jihad alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unless he repents.”

She said, “Mother of the Believers, what if I recover only my principal?”

‘Ā’ishah replied:

“Whoever receives an admonition from his Lord and desists may retain what he has already received.”

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever concludes two sales in a single transaction is entitled to the lesser of the two prices; otherwise, it is usury.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“Combining a loan with a sale is not lawful.”

Thus, he forbade a person to sell and lend in order to obtain favorable terms in the sale on account of the loan.

It is also established in the Sahīh collection that he said:

“Actions are judged only by intentions.”

Thus, if these two parties to the transaction intend to obtain

---

## Page 14692

dirhams in exchange for dirhams on deferred payment, then whatever means they use to achieve this, it is forbidden, because the intended objective is forbidden and may not lawfully be pursued. Indeed, the early generations prohibited many such transactions as a preventive measure, lest they lead to this objective.

This usurer is entitled to claim from people only what he gave them or its equivalent. He is not entitled to any of the additional amounts. However, he is excused for whatever he previously collected on the basis of an interpretation. Any usurious amounts still owed to him are cancelled, for Allah says:

“Give up what remains of usury.”

And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man wished to obtain credit from another man, who said, “I will give you each hundred at a profit of such-and-such.” They then conducted a sale involving some merchandise. When the debt fell due, the creditor demanded repayment, but the debtor was unable to pay. The debtor said, “Roll over my debt at a profit of such-and-such per hundred.” They then conducted a sale involving real estate. At the end of every year, they repeated this arrangement, their aim in all these sales being to act lawfully, until the amount reached ten thousand dirhams. Is the creditor entitled to demand from the man the additional amounts accrued over this long period? Is the ruler entitled to denounce this, or not?

He replied:

For a person to say to another, “I will extend you credit for each hundred at a profit of such-and-such”

---

## Page 14693

is forbidden. Likewise, if the debt falls due while the debtor is in hardship, he must be granted more time. By the agreement of the Muslims, he may not be compelled to roll over the debt at an increase. In any event, this transaction and similar transactions intended to exchange dirhams for a greater amount payable later are invalid, usurious transactions. Any property received through such a transaction must be returned if it still exists; if it no longer exists, its equivalent must be returned. The person who supplied it is entitled to no more than that. The ruler must prohibit these usurious transactions, punish those who engage in them, and restrict those involved to recovering their principal, without any additional amounts. This is among the forms of usury that Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.

Allah Most High says:

“Fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are believers.”

“If you do not, then be warned of war from Allah and His Messenger. But if you repent, you may have your principal, neither wronging nor being wronged.”

“If the debtor is in hardship, then grant him time until it is easy for him to repay. But if you remit the debt as charity, that is better for you, if only you knew.”

He was asked:

A man had business dealings with another man, who fell into arrears on a sum of dirhams owed to him. He demanded repayment, although the debtor was in hardship. He then bought merchandise for him from a shopkeeper and sold it to him at a markup of one hundred dirhams, in return for granting him more time. Is this transaction valid?

---

## Page 14694

He replied:

This transaction is not permissible. Rather, if the debtor is in hardship, the creditor may grant him time. A transaction in which both the debt and the repayment period are increased is usurious, even if the two parties bring in the shopkeeper as an intermediary. The creditor must demand only his principal, not the additional amount he has not yet received.

He was asked—may Allah sanctify his soul:

Is ‘īnah permissible in Islam or not? May someone follow the opinion of those jurists who consider it permissible, or must he safeguard his religion and follow the scriptural texts concerning it? If someone repents of engaging in the aforementioned ‘īnah transaction, is the profit he earned through it lawful for him, or must he relinquish that profit and return it to its owners if he can, or give it in charity?

If he returns to it, following a juristic opinion after learning that it is invalid, is that permissible for him or not? Likewise, what do you say about the “three-party transaction” and “tawarruq”?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the person seeking the money intends to obtain dirhams in exchange for a greater amount payable at a later date, and the provider intends to supply them on that basis, then this is usury, whose prohibition is beyond doubt, whatever stratagem the two may employ to achieve it; for deeds are judged only by intentions

---

## Page 14695

and each person will have only what he intended; for these two have intended the very usury whose prohibition Allah revealed in the Quran. It is the usury concerning which Allah revealed His words:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are believers.”

“If you do not, then be warned of war from Allah and His Messenger. But if you repent, you may have your principal, neither wronging nor being wronged.”

“If the debtor is in hardship, then grant him time until he can repay.”

In the pre-Islamic era, a man who was owed a debt would approach his debtor when payment fell due and say, “Either repay or increase the debt.” If he repaid, that settled it; otherwise, the debtor would increase the amount owed, and the creditor would extend the repayment period. Thus, he would have sold money for a greater sum payable later. Allah therefore commanded them, upon repenting, to demand only their principal. Those who employ stratagems seek the same end as the people of the pre-Islamic era, but they seek to deceive Allah. They have various methods:

One is to sell someone a commodity on deferred payment, then buy it back from him for a smaller sum paid immediately. Thus, the mother of Zayd ibn Arqam’s child said to ‘Ā’ishah, “I sold Zayd a slave boy for eight hundred, payable when the stipend was distributed, and bought him back for six hundred in cash.”

‘Ā’ishah said to her, “What an evil sale, and what an evil purchase! Tell Zayd that he has rendered his jihad alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, void unless he repents.”

She asked, “Mother of the Believers, what if I take only my principal?”

‘Ā’ishah then recited:

“Whoever receives an admonition from his Lord and desists may retain his past gains, and his affair rests with Allah.”

---

## Page 14696

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked, “What about a man who sells a piece of silk on deferred payment, then buys it back for less?” He replied, “Dirhams exchanged for dirhams, with a piece of silk coming between them.”

Anas ibn Mālik was asked about something similar and said, “This is among the things Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.”

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever concludes two sales in one transaction is entitled to the lower of the two prices, or else incurs usury.”

These people have indeed concluded two sales in one transaction. The same applies when two people agree upon a usurious transaction, then approach a shopkeeper and ask him for goods equivalent in value to the sum involved. The provider of the money buys the goods; the recipient then sells them on deferred payment, then returns them to the shopkeeper for less. The shopkeeper thus acts as an intermediary between them for a fee. This too is undoubtedly usury.

The same applies when they combine a loan with preferential terms in a sale, lease, or other transaction—for example, when someone lends another one hundred and sells him a commodity worth five hundred, or rents him a shop for fifty when its rental value is one hundred. This too is usury.

Al-Tirmidhī and others report from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale, to stipulate two conditions in a sale, to profit from something for which one has not assumed liability, or to sell what one does not have.”

Al-Tirmidhī said, “This is an authentic hadith.” Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited combining an advance—that is, a loan—with a sale.

The fundamental principle in this matter is that purchases fall into three categories:

---

## Page 14697

The first: someone purchases a commodity intending to use it—for eating, drinking, clothing, riding, housing, or similar purposes. This is the sale that Allah has made lawful.

The second: someone purchases it intending to trade in it, either locally or elsewhere. This is the trade that Allah has permitted.

The third: neither of these is his purpose; rather, he needs money. Unable to obtain an advance, whether through a loan or a prepaid forward sale, he purchases a commodity to sell it and receive its price. This is tawarruq. It is disliked according to the stronger of the scholars’ two opinions, which is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

As ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said:

“Tawarruq is the sister of usury.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“If you establish a cash valuation and then sell for cash, there is nothing wrong with that. But if you establish a cash valuation and then sell on deferred payment, that is dirhams exchanged for dirhams.”

His words “if you establish a valuation” mean “if the commodity is appraised.” That is, if the commodity is appraised at a cash price and you purchase it on deferred payment, your sole purpose is to exchange dirhams for dirhams. This is how tawarruq works: a person establishes the commodity’s immediate cash value, then purchases it on deferred payment for more than that.

He may say to the other party, “I want you to give me one thousand dirhams; how much profit would you want?”

The other replies, “Two hundred, or thereabouts.”

Or he says, “I have this property, worth one thousand

---

## Page 14698

dirhams.” Or the two of them bring in someone to appraise it at one thousand dirhams, after which he sells it for more than that on deferred payment. This is among the transactions prohibited according to the sound view.

Whatever wealth a person has acquired through transactions over which the Muslim community has differed—such as those asked about here and others—he is not required to relinquish if he acted on an interpretation, believing them permissible on the basis of his own legal reasoning, following another’s scholarly authority, taking certain scholars as his example, receiving a ruling from one of them, or something similar. They need not relinquish the wealth they earned and received, even if it later becomes clear to them that they were mistaken and that the scholar who gave them the ruling was mistaken. They received it on the basis of an interpretation, so they are in no worse a position than unbelievers who acquired wealth on the basis of a false interpretation.

If unbelievers trade wine or pork among themselves, believing this permissible, and both parties receive what is due to them, or engage in explicit usury that they believe permissible and both parties receive what is due to them, then embrace Islam and bring their dispute before us, we allow them to retain what is in their possession. After embracing Islam, they may benefit from it. As Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and give up what remains of usury, if you are believers.”

He commanded them to forgo what was still owed to them, but did not command them to return what they had already received. One of ‘Umar’s deputies in Iraq used to collect the jizya from non-Muslims under Muslim protection in the form of wine, then sell it to them. ‘Umar wrote to him forbidding this, saying:

“Indeed, the Messenger of

---

## Page 14699

Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Allah has cursed the Jews! Animal fat was forbidden to them, yet they sold it and consumed its proceeds.’

But leave its sale to them and take the proceeds.”

‘Umar thus forbade them to sell wine, saying, “Even if I were to sell it to unbelievers.” If they themselves sell it to people of their own religion and receive its proceeds, Muslims may take those proceeds from them. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any distribution made in pre-Islamic times shall stand as it was made; any distribution that comes under Islam shall be made according to Islam.”

Indeed, most scholars, including Mālik, Ahmad, and Abū Hanīfa, uphold what is indicated by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the practice of his rightly guided caliphs: if unbelievers at war with the Muslims seize Muslim property through warfare, then subsequently embrace Islam or conclude a treaty, that property is left in their possession. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, left in the polytheists’ possession the Muslim property they had taken while they were unbelievers. They had not believed this to be forbidden, and they had now embraced Islam, which wipes away what came before it. Through Islam, their previous unbelief and deeds were forgiven, so their acquisition of that property no longer entailed sin for them.

Since this is so, consider a Muslim who, on the basis of an interpretation, believes that the sales, contracts of hire, and other transactions he has undertaken are permissible, as some scholars rule. If money has been received through such dealings, and those who received it later discover that the correct view is that these dealings are forbidden, what they received on the basis of that interpretation does not become unlawful for them—just as it is not unlawful for unbelievers, after embracing Islam,

---

## Page 14700

to retain what they acquired while unbelievers on the basis of an interpretation. Other Muslims who believe such dealings to be forbidden may transact with them using that money, just as a Muslim may transact with a non-Muslim under Muslim protection using the latter’s proceeds from wine and other sales. However, once they learn the ruling, they must repent of these usurious dealings. They should not follow anyone who rules such dealings permissible in imitation of certain scholars. Their prohibition is established by scriptural texts and transmitted reports; the Companions did not disagree over it, and the fundamental principles of the Sharia attest to it.

The harms for which Allah prohibited usury are present in these dealings, compounded by scheming, deception, toil, and torment. Those involved are burdened with inspection, description, taking possession, and other procedures required in a genuinely intended sale. Yet the sale itself is not their aim: their sole aim is to obtain dirhams in exchange for dirhams. The route they are instructed to follow thus becomes long and circuitous, only to lead them to usury. They are therefore among those who practise usury and suffer punishment in this world before the Hereafter. Their own hearts bear witness that what they are doing is scheming, deception, and concealment of the truth.

For this reason, Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī said:

“They try to deceive Allah as they deceive children. Had they gone about it openly, I would have found it less objectionable.”

This matter is discussed at length elsewhere, and I have written a substantial book on it. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14701

He was also asked:

A man sells a commodity for a deferred price, then buys it back from the purchaser for a lower price paid immediately. Is this permissible or not?

He replied:

If someone sells a commodity on deferred payment and buys it back from the purchaser for a lower price paid immediately, this is known as an “‘īnah transaction.” It is impermissible according to most scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad, and others. This is also the position transmitted from the Companions, including ‘Ā’ishah, Ibn ‘Abbās, and Anas ibn Mālik. Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about a piece of silk sold on deferred payment and then bought back for less. He said:

“Dirhams in exchange for dirhams, with a piece of silk coming between them.”

More emphatically still, Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“If you assess its cash value and then sell it on deferred payment, that is dirhams in exchange for dirhams.”

He thus explained that if someone values the commodity in dirhams and then sells it on deferred payment, his aim is to exchange dirhams for dirhams—and actions are judged by intentions. This is called “tawarruq.” Sometimes the purchaser buys the commodity to make use of it. At other times, he buys it

---

## Page 14702

to trade in it. Both are permissible by the consensus of the Muslims. At other times, his sole aim is to obtain dirhams: he considers the commodity’s cash value, purchases it on deferred payment, then sells it in the market for cash. His aim is thus money. This is disapproved of according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views, as transmitted from ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz. It is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

As for ‘Ā’ishah, Zayd ibn Arqam’s slave woman who had borne him a child said to her:

“I bought a slave boy from Zayd ibn Arqam for eight hundred, payable when the stipend was distributed, and sold him back to him for six hundred.”

‘Ā’ishah replied:

“What an evil sale and an evil purchase! Tell Zayd that his jihad alongside the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has been nullified unless he repents.”

The woman said:

“Mother of the Believers, what if I take only my principal?”

‘Ā’ishah replied to her:

“Whoever receives an admonition from his Lord and desists may keep his past gains, and his case rests with Allah.”

The Sunan collections report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever concludes two sales in one transaction must accept the lower of the two prices or incur usury.”

This applies when the two parties have agreed beforehand that one will sell and then buy back: he is entitled only to the lower amount—that is, the lower price—or else it is usury.

The fundamental principle governing this matter is that actions are judged by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended. If he intended something Allah has permitted, there is no harm. But if he intended something Allah has forbidden and used a stratagem to attain it, then he will have what he intended. And a stipulation between people

---

## Page 14703

is whatever they regard as a stipulation, just as a sale among them is whatever they regard as a sale, a lease is whatever they regard as a lease, and a marriage is whatever they regard as a marriage. Allah mentions sale, marriage and other matters in His Book, yet no definition of these has been given in religious law, nor do they have a definition in jurisprudence. The meanings of terms are sometimes defined by religious law, as with prayer, zakat, fasting and pilgrimage; sometimes by language, as with the sun, the moon, land and sea; and sometimes by custom, as with taking possession and separation. The same applies to contracts, such as sale, lease, marriage, gifts and others. Whenever people agree upon a stipulation and enter into a contract on that basis, it constitutes a stipulation according to customary usage.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man sold another man barley on credit at sixty dirhams per sack, payable at a specified time. When payment fell due, he demanded it, but the debtor said, “I will give you nothing but barley.” Barley was then worth thirty dirhams per sack. May he accept barley or not?

He replied:

The scholars differ on this issue. The position of the Seven Jurists

---

## Page 14704

and of Mālik and Ahmad, according to the latter’s explicitly stated position, is that this is not permissible. Whoever sells a commodity subject to the rules of ribā, such as wheat, barley or the like, on deferred payment may not accept, in place of its price, wheat, barley or any other commodity that may not be exchanged for the original commodity with deferred delivery. Since the price has not been received, it is as though he had sold wheat or barley for wheat or barley in unequal quantities with deferred delivery. This is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims.

Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī held that this is permissible. This is also the position preferred by Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, one of Ahmad’s followers, because the seller is entitled only to the price owed by the buyer, and he uses that sum to make the purchase. This resembles receiving payment and then buying from someone else. If, however, he sells the wheat or barley held by the buyer and recovers what he is owed from the proceeds, that is undoubtedly permissible. If the seller has accepted the wheat or barley at less than its value, that is less objectionable. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man bought four ardebs of wheat for one hundred dirhams, payable at a specified time. When the hundred dirhams fell due, he found him selling wheat at ten dirhams per ardeb. Is it permissible for him to take ten ardebs of grain—wheat, broad beans or barley—in settlement of the hundred dirhams?

---

## Page 14705

He replied:

There is a well-known disagreement concerning this issue. If a man sells someone wheat, barley or another commodity subject to the rules of ribā on deferred payment, then accepts in place of its price wheat, barley or another commodity that may not be exchanged for the original commodity with deferred delivery, Mālik, Ahmad and others hold that this is impermissible. Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī hold that it is permissible, as do some of Ahmad’s followers.

He was asked:

What about someone who sells pure silver for debased silver, exchanging one dirham for one and a half dirhams?

He replied:

Silver may only be exchanged for silver in equal quantities. If the alloying material in the silver is not itself sought in exchange for the silver, the transaction is permissible. However, if the amount of silver on one side exceeds that on the other, it is impermissible—particularly if the silver content of the debased silver exceeds the amount of pure silver given in exchange.
