This is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims. Not knowing whether the quantities are equal is treated like knowing that they are unequal.

---

## Page 14706

He was also asked:

About exchanging silver for debased silver in unequal quantities.

He replied:

If the amount of pure silver in one is equal to the amount of pure silver in the other, and that silver is what is sought while the copper is lost, and this amount has been determined through careful estimation and judgment, then this is permissible according to one of the two scholarly opinions.

Likewise, if the unalloyed silver exceeds the silver contained in the debased silver by a small amount corresponding to the copper, this is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. But if the silver contained in the debased silver exceeds the unalloyed silver, it is not permissible. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About exchanging Frankish akādīs coins for Islamic dirhams, knowing that the difference between them is slight and does not cover the cost of minting; indeed, these dirhams contain more silver. Is a hand-to-hand exchange between them permissible or not?

---

## Page 14707

He replied:

This hand-to-hand exchange is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. Its permissibility rests on two grounds—indeed, three:

First: This silver contains copper, whereas the other silver is pure, and the silver mixed with copper is less in quantity. Thus, if one hundred dirhams of the former are exchanged for, say, seventy pure dirhams, the silver contained in those hundred dirhams is less than seventy dirhams. If the additional silver is treated as payment for the copper, this is permissible according to one of the two scholarly opinions: that of those who permit the “mudd of Ajwa dates” transaction. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. It is also the position of Mālik and, according to the well-known report, Ahmad, when the item subject to the rules of riba (usury) is incidental to something else. Examples include selling a sheep that produces milk for milk, a house gilded with gold for gold, or a sword adorned with silver for silver or gold, and similar transactions.

As for those who prohibit the “mudd of Ajwa dates” transaction—namely, exchanging a commodity subject to the rules of riba for another of the same kind when both, or either one, are accompanied by something of a different kind—some of them, among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, explain their prohibition as follows: when a transaction comprises two different items, the price is apportioned between them according to their respective values. This, however, is a weak rationale.

For when someone sells a share subject to pre-emption together with something not subject to it—such as a slave, a sword, or a garment—if apportionment is impermissible, the co-owner has recourse to the person exercising pre-emption. As for apportioning the price according to value without any need, there is no evidence supporting it.

---

## Page 14708

Most of them, however, hold that the correct rationale is that this provides a means of engaging in riba: someone might sell one thousand dirhams in a bag for two thousand dirhams, treating the additional thousand as payment for the bag—as is permitted by those followers of Abū Hanīfa who allow such a transaction. The correct view is that this is impermissible, because the intention is to exchange unequal amounts of dirhams. Whenever that is the intention, pursuing it by any means is forbidden, for actions are judged only by intentions.

The same applies when the quantity of the commodity subject to the rules of riba is unknown and is merely estimated, as in the following report:

“The necklace sold on the day of Hunayn contained beads strung on gold. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘It must not be sold until you separate its components.’”

When that necklace was taken apart, the gold accompanying the beads proved to exceed the separate gold offered in exchange. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore prohibited exchanging one for the other until its components had been separated. This was because the separate gold might be less than the gold accompanying the beads, in which case gold would have been exchanged for an equal amount of gold together with additional beads. This is impermissible. And if the taking is known.

If, however, the intention is to exchange dirhams for others of the same kind, and the pure silver exceeds the silver contained in the alloyed coins—as when pure dirhams are exchanged for debased ones—so that the additional silver pays for the alloying metal, then none of the harm inherent in riba is present. The intention is not to exchange dirhams for a greater amount of dirhams, nor is it a transaction in which there could

---

## Page 14709

be any such excess. A difference in quantity is therefore permissible.

The second rationale:

This is the rationale of those who hold that a commodity subject to the rules of riba may be exchanged for another such commodity on the basis of careful appraisal and estimation when there is a need and measurement by volume or weight is not possible. Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad hold this view concerning ‘arāyā sales based on estimation, in accordance with the Sunnah permitting fresh dates to be exchanged for dried dates on an estimated basis because of need. According to one view within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere, this is permissible for all fruits. According to a second view, it is not permissible; according to a third, it is permissible only for grapes and fresh dates, as some of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers also hold.

Similarly, Mālik and his followers permit goods normally sold by weight to be exchanged on the basis of careful appraisal when there is a need. Thus, they permit bread to be exchanged for bread on this basis, and they also permit meat to be exchanged for meat on this basis while travelling.

They argued that the need arises when people have no scales, so this is permissible just as ‘arāyā sales are permissible. They distinguished this from measurement by volume, since that remains possible even by using handfuls.

Since the Sunnah has established that careful appraisal and considered judgment may take the place of certain knowledge obtained by measuring or weighing when there is a need, it is well known that people need to exchange these debased dirhams for these pure ones, and they have ascertained the amount of silver they contain

---

## Page 14710

through the reports of coin minters, money changers and others who have melted down these dirhams and determined their silver content. No material uncertainty therefore remains; indeed, this knowledge is more evident than that obtained through estimation or similar methods. Their sole purpose is to exchange dirhams for dirhams in an amount corresponding to their share, not to obtain additional silver. If they found someone who would mint these dirhams for them in pure silver, without any choice on their part, so that the coins remained in their country, they would do so and pay him his fee. They thus benefit from the pure dirhams they receive without suffering any harm.

Likewise, the owners of the pure dirhams benefit from receiving these dirhams without suffering any harm.

This provides a “third rationale” establishing permissibility: riba was prohibited because it involves taking an excess, which is an injustice that harms the giver. It was therefore prohibited because of the harm it entails. If the exchange is more beneficial to each party than breaking up his dirhams, and each needs what he receives, it serves a benefit that both need. Preventing it would harm them both. The Lawgiver does not prohibit benefits that outweigh the harms and impose the lesser harm instead, as is established by the fundamental principles of Islamic law.

This is comparable to someone receiving a “suftaja” (a remittance arrangement) from a lender: the lender advances him dirhams to be repaid in another town. For example, the lender wishes to transport dirhams to another town, while the borrower has dirhams there but needs dirhams in the lender’s town. He therefore borrows from him in the town where the lender’s dirhams are, and writes

---

## Page 14711

him a suftaja—that is, a written document—addressed to the town where the borrower’s dirhams are held. This is permissible according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions.

It has also been said that this is prohibited because it is a loan yielding a benefit, and a loan yielding a benefit constitutes riba. The correct view, however, is that it is permissible, because the borrower sees a benefit in avoiding the hazards of the journey involved in transporting his dirhams to the town where the borrower’s dirhams are held. Thus, both parties benefit from this loan. The Lawgiver does not prohibit what benefits people, promotes their welfare and meets their needs. Rather, He prohibits what harms and corrupts them, and for which Allah has provided them with sufficient alternatives. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is it permissible for someone to purchase copper coins at fourteen qirtās to the dirham and exchange them at thirteen to the dirham?

He replied:

If he exchanges them for people at the prevailing rate, that is permissible, even if he purchased them cheaply. As for someone who has sold an item for dirhams, he is not obliged to accept copper coins in payment of any part of the price unless he chooses to do so. Likewise, someone who has purchased it for dirhams must pay the full amount in dirhams. If both parties agree to settle the price, or part of it, in copper coins at the prevailing exchange rate, that is permissible. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14712

He was asked:

About copper coins: exchanging them for one another in unequal amounts; exchanging them for silver dirhams without both parties taking immediate possession; and a person handing over a dirham and receiving copper coins for part of it and a piece of silver for the remainder.

He replied:

If someone hands over a dirham and says, “Give me silver for half of it and copper coins for the other half,” or similarly says, “Give me the equivalent weight of these heavy dirhams in half-dirhams or light dirhams,” this is permissible, whether the coins are debased or pure. Some jurists disapprove of this and classify it under the “mudd of ajwa dates” case, because he has exchanged silver and copper for silver and copper.

The underlying issue in the “mudd of ajwa dates” case is exchanging a commodity subject to the rules of riba for another of the same kind, with something of a different kind included on both sides or on one side. Scholars hold three views on this:

The first is prohibition without qualification. This is the position of al-Shāfi'ī and one opinion reported from Ahmad.

The second is permissibility without qualification, as held by Abū Hanīfa. This is also cited as an opinion reported from Ahmad.

---

## Page 14713

The third distinguishes between cases where the intention is to exchange a commodity subject to the rules of riba for an unequal amount of the same kind and cases where that is not the intention. This is the position of Mālik and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

Thus, if someone exchanges dates containing their stones for date stones or pitted dates, or a ewe with milk for a ewe without milk or for milk, and similar transactions, both regard this as permissible. By contrast, exchanging a thousand dirhams for five hundred dirhams wrapped in a cloth is not permissible. If the intention is to exchange a commodity subject to the rules of riba for an unequal amount of the same kind, it is not permissible; but if that element is merely incidental and not intended in itself, it is permissible. Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, sets this proportion at one-third.

Likewise, exchanging wheat containing a small amount of barley for wheat containing a small amount of barley is permissible according to the majority of scholars. The same applies to exchanging debased dirhams for others of the same kind: the base metal is not the object of the transaction; rather, the intention is to exchange silver for silver, with both being equal in amount.

Similarly, those who disapprove of exchanging copper coins for debased dirhams say that it amounts to exchanging silver and copper for copper. The correct view, held by the majority of scholars, is that all these transactions are permissible.

---

## Page 14714

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

As for exchanging silver for copper coins in circulation, must payment be immediately due and both parties take possession, as when exchanging dirhams for dinars?

There are two views, transmitted in two reports from Ahmad.

According to the first, payment must be immediately due and both parties must take possession, since this is a form of currency exchange. Copper coins in circulation resemble money, so exchanging them for a monetary commodity constitutes currency exchange.

According to the second, neither immediate payment nor mutual taking possession is required. These requirements apply to gold and silver, whether used as money, in currency exchange, or in broken pieces, unlike copper coins. Moreover, copper coins originally belong to “the category of merchandise”; their monetary status is incidental.

This also rests on another underlying question: is it permissible to exchange copper for copper in unequal amounts? There are two well-known views concerning this and all other commodities sold by weight, such as iron for iron, lead for lead, cotton for cotton, flax for flax

---

## Page 14715

and silk for silk:

The first view is that exchanging a commodity for another of the same kind in unequal amounts is impermissible. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and his companions, and of Ahmad according to the better-known of the two reports from him.

The second view is that this is permissible. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and of Ahmad according to the other report, which a group of his followers adopted.

Those who hold such exchanges to be prohibited disagree about articles manufactured from these materials, such as cotton and linen garments, buckets, copper pots, and other such items. Are they subject to the rules of ribā (usury)? There are three views. The soundest distinguishes between articles whose weight remains a consideration after manufacture—such as silk garments, buckets, and the like—and those whose weight is not a consideration, such as cotton and linen garments, needles, and other such items.

Accordingly, copper coins are subject to the rules of ribā according to those who hold that these rules apply to manufactured copper articles. Those who make customary consideration of weight the criterion do not apply the rules of ribā to copper coins, since their weight is not ordinarily taken into account; rather, they circulate by number.

But among those who regard them as money, do the rules of ribā apply to them on that account? They hold two views on this. Likewise, there are two views on whether zakat is due on them, whether they may be used to pay zakat, and other related matters. Both views are found in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

---

## Page 14716

He was also asked:

A man said to someone, “Give me half-dirham coins in exchange for these dirhams.” The other replied, “That is not permissible.” Is this permissible?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. The scholars disagree on this matter, but most permit it, including Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad according to the apparent position of his school. The issue discussed by the jurists as the case of “a mudd of ʿajwa dates” falls into three categories. What they have in common is the exchange of a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for another of the same kind, with something of a different kind accompanying either or both.

The first category:

The intention is to exchange a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for an unequal amount of the same kind, adding something of a different kind to the smaller amount as a legal stratagem. Examples include exchanging two thousand dinars for one thousand dinars wrapped in a cloth, or a measure (qafīz) of wheat for an equal measure plus a sack, and similar transactions. The correct view is that such transactions are prohibited, as held by Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. Otherwise, anyone engaging in ribā through unequal exchange could simply add something of this sort to the smaller amount.

The second category:

The intention is to sell an item not subject to the rules of ribā together with an item that is, the latter being included only incidentally and as an accessory. An example is selling a ewe bearing wool and producing milk for another ewe with

---

## Page 14717

wool and milk, or selling a sword containing a small amount of silver for another sword or something else, or a house gilded with gold for another house, and similar transactions. Here, the sound view in the schools of Mālik and Ahmad is that this is permissible. The same applies when the intended transaction is an exchange between an item subject to the rules of ribā and one that is not, such as selling the house, the sword, or similar items for gold; or when an item is exchanged for its own kind in equal amounts. The case of the debased dirhams used in our time belongs to this category: the silver content of one dirham is equal to that of the other, while the copper is merely incidental and not intended in its own right. Thus, the sound view is that this is permissible.

This differs from the third category, in which both components are intended in their own right. Examples include exchanging a mudd of ʿajwa dates and a dirham for a mudd of ʿajwa dates and a dirham, or for two mudds of dates, or for two dirhams; exchanging a dinar for half a dinar and ten dirhams; or exchanging ten dirhams and a ratl of copper for ten dirhams and a ratl of copper. Such transactions are the subject of a well-known disagreement. Abū Hanīfa permits them, while al-Shāfiʿī prohibits them. Two positions are reported from Ahmad, while Mālik distinguishes between one-third and other proportions.

---

## Page 14718

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning gold combined with silver: if the quantities of silver and gold it contains are known, may it be sold for either metal, provided that the quantity of the metal given alone exceeds the quantity of that same metal in the composite?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. This falls into three categories:

The first is when the intention is to exchange silver for silver in unequal amounts, or gold for gold in unequal amounts, adding something of a different kind to the smaller amount as a legal stratagem. This is not permissible at all.

The second is when the intention is to sell either metal and to sell merchandise for either metal, while the merchandise contains something not sought in its own right. Examples include selling weapons for either metal when they bear a small amount of ornamentation, or selling a property for either metal when its ceiling and walls bear similar ornamentation. This is like selling sheep bearing wool for wool, or sheep producing milk for milk. Most scholars permit this, and this is the correct view.

Selling silver coated with gold that disappears when melted down for an equal amount of silver also falls into this category. Thus, if worked, gilded silver is sold for gold, or if it is sold

---

## Page 14719

for gold received in hand, this is permissible. But if worked silver is sold for a greater quantity of silver on account of the workmanship, this is not permissible.

The third category is where both elements are sought in their own right, as when weapons bear a substantial amount of gold or silver. If the quantity of that metal is known and the item is sold for a greater quantity of the same metal, there is a well-known disagreement concerning this. The more evident view is that it is permissible. If worked, gilded silver is sold for gold, or for debased gold, this is permissible. But if worked silver is sold for a greater quantity of silver, this is not permissible. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for exchanging nuqra silver dirhams, whose silver content is approximately two-thirds, for black dirhams, whose silver content is approximately one-quarter, or somewhat less or more, this relates to the question known as “a mudd of ajwa dates.” In essence, this concerns selling a commodity subject to the rules of ribā, together with something else, in exchange for a commodity of the same kind as the former. People’s positions on this range between the two extremes of prohibition and permission, with others adopting an intermediate position.

---

## Page 14720

Thus, if the intention is to exchange a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for an unequal quantity of the same kind, and something else is included as a legal stratagem—as when someone sells two thousand dirhams for one thousand dirhams wrapped in a cloth, or two qafīz measures of wheat for one qafīz in a basket—then this is undoubtedly prohibited. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad.

If, however, the intention is to conduct a permissible sale, and the exchange of one commodity subject to ribā for another is merely incidental—as when sheep are exchanged for sheep, both having milk and wool; sheep with milk are sold for milk; or a gilded house or silver jewellery is sold for gold, each bearing only a small amount of gold plating, and similar cases—then the correct view is that this is permissible. This is the well-known position in the school of Ahmad and others, just as fruit may be included incidentally in a sale before it begins to ripen. There is also the hadith that Muslim reported as a saying of the Prophet, transmitted by Sālim from his father, and likewise by Nāfi' from Ibn 'Umar as a saying of the Prophet:

“Whoever sells a slave of his who has property, that property belongs to the seller unless the purchaser stipulates otherwise.”

But if both components are sought in their own right, this is the subject of the well-known disagreement. Some scholars prohibit it, either because it is a means leading to ribā, or because, when a transaction includes two different items, the price is apportioned between them according to their respective values. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him.

---

## Page 14721

Others permit it, as is the position of Abū Hanīfa and others. The other report from Ahmad permits it if the quantity offered alone exceeds the corresponding quantity accompanied by something else.

With this understood, exchanging debased nuqra silver dirhams for debased nuqra silver dirhams is permissible according to the correct view, just as one milk-producing sheep may be exchanged for another, provided the two are alike in quality or copper content.

As for exchanging nuqra dirhams for black dirhams, if the intention is not to exchange unequal quantities of silver, the copper in the black dirhams is sought in its own right, and these dirhams stand between nuqra dirhams and copper coins. This therefore falls under the well-known disagreement concerning the question of “a mudd of ‘ajwa dates,” since silver and copper are being exchanged for silver and copper, both metals being sought in their own right. The more likely correct view is that this is permissible, as are the other transactions in this category, provided they do not involve prohibited ribā.

The basic principle is to regard contracts as valid, and there is a need for such transactions. As for the hadith concerning beads strung with gold, it was not established that the gold offered separately exceeded the gold accompanying the beads. Valuation of the two different items exchanged was adopted out of necessity.

This applies if the copper can be put to use once separated from the silver. If it cannot be put to use, the transaction is like exchanging silver for silver: equality in quantity is required, and anything whose silver content people cannot ascertain is disregarded. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14722

He was asked:

“What of a group of people who sell for dirhams, settle part of the amount in copper coins as a concession, and then report the price as the stipulated price?”

He replied:

They may not pay in copper coins without the seller’s consent. When they do pay in copper coins, they may do so only at the prevailing exchange rate, in accordance with the Prophet’s instruction, peace and blessings be upon him, to Abd Allāh ibn Umar. He had said to him, “We sell for gold and collect payment in silver, and sell for silver and collect payment in gold.” The Prophet replied:

“There is no harm in that at the exchange rate on that day, provided you part with nothing remaining outstanding between you.”

In that case, the choice of currency for the price makes no difference in valuation, for this is profit on something for which no liability has been borne. On this understanding, all debts and their settlement in an alternative form are alike, because the two valuations operate in the same way. Receiving either currency in settlement of the other is therefore like receiving that currency in settlement of itself. This does not constitute an exchange transaction, so no stipulated increase is permissible, just as it is impermissible in a loan and similar transactions requiring equivalence.

Thus, if they agree that one of them will pay more than its value, this is like agreeing

---

## Page 14723

to settle it with a greater amount of the same kind, unlike an increase given without prior stipulation.

On this basis, copper coins in circulation may have a mixed character, with an element outweighing their monetary character. Settling a debt denominated in either of the two precious-metal currencies with these coins is therefore like settling one of those currencies with the other: both operative grounds apply, in light of Ibn Umar’s hadith. It reckons them as two currencies for purposes of the ruling, while stopping short of extending it to monetary media generally. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Copper coins are purchased for immediate payment at a specified price, then sold on deferred payment at a higher price. Is this permissible or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. There is a well-known disagreement among the scholars concerning this issue: when copper coins in circulation are exchanged for dirhams, must payment be immediate, or may it be deferred?

There are two well-known views, both held within the schools of Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad ibn Hanbal.

The first, which is Ahmad’s explicitly stated position, Mālik’s view, and one of the two reports from Abū Hanīfa, is that it is not permissible. Mālik said, “It is not, however, clearly forbidden.”

The second, which is the view of al-Shāfi'ī and of Abū Hanīfa according to the other report

---

## Page 14724

and of Ibn 'Aqīl, one of Ahmad’s followers—is that it is permissible. Some interpret Ahmad’s prohibition as indicating mere disapproval, for he said, “It resembles currency exchange.”

The stronger view, however, is that it is prohibited, because copper coins in circulation are predominantly governed by the rules applicable to money and serve as a measure of people’s wealth. The ruler should therefore mint coins for them at a fair value for their transactions, without wronging them. Those in authority must not trade in copper coins at all, whether by buying copper, minting it into coins and trading in them, or by banning the coins people already possess and minting different ones for them. Rather, whatever coins he mints should be issued at their actual value, without profit, for the public benefit, and he should pay the mint workers’ wages from the public treasury.

Trading in these coins opens a major door to oppressing people and consuming their wealth unjustly. If he prohibits transactions in them, reducing them to mere commodities, and mints other coins for people, he damages the wealth they possess by lowering its value, thereby wronging them. He also wrongs them by exchanging the coins at an inflated rate.

Moreover, when copper coins differ in size, this enables oppressors to take smaller coins, exchange them, transfer them to another town, and put the smaller ones into circulation, thus damaging people’s wealth. It is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “forbade breaking the Muslims’ coinage that circulates among them, unless it has some defect.”

If the coins are of a uniform standard and valued at the price of copper, and the ruler does not buy copper and coins no longer in circulation to mint into coins and profit from doing so, their intended purpose is achieved

---

## Page 14725

—that of serving as money. The same applies to dirhams, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade selling one dirham for two dirhams, or one dinar for two dinars,”

and “forbade exchanging dirhams for dinars except hand to hand.”

The prohibition of deferred exchange is unanimously agreed upon by the Muslim community. The prohibition of unequal exchange, even when conducted hand to hand, is established by authentic hadiths and upheld by the majority of the Muslim community. Yet the legislation of Allah and His Messenger embodies profound wisdom, perfect blessing, and universal mercy that may escape many scholars. Scholars, both early and later, have disagreed over many “questions concerning usury.”

They have disagreed over the basis for prohibiting unequal exchange of the six commodities: gold, silver, wheat, barley, dates, and salt. Is it equivalence—namely, measurement by volume and weight? Or is it monetary function and edibility? Or is it monetary function and equivalence, together with edibility, staple food, and what complements it? Or does the prohibition have no underlying legal rationale, with the ruling confined to what the text expressly mentions? These are well-known positions.

The “first” is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to the best-known reports from him. The “second” is the view of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad according to one report. The “third” is Ahmad’s view according to a third report, which Abū Muhammad preferred. Mālik’s view is close to this, and this position is stronger than the others. The “fourth” is the view of Dāwūd and his followers, and it is also reported from Qatādah. Ibn ‘Aqīl favoured this view in his Mufradāt and deemed weak

---

## Page 14726

the preceding views.

There is also an anomalous view: that the legal rationale is possessing value as property. This contradicts the scriptural texts and the consensus of the early generations. Under every view, sameness of kind is a condition for usury through unequal exchange.

Our purpose here is to discuss the legal rationale for prohibiting usury in dinars and dirhams. The more evident view, held by the majority of scholars, is that the rationale is their monetary function, not their weight. Unequal exchange is not prohibited in other goods sold by weight, such as lead, iron, silk, cotton, and flax. Evidence for this is the scholars’ agreement that gold and silver may be paid in advance for goods sold by weight. This is an exchange of one weighed commodity for another with deferred delivery; if weight were the legal rationale, this would not be permissible.

The opposing scholar says that permitting this is an exercise of juristic preference, yet it contradicts the proposed legal rationale. He also says that it was permitted because of need, although analogy would require its prohibition. On the basis of what he has said, he would thus be committed to a rationale for usury contrary to his own position.

As for making an exception to the legal rationale—what has been termed juristic preference—unless scriptural evidence establishes both that the ruling is tied to the stated rationale and that the exceptional case possesses a feature which, according to the revealed law and the hadiths, prevents the ruling from applying, the rationale is unsound.

Basing the ruling on monetary function, however, grounds it in a relevant attribute. The purpose of money is to serve as a standard of value for property, through which the value of property may be determined, and not

---

## Page 14727

to derive benefit from the money itself. Whenever one currency is sold for another with deferred payment, money becomes an object of trade, which contradicts its intended monetary function. Requiring immediate settlement and mutual receipt fulfils its purpose as a means of obtaining what people need, for that purpose is achieved only by taking possession of money, not by its being established as a debt owed. Moreover, both sides of the exchange are money, so the Lawgiver prohibited exchanging money for money with deferred payment. If copper coins come to function as money, the same rationale applies to them: money must not be exchanged for money with deferred payment.

Similarly, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “forbade exchanging one deferred obligation for another,” meaning an exchange in which both countervalues are deferred. He did not prohibit exchanging an existing debt owed by one party, which is thereby extinguished, for an existing debt owed by the other, which is likewise extinguished. This latter transaction clears both parties’ liabilities. Hence it is permissible according to the more evident of the two scholarly views, as held by Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and others.

This differs from exchanging an obligation that becomes binding upon one party and burdens his liability for another obligation that becomes binding upon the other. An example is a buyer who contracts to purchase a commodity for future delivery under an advance-payment sale but does not hand over the purchase price. This establishes an obligation upon the seller to deliver the commodity and an obligation upon the buyer to pay the purchase price, while neither has obtained any benefit. Thus, each party is burdened with a liability through contracts that are merely means to taking possession, which is the purpose of the contract.

Just as commodities are what money is intended to obtain, money must not be exchanged for money with deferred payment, just as one deferred obligation must not be exchanged for another. Such transactions involve harm and injustice contrary to the purpose of money and the purpose of contracts. This differs from property’s being weighed or measured by volume, for this is an attribute of the means

---

## Page 14728

by which its quantity is measured and ascertained. Moreover, this involves a rationale appropriate to prohibiting unequal exchange.

If it is said, “Commodities measured by volume or weight are equivalent, and the reason for the prohibition is the absence of equivalence,” the response is:

No rational person sells something for its equivalent with deferred payment. He may, however, lend something in order to receive its equivalent after a time. Lending is a gratuitous act, akin to lending an item for use, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called it:

“A gratuitous loan of silver or a gratuitous loan of gold.”

When property is handed over to someone who enjoys its benefit for a period and then returns it to its owner, the owner has freely granted its benefit for that period, although each type has its own name. In the case of a palm tree, it is called a loan for use; in the case of an animal whose milk is drunk, a loan for its milk. The recipient may then return the very same property, if that particular property is what is intended; otherwise, he returns its equivalent.

The particular dirhams themselves are not what is intended. Thus, the borrower’s returning their equivalent is like the manager in a profit-sharing partnership returning their equivalent, namely, the capital. This is why it is called a loan, and why the lender is entitled only to the equivalent of his property. He may not stipulate any increase over it, whatever the type of property, by scholarly agreement.

The lender is entitled to the equivalent of his loan in its qualities, just as he is entitled to an equivalent when his property is wrongfully seized or destroyed. No rational person would sell something for an equivalent of this kind. Something is sold for its like only when its qualities differ.

The Lawgiver sought to disregard differences in quality when exchanging money. He therefore intended dirhams to be exchanged for an equal weight of dirhams, without regard to differences in their qualities, even when each individual dirham is light in weight. This is what someone does when seeking lightweight dirhams, either to give them to oppressors or to make payments with them

---

## Page 14729

or for some other purpose. He offers fewer coins in exchange for them, but of equal weight. The usurer, however, wants to give him those coins only in return for a greater weight. This diverts money from its intended purpose, and is undoubtedly among the things the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited, unlike the matters over which scholars disagree. This is not the place to discuss those in detail. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“What about someone who gives short measure and weight?”

He replied:

Giving short measure and weight is among the deeds for which Allah destroyed the people of Shuʿayb. He has related their story to us in several places in the Quran so that we may take heed. Persisting in this is among the gravest major sins, and its perpetrator deserves severe punishment. Whatever wealth he has defrauded Muslims of over the years should be recovered from him and spent in the Muslims’ common interests if it cannot be returned to its owners.

A measurer or weigher who cheats others is liable for restitution, deprived of good, and guilty of sin. His is among the most ruinous bargains, for he has sold his Hereafter for someone else’s worldly gain. It is unlawful to appoint someone who cheats or shows favouritism to measure or weigh for people. Likewise, it is unlawful to have an appraiser who shows favouritism, measuring, weighing, or appraising for someone from whom he hopes to benefit, whose harm he fears, or who enjoys influence or the like, differently from the way he measures or weighs

---

## Page 14730

or appraises for others, or who wrongs those he dislikes and gives extra to those he likes.

Allah Most High says:

“And give full measure and weight with justice. We do not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

He also says:

“O you who believe, stand firmly for justice as witnesses for Allah, even against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives. Whether a person is rich or poor, Allah is best able to care for both. So do not follow your desires, lest you deviate from justice. If you distort your testimony or refuse to give it, Allah is indeed All-Aware of what you do.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14731

Chapter on the Sale of Real Estate and Fruit

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him and be pleased with him—was asked:

A man purchased a house that had no projecting structure. He then demolished and rebuilt it, adding a projecting structure, a stairway, and a doorway in a dead-end alley. He feared being sued and having to swear oaths by Allah Most High that he had made no alterations to this house. He therefore transferred ownership to someone else, stating that he had sold it on deferred payment terms. He arranged this sale as a collusive ruse to defeat the rightful claim. Is the person who made the alterations and then sold the house required to take the oath, or is the buyer required to take it, even though he made no alterations?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Selling the house does not extinguish the claim or the obligation to take an oath arising from it. The holder of the right may bring a claim against the buyer, who has possession of the additions, to have them removed. He may also bring a claim against the seller, who made the additions and enabled the buyer to take possession of them. Whichever of the two he sues, his claim is valid.

---

## Page 14732

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man built a house with an upper and a lower storey, directing the drainage from the upper onto the lower. He then sold the house to two people in two separate transactions, without informing the buyer of the lower storey that it was subject to a drainage right. The buyer has suffered harm as a result. What is the ruling?

He replied:

The sale applies to the property as it actually stands. However, if the buyer did not know that someone else had a right over his roof, he may rescind the sale or receive compensation for the reduction in value.

He was also asked:

A man sold a green crop before it had matured. Is this permissible?

He replied:

Selling a crop on condition that it be left standing is impermissible by agreement of the scholars. Purchasing it on condition that it be cut is permissible by agreement. Selling it without either stipulation is impermissible according to the vast majority of scholars, for “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling grain until it hardens and grapes until they turn black.”

---

## Page 14733

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning ownership of an orchard containing different kinds of trees: some fruits begin to ripen early, such as apricots; others begin to ripen late, such as pomegranates; and others begin to ripen between these times, such as grapes, figs and fresh dates. You consider a sale valid only after the fruit begins to ripen. How, then, can one ensure compliance with Islamic law when the onset of ripening varies between early, late and intermediate times? If, for example, he sells the apricots when they begin to ripen, and you do not permit the sale of the grapes because they are still unripe at that time, with regard to what was not theirs—please give us your ruling.

He replied—may Allah be pleased with him:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

This matter has two forms. The first is that a person takes on the orchard under contract, cultivating its land and watering its trees himself, like someone who leases land. The second is that he purchases only the fruit: the expense of watering and tending the orchard falls upon the seller, not the buyer. The buyer is entitled only to the fruit and bears none of these expenses.

---

## Page 14734

As for the “first form,” scholars hold three opinions concerning it. The first is that it falls under the prohibition on selling fruit before it begins to ripen.

On this view, some resort to a legal stratagem: leasing the land and entering into a crop-sharing agreement for tending the trees. This is the position of a group of followers of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, and some followers of Ahmad, including al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā in his “Book on Invalidating Legal Stratagems.” Ahmad’s explicitly recorded position, however, is that these stratagems are invalid. This is also the position of Mālik and others, and many such arrangements are invalid by scholarly consensus.

The second opinion distinguishes between cases where the proportion of land is large and those where it is small. If treeless land constitutes more than two-thirds and the trees less than one-third, the land may be leased, with the sale of its fruit included implicitly as a subsidiary part of the transaction. This is Mālik’s opinion. There are two opinions concerning the one-third limit.

The third opinion permits this without qualification, whether the proportion of land is smaller or larger. This is the position of a group of earlier and later scholars, including Ibn ʿAqīl, a follower of Imam Ahmad, and others. It is also what has been transmitted from the Companions. Harb al-Kirmānī, Abū Zurʿah al-Dimashqī and others narrated through an established chain of transmission that, after Usayd ibn Hudayr died, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—leased out his orchard for three years, collected the rent in advance and used it to discharge a debt Usayd owed. And the like of

---

## Page 14735

this incident was bound to become widely known, yet no one objected to it. Moreover, he imposed kharāj (land tax) on kharāj land and tangible property—kharāj being rent according to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and the well-known position of Ahmad. The land bore trees, so he leased the whole together. This is the soundest opinion, and on this basis kharāj was waived for Muslims in such cases. It has two grounds.

The first is that the land must be leased, and this is possible only together with the trees. It is therefore permissible because of the need, since the two cannot be separated. Likewise, when some of the fruit on the trees begins to ripen, all of it may be sold by agreement. Indeed, according to the vast majority of scholars, when the fruit of one tree begins to ripen, this establishes ripeness for that variety throughout the orchard. There is disagreement concerning other orchards.

This is because individual cases and contracts may include, as subsidiary elements, things that cannot be included independently: the foundations and inner parts of walls, the work performed by animals, and the additional growth that occurs after the fruit begins to ripen. Likewise, it is authentically reported from the Prophet in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“If someone sells date palms that have been pollinated, their fruit belongs to the seller unless the buyer stipulates otherwise.”

If the buyer stipulates that the pollinated fruit is included, this is permissible by the explicit text and scholarly consensus. Although the fruit has not yet begun to ripen, its sale is permitted as a subsidiary part of the sale of something else. There are other such examples.

Need may permit what would otherwise be impermissible, as with arāyā sales—exchanging fresh dates still on the palms for dried dates. Similarly, some scholars have permitted profit-sharing investment partnerships, orchard-sharing agreements and sharecropping as subsidiary arrangements, although their own analogical reasoning would rule them out, because they regard them as contracts of hire, as

---

## Page 14736

is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. Those who regard these arrangements as partnerships and recognize partnership as a separate contractual category permit them on the basis of explicit texts, not analogy, without considering them contrary to analogy. This is the position of the majority of the early scholars and groups of later scholars, including followers of Abū Hanīfa, such as his two leading disciples, and followers of al-Shāfiʿī, such as al-Khattābī and others. It is also the position of Ahmad and others. This approach is more thorough in its reasoning here.

The second ground is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only prohibited selling fruit before it begins to ripen, just as he prohibited selling grain before it hardens and grapes before they turn dark. Nevertheless, all four imams permit leasing land to someone who cultivates it until crops grow. This is not merely incidental to the grain. Likewise, leasing trees to someone who tends them until they bear fruit is not merely incidental to the fruit. Do you not see that sharecropping land in return for a share of the land is like tending trees in return for a share of their fruit; that lending land for use is like lending trees for use; and that the beneficiaries of an endowment benefit from the land’s crops just as they benefit from the trees’ fruit?

Thus fruit, although tangible property, is treated as yield and benefit in endowments, profit-sharing investment partnerships and orchard-sharing agreements, because it is continually replaced. Likewise, engaging a wet nurse is treated as obtaining a benefit because her milk is replenished. Hence, when fruit is sold after it begins to ripen, the seller bears the expense of bringing it to full ripeness.

As for the lease arrangement made by ʿUmar, it is the lessee who waters the trees and bears the expense of producing the fruit, so one cannot

---

## Page 14737

draw an analogy between the two. Undoubtedly, the Prophet’s prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against selling fruit before it begins to ripen did not encompass this lease arrangement. If the trees should fail to bear fruit, or the fruit should perish after appearing without negligence on the lessee’s part, this is equivalent to the loss of the benefit under a lease: rent is due only when the tenant is able to benefit from the property.

The second case:

Only the fruit is purchased, while the seller bears the cost of watering. If the orchard contains several varieties, there are likewise two opinions concerning this case. The first—the opinion of al-Layth ibn Saʿd—is that all the orchard’s fruit may be sold once one variety begins to ripen, just as an entire variety may be sold once some of it begins to ripen. This is because selling the varieties separately entails considerable harm: a purchaser for one variety may also be available for another, whereas someone willing to buy one variety on its own may not be found. This opinion is stronger than the second, which categorically prohibits this, as is the well-known position.

Need alone establishes permissibility here. This is because muzābana is graver than selling fruit before it begins to ripen: it involves exchanging, by estimate, a commodity subject to the rules of ribā for another of the same kind. Ribā is graver than contractual uncertainty. Moreover, exceptions have been made to his prohibition against selling fruit before it begins to ripen, including its sale together with the trees. It is therefore clear that the prohibition does not encompass every sale of fruit together with something else.

Indeed, it may even be said: it was only

---

## Page 14738

prohibited when sold separately, just as gold and silk are prohibited when used on their own. What is impermissible on its own may be permissible in combination with something else. Moreover, muzābana involves exchanging fresh produce for dried produce of the same kind subject to the rules of ribā. This too is prohibited by explicit textual evidence, as is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. Furthermore, sales of fruit have been permitted to include produce that does not yet exist, whereas no comparable allowance has been established for muzābana. Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted ʿarāyā sales as an exception to muzābana because of need, there is all the more reason to permit selling one variety as part of the sale of another, especially when the need for this is greater. Prohibiting something from being sold separately does not necessarily entail prohibiting its sale together with something else. Consider that an unborn animal may not be sold separately, yet selling a pregnant animal is permissible by consensus, even if its being pregnant is stipulated as a condition. There are many comparable examples in the Sharia.

The underlying wisdom of the Sharia in all this is that an act involving harm is prohibited unless that harm is outweighed by a greater benefit, as in permitting someone compelled by necessity to eat carrion. Sales involving contractual uncertainty are prohibited because they are a form of gambling that leads to the wrongful consumption of wealth. If, however, this prohibition would entail greater harm, such sales are permitted to avert the greater of two harms by tolerating the lesser. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14739

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for selling crops such as melons, cucumbers, long cucumbers and the like, some scholars within the schools of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad maintain that they may only be sold one picking at a time, classifying this as selling fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent. The correct view, however, is that they may be sold altogether with their roots. This is the position of some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and is also the school of Mālik and others. There are, however, two grounds for this view.

The first is that their roots are comparable to the rootstocks of trees. Selling vegetables together with their roots before their ripeness becomes apparent is therefore like selling trees together with their fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent: the produce may permissibly be included as a subsidiary part of the sale. This is the reasoning adopted by a group of followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, although it runs counter to his principles.

The second ground—which is the correct one—is that these sales do not fall within the prohibition of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, their sale is valid under contracts involving the picking

---

## Page 14740

already present as well as future pickings, until the melon patch dries up—even though those future pickings do not yet exist. This is because there is a need for such sales, and they can only be conducted in this manner. Selling the produce one picking at a time is impossible or unduly difficult, because the pickings cannot be distinguished; Islamic law rules out imposing either impossibility or undue difficulty. It is an established principle of Islamic law that whatever people need to sell may be sold even if it does not yet exist, as with usufructs and payment for fruit whose ripeness has not yet become apparent when sold together with its rootstock, and fruit whose ripeness has become apparent without that restriction.

They also maintain that this produce is sufficiently known through customary practice, just as fruit is known. Its subsequent loss is comparable to the loss of fruit through a calamity, and the loss of benefits under a lease is of the same kind. Explicit textual evidence establishes that allowance must be made for losses caused by calamities, so there is nothing objectionable in this at all. Rather, prohibiting such sales causes harm, and Allah does not love harm. Even if selling such produce may lead to some harm, the harm resulting from prohibiting it is greater. The greater of two harms must therefore be averted by tolerating the lesser: this is an established principle of Islamic law.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has irrigated plots in which he grows turnips, carrots, radishes, cane and taro. Is it permissible to sell these while they are still in the ground?

He replied:

As for selling cane and similar crops, whether sold on the condition that they be uprooted or cut at the customary point, even if they are covered by their leaves, this covering

---

## Page 14741

does not invalidate the sale, just as grain may be sold in its ears, and walnuts and almonds within their two shells. Selling all these is permissible according to the great majority of earlier and later Muslim scholars, including Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and Ahmad, and according to one view within the school of al-Shāfiʿī. This has been the practice of Muslims from the time of their Prophet until the present, in every age and land. This is supported by the report:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited selling grain until it hardens, and selling grapes until they turn black.”

This indicates that grain may be sold once it has hardened, just as his prohibition of selling fruit before its ripeness becomes apparent indicates that it may be sold once its ripeness becomes apparent. Moreover, this is not a sale involving undue uncertainty, since its characteristics are known through customary practice.

As for selling carrots, turnips, radishes, taro and similar crops, there are two well-known views:

The first is that they may not be sold until they have been uprooted, on the grounds that they are concealed and have neither been seen nor described, like other goods not present that have neither been seen nor described. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, and the best-known position within Ahmad’s school.

The second is that they may be sold if the buyer has inspected what is visible of them in the customary manner. This is Mālik’s view and one view within Ahmad’s school. It is the sounder of the two views and has been the practice of Muslims in earlier and later times. People’s interests cannot be adequately served otherwise, for delaying their sale until they are uprooted is sometimes impossible and sometimes difficult, and leads to

---

## Page 14742

damage to property.

As for the claim that their being concealed makes this a sale involving undue uncertainty, that is not so. If the visible part of the goods indicates the nature of what remains unseen, the sale is permissible by the consensus of Muslims, as in the sale of real estate and animals. Likewise, where inspecting something in its entirety would cause hardship, it is sufficient to inspect as much as possible, as with the sale of walls, foods whose edible parts are concealed within, pregnant animals, and other such things. The correct view is that sales of this kind are permissible. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is it permissible to sell turnips, carrots, taro and similar crops while they are still underground?

He answered:

Regarding the sale of crops planted in the ground whose leaves are visible, such as turnips, carrots, taro, radishes, garlic, onions and the like, scholars hold two views.

The first is that it is not permissible. This is the well-known position among the followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others. They argue that these are goods not present which have neither been seen nor described, so selling them is impermissible, just as with other goods not present. This falls within the prohibition issued by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 14743

against sales involving undue uncertainty.

The second view is that selling such crops is permissible. This is held by some followers of Mālik and others, and is also one position within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. This is the correct view for several reasons.

One is that this does not involve undue uncertainty. Rather, experts infer the condition of what is concealed underground from the visible leaves, just as they infer the hidden condition of real estate from its outward appearance, and the internal condition of animals from their outward appearance. Anyone who consults experts will receive this information from them, and their judgment is authoritative in such matters.

Second, the knowledge required of anything sold depends on its nature. Where part of an item is visible and part concealed, and exposing the concealed part would entail hardship and difficulty, inspecting its exterior is sufficient. With real estate, for example, seeing its foundations and the interiors of its walls is not a condition of sale. The same applies to animals and similar things.

Third, where there is a need to sell something, greater latitude is allowed than in other cases. The Lawgiver permits it because of that need, even though the specific grounds for prohibition remain. Thus, he permitted arāyā sales on the basis of an estimate, allowing estimation to take the place of measurement by volume when there is need. This was not deemed to fall within the prohibited practice of muzābana, for muzābana means selling property for property of the same kind without measuring or weighing, if it is

---

## Page 14744

subject to the rules of riba; on this there is consensus. If it is not subject to those rules, there are two views. Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted purchasing fruit once it begins to ripen, on condition that it be left to mature, even though its remaining growth has not yet been created or seen. He thus treated what did not yet exist, had not been created, and was not known as incidental to it. People likewise need to sell these plants while they are still in the ground.

A similar case is the sale of crops grown in patches, such as melons, cucumbers, qiththāʾ (a kind of cucumber), and the like. Some followers of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others hold that these may only be sold one picking at a time.

Many scholars among the followers of Mālik, Ahmad, and others, however, hold that they may be sold without this restriction, in the customary manner. This is the correct view, for ordinarily they cannot be sold in any other way. Selling them one picking at a time is either impossible or difficult, since one picking cannot be clearly distinguished from another: much of the produce cannot be picked, and its picking may be deferred. Thus, selling the crop of a patch once it shows signs of ripening is like selling an orchard’s fruit once it begins to ripen, even though some of what is sold has not yet been created or seen.

For this reason, when some of a tree’s fruit begins to ripen, this counts as the onset of ripening for the rest of it, by scholarly consensus. It also counts as the onset of ripening for all fruit of the same kind in the orchard, according to the stronger of the two scholarly views, which is also the majority view. Indeed, according to one of the two scholarly views, it counts as the onset of ripening for all the orchard’s fruit that is customarily sold together. These issues, along with others mentioned in this answer, are discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 14745

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About selling sugar cane, taro, turnips, carrots, and similar crops while they are still in the ground, and about selling melons and similar crops grown in patches.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. There is no objection to selling sugar cane except the concern raised about its being enclosed in its protective rind. Selling it is thus like selling walnuts, almonds, and broad beans with both their coverings intact. Selling these is permissible according to the vast majority of Muslim scholars. This is the view of the early generations of the Muslim community and its uninterrupted practice from the time of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, until the present. People’s needs cannot be fully met otherwise.

This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and one view within the school of al-Shāfiʿī. Indeed, when al-Shāfiʿī fell ill, he instructed that fresh green broad beans be purchased for him. This was during his final illness, so it came after the prohibition recorded in his books.

Further evidence for this is the report that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade selling grapes until they turn black, and selling grain until it hardens.”

This indicates that selling them is permissible once the grapes have turned black and the grain has hardened. It therefore indicates that it is permissible to sell grain while it is still in its ears

---

## Page 14746

, which is one of the disputed cases, like broad beans with both their coverings intact. Anyone who disapproves of selling such produce considers it to involve the uncertainty prohibited by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. But this is not so, for two reasons:

First, buyers know what they are buying just as they know many goods whose sale is unanimously permitted. Indeed, their knowledge of this produce is greater than their knowledge of many such goods.

Second, even supposing that some lack of knowledge is involved, the Sharia has established the permissibility of selling things that people need to sell despite the uncertainty involved. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted fruit to be sold once it shows signs of ripening, while leaving it on the trees until fully ripe. He then ordered that losses caused by calamities be deducted from the price if they struck the fruit. He also permitted the sale of real estate, saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever owns a share in land, a dwelling, or an orchard may not sell it until he informs his co-owner. If the co-owner wishes, he may buy it; if he wishes, he may decline.”

The Muslims have unanimously agreed that selling real estate is permissible, even though the foundations and inner structure of its walls are hidden.

Likewise, he permitted fruit to be sold before it shows signs of ripening when included in the sale of the trees themselves. He said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“Whoever sells palm trees that have been pollinated, their fruit belongs to the seller unless the buyer stipulates otherwise.”

Sales involving uncertainty were prohibited because they involve games of chance and gambling, which entail consuming wealth wrongfully. Thus, if

---

## Page 14747

in some cases prohibiting the sale causes owners greater loss, spoilage, or depreciation of their property than permitting it would, it is not permissible to avert a lesser harm by accepting a greater one. Rather, what is obligatory is what the Sharia prescribes: securing the greater of two benefits by forgoing the lesser, and averting the greater of two harms by accepting the lesser. This fatwa does not allow for a more extensive discussion.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

As for selling taro, carrots, turnips, and similar produce, a number of scholars permit it. This is Mālik’s position and one view within the school of Imam Ahmad, although the well-known position reported from him—like that of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī—is that it is not permissible.

The first view is correct. The principle on which scholars agree in this matter is that the item being sold must be known to the degree required in sales. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited sales involving uncertainty, and in this matter one must refer to those with the relevant expertise.

Those with expertise say that they can assess this produce while it is still in the ground sufficiently for its sale no longer to involve such uncertainty. They ascertain this by examining what they pull up, just as one can assess

---

## Page 14748

goods already removed from the ground by inspecting a portion of them, provided their parts are alike.

If what was hidden then proves inferior to what was visible in a way not ordinarily encountered, this amounts either to an unfair bargain or to deception. Indeed, those with expertise say that they can assess such produce more accurately than they can assess many goods already removed from the ground. Whether an item offered for sale is sufficiently known is not a question reserved specifically for jurists; rather, one refers to those with expertise in that particular item. What is learned from jurists is their distinctive field of knowledge: legal rulings and their supporting evidence.

Allah Most High says:

“Who believe in the unseen.”

Belief in something requires evidence indicating its existence. Thus, things absent from direct observation may be known through evidence pointing to them. If those with expertise say that they can assess such produce, they are the ones to consult, rather than those who lack their expertise, even if the latter are more knowledgeable about religion. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told them concerning the pollination of date palms:

“You know your worldly affairs better. As for matters of your religion, refer them to me.”

The legal ruling is then based on what those with expertise know, just as it is based on valuation, al-qiyāma, estimates of crop yields, and similar assessments.

---

## Page 14749

He was also asked:

A man contracted with another to purchase sugar cane and taro while it was still underground, before it had matured. When it matured, it was flooded. The seller had demanded payment without any written agreement or delivery. What is required in this case?

Answer

He replied:

The seller bears the loss of whatever was destroyed, whether the sale was valid or invalid. It is established in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a calamity befalls it, none of its price is lawful for you. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s wealth?”

---

## Page 14750

Chapter on Salam (Advance-Payment Sales)

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

Is a salam contract for olives permissible?

He replied:

Salam contracts for olives and similar commodities sold by measure or weight are permissible. I know of no disagreement among the imams concerning this. The disagreement concerns salam contracts for things not sold by measure or weight, such as animals and the like. Two positions are reported from Ahmad on this matter. The better-known position permits it, which is also the view of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. The second prohibits it, agreeing with the view of Abū Hanīfa.

He was also asked:

A man has wheat worth eighteen dirhams by weight. He sold it for twenty-five dirhams, with payment deferred. Is this permissible? And are salam contracts for grain lawful or unlawful?

He replied:

Advance-payment sales are permissible by consensus, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever pays in advance must do so for a specified measure and a specified weight

---

## Page 14751

with a specified delivery date.”

As for valuing a commodity at its cash price and then selling it on deferred payment for more than that, this is prohibited according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. As Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“If you value it at a cash price and then sell it for cash, there is no harm. But if you value it at a cash price and then sell it on deferred payment, that is dirhams exchanged for dirhams.”

By “value it,” he meant appraise it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman buys cloth for immediate payment and sells it at a one-third markup, with payment deferred until a specified date. Is this usury?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the buyer purchases it to use it or trade in it—not to sell it and obtain the proceeds because he needs the money—there is no harm in that. However, if the buyer is in need, the seller should charge him only the customary profit. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Is it permissible to sell one sheep for another, with delivery deferred until a specified date?

He replied:

It is permissible to sell one sheep for another, with delivery deferred until a specified date.

---

## Page 14752

He was asked:

A man purchases soot from bathhouses, paying silver in advance for the entire year’s soot. Before witnesses, he specifies a fixed quantity in irdabbs (measures of capacity), although no measuring actually takes place; he does this only to make the advance-payment sale valid. The customary practice is that whatever accumulates is placed in its container, sealed, and sold in its entirety. Is this valid or not?

He replied:

This question rests on two fundamental issues:

“The first”: Is this deposit formed from smoke ritually pure or impure? This requires distinctions, and there is scholarly disagreement concerning it. If the fuel is pure, such as the fuel used in ovens or pure fuel used to heat a bathhouse, then the resulting deposit is pure. If, however, an impure substance is used as fuel, is the resulting deposit pure? Scholars hold two opinions on this. The same disagreement applies to every impure substance that undergoes transformation, as with ashes, qasarmal, jarsīf, and similar materials.

If it results from the transformation of an impure substance, it is impure according to the school of al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two opinions in the schools of Mālik and Ahmad; this is also an apparent view within the school of Abū Hanīfah. The other opinion in the schools of Mālik and Ahmad is that it is pure, and this view

---

## Page 14753

has stronger support in the Quran, the Sunnah, and analogical reasoning. For scholars agree that wine becomes pure when Allah causes it to turn into vinegar. This substance falls under neither the wording nor the meaning of the prohibition, so it is neither forbidden nor impure.

Those who consider it pure permit its sale. Some permit its sale even while considering it impure. The disagreement over this is well known in “the issue of impure manure.”

“The second fundamental issue”: If selling it is permissible, one cannot therefore say that it is being sold in the prescribed manner. There is no doubt that it may be sold through an advance-payment contract. The question is not about selling a particular, identified item, which would require inspection and the like. Rather, when an advance-payment contract is made for it, a known quantity and a known delivery date must be specified, the seller must receive the payment during the contractual session, and the other conditions of an advance-payment sale must be fulfilled.

If, however, they have merely adopted the outward form of an advance-payment sale, while the buyer receives whatever accumulates—and this is what they actually intend—whether it is more or less than the specified quantity, then this is an invalid contract. It must be prohibited, and those engaging in it must be stopped.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man in need who said to a merchant who had some cloth: “Give me this piece

---

## Page 14754

,” and the merchant said, “It cost me thirty, and I will sell it only for fifty, payable at a later date.” Is that permissible or not?

He replied:

Buyers fall into three categories:

The first is someone who wants the goods for personal use, whether for eating, drinking, clothing, riding, or other purposes.

The second is someone who intends to trade in them. These two kinds of purchase are permissible according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Allah Most High says:

“Allah has permitted trade.”

And He Most High says:

“Unless it is trade conducted by mutual consent among you.”

However, the conditions prescribed by Islamic law must be observed. If the buyer is compelled by necessity, the goods may only be sold to him at their fair market value. For example, if someone urgently needs to buy food that is available from only one person, that person must sell it to him at its fair market value. If he refuses to sell it for anything less than a higher price, the buyer may take it without his consent, paying its fair market value. If the seller hands it over to him, the buyer owes only its fair market value. If he sells it to him at its fair value with payment deferred until that date, the period of deferment accounts for part of the price.

The third category is someone whose sole purpose in buying is to obtain money—for example, to settle a debt and buy something with it. The two agree that he will give him, for example, one hundred in exchange for one hundred and twenty payable at a later date. All of this is prohibited. If they agree that he will return

---

## Page 14755

the goods to him, this constitutes “two sales in one transaction.” The same applies if they bring in a third party who buys the goods from him, after which they are returned to him. Likewise, if he combines a sale to him with a loan, the same applies; the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited this.

If, however, the buyer takes the goods and sells them elsewhere—buying them for one hundred and selling them for seventy because he needs cash—this is known as “tawarruq.” Scholars disagree about it, but the stronger view here too is that it is prohibited and that it is the root of usury, as stated by ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and others. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a man who advances money against wheat, barley, broad beans, chickpeas, and similar produce. When the time comes to collect it, he sells it, at its market value, to the person holding it, before taking possession of it from him. Is this lawful or unlawful? What must he do about the past years and the transactions he used to conduct?

He replied:

This is called “salam” or “salaf” (a sale with advance payment and deferred delivery). According to the four imams’ schools, it is not permissible to sell this debt—the produce owed under the salam contract—before taking possession of it, whether to the person who received the advance or to anyone else. Rather, this falls within the Messenger of Allah’s prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against selling what one has not taken possession of. It may also constitute profiting from something for which one has not assumed liability

---

## Page 14756

as well. If such a sale takes place, it is invalid. The seller who advanced the money is entitled only to what is owed under the salam contract, not to the consideration received in exchange for it. He must return that consideration if he has received it and demand what is owed under the salam contract. If this proves impossible—for example, because a long time has passed, he no longer knows the relevant details, or something similar—he should retain from those proceeds an amount equivalent to the salam debt and give the profit in charity. By taking the equivalent of the salam debt, he has taken only what is rightfully his from that money. The excess is profit from something for which he assumed no liability, and it is not lawful for him. He should therefore give it in charity on behalf of its owners. If, however, he made no profit, and the recipient of the advance merely sold it at its price, he is not required to give away his own money.

He was asked:

About a man who owns a horse that he bought for one hundred and eighty dirhams. Someone offered him three hundred dirhams for it, payable in three months. Is that lawful?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the buyer purchases it to use it or trade in it, there is nothing wrong with selling it on deferred payment. However, when selling to someone in need, the seller must take no more than the customary profit and must not increase it because of that person’s pressing need. But if the buyer needs money and purchases the horse to sell it immediately and obtain its price, this is disapproved according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views.

---

## Page 14757

He was asked:

About a man who has a commodity for which one person has offered him 2,100 in currency reckoned by weight, while another has offered him 2,700 payable at a specified date within the year.

He replied:

If the person buying it on deferred payment intends to trade in it or make use of it, the seller may sell it either for immediate payment or on deferred payment, as he wishes. But if the buyer’s aim is to obtain money, and he intends to sell the commodity once he has purchased it and collect the money, this is called “tawarruq.” It is disapproved according to the stronger of the scholars’ two views.

---

## Page 14758

He was asked:
