About a man who paid fifty dirhams in advance for one ritl (a unit of weight) of silk, to be delivered at a specified date. When that date arrived, the silk was unavailable. May he accept the value of the silk, or accept anything else in its place?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. There are two positions reported from Imam Ahmad on this question.

The first is that it is not permissible to accept something else in place of the goods owed under a salam contract (an advance-payment sale). This is also the view of al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfah, based on the report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever pays in advance for something must not divert it to something else.”

This is the position commonly recognized among the later scholars of Imam Ahmad’s school, and it is the one mentioned by al-Khiraqī and others.

The second view is that this is permissible, just as it is permissible with other debts and with sales of individually specified goods. This is Mālik’s position. Ahmad explicitly stated this in several places, treating goods owed under a salam contract like other goods sold.

Thus, if he accepts a substitute that is sold neither by volume nor by weight, equal in value to the goods owed under the salam contract at the time of substitution, without any excess, or accepts something of the same category in an equal quantity—for example, if he pays in advance for wheat and receives

---

## Page 14759

barley equal in quantity to the wheat, or pays in advance for silk and accepts horses, cattle, or sheep in its place—this is permissible. A number of scholars of the school, including Ibn Abī Mūsā and al-Sāmirī, the author of al-Mustawʿib, have mentioned this, though only for certain cases. As stated in al-Mustawʿib:

“According to one of the two reported positions, whoever pays in advance for something may not, under any circumstances, accept something of a different kind in its place. According to the other, this is permissible, and he may accept grains inferior to wheat, such as barley and the like, in a volume equal to that of the wheat, but no greater, and not on the basis of its value. Ahmad explicitly stated this.”

In the report transmitted by Abū Tālib, he said:

“If you pay in advance for a kurr of wheat and accept barley instead, there is no harm, since it is less than your entitlement. But do not accept wheat in place of barley.”

Those well acquainted with Ahmad’s explicit statements, however, have reported a broader position: goods owed under a salam contract may be exchanged for anything not sold by volume or weight, without restriction. Abū Hafs al-ʿUkbarī mentioned this in his collection, and Judge Abū Yaʿlā copied it from him in his own handwriting. Thus, if the goods contracted for are sold by volume or weight, he may accept an equal volume of a different variety that is inferior in quality. Likewise, he may accept their value in goods not sold by volume or weight, in whatever form he wishes.

Ibn al-Qāsim related from Ahmad:

“I said to Abū ʿAbd Allāh, ‘If he cannot find the goods for which he paid in advance, but finds something else of the same kind, may he accept it?’

“He said, ‘Yes, if it is inferior to what he is owed.’

“I said, ‘What if he paid in advance for a qafīz of Mosul wheat?’

“He said, ‘Then he may take sult barley in its place, or a qafīz of barley, using the same measure

---

## Page 14760

without taking any extra. If it is superior, however, he must not accept it.’” He also cited the report of Ibn ʿAbbās, transmitted by Tāwūs from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“If you pay in advance for something and the due date arrives, but you cannot find what you paid for, accept a substitute of lesser value, and do not make a profit twice.”

Ahmad ibn Asram also reported that Ahmad was asked about a man who paid in advance for food to be delivered at a specified date: when it fell due, could he purchase land or a house from the seller instead? He replied:

“Yes, he may purchase from him goods not sold by volume or weight.”

Harb al-Kirmānī said:

“I asked Ahmad, ‘A man paid another man dirhams in advance for wheat, but when delivery fell due, the seller did not have it. Some people said, “Take barley in exchange for the dirhams.”’

“He replied, ‘He may accept barley from him only in a volume equal to that of the wheat, or less.’

“I said, ‘If the wheat amounts to ten jarībs, may he accept ten jarībs of barley?’

“He replied, ‘Yes.’”

Others have transmitted the same position from Ahmad. This position occurs more frequently in Ahmad’s explicit statements and accords more closely with his principles. His reason for prohibiting the sale of goods owed under a salam contract is that they are goods already sold and therefore may not be resold before possession is taken. Yet, according to the apparent position of his school, Ahmad does not prohibit every sale before possession; rather, he makes distinctions and has several well-known positions on the matter. For this reason, he distinguished between selling back to the original seller and selling to someone else. Similarly, Mālik’s school permits exchanging the goods owed under a salam contract with their seller for a substitute worth their original price or less, but not more. This is not permissible in the case of food.

Ibn al-Mundhir said:

“It is established that Ibn ʿAbbās said, ‘If you pay in advance for something

---

## Page 14761

and the due date arrives, accept what you paid for if it is available; otherwise, accept a substitute of lesser value.’”

This same Ibn ʿAbbās, after reporting that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) “forbade selling food before taking possession of it,” said: “I consider everything else to be like food.”

As for what Shaykh Abū Muhammad wrote in his al-Mughnī, when discussing al-Khiraqī’s statement:

“Selling the goods owed under a salam contract back to their seller or to anyone else before taking possession of them is invalid,”

Abū Muhammad said:

“No disagreement is known regarding the prohibition of selling goods owed under a salam contract before taking possession of them.”

He said this—may Allah have mercy on him—according to what he knew. Mālik’s position, however, permits selling them to someone other than the recipient of the advance payment, just as he permits selling other debts to someone other than the debtor. This is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. He explicitly affirmed in several places that a debt may be sold to someone other than the debtor, just as he explicitly affirmed that a salam debt may be sold to the debtor himself. Both positions are expressly stated in many of Ahmad’s answers, even though they do not appear in the books of many later scholars of his school. This position is more sound and follows by analogy from Ahmad’s principles.

This is because the goods owed under a salam contract have already been sold. Scholars have disagreed over whether an item sold may be resold before possession is taken but after taking possession has become possible, and over who bears the risk for it. Al-Shāfiʿī prohibits such sales without exception and says: “The risk remains with the seller.” This is also a weak report from Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah prohibits such sales except in the case of real estate, and says: “The risk remains with the seller.” These scholars explain the prohibition in terms of

---

## Page 14762

the resulting double liability.

Mālik, Ahmad—according to the well-known position reported from him—and others, however, say that when the buyer is able to take possession of an item specifically identified in the contract—such as a slave, a horse, or the like—the risk passes to the buyer. They make further distinctions here and disagree concerning certain specifically identified items. Their evidence is the report transmitted by Ahmad and others from al-Zuhrī, from Sālim, from Ibn ʿUmar, who said:

“The established Sunnah is that whatever is alive and intact when the bargain is concluded is at the buyer’s risk.”

Thus, Ahmad’s apparent position is that the risk passes to the buyer when taking possession becomes possible, not when possession actually takes place. His apparent position is therefore that permission to dispose of the goods neither necessarily accompanies liability nor depends upon it. Rather, disposal may be permissible while the risk remains with the seller, as he mentioned concerning fruit and the benefits provided under a lease. Conversely, the buyer may bear the risk while disposal remains impermissible, as with a specified heap of grain.

Al-Khiraqī mentioned both cases in his Mukhtasar, saying:

“If someone purchases the fruit without the trees, and it is destroyed by a natural calamity, he may recover its price from the seller.”

The scholars of the school said:

“Permission to dispose of something does not necessarily mean that possession has been completed, as demonstrated by the benefits provided under a lease.”

Al-Khiraqī then said:

“If a sale concerns goods measured by volume, weight, or number, and they perish before possession is taken, the loss falls upon the seller.”

According to the majority of the school’s scholars, this refers to whatever is sold by volume, weight, or number, whether specifically identified or not.

Al-Khiraqī then said:

“Whoever purchases something requiring possession may not resell it

---

## Page 14763

until he takes possession of it.” He thus distinguished between what requires possession and what does not. For the latter, the ability to take possession is sufficient, as with property held in safekeeping.

He then said:

“Whoever purchases a heap of grain may not sell it until he moves it.”

Thus, the heap passes to the buyer’s risk once it is made freely available to him and he is able to take possession, yet he may not sell it until he moves it? All of this is explicitly stated by Ahmad. However, the scholars of the school disagree on these matters, and there are differing reports of his position whose discussion belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that, according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school, the goods may remain at the seller’s risk while the buyer is permitted to resell them. This applies, for example, to fruit sold after it begins to ripen: according to the schools of Mālik and Ahmad, it remains at the seller’s risk. This is also a position conditionally held by al-Shāfiʿī, on the basis of the report narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Jābir, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If you sell fruit to your brother and a natural calamity destroys it, it is not lawful for you to take anything from him. On what grounds would any of you take his brother’s wealth without right?”

Nevertheless, according to the sounder of the two reports from Ahmad, the buyer may resell this fruit even though it remains at the seller’s risk. Likewise, a tenant may, without disagreement, sublet what he has rented for the same rent, even though the benefits remain at the seller’s risk.

However, if he sublets it for a higher rent without making any addition to the property, there are two reports:

The first permits this, in agreement with al-Shāfiʿī’s position.

The second does not permit it, in agreement with the position of

---

## Page 14764

Abū Hanīfah, because this constitutes profit on something for which one has not borne the risk, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade making a profit on something for which one has not borne the risk.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is an authentic hadith.”

The first position is sounder, because if the purchaser left unused the premises he had rented and taken possession of, the loss of their benefits would be at his own risk. If the house collapsed, however, the loss would be borne by the lessor. These issues are discussed at length in their proper place.

The point here is that the underlying principle adopted by Ahmad and Mālik permits disposal of the property and allows greater latitude in selling before the risk passes to the buyer. This differs from the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, and the other report from Ahmad: according to their principle, a sale is permissible only once the risk has passed to the buyer and the goods have become his responsibility.

They said that this prevents two liabilities from overlapping: before possession, the goods remain at the first seller’s risk. If they are sold before the buyer assumes that risk, they also become his responsibility, so that two liabilities attach to the same goods.

According to Mālik’s position and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, this is a weak rationale, for there is nothing objectionable in it. If the goods perish before possession can be taken, the seller must repay the price he received from the second buyer. The obligation arising from one party’s liability is thus distinct from that arising from the other’s.

Once this is understood, according to those who hold the former position, selling the goods owed under a salam contract is prohibited, because the advance-paying buyer has not yet assumed their risk. He assumes their risk only upon taking possession, and therefore may not sell something for which he has not borne the risk. And according to

---

## Page 14765

the position of Mālik and the well-known position reported from Ahmad, this is permissible, as is established from Ibn ʿAbbās. However, it may not be sold at a profit, but only at its value, so that the advance-paying buyer does not profit from something for which he has not borne the risk. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“forbade making a profit on something for which one has not borne the risk.”

The evidence for this is that accepting something else in settlement of the price before receiving it is permissible, according to the established Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Ibn ʿUmar said:

“We used to sell camels at al-Naqīʿ”—al-Naqīʿ, with an n, is the market of Medina, whereas al-Baqīʿ, with a b, is its cemetery. He continued: “We used to sell for gold and receive payment in silver, and sell for silver and receive payment in gold. I asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about this, and he said: ‘There is no harm, provided it is at the exchange rate on that day and you part with nothing left outstanding between you.’”

Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted them to accept something else in settlement of the debt constituting the price, even though the buyer remained liable for that price and liability had not passed to the seller. Likewise, the goods owed under a salam contract may be sold, even though they remain at the seller’s risk and the risk has not passed to the buyer. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted such settlement only at the exchange rate on that day, so that one would not profit from something for which one had not borne the risk.

Ahmad likewise explicitly stated that, when accepting a substitute in repayment of a loan or another debt, it must be valued at the rate on that day, so that this does not constitute profit on something for which one has not borne the risk. Imam Ahmad also related this from Ibn ʿAbbās when answering a question about salam, quoting him as saying: “If you pay in advance

---

## Page 14766

for something, and the delivery date arrives but you cannot find what you paid for in advance, then accept a substitute of lesser value, and do not profit twice.” Likewise, Mālik’s school permits accepting a substitute at the price prevailing that day, as Ahmad ruled and related from Ibn ʿAbbās. Mālik excluded foodstuffs, because one of his principles is that foodstuffs may not be sold before taking possession of them. This is also one position reported from Ahmad. Ahmad distinguished between exchanging goods sold by measure or weight for other goods sold by measure or weight, and exchanging them for something else.

If they are exchanged for something else—for example, if someone accepts horses or cattle in place of goods sold by measure or weight, such as the wheat or barley for which he paid in advance—Ahmad permitted this, as did Mālik and, before them, Ibn ʿAbbās, provided the exchange is at the price prevailing that day. But if he accepts other goods sold by measure or weight in their place, such as barley in place of wheat, Ahmad disapproved of this unless it was for an equivalent amount. This is because exchanging goods measured by volume for other such goods, or goods measured by weight for other such goods, requires immediate settlement and both parties taking possession. Hence, wheat may only be exchanged for barley hand to hand, and gold may only be exchanged for silver hand to hand.

The advance-paying buyer has not taken possession of what is owed under the salam contract, so Ahmad disapproved of this, just as he, according to one of the two positions reported from him, and al-Shāfiʿī, according to one of his two opinions, disapprove of selling a debt to the person who owes it in all cases, on the grounds that this constitutes selling something whose risk one has not borne and of which one has not taken possession. The correct view, held by the majority of scholars and reflected in the apparent position of al-Shāfiʿī

---

## Page 14767

and Ahmad, is that a debt may be sold to the person who owes it, because what is owed is already in the debtor’s possession. However, if it is sold in exchange for something for which it may not be sold on deferred terms, immediate settlement and taking possession are required, lest the transaction involve usury. The same applies if it is sold in exchange for something specified by description and owed as a liability.

If it is sold for something falling into neither category, there are two views. According to the first, these conditions are not required, just as they are not required in other such transactions. According to the second, they are required, because delaying possession constitutes deferment, as in selling a debt for a debt.

Mālik did not permit selling goods owed under a salam contract if they were foodstuffs, because this constitutes a sale. Ahmad, however, permitted selling them back to their seller, even if they were foodstuffs or goods sold by measure or weight, provided they were exchanged for something not sold by measure or weight. This is because the prohibition on selling foodstuffs before taking possession of them concerns specifically identified foodstuffs. As for goods owed as a liability, accepting a substitute for them is a form of collecting what is due. Its effect is to discharge the debtor’s liability, not to confer new ownership upon him; the two cases therefore cannot be treated as analogous.

In an ordinary sale, the buyer acquires ownership of what he purchases. Here, however, he acquires nothing; rather, the debt is discharged from his liability.

If he paid the other party what he owed, this would not be described as selling him dirhams for dirhams. Rather, it would be said that he had paid him his due. This differs from selling him specific dirhams for other specific dirhams, which does constitute a sale. Thus, since, in the case of tangible assets, selling them for their own kind does not constitute a sale, settling the obligation with something of a different kind likewise does not constitute a sale. Rather, it is a settlement involving an element of exchange.

---

## Page 14768

For this reason, if a man swears that he will pay someone his due the following day, then gives him a substitute, he has fulfilled his oath according to the sounder of the two views. There is disagreement as to whether the Prophet’s prohibition on selling foodstuffs before taking possession of them applies only to selling them to someone other than the original seller. Those who explain the prohibition in terms of successive liabilities extend it to this case as well. Others explain it as ensuring that the buyer receives his purchase in full and that the seller’s attachment to it is severed, so that, upon seeing the buyer make a profit, the seller will not seek to rescind the sale or withhold delivery. They apply this rationale to a heap of foodstuffs before it is moved, even if possession has already been taken. This rationale does not apply when the goods are sold back to the original seller.

Moreover, selling the goods back to the original seller resembles cancellation by mutual agreement. According to one of the two scholarly views, such cancellation is permissible before possession is taken. Is cancellation by mutual agreement a rescission or a sale? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad. If we say it is a rescission, it is permissible only in exchange for the equivalent of the original price. If we say it is a sale, there are two views on this. As for a debt owed under a salam contract, cancellation by mutual agreement is permissible without dispute.

It is therefore clear that the rules concerning a debt owed under a salam contract are less stringent than those governing sales of specifically identified goods. Most scholars do not permit selling purchased goods back to their seller before the buyer is able to take possession of them, yet they permit cancellation by mutual agreement in the case of a salam debt. Accepting a substitute for that debt is likewise permissible, just as cancellation by mutual agreement is permissible. However, it constitutes such a cancellation only if he takes back his original capital or its equivalent, even if accompanied by an additional amount. If he sells the debt for something else, this is not cancellation by mutual agreement; rather, it is a settlement that has the character of selling something not yet received.

Ahmad permitted selling a debt owed under a salam contract to the recipient of the advance payment, following Ibn Abbās; and Ibn

---

## Page 14769

Abbās says: “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade selling foodstuffs before taking possession of them,” and, “I consider everything else to be no different from foodstuffs.”

Thus, Ibn Abbās did not permit selling goods before taking possession of them, yet he permitted selling a debt owed under a salam contract to the person who owed it, provided no profit was made. Ibn Abbās did not distinguish between foodstuffs and other goods, nor between goods sold by measure or weight and other goods. This is because the sale here is back to the seller who owes the goods, so he would be taking possession of them from himself for himself. Indeed, no taking of possession occurs here; rather, his liability is discharged. There is therefore no benefit in receiving the goods from him only to return them to him. This reflects Ibn Abbās’s understanding of the law.

Mālik treated this as equivalent to selling specifically identified goods to a third party, and therefore prohibited selling foodstuffs owed under a salam contract back to the recipient of the advance payment. Ahmad, however, did not equate this with selling foodstuffs to a third party before taking possession of them, as Mālik did. Rather, he permitted it in exchange for things other than goods sold by measure or weight, just as Ibn Abbās permitted it. As for exchanging it for goods sold by measure or weight, he disapproved of this lest it resemble exchanging measured goods for measured goods without mutual possession, since no actual exchange of possession had occurred on both sides.

However, if the creditor accepted goods of the same kind and in the same measured quantity, but of inferior quality, Ahmad permitted this, because it constitutes receiving payment in goods of the same kind, not a sale—just as one may accept inferior goods in settlement of an entitlement to superior goods. Wheat and barley may be treated as a single kind. Accordingly, there are two views reported from Ahmad concerning the permissibility of exchanging one for the other in unequal quantities:

The first is that it is prohibited, as Mālik held.

---

## Page 14770

And the second is that it is permissible, as Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī held. Ahmad’s disapproval of exchanging goods sold by measure or weight for other goods sold by measure or weight may be understood as discouragement rather than prohibition. Alternatively, it may apply when taking possession is delayed. This latter interpretation is more consistent with Ahmad’s principles and explicit statements, and is what the scriptural evidence requires.

For when goods sold by measure are exchanged for goods sold by measure, or goods sold by weight for goods sold by weight, he requires that both countervalues be due immediately and that both parties take possession. If one category is exchanged for the other, two views are reported from him. This rests on the view that the effective cause of the prohibition concerning the six commodities is likeness: being of the same kind and sold by measure, or being of the same kind and sold by weight.

The scholars agree that exchanging gold for silver on a deferred basis is impermissible. Likewise, exchanging wheat, dates, barley, or salt for one another on a deferred basis is impermissible. Thus, whoever identifies the effective cause as likeness—whether in being sold by measure or weight, in being food, or in combining both characteristics—prohibits deferment whenever the two commodities share a single effective cause. All these positions are reported from Ahmad.

Likeness, meaning that the goods are of the same kind and sold by measure, or of the same kind and sold by weight, is the position most widely reported from him. It is also the school of Abū Hanīfah.

Being food is the criterion adopted by al-Shāfiʿī. Combining the two characteristics is the position of Ibn al-Musayyib and others, one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions, and the view preferred by Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī. Mālik’s position is close to this: his criterion is being a staple food or something used to prepare it.

Accordingly, when a debt arising from a salam contract, or any other debt, is settled with goods sold by measure, possession of those goods must be taken during the session in which the exchange is agreed. The same applies to goods sold by weight when they are exchanged

---

## Page 14771

for other goods sold by weight, such as exchanging silk for cotton or flax. If goods sold by measure are exchanged for other goods sold by measure in an unrestricted sale, such that possession of the countervalue is not taken during the session, this is impermissible. This differs from exchanging them for an animal or real estate: according to the sounder of the two views within the school, possession of these need not be taken during the session. This is also Ahmad’s explicitly stated position. His words can therefore be understood on this basis, and his prohibition of the sale should be interpreted accordingly.

For this reason, he said:

“When the debt falls due, he may purchase from him something that is sold neither by measure nor by weight.”

He thus permitted this without qualification. Goods sold by measure or weight, however, may not be purchased without qualification; rather, possession must be taken during the session, just as when they are sold in exchange for a specific asset. This interpretation is supported by Ahmad’s adherence to Ibn Abbās’s statement on this matter. Ibn Abbās said:

“If you pay in advance for something under a salam contract, and the due date arrives but you cannot obtain what you contracted for, take a substitute for less, and do not profit twice.”

The profit he prohibited was specifically that obtained by selling the debt arising from the salam contract to the debtor for more than its value at the time of settlement. This is why Ahmad prohibited taking an excess when accepting goods sold by measure—such as barley—in settlement of it. Ibn Abbās made no distinction between selling it for goods sold by measure or weight and selling it for anything else.

This is not a matter of usury through unequal exchange, such that one could object: “Ibn Abbās permits usury through unequal exchange.” Rather, selling gold for silver on a deferred basis is forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims, as is selling wheat for barley on a deferred basis. This is what follows from the principles of Ahmad’s school and those of others: what is owed as a debt is considered to be in possession. Thus, if it consists of goods sold by measure or weight, and he exchanges it for goods sold by measure or weight without taking possession of the latter, then he has exchanged goods sold by measure for goods sold by measure without taking possession. If, however, he takes possession, this is permissible.

---

## Page 14772

It is also established in Ahmad’s school that if someone sells for gold, he may accept silver in its place, and if he sells for silver, he may accept gold in its place during the session, as in the hadith of Ibn Umar. This is accepting one commodity sold by weight in place of another.

If this is permissible with the price, it is permissible with the goods sold; there is no difference between them, except according to those who say:

“These are goods sold but not yet received, so they may not be resold.”

The unsoundness of this reasoning in the case of salam has already become clear. Ibn Abbās himself, who prohibited selling before taking possession, permitted this. Moreover, selling the debt arising from a salam contract back to its seller entails none of the concerns involved in selling it to someone else—whether successive liabilities or anything else.

As for those who prohibit selling a salam debt on the strength of the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever pays in advance for something must not divert it to something else,”

there are two answers. First, the hadith is weak. Second, what it means is that one must not turn the original advance payment into a salam contract for something else. It would thus prohibit selling the debt in exchange for a specified item to be delivered at a future date, which is a form of selling debt for debt. This is why he said:

“He must not divert it to something else.”

That is, he must not convert the goods owed under one salam contract into goods owed under another. Someone who accepts something else in their place and takes possession of the substitute has not thereby made them an advance payment under another salam contract.

A detailed discussion of these issues is beyond the scope of this answer. Nevertheless, this concession is established from Ibn Abbās, and it is the position of

---

## Page 14773

Mālik. Ahmad allowed greater latitude in this matter than Mālik.

The view mentioned by al-Khiraqī and others has been described as another position transmitted from Ahmad, as reported by Ibn Abī Mūsā and others. The correct view is that this is permissible, for there is no evidence prohibiting it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

If a man pays in advance for something under a salam contract, may he accept something else from the seller? For example, if he pays in advance for wheat, may he accept barley in its place, whether or not the contracted goods can be delivered?

He replied:

If someone pays in advance for wheat and accepts barley in its place, or makes a similar substitution, scholars hold two views on this.

The first is that accepting something else in place of the goods owed under a salam contract is impermissible. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī, and one of the two positions transmitted from Ahmad.

The second is that such substitution is permissible in principle, provided it is at the current market price or less. This is what is reported from Ibn Abbās: he permitted someone who had paid in advance for something to accept a substitute of equivalent value, without making a profit twice. This is also the other position transmitted from Ahmad, who permitted accepting barley in place of wheat, provided its value was no greater than

---

## Page 14774

the value of the wheat.

He followed Ibn Abbās’s position on this. Mālik’s school permits accepting merchandise in place of food or merchandise owed. Those holding the first view cited the report in the Sunan that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever pays in advance for something must not divert it to something else.”

They said that this entails that one may not sell a debt arising from a salam contract, either to the debtor himself or to anyone else. The second view, however, is more sound. It is the view of Ibn Abbās, and no Companion is known to have disagreed with him. This is because a debt arising from salam is an established debt, so accepting a substitute for it is permissible, just as with the repayment of a loan or the price owed for a sale. Moreover, it is one of the two things exchanged in a sale, so accepting a substitute for it is permissible, just as it is for the other.

As for the hadith, its chain of transmission is questionable. Even if it is authentic, what it means is that one must not turn a debt arising from salam into an advance payment for something else. Hence his words:

“He must not divert it to something else.”

That is, he must not convert it into another advance-purchase transaction. This is impermissible because it entails making a profit on something for which one has not assumed liability. Likewise, when accepting a substitute for a sale price or the repayment of a loan, one must do so at its current value. Thus, it is reported in the Sunan from Ibn Umar that they asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“We sell camels at al-Naqī for gold and receive silver, and we sell them for silver and receive gold.”

He replied:

“There is no harm, provided it is at the exchange rate on that day and you part with nothing remaining outstanding between you.”

Accepting a substitute at the current rate is therefore permissible, so that one does not profit from something for which one has not assumed liability.

If it is objected, “A debt arising from salam falls under that ruling, since selling what has not been received is prohibited,” the reply is: “The prohibition concerns specific items, not debts.”

---

## Page 14775

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section

“Fair compensation” is an expression frequently used by scholars. It is indispensable to the justice through which well-being in this world and the Hereafter is secured, and is thus a pillar of the Sharia. Related expressions include “fair value,” “fair remuneration,” “the dower customary for comparable women,” and the like. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever frees his share in a slave, while possessing enough wealth to cover the slave’s price, must have the slave fairly valued, with neither undervaluation nor excess. He must pay his co-owners their shares, and the slave becomes entirely free at his expense.”

Another hadith relates that he awarded Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq the dower customary for women comparable to her, with neither deficiency nor excess.

Fair compensation is needed in assessing liability for the loss of life, property, sexual rights, and benefits; in liabilities requiring an equivalent in property, benefits, and certain cases involving human life; and in liabilities arising from invalid and valid contracts, for the purpose of assessing compensation for injury to persons and damage to property.

---

## Page 14776

It is also needed in transactions undertaken on behalf of others: those conducted by someone entrusted with the affairs of Muslims, an orphan, an endowment, or others; those conducted by an agent, such as an agent authorized to transact, a business partner, or the managing partner in a profit-sharing venture; and those conducted by someone whose property is subject to another person’s rights, such as a sick person.

It is also needed for purchases required in fulfilment of duties to Allah Most High—such as water for purification, clothing to cover oneself in prayer, and equipment for Hajj—or duties owed to other people, as in obligatory transactions, such as … Its basis is analogy and comparing a thing with its like.

It is justice itself, and the very conduct recognized as right that is encompassed by His words:

“He enjoins upon them what is right,”

and His words:

“Enjoin what is right.”

This is agreed upon among Muslims—indeed, among all the people of the earth—for it involves comparison between particular instances to which rulings apply, rather than between categories of rulings. It is part of the equity for which Allah sent the messengers and revealed the scriptures. It means repaying good with its like, and evil with its like, as Allah Most High says:

“Is the reward for good anything but good?”

And He says:

“When you are greeted with a greeting, respond with a better greeting or return it in kind.”

And He says:

“The recompense for an evil deed is an evil deed like it.”

And He says:

“Prescribed for you is retribution in cases of killing.”

And He says:

“So whoever attacks you, retaliate against him in the same measure as his attack upon you.”

And He says:

“If you retaliate, then retaliate to the same extent as the injury inflicted upon you.”

However, repaying good with its like is obligatory justice, while giving more is a recommended act of kindness

---

## Page 14777

and giving less is forbidden injustice. Repaying evil in kind is permissible justice; exceeding it is forbidden, while exacting less is a recommended act of kindness. Thus, injustice belongs to the wrongdoer, justice to the one who takes a middle course, and recommended kindness to the one foremost in good deeds.

The community comprises three categories: one who wrongs himself, one who takes a middle course, and one who is foremost in good deeds. Jurists often find it difficult to determine precisely what constitutes fair compensation, in both kind and amount, and disagree over this in many cases. This is because it varies according to places, times, circumstances, the consideration given, what is received in return, and the parties to the exchange.

We therefore say: “Fair compensation” is the equivalent of the consideration customarily stipulated, known as the price and the customary rate. The consideration stipulated in contracts is of two kinds: one that people habitually use and recognize, which is the familiar, customary compensation; and another that is exceptional, arising from intense desire, an intention to cause harm, or other factors.

Fair compensation is also called the “fair price” and the “equivalent,” because it corresponds to the equivalent of the item itself and is then assessed at the price of its like. Its basis is therefore people’s choice, will, and desire.

For this reason, many scholars have said: “Fair value is what a thing is worth in the estimation of those who desire it.” The qualification “under customary circumstances” must be added. Its basis, then, is people’s will and desire. Reason establishes that a thing is to be treated like its equivalent. This belongs to the justice, analogical reasoning, comparison, and drawing of parallels that Allah has made innate in His servants.

---

## Page 14778

Thus, when it is known that people are ordinarily willing to give a certain amount for something, that amount is known to be its fair price—the value of the thing and of its equivalent. But if that desire and willingness serve a forbidden purpose, such as making idols, crosses, or the like, then that compensation is forbidden under Islamic law.

Fair compensation under Islamic law is therefore assessed by reference to lawfully stipulated consideration: the stipulation must itself be lawful, meaning permissible. A prohibited stipulation, however, may be prohibited because of the kind of thing involved, such as wine or swine; because the item is intended for a forbidden use, such as grapes for someone who will make wine from them, or a boy for someone who will commit sexual immorality with him; or because the stipulated amount is merely for boasting and ostentation, with no intention of paying it, or harms one of the contracting parties. Examples include dowries that are not intended to be paid and that burden the husband until a specified date, as is the practice of uncouth people among both the Bedouins and settled populations, and similar cases. Such a stipulation is not lawful and therefore cannot serve as a legally valid benchmark for assessing an equivalent where no consideration has been stipulated.

Reflect on this, for it is useful, particularly regarding these burdensome deferred dowries that Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. Some jurists take them as a benchmark in matters such as the requirement that an unmarried woman be married only for her customary dowry. They consequently regard abandoning what Allah has forbidden as contrary to Islamic law, on the grounds that it constitutes the customary dowry—even in cases such as a father arranging the marriage. This, then, is a principle . . . (1)

Once this is understood, people’s desire varies greatly and takes many forms, for it

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 14779

varies with the abundance or scarcity of the thing sought. When it is scarce, people desire it more than when it is plentiful. It also varies with the number of people seeking it: something sought by many rises in price, unlike something sought by few. It likewise varies with the extent and intensity of need. When need is widespread and pressing, the value rises to a degree it does not reach when need is limited and weak.

It also depends on the other party to the transaction. If he is financially able and religiously upright, people are willing to transact with him at a low price at which they would not sell to someone they suspect of being unable to pay, delaying payment, or denying the debt.

In our school, a fully solvent person is one who has the financial means to pay, is reliable in his word, and can be brought before a court in person. This is expressly stated by Ahmad. Although the jurists have taken this consideration into account when assessing the customary dowry, it must also be taken into account when assessing a fair price and fair remuneration.

It also depends on the consideration offered. A lower price may be accepted when payment is in a widely circulating currency than when it is in another currency that circulates less widely. Such is the case with dirhams and dinars in Damascus at the present time, where payment in dirhams is customary.

The purpose of contracts is for each party to receive what is due from the other. When the party offering payment or delivery is able to fulfil his obligation and keeps his commitment, contracting with him achieves the intended purpose. This is unlike the case of someone whose ability or reliability is deficient.

People vary in their ability to fulfil their obligations and their faithfulness to their commitments. This is the goodness mentioned in His words:

“Then make a contract for their emancipation with them, if you know there is good in them.”

They explained this as the ability to earn and faithfulness to one’s commitments.

---

## Page 14780

This applies to the seller and buyer, the lessor and lessee, and the husband and wife. The goods sold may be present or absent, and the price of goods that are present is lower than that of goods that are absent. Likewise, the buyer may be able to pay immediately because he has the money, or he may not have it but intend to borrow it or sell the goods. The price charged to the former is lower.

Similarly, the lessor may be able to deliver the use to which the lessee is entitled under the contract, allowing him to enjoy it without difficulty. Alternatively, the lessee may be unable to enjoy it without incurring trouble and expense. Such is the case with villages repeatedly visited by oppressors, whether powerful officials or thieves, or frequented by beasts of prey. Their value is not equal to that of land requiring no such effort.

Indeed, some properties can be fully utilized only by someone powerful enough to ward off harm, allocating some of their proceeds to his helpers and supporters. Someone else may derive only a slight benefit from such a property, whereas a person with power obtains its full benefit because he can protect it from harm.

Accordingly, the benefit derived from what is rented—and indeed from what is purchased, from a spouse, and from other things—varies. Someone with the necessary means may derive many times the benefit that another person obtains, because he can secure the conditions that increase its usefulness and remove the obstacles that prevent its use. Yet the greater benefit resulting from the conditions he has established and the obstacles he has removed does not necessarily warrant including that benefit in the valuation, unless a comparable case is assumed. Land may command only a small rent because of obstacles posed by aggressors or beasts of prey, or because obtaining its benefit requires strength and money.

---

## Page 14781

He was asked:

A man is owed one hundred and eighty by another man. Someone said to him, “Will you sell it for one hundred and fifty?” Is that permissible?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. If the debt is payable at a future date and he sells it for a smaller amount payable immediately, this is usury. But if it is already due and he takes part of it and releases the debtor from the remainder, his reward rests with Allah, and he has done good.

He was asked:

A debt arising from a salam sale—payment in advance for later delivery—has fallen due, but the recipient of the advance payment does not have the means to fulfil it. He says, “Sell it to me for more than the original price.”

He replied:

It is not permissible to sell a debt arising from a salam sale before taking possession of what is owed, nor to sell a debt for a debt. Thus, this is forbidden on two grounds.

There is also a third ground: if he sold him dirhams for dirhams, as in the case of someone who makes a usurious sale on deferred terms, he may not accept a substitute for its price

---

## Page 14782

in the form of something that may not be exchanged for it on deferred terms. Likewise, someone who purchases a debt on deferred terms may not accept in its place anything that may not be exchanged on deferred terms for the price paid for that debt. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If a man incurs a debt, then becomes insolvent and dies, is he still held liable for it?

He replied:

Yes, the creditor will recover what he is owed, for a debt must be repaid. Thus, it is authentically reported:

“A martyr is forgiven everything except debt.”

---

## Page 14783

Chapter on Loans

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

A man lent another man one thousand dirhams, then demanded repayment. The borrower said, “I am insolvent. I will buy some goods from you at an increased price in return for your allowing me six months’ respite.” Is that permissible?

He replied:

It is established that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A loan combined with a sale is not lawful.”

Thus, if he sells to him and lends him money, that is among the things Allah and His Messenger have forbidden. Both parties deserve discretionary punishment if the prohibition has reached them. The loaned money and the goods must be returned to their respective owners. If that is impossible, the lender is entitled only to the equivalent of the loan and the seller only to the fair market value of the goods. He is not entitled to anything beyond that. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14784

He was asked:

A man wishes to borrow some dirhams from another man for one year to improve his property and purchase land. Yet no one will provide his money without making a profit. How can the lender earn a profit lawfully?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. One way is for the property owner to lease out his property, or part of it, receive the rent in advance, and use it to make the improvements. If part of the property is in ruins and he stipulates that the tenant carry out specified repairs, that is permissible. This is a lawful means by which both parties can achieve their aims.

But if they agree to exchange dirhams for dirhams payable at a later date, and employ some device to accomplish this, Allah will bless neither party. An example is selling part of the property through a bay al-amānah (trust sale), on condition that the seller subsequently repurchase the property for more than the sale price. This is among the forms of usury that Allah and His Messenger have forbidden.

If, however, the person providing the funds has goods that the other party needs, such as rubble for renovating the bathhouse, the latter may purchase those goods on deferred payment, with whatever profit they agree upon. Nevertheless, the seller should not make a profit beyond what is customary in comparable transactions.

---

## Page 14785

He was asked:

A man lends another man money, but the borrower refuses to repay him except in another town, requiring the lender to travel and transport the money. Must the lender bear the travel expenses?

He replied:

The borrower must repay the lender in the town where he borrowed the money, without burdening him with any travel or transport expenses. If he says, “I will repay you only in another town,” he is liable for the reasonable expenses the lender incurs.

He was asked:

If a man lends another man dirhams to be repaid in another town, is that permissible or not?

He replied:

If he lends him dirhams to be repaid in another town—for example, the lender wishes to transfer the dirhams to another town, while the borrower has dirhams there but needs dirhams in the lender’s town, so he borrows from him and writes him a “bill of transfer,” that is, a document addressed to the borrower’s town—this is valid according to

---

## Page 14786

one of the two scholarly opinions.

Another view holds that it is prohibited because it is a loan that yields a benefit, and any loan that yields a benefit constitutes usury. The correct view, however, is that it is permissible: the borrower sees the benefit of avoiding the hazards of the journey when transferring his dirhams to that town. The borrower also benefits by making repayment in that town and avoiding the hazards of the journey. Thus, both parties benefit from this loan. The Lawgiver does not prohibit what benefits people and promotes their welfare; He prohibits only what harms them.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Is it permissible to lend debased dirhams and receive repayment by count?

He replied:

It is permissible to lend debased dirhams if their degree of debasement is uniform, as with the dirhams people commonly use in their transactions. Likewise, if the degree of debasement varies slightly, the correct view is that lending them is permissible—for example, dirhams whose stated fineness is seventy and others whose fineness is sixty-nine.

The correct view is also that it is permissible to lend wheat and other grains, even when mixed with soil and barley, for the “rules governing loans” are more lenient than the “rules governing sales.” Hence, according to the correct view, it is permissible to lend bread by count and to lend leaven

---

## Page 14787

even though lending it by count is not permissible. In a loan, it is permissible to repay with something better than what was borrowed, provided this was not stipulated, as in the report:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—borrowed a camel and repaid with a better one. He said, ‘The best people are those who are best in repaying their debts.’”

Likewise, according to the more correct of the two scholarly opinions, it is permissible to lend eggs and similar items counted individually. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—borrowed an animal, and animals vary more than eggs do.

He was asked:

A soldier holds a land grant and comes to me where his farmers are, and they offer him food. May he eat?

He replied:

If he eats and compensates them for what he has eaten, there is no harm. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A master craftsman is owed money by a worker whom he employs on that account. May he take a portion of the worker’s wages?

He replied:

The master may not pay the worker less than the customary wage for someone like him because of the money he has lent him. If he does so, even with the worker’s consent, he is engaging in usury, committing injustice and sin, and deserving discretionary punishment. Nor may he treat him oppressively when collecting his debt.

---

## Page 14788

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man holds a land grant that yields him four hundred irdabbs. He gives the farmers a cultivation advance of nearly two hundred irdabbs, which they record at seven hundred dirhams. Is this usury?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Every loan that brings a benefit is usury—for example, when someone enters into a sale or lease with the lender and gives him preferential terms because of his loan. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“It is not lawful to combine a loan with a sale.”

If someone lends another one hundred dirhams and sells him goods worth one hundred for one hundred and fifty, that increase is usury. The same applies if he lends him one hundred dirhams and employs him for two dirhams a day when his work merits three. Indeed, the practice of many master craftsmen, who lend money to their workers so that they will accept lower wages, is usury.

Likewise, if the fair rent for land, a house or a shop is one hundred dirhams, but he lets it for one hundred and fifty because of the one hundred he has lent the tenant, this is usury.

As for the “cultivation advance,” it is not purely a loan, for he stipulates as a condition of it that

---

## Page 14789

he sow it in the land, whether he is a cultivator or a tenant. It is thus as though he leased him land, supplying the means to cultivate it, for the stipulated rent. When the lease expires, he recovers the land; the cultivation advance is analogous to this. Scholars disagree about this arrangement.

Some say:

The benefit here is shared by the lender and the borrower, for the lender has an interest in having his land cultivated. This resembles a “suftaja,” whereby someone lends money in one town to receive repayment in another: the lender avoids the risks of the journey and the expense of transporting the money, while the borrower benefits from the loan. Likewise, with the “cultivation advance,” the provider does not intend to obtain an increase over what he advanced. Rather, he needs to lease out his land, and the other party needs to rent it. The arrangement can only serve its purpose fully if the lessor provides an advance to meet the tenant’s need. In reality, the purpose of the advance is not lending, but supplying him with seed, just as he might supply him with cattle.

Others classify it as a loan that brings a benefit. The cultivation advance merely completes the benefit derived from the land, just as when cattle are supplied with the land for ploughing, so that he has leased out both land and cattle. This is undoubtedly permissible. However, the advance itself does not remain intact; instead, he recovers its equivalent, just as the equivalent of the capital is recovered in a profit-sharing partnership. This is why those scholars who prohibit the arrangement have prohibited it: in a lease, the very same asset is returned to the lessor after the tenant has enjoyed its use. Something of this kind is not permissible in

---

## Page 14790

a loan, in which only the equivalent must be repaid, without any addition.

If he were to rent him wheat or the like for him to use and then return its equivalent together with rent, this would be a loan stipulating an addition to the equivalent repaid.

This disagreement concerns a case in which he leases the land to him at its fair rental value and provides him with a cultivation advance or similar assistance by way of a loan. This is like renting him a shop in which to pursue a craft or trade and lending him what he needs to establish that craft or trade. But if he charges more than the fair rental value because of the loan, there is no good in this; rather, it is a loan that yields usurious gain.

---

## Page 14791

Chapter on Pledges

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man pledged his house to another man as security for a sum payable on a specified date. When payment fell due, he was unable to pay. The pledgee said, “Sell me the house on condition that, if you repay me, you may take it back for the price; and if you live in it, I will not charge you rent.” Is this sale valid? The buyer has also erected a structure above the house; what is the ruling concerning it?

He replied:

This is not a valid sale. Rather, the house must be returned to its owner, and he must pay the outstanding debt. The buyer is to be credited for the construction he has undertaken. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man owns half an orchard, while the remainder belongs to another man. He borrowed his partner’s half to pledge it as security for a debt payable on a specified date, informing him of the amount of the debt and its term. His partner lent it to him, and he pledged the orchard to the creditor. He then redeemed his own share and sold it to the creditor for a specified price, and they offset the purchase price against the debt. Is he entitled to do this? And does the share of

---

## Page 14792

the lender remain pledged as security for the remaining debt, or may he revoke the loan at any time?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, the debtor may sell his share to discharge his debt, as described. If he sells it and it is subject to the right of pre-emption, his partner may acquire it by exercising that right. As for the lender’s share, it remains pledged as security for the remaining debt, just as before. The lender may not revoke a loan of this kind, because the pledgee’s right is attached to it. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man had property pledged as security for a sum payable on a specified date. When payment fell due, he paid the creditor what he was owed except for one hundred dirhams. The original document was then torn up, and a written acknowledgment was drawn up for the one hundred dirhams, without mentioning the pledge again. Do these remaining one hundred dirhams retain any claim upon the pledged property?

He replied:

If the debtor pays part of the debt while some remains outstanding, the property remains pledged as security for the outstanding amount, unless something occurs that requires its release, such as the pledgee releasing it, or something similar.

---

## Page 14793

He was asked:

A man pledged his house, then made a witnessed acknowledgment that he had given the house to his wife in settlement of her entitlement ten years earlier. Does this invalidate the pledge? May the pledgee rent out the house?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

A pledgor’s acknowledgment is not accepted insofar as it would invalidate the pledge, even if it is said that, when he makes an acknowledgment concerning the pledged property, the person in whose favor it is made undoubtedly has the right to invalidate the pledge on the strength of that acknowledgment. For if he acknowledges that the pledged property belonged to someone else and that he pledged it without that person’s permission, this alone does not invalidate the pledge. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man is owed a debt by someone who has given him property as security for it. The debt is due, and the creditor needs his money. May he sell the pledged property or not?

He replied:

If the debtor has authorized him to sell it, he may do so. Otherwise, the judge should sell it, if possible, and pay him what he is owed from the proceeds.

Some scholars say that, if this is not possible, he may hand it over to a trustworthy person to sell it, taking the precaution of having this witnessed, and recover what he is owed from the proceeds. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14794

He was asked:

A man instructed his employee to pledge an item with a particular person, but the employee pledged it with someone else, and the item went missing. Its owner swore that he would not employ him unless he brought it back, believing that it had not been lost. He later discovered that it had been lost. Would he break his oath if he employed him?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If, when he swore, he believed that the pledged item still existed and had not been lost, and he swore in order to make the employee bring it back, then he does not break his oath in these circumstances. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An item was pledged with a man as security for a sum due after a specified period, which has now elapsed. With its owner’s permission, he then pledged it with another person as security for that sum. The second pledgor has demanded payment of the amount secured by the pledge and had the debtor imprisoned over it, but the debtor has no means of redeeming the property. May it be sold or not?

He replied:

Yes, the pledged property may be sold to recover the debt in these circumstances, particularly since the first pledgor authorized its being pledged as security for the debt. It may therefore be sold

---

## Page 14795

in these circumstances to recover this debt from its proceeds. If it can be sold and the debt recovered from its proceeds, the debtor may not be imprisoned. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman was taken captive. She owned property and had a husband and a brother. They pledged her property to secure a sum of money for her ransom. Her brother took the money and went in search of her, but found that she had been freed without payment. She returned to her hometown, while her brother stayed behind to attend to his own affairs. When she arrived and found her property pledged as security for the money, she said, “Has my property been pledged without my permission?” She denied that her brother had given her any of the money. Is the pledge binding upon her or not?

He replied:

They bear no liability in these circumstances. Rather, what her brother received must be returned to her, and the pledge on her property released. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14796

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man lent his paternal uncle five thousand dirhams. His cousin then borrowed money from other people, purchased five male slaves and a female slave, and drew up a document stating that the five male slaves, but not the female slave, were pledged as security to the creditors. He then sold the male slaves and handed over [the proceeds] to those who held them in pledge. The creditor owed the five thousand then purchased the female slave in settlement of the debt owed to him. The creditors who had received the proceeds from the male slaves detained him, seeking to take her as well towards repayment of their debts. Are they entitled to do this, even though he was neither a guarantor nor a surety?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If the female slave was not pledged to the creditors of the second debt, then, by agreement of the Muslims, those creditors had no exclusive claim to her over the other creditors. How, then, could they have such a claim when she had already been transferred in settlement of a debt owed to others? Equitable repayment among creditors, after an insolvent debtor has been legally barred from disposing of his assets, is obligatory by agreement of the imams. Before that restriction is imposed, however, there is disagreement.

---

## Page 14797

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man was owed a debt by someone. He found the debtor’s son riding a mare and took the mare from him. The debtor came to the man holding the mare, and the creditor demanded repayment. The debtor said, “Keep this mare with you until I repay your debt.”

The creditor replied, “You owe me silver, not a mare. This is a living creature, and life and death are in the hands of Allah, glorified and exalted be He.”

The debtor said, “May Allah absolve you of liability for this silver. Whatever happens, I shall be responsible.”

The mare remained with the creditor for several days. He fed and watered her but did not ride her. Then, by Allah’s decree and ordainment, she miscarried a dead foal, which gave no cry at birth. Another man, other than the debtor, came forward, claiming that the mare belonged to him and making a claim for the miscarried foal.

The creditor said, “I do not know you, and you have no dealings with me. I will swear to you that I did not ride the mare, that no one rode her while she was with me, and that I did not strike her.”

Must either the creditor or the man who pledged the mare pay the value of the miscarried foal, or not? How much would the foal be worth?

He replied:

If you took possession of the mare from her owner without right, he is entitled to compensation for any diminution in her value, namely the difference between the valuations. If the person who took possession of her was a usurper acting unlawfully,

---

## Page 14798

ultimate liability rests with him. But if he was misled and the loss was not caused by him, ultimate liability rests with the first man, who misled him and undertook to indemnify him against any resulting liability. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man held property as security for a debt. Its owner then sold it, and the pledgee wished to establish the validity of the pledge agreement and have the sale rescinded. Against whom should he bring his claim?

He replied:

Property subject to a binding pledge may not be sold without the pledgee’s permission. The pledgee may demand repayment from the debtor who pledged it, if the debt has fallen due. He may also demand the return of the pledged property or satisfaction of his claim from it. He may pursue either its seller or its buyer. However, if the buyer was misled, ultimate liability rests with the seller, who must compensate for the rental value of the property sold. If the buyer knew the circumstances, he is a wrongdoer and is liable for the value of its use.

---

## Page 14799

He was also asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

A man pledged a belt, and the pledgee used it, breaking its strap and wearing away its coating. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If its value has diminished through the pledgee’s use, he must compensate for the diminution caused by that use. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 14800

Chapter on Guarantees

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man acted as guarantor for a particular person, and the creditor demanded payment from him, although the guarantor lacked the means to pay. The creditor brought a claim against him, and he pleaded insolvency. Must he produce evidence, or is his statement accepted upon his oath?

He replied:

If the guarantor was not previously known to possess any assets and pleads insolvency, his statement concerning this is accepted upon his oath, and he need not produce evidence. This is the established position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. It is also the position of a group of Hanafi scholars, according to what they have reported concerning the school of Abū Hanīfah. The contrary has also been reported; nevertheless, this is in fact his school’s position, for he does not require him to produce evidence if it becomes clear that the circumstances are as they have described. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14801

He was asked:

A man guaranteed another person’s debt without that person’s permission. Is this permissible?

He replied:

Yes, a guarantee of another person’s debt is valid without the debtor’s permission, and the creditor may demand payment from the guarantor. However, if the guarantor pays without the debtor’s permission, may he seek reimbursement from the debtor? The scholars hold two views on this.

One view is that he may seek reimbursement. This is the position of Mālik and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

The other view is that he may not seek reimbursement. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī. And Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man under his father’s guardianship acted, without his father’s consent, as guarantor for some people who had leased an orchard for four years. They subsequently terminated the lease for which he had guaranteed them. A sum remained owing from them, for which a written acknowledgment of debt was drawn up against them separately from the lease. Their guarantor has now been called upon to pay. May payment be demanded of him after the lease has been rescinded?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he guaranteed them, in a manner valid under Islamic law, for their liability in respect of

---

## Page 14802

the debt, the creditor may demand that the guarantor pay that debt or whatever remains of it, but may not demand anything beyond what he guaranteed. If he was under his father’s guardianship, his guarantee was not valid. The guarantor may demand payment from the debtors when payment is demanded of him.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man had assumed financial liability for certain properties, which were then established to belong to someone else. He had no cash, but owned property sufficient to cover the value and more. May the creditor have the guarantor imprisoned before that property is sold? What if the guarantor is imprisoned and asks to be allowed out under guard, or to hand over the property to someone who will sell it so that the creditor may receive his due?

He replied:

If he offers to sell his property in the customary manner, he may not be punished by imprisonment or otherwise. Punishment is imposed only for neglecting an obligation or committing a prohibited act. If he offers to discharge his obligation to pay, he has not neglected an obligation. However, if the creditor fears that he may abscond or fail to discharge his liability, he may take precautions, either by remaining with him or by having someone keep watch over him. Placing him under guard is a form of keeping watch over him.

If the judge has imprisoned him and he then offers to sell his property and asks to be enabled to do so, he must be allowed to do so: either by going out under guard or by appointing someone to sell

---

## Page 14803

the property and hand it over, if this cannot be done without allowing him out. In short, he may not be punished by imprisonment when he has not neglected his obligation; precautions may, however, be taken by keeping watch over him.

He was asked:

About a contractor whose agreement provided that people’s animals would be lodged at the al-Barāh inn, and who collected a fixed charge for their lodging and fodder, but then increased that charge.

He replied:

Neither the prophetic Sharia nor the sultan’s administrative regulations permit the contractor to alter the established arrangement or impose charges on people that the authorities had not previously prescribed. Rather, he must be [restrained] from doing so, punished for it, and required to return whatever money he has taken from people without authorization.

As for the ruling of the Sharia, an animal’s owner may lodge wherever he wishes, provided this entails no harm prohibited by the Sharia, and may feed the animal himself. He must not be compelled to rent accommodation for it or purchase anything from any particular person. Even if he is compelled to do so, he may not be charged more than the fair market price. Taking the excess is like taking meat from a pig that has died of itself: it is forbidden on two counts. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 14804

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Does someone who draws up market guarantees and other documents that are impermissible under the Sharia incur sin? He records what those present consent to and bears witness to it. If this is impermissible, scribes are inevitably involved in such matters; do they incur sin thereby or not?

He replied:

A market guarantee—whereby a guarantor guarantees the debts a merchant may incur and the tangible property he receives for which he is liable—is valid. This constitutes guaranteeing obligations that have not yet arisen and guaranteeing an unspecified liability. This is permissible according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad ibn Hanbal.

The Book provides evidence for this, as in His words:

“Whoever brings it shall have a camel-load, and I guarantee it.”

Al-Shāfiʿī considers it invalid. Nevertheless, a scribe may draw it up and a witness may attest to it, even if he does not consider it permissible, because this is a matter open to scholarly judgment, and the ruler adjudicates according to whichever of the two opinions he considers correct.

---

## Page 14805

He was asked:

A man provided a market guarantee for another man with his permission. When payment was demanded from him, the debtor fled, leaving the guarantor unable to produce him. The guarantor incurred financial losses as a result. Is he entitled to recover from the debtor what he lost in this matter?

He replied:

He is entitled to recover what he spent on account of his guarantee, provided that the expenditure was reasonable.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man guaranteed another man’s liability for a sum of money. When the sum fell due, the creditor seized the guarantor and had him imprisoned. The creditor then sought out the debtor, apprehended him and had him imprisoned. Both the guarantor and the debtor remained in prison. Is it permissible to imprison the guarantor?

He replied:

The position of Abū Hanīfa, Muhammad, al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad is that the creditor may demand payment from whichever of the two he wishes. Once he has received payment in full, he has no further claim. He may also demand payment from both of them.

---

## Page 14806

He was asked:

A debt was claimed against a man’s son, who then went into hiding, so payment was sought from his father. What is the ruling?

He replied:

If the father has not guaranteed his son’s debt and holds none of his son’s money, he may not be pursued for what his son owes. However, if the father can help the creditor have his son brought forward by disclosing his whereabouts or similar means, he must do so. Otherwise, he bears no responsibility. It is unlawful to demand anything from him on his son’s account, and the ruler must protect him from wrongful treatment.

He was asked:

A scribe worked for an emir who borrowed money from someone. The emir forcibly compelled the scribe to guarantee the debt personally, and he did so, although he was under his father’s guardianship with his legal capacity restricted. Is he bound by the guarantee he gave or not?

He replied:

If it is established by an admission and evidence, or by his handwriting, that he gave the guarantee, he is bound by it, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled:

“The guarantor is liable.”

If, however, it is established that he was under legal restrictions and was not entitled to manage his own affairs independently, his guarantee is invalid;

---

## Page 14807

but the contract is not invalidated merely by his claim that he was under legal restrictions.

If he says, “The creditor knows that I was under legal restrictions,” he may require the creditor to swear an oath. Likewise, if he claims coercion, he may require the creditor to swear an oath.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A guarantor is required by the ruler to pay money in connection with wedding celebrations involving certain objectionable practices, such as free women singing to non-mahram men and the like. If the ruler orders that activity stopped, he stops it but demands from the guarantor the money he undertook to pay only in connection with that activity, since the guarantee became binding on that account. The person on whose behalf the guarantee was given believes that this was not included in the guarantee, while the guarantor believes that it was, since those who preceded him customarily included it in their guarantees, and the guarantee was concluded on the basis of the previously prevailing circumstances and customary practice.

He replied:

Wronging the guarantor by demanding from him something he does not owe under the contract he entered into—even if that contract is unlawful—is more strictly prohibited than a non-mahram woman singing to men. This is because injustice is prohibited both by reason and by Islamic law, whereas this singing was prohibited only because it may lead to unlawful sexual intercourse, just as looking at a non-mahram woman was prohibited. Moreover, there is an anomalous dissenting opinion concerning it, and the Companions used to listen to the singing of slave women at

---

## Page 14808

wedding celebrations, just as they would look at slave women, since seeing them and hearing their voices involved no temptation. This, then, is less seriously prohibited than deliberately committing injustice; the lesser of two prohibited acts must not be prevented by committing the graver one.

As for men singing to men, no report has reached us that this occurred during the time of the Companions. There remains the matter of women singing to women at weddings. As for free women singing to men with a tambourine, this is sanctioned on joyous occasions, as in the hadith of the woman who made a vow and sang while accompanying herself with it.

However, engaging a female singer to perform for women and men is objectionable in all circumstances, unlike a woman for whom singing is not a profession. The same applies to receiving payment for it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man undertook a financial guarantee for someone to whose family he belonged, the guarantor being married to that man’s daughter. The guarantor spent five months in prison and incurred expenses of three hundred dirhams. Is the man on whose behalf he gave the guarantee liable for the expenses he incurred during his imprisonment?

He replied:

Yes. Whatever the guarantor was compelled to bear because of the wrongdoing of the person on whose behalf he gave the guarantee—for example, if that person was able to pay but absented himself until the creditor had the guarantor detained and made him incur those expenses—the guarantor is entitled to recover it from the person who wronged him.

---

## Page 14809

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A group of people had someone stand surety to a man, the guarantor’s undertaking being to produce the person concerned at the court’s prison. Can the man require them to bring that person to his house?

He replied:

If the guarantor hands him over to the creditor in the court’s prison, he is thereby discharged and is not obliged to have him taken out of prison for the creditor. Rather, the creditor may then claim and recover what is owed to him in accordance with Islamic law, even while the debtor is imprisoned. The judge may have him brought out of prison to litigate with his creditor, then return him there. None of the leading Muslim jurists holds that the guarantor is obliged to bring him to the creditor while he is in the court’s prison.

He also replied:

If the guarantor whom they charged with securing the person’s appearance hands him over in the court’s prison, they are discharged from the guarantee. Those to whom he owes obligations may then recover their due from him. If they need to bring a claim against him, they must be allowed to have him brought before the court and pursue their claim against him. This is the position of the leading Muslim jurists, including Mālik, Ahmad, and others.

---

## Page 14810

He was asked:

A camel driver tethered his camels on grazing land, each location having its own guards. One of his camels was then stolen, and none of the guards was visibly present. Is any liability incurred, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If they were hired to guard the camels, they are liable for whatever was lost through their negligence. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

A boy of discerning age incurred a debt, and his father and three others stood surety for him with his permission. His father then went away, and the creditor demanded the money from one of the guarantors and compelled him to pay it. May the guarantor who paid recover that amount from the boy, from the property of his absent father, or from his fellow guarantors? Or is the amount he paid simply lost to him?

He replied:

He may recover it from the person for whom he stood surety, for his father’s guarantee on his behalf implies

---

## Page 14811

that he acted with his father’s permission. The debt is therefore binding on him, and the guarantee on his behalf is valid. If, in reality, he borrowed on his father’s behalf at his father’s instruction, then the debt is the father’s responsibility. Otherwise, the guarantor may require the father to swear that the borrowing was not on his behalf.

Abū al-ʿAbbās was asked:

What about someone who handed his debtor over to a jailer, who then neglected to guard him, allowing him to escape?

He replied:

The jailer, and anyone similarly entrusted with custody of the debtor’s person, stands in the same position as someone who guarantees another’s appearance: he must produce the opposing party. If he cannot produce him—as when the person whose appearance has been guaranteed fails to appear—he is liable for what that person owes, according to our view and that of Mālik.

---

## Page 14812

Chapter on the Transfer of Debt

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What about someone who transferred a debt against a dower that was immediately payable, then collected payment from the new debtor? Is the transfer valid in these circumstances? Is this receipt of payment valid, discharging the new debtor’s liability? May the creditor demand repayment from the transferor of the amount collected?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, the transfer is valid provided its conditions are met. The transferor has no right to collect the transferred debt after the transfer, and payment to the transferor does not discharge the new debtor’s liability unless the creditor instructed that payment be made in this way.

The creditor may demand payment from either the new debtor, to recover the debt charged to him, or the person who collected his debt without his permission. Even if the wrongful taker’s unauthorized receipt is comparable to the wrongful seizure of jointly owned property, setting apart a specific portion through wrongful seizure is like partition; thus, he has no right to demand partition from the wrongful taker.

The new debtor may recover from the transferor whatever the latter collected from him without right. However, the opposing party may require the person in whose favor the acknowledgment was made to swear that its true substance accords with its outward terms. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> End of Volume Twenty-Nine.
